| Lynch syndrome | Mismatch repair (MLH1, MSH2, MSH6, PMS2) | Microsatellite instability; familial colorectal and endometrial cancer |
| Xeroderma pigmentosum | Nucleotide excision repair | Thymine dimers aren't fixed; severe, long-lasting sunburn; high rate of fatal skin cancer; avoid UV light |
| BRCA1 mutation | Double-strand break repair | The error-prone pathway takes over and the genome becomes unstable |
| Fanconi anemia | Double-strand break repair | Aplastic anemia, bone marrow failure, absent thumb, café-au-lait spots |
| Ataxia telangiectasia | ATM | DNA damage isn't detected, so the cell cycle isn't halted; radiation sensitivity; ataxia, telangiectasias, IgA deficiency, high AFP; lymphoma or leukemia |
| Spo11 defect | Meiotic double-strand breaks | Recombination can't start; sterility in men |

## Management of Lynch syndrome

Aspirin, endoscopy surveillance, and immunotherapy.

> Lynch syndrome is a mismatch repair defect that causes microsatellite instability.

Library: Homologous recombination
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / DNA Recombination

## Functions of recombination

- Repairs damaged DNA and restarts stalled replication forks
- Segregates chromosomes in meiosis
- Creates new alleles and genetic variation

## Key findings

- Needs a long stretch of sequence similarity, so it only happens between homologous DNA molecules
- Most important in G2
- Makes permanent, heritable changes in a relatively conservative way

## Steps

1. A DNA break allows strand invasion into the homologous chromosome
2. Strand invasion forms a Holliday junction
3. Branch migration moves the junction along
4. Resolution produces recombinant or non-recombinant products

## Gene conversion

If the two alleles differ, the paired strands contain mismatches. Mismatch repair can then convert one allele into the other, lose an allele, or create a new one.

## Sources of breaks

- Mitotic cells: ionizing radiation, DNA-damaging agents, replication errors, failed topoisomerase reactions, transposon movement
- Meiotic cells: Spo11, an endonuclease expressed in prophase I, makes double-strand breaks to start recombination

## Disease

- Decreased recombination can cause nondisjunction and trisomy, such as Down syndrome
- Repetitive DNA is a hotspot for recombination errors (ectopic recombination)

> Reduced meiotic recombination can lead to nondisjunction and trisomy.

Library: Non-homologous end joining and BRCA1
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / DNA Recombination

## Key findings

- Also called illegitimate recombination
- Most important in G1
- The primary double-strand break repair pathway
- Modifies and ligates broken ends with little or no homology, so it can cause insertions, deletions, and translocations

## BRCA1

BRCA1 promotes the error-free pathways (error-free NHEJ or homologous recombination). It binds DNA and prevents excessive nuclease activity that could cause more errors. When BRCA1 is lost, error-prone repair takes over and the genome becomes unstable.

> NHEJ joins ends without a template, which can cause deletions and translocations.

Library: Transposons
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / DNA Recombination

## Key findings

Transposons move sections of DNA to random new positions. Many came from past viruses that lost the env gene they needed to reinfect cells.

| Type | Mechanism | Share of genome | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| DNA transposons | Cut and paste ("jumping genes") | 3% | Stay as DNA; no reverse transcription; leave a double-strand break |
| LTR (viral-like) retrotransposons | Copy and paste | 11% | Human endogenous retroviruses |
| Poly-A retrotransposons | Copy and paste | 31% | Can create pseudogenes |

- Retrotransposons (42% overall) keep the original copy in place and are the major source of repetitive DNA
- Autonomous transposons code for their own enzymes; nonautonomous ones depend on enzymes from autonomous transposons
- Processed pseudogenes form when reverse transcriptase copies a cellular mRNA, making a non-functional copy

## Effects

- Insertion into the middle of a gene
- Double-strand breaks when DNA transposons move, which may not be repaired well
- Multiple copies of a sequence raise the chance of unequal crossover during recombination
- A common source of new mutations and gene rearrangements

> Retrotransposons copy and paste through an RNA intermediate and are the main source of repetitive DNA.

Library: Bacterial transformation and conjugation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Bacterial Genetics and Virulence Determinants

## Transformation

- Uptake of free DNA; sensitive to external DNase
- Gram-positive bacteria need a competence factor to take up single-stranded donor DNA
- Gram-negative bacteria have no competence factors and need an uptake sequence on double-stranded DNA
- Homologous recombination between the new DNA and the chromosome produces the transformant

## Conjugation

- F+ cells are donors; the F plasmid encodes the conjugation pilus and other genes needed for transfer
- Hfr (high frequency of recombination) cells form when the plasmid integrates into the chromosome, so large segments of chromosome can be transferred
- Adhesins connect the two bacteria for exchange
- Homologous recombination is needed only when transferred DNA joins the main chromosome

> Transformation is blocked by DNase because the DNA is taken up naked.

Library: Transduction and lysogeny
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Bacterial Genetics and Virulence Determinants

## Phage types

| Phage | Behavior |
|---|---|
| Virulent (lytic) | Replicates inside the bacterium, then lyses it |
| Temperate (lysogenic) | Can become a quiescent prophage without lysing the cell |

## Transduction

| Type | How | What moves |
|---|---|---|
| Generalized | A defective phage packages bacterial DNA and delivers it; homologous recombination produces the transductant | Any segment of chromosomal DNA |
| Specialized | Rare incorrect excision of an integrated prophage carries neighboring bacterial DNA | Only DNA next to the prophage attachment site |

## Induction

Induction excises the phage genome from the bacterial chromosome, usually after DNA damage. Treating EHEC with antibiotics can worsen disease, because antibiotic-induced DNA damage triggers induction.

> Antibiotics can worsen EHEC by inducing the prophage.

Library: RNA polymerase basics
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Transcription

## Why transcribe

- It protects fragile DNA by sending out an expendable copy
- It amplifies output, since each mRNA can be translated many times

## RNA polymerase

- DNA-dependent RNA polymerase, found in all organisms and many viruses
- Builds a complementary, antiparallel RNA strand in the 5' → 3' direction
- Needs no primer (de novo synthesis)
- Has no proofreading, so the error rate is high, which is tolerable because many copies are made

## Steps

Initiation (DNA unwinds), elongation, termination, and in eukaryotes only, processing (5' cap, splicing, poly(A) tail).

> RNA polymerase needs no primer and doesn't proofread.

Library: Prokaryotic transcription
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Transcription

## Key findings

- Transcription and translation are coupled in space and time, and one RNA polymerase does all transcription
- The sigma factor binds specific promoters; core enzyme plus sigma factor makes the holoenzyme
- Bacterial promoters have two essential elements: the -10 Pribnow box and the -35 sequence
- Bacterial mRNA is polycistronic (one transcript can encode several proteins); eukaryotic mRNA is monocistronic

## Stages

1. Initiation: sigma factor recognizes the -10 and -35 sequences and forms the holoenzyme with the core enzyme
2. Elongation: RNA polymerase escapes the promoter by scrunching, and NusA replaces sigma factor
3. Termination: NusA dissociates and the polymerase is recycled

| Termination | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| Rho-dependent | Rho helicase moves 5' → 3' and separates the mRNA from the template |
| Rho-independent (intrinsic) | A G-C rich hairpin loop pulls the mRNA out of the polymerase |

## Drug link

Rifampin (rifampicin) binds the beta subunit of bacterial RNA polymerase and slows transcription. It's used for mycobacterial infections and meningococcal prophylaxis, turns body fluids red, induces CYP enzymes, and isn't used as monotherapy.

> Rifampin blocks the beta subunit of bacterial RNA polymerase.

Library: Eukaryotic transcription and RNA polymerase II
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Transcription

## Key findings

- Transcription (nucleus) and translation (cytoplasm) are separated
- Eukaryotes have several RNA polymerases, each for a distinct set of RNAs
- RNA polymerase II core promoter elements sit -25, -80, and -100 bp upstream of the start site
- Transcription factors bind specific DNA sequences and direct the polymerase

## Steps

1. Initiation: TATA-binding protein (TBP) starts RNA polymerase II assembly at the promoter. TFIIH helicase unwinds DNA to form the transcription bubble, and TFIIH kinase phosphorylates the carboxy-terminal domain (CTD), activating the polymerase
2. Elongation: after TFIIE and TFIIH leave, elongation factors bind to boost processivity and coordinate processing
3. Termination: elongation factors dissociate, termination factors act, and the CTD is dephosphorylated

## Alpha-amanitin

Alpha-amanitin from the Amanita phalloides (death cap) mushroom inhibits RNA polymerase II and shuts down transcription. Eating more than 3 grams can be fatal.

> Phosphorylation of the CTD by TFIIH activates RNA polymerase II.

Library: mRNA capping and polyadenylation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Transcription

## 5' cap

- A capping enzyme complex bound to RNA polymerase II adds 7-methylguanosine to the 5' end
- Only nuclear RNAs are capped; mitochondrial and prokaryotic mRNAs aren't
- Functions: regulates nuclear export, prevents degradation, promotes translation

## Poly(A) tail

- Cleavage signal: AAUAAA
- An endonuclease cuts the RNA, and poly(A) polymerase adds the tail
- Functions: helps terminate transcription, regulates nuclear export, prevents degradation, promotes translation
- The tail shortens in the cytoplasm, so shorter tails are translated less and degraded sooner
- Polyadenylation defects are linked to some cancers and muscular dystrophy

## Antiviral targets

Some viruses, such as influenza, "snatch" caps from host mRNA. Cap-binding inhibitors and endonuclease inhibitors target this step.

> The poly(A) signal is AAUAAA, and tail length controls how long an mRNA lasts.

Library: mRNA splicing and the spliceosome
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Transcription

## Key findings

- Splicing removes non-coding introns and joins coding exons
- The spliceosome is made of small nuclear ribonucleoproteins (snRNPs) built on five uridine-rich snRNAs: U1, U2, U4, U5, and U6
- snRNPs recognize intron-exon borders using three sites: the 5' splice (donor) site, the branch site near the 3' end, and the 3' splice (acceptor) site
- Introns typically begin with GU at the 5' end and end with AG at the 3' end

## Alternative splicing

Certain exons can be included or left out, so one gene can make several different proteins.

> Introns start with GU and end with AG.

Library: Retinitis pigmentosa and lupus: splicing disorders
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Transcription

## Key findings

| Disorder | Link to splicing | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Retinitis pigmentosa | 4 of the associated genes encode pre-mRNA processing factors (PRPFs) | Progressive loss of peripheral and night vision; rhodopsin malfunction; dark pigment deposits streaking the retina |
| Systemic lupus erythematosus | Autoantibodies against snRNPs (anti-Sm) | Chronic autoimmune inflammation; butterfly rash, arthritis, muscle aches, nephritis; positive ANA test |

> Anti-snRNP (anti-Sm) antibodies are the splicing link to lupus.

Library: Genetic code and mutation types
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Genetic Code and Translation

## Features of the code

Universal, degenerate (redundant, which softens the effect of mutations), unambiguous, and nonoverlapping.

## Mutation types

| Type | Change |
|---|---|
| Silent | New codon, same amino acid |
| Conservative missense | Amino acid replaced by a similar one |
| Non-conservative missense | Amino acid replaced by a dissimilar one |
| Nonsense | Codon becomes a stop codon; premature termination |
| Frameshift | Insertion or deletion not in a multiple of three, which changes the reading frame |

## Examples from the course

- Sickle cell disease: missense mutation (Glu → Val at position 6 of beta-globin)
- Duchenne muscular dystrophy: frameshift or nonsense mutations in dystrophin
- Tay-Sachs disease: frameshift in hexosaminidase A
- Cystic fibrosis: deletion (ΔF508 in CFTR)

## Exons and reading frame

- There's always one more exon than intron
- Codons can span exon junctions, so skipping an exon can shift the reading frame
- After an exon deletion that shifts the frame, the N-terminal sequence stays normal up to the point where the frame is lost
- Exon-skipping gene therapy blocks a chosen exon to restore the reading frame and make a functional protein; it's used in muscular dystrophy

> Frameshifts come from insertions or deletions that aren't multiples of three.

Library: tRNA charging and wobble pairing
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Genetic Code and Translation

## Key findings

- tRNAs recognize codons by antiparallel complementary base pairing
- Wobble pairing: non-Watson-Crick pairing is tolerated at the third position of the codon (first of the anticodon), which is why synonymous codons often differ in the third base
- Amino acids are attached to tRNA using ATP, making an aminoacyl-tRNA
- There are 20 aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases, one per amino acid; they're highly specific and proofread
- A wrong amino acid on a tRNA puts the wrong amino acid in the protein (a missense change)

> Wobble at the third codon position explains why synonymous codons usually differ in the last base.

Library: Ribosome sites, elongation, and termination
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Genetic Code and Translation

## Ribosome

- Prokaryotic 70S (30S + 50S) and eukaryotic 80S (40S + 60S)
- Eukaryotic rRNAs: 28S, 18S, 5.8S, 5S
- A polysome is several ribosomes translating the same mRNA
- Four RNA binding sites: one for mRNA and three for tRNA

| Site | Holds |
|---|---|
| A (aminoacyl) | The incoming tRNA carrying an amino acid |
| P (peptidyl) | The tRNA attached to the growing chain |
| E (exit) | The empty tRNA leaving |

## Elongation and termination

- The chain grows from the N terminus to the C terminus
- The growing chain moves from the P-site tRNA to the A-site tRNA; the ribosome then translocates, moving the empty tRNA to E and the new peptidyl-tRNA to P
- At a stop codon, a release factor and GTP end translation

## Drugs that target bacterial ribosomes

| Subunit | Drugs |
|---|---|
| 30S | Aminoglycosides (mRNA misreading), tetracyclines (block tRNA binding) |
| 50S | Macrolides (block translocation), clindamycin, linezolid |

> The A site takes the incoming aminoacyl-tRNA; the P site holds the growing chain.

Library: Translation initiation: prokaryotes vs. eukaryotes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Genetic Code and Translation

## Key findings

| Feature | Prokaryotes | Eukaryotes |
|---|---|---|
| First amino acid | N-formylmethionine (tRNA fMet) | Methionine (AUG) |
| Start-site signal | Shine-Dalgarno sequence pairs with the 3' end of 16S rRNA and puts the start codon in the P site | Kozak sequence (gccRccAUGG, R = A or G) |

- The initiator Met-tRNA is different from the Met-tRNAs used later in the chain
- N-formyl peptides are cleavage products of bacterial and mitochondrial proteins, and they attract phagocytes
- Peptide deformylase is a bacterial enzyme and a target of newer drugs
- Losing the Kozak sequence lowers initiation efficiency

## Clinical link

Beta-thalassemia minor can come from Kozak sequence mutations in the beta-globin gene, which reduce translation efficiency.

> Kozak sequence mutations in beta-globin reduce translation and can cause beta-thalassemia minor.

Library: Regulation of translation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Genetic Code and Translation

## eIF2

- A trimer (alpha, beta, gamma) that binds GTP on its alpha subunit, like a G protein
- After use, eIF2-GDP needs eIF2B to exchange GDP for GTP
- Phosphorylating a serine on the alpha subunit blocks that exchange and slows global translation
- Triggers: heme deficiency, dsRNA, ER stress, amino acid starvation

## eIF4E

- Inhibited by hypophosphorylated 4E-BP
- Insulin triggers hyperphosphorylation of 4E-BP, which releases eIF4E and promotes translation

## Iron response elements (IREs)

| IRE location | Effect |
|---|---|
| 5' end | Iron response protein (IRP) bound to the IRE blocks translation; iron releases the block |
| 3' end | An unbound IRE leads to mRNA degradation; IRP binding stabilizes the mRNA |

## Viruses

Only capped mRNA is translated efficiently. Viral proteases can destroy cap-dependent initiation factors, and internal ribosome entry sites (IRES) let ribosomes assemble on uncapped viral mRNA.

> Phosphorylated eIF2 alpha shuts down global translation during stress.

Library: Protein sorting and signal sequences
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Endomembranes

## Key findings

- A signal sequence is usually a stretch of amino acids that tells the cell where a protein belongs; the cytosol is the default
- Apart from proteins bound for the nucleus, mitochondria, and peroxisomes, proteins go through the ER

| Transport type | Destination |
|---|---|
| Gated | Nucleus |
| Transmembrane | Mitochondria and ER |
| Vesicular | ER → Golgi → endosome, lysosome, or cell exterior |

## Mitochondrial import

Most mitochondrial proteins are encoded in the nucleus and imported unfolded, using a signal sequence and an import receptor.

> Proteins without a signal sequence stay in the cytosol.

Library: Nuclear pores and nuclear localization signals
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Endomembranes

## Key findings

- Nuclear pores let larger proteins in and out; proteins are made in the cytoplasm
- More pores per nucleus means a more proliferative cell
- Molecules under 40 kD pass by passive diffusion
- Molecules over 40 kD need a nuclear localization signal (NLS) and energy (ATP/GTP)
- An NLS is a short stretch of basic amino acids recognized by nuclear transport receptors (importins and exportins, also called karyopherins)
- Ran-GTP releases the cargo from importin inside the nucleus, and the importin is exported

> Proteins over 40 kD need a nuclear localization signal; Ran-GTP releases them from importin.

Library: Nuclear lamina, nuclear matrix, and nucleolus
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Endomembranes

## Key findings

| Structure | Key points |
|---|---|
| Nuclear lamina | Thin fibrillar layer under the inner membrane that reinforces the envelope and shape; a meshwork of lamins A, B, and C (intermediate filaments); peripheral chromatin attaches to it |
| Nuclear matrix | Scaffold of lamins and nucleoproteins; DNA attaches transiently at AT-rich scaffold-associated regions; organizes chromosome domains and helps regulate transcription, replication, and repair |
| Nucleolus | rRNA transcription and processing and ribosomal subunit assembly; rRNA genes on 10 chromosomes cluster here; depends on the nuclear matrix |

## Laminopathies

| Disorder | Defect | Key findings |
|---|---|---|
| Laminopathies (general) | Lamin A/C | Cardiomyopathy, progeria, short stature, osteoporosis |
| Hutchinson-Gilford progeria | Lamin A | Premature aging; treated with farnesylation inhibitors |
| Emery-Dreifuss muscular dystrophy | Lamin A | Progressive weakness, joint contractures |
| Congenital muscular dystrophy | Lamin A | Skeletal muscle wasting |

> Lamin A mutations cause progeria and Emery-Dreifuss muscular dystrophy.

Library: ER import and the unfolded protein response
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Endomembranes

## Key findings

- ER import happens co-translationally
- The signal recognition particle (SRP), a ribonucleoprotein of 6 proteins and one RNA, recognizes the N-terminal signal sequence
- GTP-bound SRP binds the SRP receptor; GTP hydrolysis releases it
- The translocator next to the SRP receptor is normally closed; binding of the signal peptide opens it

## Folding and quality control

- Proteins in the rough ER must fold within a limited time; if they don't, they're exported, ubiquitinated, and degraded
- The unfolded protein response (UPR) starts when too many unfolded proteins build up. It lowers general transcription and translation, raises production of chaperones, and can trigger apoptosis

> Misfolded ER proteins are ubiquitinated and degraded; too many trigger the unfolded protein response.

Library: Vesicle coats and docking: COPI, COPII, Rab, and SNAREs
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Endomembranes

## Coats

Vesicle formation isn't thermodynamically favorable, so protein coats with high affinity for each other drive budding.

| Coat | Direction | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| COPII | ER → Golgi | |
| COPI | Golgi → ER | Recognizes retrieval signals such as KDEL |
| Clathrin | Plasma membrane → endosome | Triskelions; dynamin pinches the vesicle off |

## Docking

- Rab-GTP on the vesicle gives docking specificity by binding tethering proteins
- v-SNAREs (vesicle) and t-SNAREs (target membrane) pull the vesicle to the membrane

## Botulinum toxin

Botulinum toxin enters by endocytosis, escapes the endosome, and cleaves SNARE proteins. Acetylcholine release is blocked, causing flaccid paralysis.

> COPII moves cargo forward from the ER; COPI brings it back.

Library: Lysosomal targeting and storage diseases
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Endomembranes

## Targeting

Lysosomal enzymes are tagged with mannose-6-phosphate (M6P) in the Golgi, which sends them to the lysosome.

## Key findings

| Disorder | Defect | Inheritance | Key findings |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hurler disease | Alpha-L-iduronidase (GAG breakdown) | Autosomal recessive | Skeletal abnormalities, heart disease, cognitive deficits, corneal clouding; death usually by age 10 |
| Hunter disease | Iduronate sulfatase | X-linked recessive | Very similar to Hurler |
| I-cell disease | NAG-1 phosphotransferase | Autosomal recessive | No M6P tag, so hydrolases never reach the lysosome; empty lysosomes and inclusions; children rarely live past 6 |

> I-cell disease is a Golgi tagging defect: without M6P, enzymes never reach the lysosome.

Library: Cystic fibrosis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Endomembranes

## Mechanism

CFTR is a plasma membrane channel for chloride and bicarbonate. It has to travel from the ER to the plasma membrane. The ΔF508 deletion keeps CFTR from folding correctly, so it's degraded in the ER. Without it, water can't follow the ions, and mucus becomes thick, dehydrated, and hard to clear.

## Key findings

- Autosomal recessive
- Recurrent respiratory infections
- Greasy, foul-smelling diarrhea
- Infertility (more than 95% of men with CF) and thicker cervical mucus
- Allelic heterogeneity

## Treatment

Trikafta helps CFTR fold so it can reach the membrane. A second compound is still needed to make the channel work better once it's there.

> ΔF508 CFTR misfolds and is degraded in the ER before reaching the membrane.

Library: Exocytosis, endocytosis, and autophagy
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Endomembranes

## Exocytosis

| Type | Example |
|---|---|
| Constitutive (always on) | Collagen from fibroblasts |
| Regulated | Synaptic vesicles, mast cell granules |

## Endocytosis

- Cargo moves from early to intermediate to late endosome, then to the lysosome
- The pH drops at each stage, releasing cargo from its receptor so the receptor can be recycled
- Clathrin triskelions form the coat, and dynamin (a GTPase) wraps the neck of the pit and cuts it off with GTP hydrolysis

## Phagocytosis

A specialized endocytosis done mostly by neutrophils and macrophages, using an actin-based pseudopod that wraps the target.

## Autophagy

The cell digests its own components during stress or nutrient shortage, and clears old or nonfunctional organelles.

> Falling endosome pH releases cargo from receptors so the receptors can be recycled.

Library: Cyclins, CDKs, and CDK inhibitors
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Cell Birth, Cell Death, and Tissue Homeostasis

## Key findings

- Cyclin-dependent kinases (CDKs) must bind cyclins to be active
- CDKs have both activating and inhibitory phosphorylation sites, and the inhibitory phosphate must be removed for activity
- Specific cyclins and CDKs rise and fall in each phase of the cell cycle
- Cyclins are regulated by proteasomal degradation
- CDK inhibitors (CKIs), such as p21cip1, add another layer of control
- G0 is the quiescent phase

## Tissue homeostasis

The balance between proliferation and death depends on growth factor availability and on signaling that controls cyclins and CDKs. Growth factors are secreted proteins and cell-surface receptors that drive renewal, death, and differentiation.

> CDKs need a bound cyclin and removal of inhibitory phosphates to be active.

Library: Rb and the restriction point
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Cell Birth, Cell Death, and Tissue Homeostasis

## Key findings

- Unphosphorylated Rb holds the E2F transcription factor and blocks transcription of S-phase genes
- Growth factors activate cyclin D/CDK4/6, which phosphorylates Rb and releases E2F
- This is the restriction point between G1 and S
- Rb is a tumor suppressor

## Clinical links

- CDK4/6 inhibitors such as ribociclib treat cancers with upregulated cyclin D or CDKs
- Cyclin D amplification inactivates Rb and releases E2F
- Retinoblastoma (loss of Rb) causes a retinal tumor and vision loss

> Cyclin D/CDK4/6 phosphorylates Rb, releasing E2F and pushing the cell past the G1/S restriction point.

Library: p53 checkpoint
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Cell Birth, Cell Death, and Tissue Homeostasis

## Key findings

p53 blocks the G1 to S transition when DNA is damaged and chooses one of two outcomes.

| Outcome | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| Growth arrest (cytostasis) | p53 upregulates p21, a CDK inhibitor that binds and inactivates CDKs |
| Cell death (cytotoxicity) | p53 upregulates Bax, starting the intrinsic apoptosis pathway |

## Clinical links

- Li-Fraumeni syndrome: germline p53 mutation; sarcomas, breast cancer, leukemia, adrenal tumors (SBLA)
- MDM2 amplification: MDM2 is a ubiquitin ligase that marks p53 for degradation; seen in sarcoma

> p53 arrests the cell through p21 or kills it through Bax.

Library: Flow cytometry of the cell cycle
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Cell Birth, Cell Death, and Tissue Homeostasis

## Key findings

- Flow cytometry measures laser light from fluorescent dyes to analyze many cells quickly
- Propidium iodide binds DNA, so DNA content shows each cell's phase

| Phase | DNA content |
|---|---|
| G1 | 2n |
| S | Between 2n and 4n |
| G2 | 4n |
| Sub-G1 | Less than 2n: cells dying by apoptosis |

- Annexin V binds phosphatidylserine (calcium-dependent); tagged with FITC, it detects apoptotic cells by flow cytometry
- Taxol-type drugs disrupt the mitotic spindle, and the effect shows up in flow cytometry results

> A sub-G1 peak means cells with fragmented DNA are dying by apoptosis.

Library: Intrinsic apoptosis and caspases
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Cell Birth, Cell Death, and Tissue Homeostasis

## Pathway

p53 → Bax → mitochondria release cytochrome c → caspase-9 activation

## Caspases

| Group | Caspases |
|---|---|
| Initiator | 8, 9, 10 |
| Executioner | 3, 6, 7 |

| Caspase | Action |
|---|---|
| 3 | Activates endonucleases |
| 6 | Cleaves lamin A |
| 7 | Cleaves PARP (poly ADP-ribose polymerase), a DNA repair enzyme |

## Detecting apoptosis

- Endonuclease activity fragments DNA into a DNA ladder
- Antibodies to cleaved lamin A or activated caspases
- Annexin V binding to phosphatidylserine

> Cytochrome c released from mitochondria activates caspase-9 in the intrinsic pathway.

Library: Bcl-2 family proteins
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Cell Birth, Cell Death, and Tissue Homeostasis

## Key findings

| Group | Members | Role |
|---|---|---|
| Pro-apoptotic | Bax, Bad, Bim, Bak | Promote cell death |
| Anti-apoptotic | Bcl-2, Bcl-xL, Mcl-1 | Block cell death |

- Cells overexpressing Bcl-2 won't undergo apoptosis, which makes those cancers hard to treat and multidrug resistant
- Bcl-2 inhibitors are a cancer therapy target

## Clinical link

Follicular lymphoma: t(14;18) puts BCL2 under the IgH promoter, and BCL2 overexpression blocks apoptosis in germinal center B cells.

> Bcl-2 overexpression, as in follicular lymphoma t(14;18), blocks apoptosis and causes drug resistance.

Library: Extrinsic apoptosis and necrosis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Cell Birth, Cell Death, and Tissue Homeostasis

## Extrinsic pathway

- Death receptors are often in the TNF receptor superfamily
- TNF inhibitors (such as etanercept, adalimumab, and infliximab) treat inflammatory and autoimmune disease
- Autoimmune lymphoproliferative syndrome comes from defective Fas/Fas-ligand interaction

## Apoptosis vs. necrosis

| Feature | Apoptosis | Necrosis |
|---|---|---|
| Membrane | Stays intact early | Compromised early |
| DNA | Fragmented in a ladder | Spills out of the cell |
| Clearance | Engulfed by neighboring cells | Triggers inflammation |

> Necrosis breaks the membrane early and causes inflammation; apoptosis doesn't.

Library: Promoters, enhancers, and transcription factors
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Regulation of Gene Expression

## Key findings

- The promoter is 5' to the transcription start site. Prokaryotes use sigma factor recognition; eukaryotes use basal transcription factors at the core promoter and can have more than one promoter
- Enhancers and silencers are sequences that raise or lower transcription when transcription factors bind them; in eukaryotes they can be anywhere
- Coactivators and corepressors lack DNA-binding domains. In prokaryotes, they're often metabolites or nutrients that change a repressor's function; in eukaryotes, they link basal and accessory transcription factors
- Response elements are cis-acting sequences that can act as enhancers or silencers
- Eukaryotic genes are regulated at many levels: transcription, processing, translation, and protein modification

## Transcription factor structure

- Nuclear localization signals and modular architecture; many have a ligand (hormone) binding domain
- Usually recognize palindromic sequences, with alpha helices in the major groove

| Motif | Key feature |
|---|---|
| Leucine zipper | Hydrophobic leucine interactions |
| Zinc finger | Cysteine and histidine coordinate zinc; common in steroid hormone receptors |

## Drug link

Tamoxifen is a synthetic estrogen antagonist that competes with estrogen for the receptor's ligand-binding site, used to prevent and treat ER-positive breast cancer.

> Zinc finger motifs are typical of steroid hormone receptors.

Library: DNA methylation and histone modification
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Regulation of Gene Expression

## DNA methylation

- CpG islands sit in regulatory regions (promoters and first exons) and are methylated by DNMTs
- Methylation is often cell-specific: inactive CpG islands and enhancers are methylated, and active ones aren't
- Heterochromatin is methylated; euchromatin isn't

## Histone modification

| Modification of histone tails | Result |
|---|---|
| Lysine acetylation | Euchromatin (open) |
| Lysine or arginine methylation | Heterochromatin (closed) |

## Clinical link

Hypermethylation of tumor suppressor genes can cause cancer, so drugs that inhibit hypermethylation have been developed.

> Histone acetylation opens chromatin; DNA methylation silences it.

Library: Genomic imprinting: Prader-Willi and Angelman syndromes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Regulation of Gene Expression

## Imprinting

- An imprinted gene is silenced, and only one parent's copy is expressed
- Imprinting is set by methylation during sperm and egg development, so it matters which parent the gene came from

## Key findings

| Syndrome | Region | What's missing | Key findings |
|---|---|---|---|
| Prader-Willi | 15q11-13 | Father's copy is deleted; mother's copy is silenced | Hyperphagia, obsession with food, hypotonia, motor delay, intellectual disability, hypogonadism |
| Angelman | 15q11-13 | Mother's copy is deleted; father's copy is silenced | Happy, laughing demeanor ("happy puppet"), jerky gait, ataxia, seizures |

> Prader-Willi is a paternal deletion; Angelman is a maternal deletion.

Library: miRNA, siRNA, and lncRNA
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 4: Foundations of Genetics / Regulation of Gene Expression

## Key findings

| RNA | Source | Pairing | Action |
|---|---|---|---|
| miRNA (19-25 nt) | Its own genes | Full or partial complementarity | Represses translation, speeds mRNA degradation, sequesters mRNAs |
| siRNA | dsRNA or synthetic RNA | Full complementarity | Degrades target mRNA |
| lncRNA (over 200 nt) | Its own genes | Not applicable | Marks regions for epigenetic silencing and regulates many processes |

## X inactivation

Xist (X-inactive specific transcript) is an lncRNA made by the X chromosome that coats and silences the same X. This lyonization is random, so X-linked genes are expressed in a mosaic pattern.

> siRNA needs full complementarity; miRNA can work with partial complementarity.

Library: Tumor nomenclature
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Introduction to Neoplasia

## Parts of a tumor

- Neoplasia means new growth
- A tumor has proliferating tumor cells and stroma (extracellular matrix and vessels)
- The stroma sets the consistency: scant stroma makes a soft, fleshy tumor, and desmoplasia (sclerosis) makes a firm, hard one

## Benign tumors

- Named with the tissue type plus "-oma"
- Adenomas are gland-forming or arise from a gland (for example, an adrenal adenoma or a tubular adenoma of the colon)
- Papillomas are named for their papillary architecture, whatever their size or lining epithelium

## Malignant tumors

| Cell of origin | Name | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Epithelial | Carcinoma (most common in adults) | Adenocarcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma |
| Mesenchymal | Sarcoma (tissue + "sarcoma") | Osteosarcoma, chondrosarcoma |
| Hematopoietic | Lymphoma or leukemia | Named for the cell of origin |
| Melanocytic | Melanoma | |

> Carcinomas come from epithelium and are the most common malignancies in adults; sarcomas come from mesenchyme.

Library: Teratoma, choristoma, and hamartoma
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Introduction to Neoplasia

## Key findings

| Term | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Teratoma | Arises from germ cell rests and forms elements of all three germ layers | Ovary or testis tumor containing thyroid, glands, fat, hair, or teeth |
| Choristoma | Normal tissue in an abnormal location | Adrenal rest under the kidney capsule; normal pancreas in the stomach or small intestine |
| Hamartoma | Disorganized but mature tissue native to that site; mostly benign | Lung lesion made of cartilage, vessels, and bronchial epithelium |
| Pleomorphic adenoma of the salivary gland | Divergent differentiation from a single cell line | Epithelial and myoepithelial cells in one tumor |

> A choristoma is normal tissue in the wrong place; a hamartoma is disorganized tissue in the right place.

Library: Benign vs. malignant tumors
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Introduction to Neoplasia

## Key findings

| Feature | Benign | Malignant |
|---|---|---|
| Differentiation | Resembles the tissue of origin | Ranges from well differentiated to anaplastic; poorly differentiated tumors make the site of origin hard to identify |
| Nuclear-to-cytoplasmic ratio | Near normal | Increased (nuclei stain dark) |
| Mitoses | Few | Increased, often atypical |
| Growth rate | Slow | Faster |
| Local invasion | Usually none | Often |
| Metastasis | None | Defines malignancy; markedly lowers the chance of cure |

## Terms

- Anaplasia: complete lack of differentiation
- Hyperchromasia: darkly staining nuclei
- Pleomorphism: variation in cell size and shape
- Dysplasia: disordered growth, mostly in epithelium, with all changes confined above an intact basement membrane. Dysplasia isn't cancer, but it can become cancer

> Metastasis is the feature that marks a tumor as malignant.

Library: Clonality and tumor cell kinetics
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Introduction to Neoplasia

## Clonality

- Tumors arise from a single precursor cell (monoclonal)
- Non-neoplastic proliferations usually arise from several precursor cells (polyclonal)
- Clonality testing is most often used for lymphoid proliferations

## Cell kinetics

- Growth rate tracks the degree of differentiation, and more dividing cells means faster growth
- Chemotherapy acts on dividing cells
- Slow-growing tumors resist chemotherapy
- Fast-growing tumors are aggressive but respond best to chemotherapy, though they can also mutate and develop resistance faster

> Fast-growing tumors are aggressive but respond best to chemotherapy.

Library: Clinical presentation of tumors
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Introduction to Neoplasia

## How tumors come to attention

| Route | Examples |
|---|---|
| Screening | Lab work, imaging, pathology, or exam (breast exam, prostate exam, skin check) |
| B symptoms | Fever, night sweats |
| Obstructive or local symptoms | Painless jaundice (pancreatic cancer), pneumonia (lung cancer), bleeding, weight loss |
| Incidental finding | Found on a scan done for another reason |

## When screening makes sense

- It causes little harm to the patient (radiation from CT is controversial)
- Finding the disease early leads to beneficial treatment

> Painless jaundice is the classic obstructive presentation of pancreatic cancer.

Library: Tumor staging and grading
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Introduction to Neoplasia

## Staging (TNM, from the AJCC)

| Letter | Meaning |
|---|---|
| T | Tumor size, usually pT1 to pT4, with criteria specific to each cancer and site |
| N | Nodes, usually based on the number of involved lymph nodes |
| M | Metastasis, yes or no |

## Grading

Grading describes how the tumor cells look under the microscope. Many tumors lack a good grading standard, but some have well-defined systems.

| System | Cancer |
|---|---|
| FIGO | Endometrial |
| Nottingham | Breast |
| Fuhrman | Kidney |

> Staging (TNM) describes spread; grading describes microscopic appearance.

Library: Sporadic vs. hereditary cancer and genetic testing
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Genetic Testing and Familial Cancer Syndromes

## Key findings

- The hallmarks of cancer (sustained proliferative signaling, evading growth suppression, tumor-promoting inflammation, genomic instability, and others) are all secondary to genetic alterations

| Type | Key points |
|---|---|
| Sporadic | Most cancers; usually environmental (smoking, UV); not in the germline, so not heritable, and germline testing is negative |
| Familial (hereditary) | Mutation is in the germline and shows up in white blood cells; usually one normal and one abnormal copy, so a 50% chance of passing it on |
| Epigenetic | Mostly not heritable |
| Monogenic | Caused by a mutation in a single gene |

## Oncogenes vs. tumor suppressors in inherited cancer

- Activated oncogenes don't contribute to hereditary cancer and are often lethal to the developing fetus
- Inactivated tumor suppressor genes are the ones that are inherited; one normal copy prevents developmental effects

## Where genetic testing is used

- Cancer treatment (99% of cancer care)
- Early detection and risk reduction (germline testing)
- Surveillance, survivorship, and hospice (cell-free DNA)

> Hereditary cancer syndromes come from germline tumor suppressor mutations, passed on with a 50% chance.

Library: Familial cancer syndromes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Genetic Testing and Familial Cancer Syndromes

## DNA repair genes and cancer

| Repair defect | Genes | Cancers |
|---|---|---|
| Mismatch repair (Lynch syndrome) | MLH1, MSH2, MSH6, PMS2, EPCAM | Colon, endometrial, upper GI, ovarian |
| Polymerase editing | POLE, POLD1 | Ultra-hypermutated tumors with millions of mutations |
| Homologous recombination (HBOC) | BRCA1, BRCA2, ATM, CHEK2 | Breast, ovary, pancreas, prostate |

- UV damage is repaired by nucleotide excision repair, oxidative damage by base excision repair, and mispaired bases by mismatch repair
- Non-homologous end joining is low fidelity

## Hereditary breast and ovarian cancer (HBOC)

- Pathogenic BRCA1 or BRCA2 variants disrupt double-strand break repair by homologous recombination
- Autosomal dominant
- Management: risk-reducing surgery, PARP inhibitors, surveillance

## Li-Fraumeni syndrome

A germline p53 mutation causes sarcomas, breast cancer, leukemia, and adrenal gland tumors (SBLA).

> BRCA1, BRCA2, ATM, and CHEK2 are all double-strand break repair genes.

Library: Oncogenes and proto-oncogenes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Carcinogenesis and Epidemiology

## Key findings

- A proto-oncogene is the normal gene that helps drive cell division
- An oncogene is the mutated version that tells the cell to divide continuously
- Only one mutated copy is needed (activation)

| Oncogene | Example |
|---|---|
| HER2/neu | Amplified in breast cancer |
| MYC | Amplified in many cancers, including Burkitt lymphoma |
| ABL | BCR-ABL fusion in CML (Philadelphia chromosome) |
| RAF | BRAF mutation |
| MYCN | Amplified in neuroblastoma |
| BCL2 | Overexpressed in follicular lymphoma |

> An oncogene is a gain-of-function version of a normal growth gene; one mutated copy is enough.

Library: Tumor suppressor genes and the two-hit model
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Carcinogenesis and Epidemiology

## Key findings

- Tumor suppressor genes keep cell growth in check
- Two-hit model: both copies must be lost before function is gone
- In inherited syndromes, one defective copy is inherited, so only one more hit is needed

| Gene | Cancer |
|---|---|
| BRCA1, BRCA2 | Breast |
| p53 | Li-Fraumeni syndrome |
| CDKN2A | Melanoma |
| APC | Not specified in the sources |
| RB1 | Retinoblastoma |

## Apoptosis and DNA repair genes

- Anti-apoptotic genes such as BCL2 cause cancer when overactive; in follicular lymphoma, t(14;18) places BCL2 next to the IGH promoter and makes it constitutively active
- Pro-apoptotic genes cause cancer when knocked out
- DNA repair gene mutations leave damage unrepaired; loss of nucleotide excision repair causes xeroderma pigmentosum

> Inheriting one bad copy of a tumor suppressor means only one more hit is needed.

Library: Radiation as a carcinogen
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Carcinogenesis and Epidemiology

## Ultraviolet radiation

| Type | Effect |
|---|---|
| UVA | Minor; damage mostly through free radicals |
| UVB | Major cause of skin cancer; directly dimerizes adjacent thymines |
| UVC | Most harmful but usually blocked by the ozone layer; welders are exposed, which is why they wear masks and should see a dermatologist yearly |

## Ionizing radiation

- Electromagnetic and particulate radiation all damage DNA; the cell repairs it or, failing that, undergoes apoptosis
- Iatrogenic radiation from medical imaging and treatment can cause secondary tumors

> UVB is the major cause of skin cancer through thymine dimers.

Library: Microbial and chemical carcinogens
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Carcinogenesis and Epidemiology

## Viruses

| Virus | Associated cancer |
|---|---|
| HPV | Cervical, vaginal, and anogenital cancers (mostly types 16 and 18) |
| HBV, HCV | Hepatocellular carcinoma |
| EBV | Burkitt, Hodgkin, and non-Hodgkin lymphoma |
| HTLV-1 | Adult T-cell leukemia |
| HHV-8 | Kaposi sarcoma |
| Merkel cell polyomavirus | Not specified in the sources |

## HPV mechanism

HPV integrates into the host DNA, and its proteins inhibit tumor suppressors: E6 inhibits p53, and E7 inhibits p21 and the Rb-E2F complex.

## Other carcinogens

- Bacteria: H. pylori, one of the few cancer causes treated with antibiotics when caught early (gastric MALT lymphoma)
- Fungus: Aspergillus flavus produces liver toxins
- Direct alkylating agents change DNA directly; they're used in cancer treatment and can cause therapy-related cancers
- Polycyclic hydrocarbons: cigarettes, smoked meats
- Occupational exposures: asbestos, vinyl chloride, arsenic

> HPV E6 inhibits p53, and E7 inhibits Rb.

Library: Cancer epidemiology
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Carcinogenesis and Epidemiology

## Adults (United States)

- Cancer was the #2 cause of death in 2022, behind heart disease and ahead of accidents
- Non-melanoma skin cancer is usually left out of incidence counts because it's rarely deadly

| Measure | Men | Women |
|---|---|---|
| Most common | Prostate, then lung, then colorectal | Breast, then lung, then colorectal |
| Most deaths | Lung, then prostate, then colorectal and pancreatic | Lung, then breast, then colorectal and pancreatic |

## Children

- A higher share of pediatric cancers are inherited, since children have had little carcinogen exposure
- Most have an excellent prognosis (about 92%)
- Leukemia is the most common childhood cancer, followed by brain tumors; brain tumors cause the most deaths, followed by leukemia
- B-cell ALL is the most common pediatric cancer and has about a 92% cure rate

> Lung cancer causes the most cancer deaths in both men and women.

Library: PRPP synthesis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Nucleotide Metabolism

## Key findings

- PRPP (5-phospho-D-ribosyl-1-alpha-pyrophosphate) is made by PRPP synthetase
- It's the common precursor for both purine and pyrimidine synthesis, and an allosteric stimulator of nucleotide synthesis
- PRPP synthesis is inhibited by purines and pyrimidines and promoted by glucose-6-phosphate and intracellular free phosphate

## Clinical link

In von Gierke disease (glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency), high glucose-6-phosphate and free phosphate drive PRPP synthesis up, which increases nucleotide synthesis and turnover.

> More PRPP means more nucleotide synthesis, which is why von Gierke disease and HGPRT deficiency raise uric acid.

Library: De novo purine synthesis and salvage
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Nucleotide Metabolism

## Key findings

- About 97% of purine nucleotides come from salvage and about 3% from de novo synthesis
- All purine synthesis enzymes are cytosolic

## De novo

- Glutamine PRPP amidotransferase catalyzes the first step
- It's feedback-inhibited by IMP, GMP, and AMP and stimulated by PRPP
- N10-formyltetrahydrofolate, from folate, is a cofactor, so folate deficiency impairs nucleotide synthesis and causes megaloblastic anemia

## Salvage

- Hypoxanthine-guanine phosphoribosyltransferase (HGPRT) catalyzes the most important salvage step and has a high affinity for PRPP
- When salvage fails, unused PRPP builds up and drives de novo synthesis, and more purines are degraded to uric acid

> Most purines are recycled by salvage; HGPRT is the key salvage enzyme.

Library: Lesch-Nyhan syndrome
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Nucleotide Metabolism

## Mechanism

HGPRT activity is under 2%. PRPP isn't used for salvage, so it stimulates de novo purine synthesis, and guanine and hypoxanthine are degraded instead of salvaged, sharply raising uric acid.

## Key findings

- X-linked
- Hyperuricemia, gout, kidney stones, and renal damage
- Severe neurological and behavioral abnormalities, including self-mutilation such as chewing the lips and fingertips; dental extraction is often used to prevent tissue injury
- Memory hook (HGPRT): hyperuricemia, gout, aggression and self-harm, intellectual disability, dystonia

## Treatment

Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase and lowers uric acid. It helps control the arthritis but doesn't prevent the neurological problems.

> Allopurinol treats the hyperuricemia of Lesch-Nyhan syndrome but not the neurological symptoms.

Library: Uric acid and gout
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Nucleotide Metabolism

## Purine degradation

- Purines are degraded to uric acid
- High AMP and low phosphate increase degradation
- Lactate and some diuretics compete with uric acid for renal excretion, so high lactate raises uric acid

## Gout

- Uric acid is sparingly soluble and forms needle-shaped sodium urate crystals
- Under polarized light the crystals are negatively birefringent
- NSAIDs are the first treatment for an acute attack; colchicine and uricosuric drugs are used in severe cases
- Allopurinol isn't used to treat the acute attack
- Drugs listed for gout overall: NSAIDs, allopurinol, febuxostat

> Gout crystals are needle-shaped and negatively birefringent.

Library: Tumor lysis syndrome
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Nucleotide Metabolism

## Mechanism

Cytolytic drugs for hematologic cancers kill large numbers of cells at once. The released nucleic acids are salvaged or degraded, and the excess uric acid precipitates as needle-shaped crystals.

## Labs

| Value | Change |
|---|---|
| Uric acid | High |
| Potassium | High |
| Phosphate | High |
| Calcium | Low |

## Treatment

- Flushing the kidneys with fluids helps correct the hyperuricemia, hyperkalemia, hyperphosphatemia, and hypocalcemia
- Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase, so xanthine is excreted instead of uric acid

> Tumor lysis syndrome raises uric acid, potassium, and phosphate and lowers calcium.

Library: ADA deficiency and SCID
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Nucleotide Metabolism

## Key findings

- Adenosine deaminase (ADA) converts deoxyadenosine to deoxyinosine during purine degradation
- ADA deficiency causes about 15% of SCID and is autosomal recessive
- Deoxyadenosine builds up and is phosphorylated to dATP, which inhibits ribonucleotide reductase and blocks deoxynucleotide synthesis
- T cells, B cells, and NK cells are all absent
- Failure to thrive, chronic diarrhea, thrush, and recurrent viral, bacterial, fungal, and protozoal infections; no thymic shadow
- No live vaccines; antimicrobial prophylaxis

## Look-alike

The most common cause of SCID is an X-linked recessive defect in the IL-2 receptor gamma chain.

> In ADA deficiency, dATP builds up and shuts down ribonucleotide reductase.

Library: Pyrimidine synthesis and orotic aciduria
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Nucleotide Metabolism

## Key findings

- Unlike purines, the pyrimidine ring is built first, and a phosphoribosyl group from PRPP is added afterward
- Enzymes 1-3 form the CAD complex, enzyme 4 is mitochondrial, and enzymes 5-6 form UMP synthase
- Orotate is an intermediate formed before PRPP is added

## Carbamoyl phosphate

| Enzyme | Pathway | Substrates | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| CPS I | Urea cycle | Ammonia and bicarbonate | Needs N-acetylglutamate |
| CPS II | Pyrimidine synthesis | Glutamine and bicarbonate | Rate-limiting step; inhibited by UTP |

## Orotic aciduria

| Cause | Findings |
|---|---|
| Hereditary orotic aciduria (UMP synthase deficiency) | Orotate accumulates; severe megaloblastic anemia and poor growth; autosomal recessive |
| Urea cycle defect (OTC deficiency) | Carbamoyl phosphate spills into pyrimidine synthesis; orotic aciduria with hyperammonemia |

## Treatment of hereditary orotic aciduria

Dietary uridine is converted to UMP and then UTP, which inhibits CPS II and lowers orotate.

> Orotic aciduria with megaloblastic anemia and no hyperammonemia points to UMP synthase deficiency.

Library: Ribonucleotide reductase and thymidylate synthesis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Nucleotide Metabolism

## Ribonucleotide reductase

- Converts all four ribonucleotides to deoxyribonucleotides
- Hydroxyurea inhibits it and stops cancer growth; its use in sickle cell disease works differently, by raising fetal hemoglobin
- In ADA deficiency, dATP binds and blocks it

## Thymidylate synthesis

- Thymidylate synthase and dihydrofolate reductase make thymine nucleotides, which depends on folate
- 5-fluorouracil inhibits thymidylate synthase
- Methotrexate inhibits dihydrofolate reductase

> 5-FU blocks thymidylate synthase, and methotrexate blocks dihydrofolate reductase.

Library: Drugs that inhibit nucleotide synthesis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Nucleotide Metabolism

## 6-Mercaptopurine

- Used for leukemia (ALL) and as an immunosuppressant; azathioprine is its prodrug
- HGPRT converts it to 6-thioinosine monophosphate (TIMP)
- Inhibits amidophosphoribosyltransferase (the first rate-limiting step of purine synthesis) and the conversion of IMP to AMP and GMP, and it's incorporated into DNA and RNA
- Xanthine oxidase breaks it down to thiouric acid, so allopurinol raises its levels (greater efficacy and toxicity)
- Side effects: myelosuppression, GI and liver toxicity, pancreatitis

## 5-Fluorouracil

- Used for colorectal cancer
- Converted to 5-fUTP and 5-fdUMP; 5-fdUMP inhibits thymidylate synthase and depletes thymine for DNA synthesis
- Highly mutagenic, so it can't be used as an immunosuppressant
- Side effects: myelosuppression, hand-foot syndrome, diarrhea, photosensitivity

## Methotrexate

- Antifolate used as an anticancer drug, as an immunosuppressant, and to terminate pregnancy
- Competitively inhibits dihydrofolate reductase, so DHF accumulates and both purine and TMP synthesis fall
- Side effects: myelosuppression, hepatotoxicity, mucositis, pulmonary fibrosis, folate deficiency, nephrotoxicity

> Allopurinol blocks 6-MP breakdown, raising its effect and its toxicity.

Library: Chromosomal translocations in cancer
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Cytogenetics of Cancer

## Key findings

A translocation exchanges material between non-homologous chromosomes, producing two derivative (der) chromosomes, each named for the centromere it came from.

| Translocation | Genes | Cancer | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| t(9;22)(q34;q11) | BCR-ABL (Philadelphia chromosome) | Chronic myeloid leukemia | Constitutively active tyrosine kinase |
| t(14;18) | IgH-BCL2 | Follicular lymphoma | BCL2 overexpression blocks apoptosis; chemotherapy resistance |
| t(8;14) | c-MYC-IgH | Burkitt lymphoma | MYC drives massive growth |
| t(15;17) | PML-RARA | Acute promyelocytic leukemia | Blocks promyelocyte maturation |
| t(11;14) | Cyclin D1-IgH | Mantle cell lymphoma | Cyclin D1 drives G1 to S |

## BCR-ABL

- Part of the ABL gene (a nonreceptor tyrosine kinase) on chromosome 9 fuses with the breakpoint cluster region (BCR) on chromosome 22
- Detected by karyotyping, FISH, and RT-PCR
- The kinase is a drug target: imatinib occupies its ATP-binding site, and dasatinib and nilotinib cover resistance mutations

## BCL2 and drug resistance

Constitutive BCL2, like loss of p53 and Bax activation, causes multidrug resistance.

> The Philadelphia chromosome, t(9;22), makes the BCR-ABL tyrosine kinase that imatinib targets.

Library: Genomic instability and gene amplification
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Cytogenetics of Cancer

## Genomic instability

- Unstable chromosomes are prone to amplifications, translocations, and deletions; cancer cells often have this mutator phenotype
- At least 90% of cancers have lost p53 or Rb function
- Losing p53, Rb, or both raises gene amplification about 1000-fold, with more deletions and aneuploidy

## Gene amplification

Amplification means more copies of a gene than the normal 2n, usually from a complex recombination event followed by repeated replication.

| Type | Location | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| Intrachromosomal | Within a chromosome | |
| Extrachromosomal | Double minute chromosomes | Excised from a chromosome; have a replication origin but no centromere or telomeres, so they split unequally in mitosis; drive intratumor heterogeneity; can carry oncogenes or drug-resistance genes; detected by FISH |

> Double minutes lack centromeres, so they're divided unequally and spread heterogeneity through the tumor.

Library: Oncogene amplification: MDM2, cyclin D, MYCN, and HER2
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Cytogenetics of Cancer

## Key findings

| Gene | Cancers | Mechanism |
|---|---|---|
| MDM2 (double minute) | Sarcomas and other mesenchymal cancers; lung cancer with poor survival | Ubiquitin ligase that tags p53 for proteasomal degradation; without p53, Bax and p21 aren't induced, causing multidrug resistance |
| Cyclin D (CCND1) | Breast and bladder | Binds CDK4/6 constitutively, inactivating Rb so G1 to S goes unchecked; CDK4/6 inhibitors exist |
| MYCN | Neuroblastoma (usually adrenal gland) | Transcription factor that drives proliferation; harder to target than kinases |
| HER2/neu | About 30% of breast cancers | EGFR-family receptor that signals through RAS, MEK, and AKT, increasing survival and drug resistance |

## HER2 treatment

- Trastuzumab (Herceptin) is an antibody that blocks HER2, but it can't cross the blood-brain barrier, so patients often relapsed in the brain; it can cause dilated cardiomyopathy
- Small-molecule tyrosine kinase inhibitors cross the blood-brain barrier

> MDM2 amplification degrades p53, so apoptosis and p21 arrest are both lost.

Library: Principles of cancer screening
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Screening and Diagnosis

## Background

A cancer goes through about 30 divisions, usually about 5 years, before it's clinically palpable, and it accumulates mutations along the way before growth accelerates.

## Criteria for effective screening

- The cancer is recognizable at a latent or early stage, before symptoms
- A suitable, accurate test exists
- Effective treatment exists and improves cancer-specific mortality
- The cost is balanced against the benefit
- Screening is continuous, not a one-time project

> Screening only makes sense when early treatment actually lowers cancer-specific mortality.

Library: Sensitivity, specificity, and predictive values
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Screening and Diagnosis

## Key findings

| Measure | Meaning | Formula |
|---|---|---|
| Sensitivity | Correctly identifies people who have the disease | TP / (TP + FN) |
| Specificity | Correctly identifies people who don't have the disease | TN / (TN + FP) |
| Positive predictive value | Likelihood of disease when the test is positive | |
| Negative predictive value | Likelihood of no disease when the test is negative | |

- Poor sensitivity means many false negatives; poor specificity means many false positives
- For screening, the ideal test has high sensitivity and high negative predictive value

> High sensitivity means few false negatives, which is what a screening test needs.

Library: Lead-time and length-time bias
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Screening and Diagnosis

## Key findings

| Bias | What happens | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Lead-time bias | Screening moves diagnosis earlier without changing the time of death, so survival looks longer | Death at 65 either way, but diagnosis at 45 instead of 60 looks like 20-year rather than 5-year survival |
| Length-time bias | Screening picks up a disproportionate number of slow-growing, indolent cancers | |

- Lead-time bias is most likely when treatment doesn't change mortality

> Lead-time bias makes survival look longer without anyone living longer.

Library: Breast and lung cancer screening
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Screening and Diagnosis

## Breast cancer

Incidence rises with age.

| Source | Recommendation |
|---|---|
| American Cancer Society | Yearly mammogram from 40; clinical breast exam every 3 years in the 20s and 30s, yearly from 40 |
| USPSTF | Mammogram every 2 years from 40 through 74; no evidence of benefit after 74; no recommendation on clinical or self exams, with evidence of harm from self-exams |

- Breast MRI has high sensitivity but low specificity and outperforms mammography in high-risk patients

## Lung cancer

- High-risk patients are aged 50-80 with 20 pack-years of smoking
- Screen yearly until they've quit for more than 15 years

> Lung cancer screening covers ages 50-80 with a 20 pack-year history.

Library: Cancer surgery and biopsy types
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Treatment

## Three pillars of cancer management

Surgery, radiation, and pharmacotherapy (chemotherapy and targeted therapy).

## Surgery

- Best candidates: slow-growing tumors with a long cell cycle and low metastatic potential
- Adverse events: bleeding, infection, pneumonia
- Risk factors: physiologic old age, poor cardiopulmonary status, smoking, obesity

| Type | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Prophylactic | Remove tissue likely to become cancerous |
| Diagnostic | Biopsy |
| Definitive (curative) | Remove cancer confined to its site; cytoreduction helps other treatments work |
| Palliative | Relieve symptoms when cure isn't possible |
| Reconstructive | Restore appearance after definitive treatment |

## Biopsy types

| Biopsy | Method |
|---|---|
| Fine-needle aspiration (FNAC) | Least invasive; a positive result may need follow-up |
| Core needle | 1-1.2 mm core through a hollow needle under local anesthesia |
| Incisional | Small wedge of the tumor removed with a scalpel or punch |
| Excisional | Entire suspected tumor removed with little or no margin |

- Only positive biopsy findings are definitive; if symptoms persist after a negative biopsy, repeat it

> A negative biopsy doesn't rule out cancer; only positive findings are definitive.

Library: Radiation therapy
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Treatment

## Biology

- Ionizing radiation causes DNA damage, chromosomal instability, mutation, and apoptosis, and irradiated cells can signal to non-irradiated neighbors
- Proliferating cells are more radiosensitive than quiescent ones
- Cancer cells repair double-strand breaks by homologous recombination and NHEJ, so their radiosensitivity depends on that repair capacity

| Cell cycle phase | Sensitivity | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Late G2 and M | Most sensitive | Rely on error-prone NHEJ |
| S and early G2 | Most resistant | Homologous recombination repair is available |

## Sources

- Photons: gamma rays (from radioactive nuclei, as in a cobalt machine) and X-rays (from a linear accelerator)
- Particle beams: protons, electrons, neutrons

## Modalities

| Modality | Key points |
|---|---|
| External beam (EBRT) | 90% of treatments; covers large areas such as the tumor and nearby nodes; given in daily fractions |
| Brachytherapy | Radioactive implants next to the tumor; spares other tissue |
| SRS/SBRT | Precisely targeted large doses; spares normal tissue |
| Total body irradiation | Part of conditioning for stem cell transplant |
| Systemic | Iodine-131 concentrates in the thyroid; strontium-89 and samarium-153 go to bone |
| Whole brain irradiation | |

> Cells in late G2 and M are the most radiosensitive; S and early G2 cells are the most resistant.

Library: The five Rs of radiobiology
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Treatment

## Key findings

| R | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Radiosensitivity | Give normal tissue room to recover and repair |
| Repair | Normal cells get time between fractions to repair sublethal damage |
| Repopulation | A trade-off: healthy tissue repopulates, but so can the tumor |
| Redistribution | Stress pushes tumor cells into the cell cycle, into more sensitive phases |
| Reoxygenation | The hypoxic tumor core is protected; killing outer layers ("peeling an onion") lets oxygen reach it |

- Hypoxic cells stay in G0 and G1 and resist radiation

## Side effects

- Treatment itself is painless, but it affects all tissue in the beam's path, and long-term quality of life can suffer
- Local skin changes and fatigue appear within a few weeks of external beam treatment
- The risk of a second cancer is low

> Hypoxic tumor cells resist radiation, which is why reoxygenation between fractions matters.

Library: Principles of chemotherapy and log-kill
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Treatment

## Goals

| Use | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Curative | Cure without other treatment when possible |
| Adjuvant | Kill cells left behind after other treatment |
| Neoadjuvant | Shrink the tumor before other treatment |
| Palliative | Ease symptoms |

## Kinetics

- The faster a tumor divides, the more chemosensitive it is
- Growth fraction = cells actively cycling / total cells
- Log-kill principle (first-order kinetics): each dose kills a constant fraction of tumor cells, not a constant number
- Pulsed intermittent chemotherapy spaces doses to maximize tumor kill and normal cell recovery, depending on the last response, the dosing interval, and the tumor's doubling time

## Cell cycle specificity

| Type | Acts on | Dosing |
|---|---|---|
| Phase-specific | Cells in one phase; best for tumors with many cells in that phase | Schedule dependent |
| Phase-nonspecific | Cells in all phases, including G0; works on slow-growing tumors | Dose dependent |

## Combination therapy

Combination therapy replaced single-agent therapy, which rarely achieved lasting remission and encouraged resistance. Combinations lower the chance of resistance and improve cell kill and response rates.

> Each chemotherapy dose kills a constant fraction of tumor cells (log-kill).

Library: Chemotherapy drug classes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Treatment

## Key findings

| Class | Mechanism | Phase | Examples |
|---|---|---|---|
| Alkylating agents | Crosslink DNA, break strands, cause abnormal base pairing | Mostly cycling cells, still active in G0 | Cyclophosphamide, cisplatin, carboplatin |
| Antimetabolites | False metabolites that block DNA and RNA synthesis | S phase; fast-growing tumors | Methotrexate, 5-fluorouracil, 6-mercaptopurine, hydroxyurea |
| Antimicrotubule agents | Bind microtubules and cause mitotic arrest | M phase | Vinca alkaloids (vincristine, vinblastine); taxanes (paclitaxel, docetaxel) |
| Topoisomerase inhibitors | Block strand rejoining, disrupting transcription, replication, and mitosis | G2 | Etoposide, teniposide (topo II); irinotecan, topotecan (topo I); doxorubicin |

## Toxicities worth knowing

| Drug | Toxicity |
|---|---|
| Doxorubicin, daunorubicin | Dilated cardiomyopathy (dexrazoxane prevents it) |
| Bleomycin | Pulmonary fibrosis; little myelosuppression |
| Vincristine | Peripheral neuropathy |
| Paclitaxel | Peripheral neuropathy, myelosuppression |

- Cyclophosphamide needs activation by the liver

> Antimetabolites act in S phase; vinca alkaloids and taxanes act in M phase.

Library: Chemotherapy side effects
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Treatment

## Key findings

Chemotherapy has a narrow therapeutic window, and toxicity hits tissues that normally divide quickly: hair, GI tract, and bone marrow.

| Effect | Key points |
|---|---|
| Hypersensitivity | Most drugs cause a mild reaction; true allergy is much more severe |
| Extravasation | Drug leaks out of the vein into tissue; severity depends on drug, concentration, and site |
| Bone marrow suppression | Low WBCs, RBCs, and platelets; anemia impairs oxygen delivery |
| Neutropenic sepsis | Infection while neutrophils are low |
| Thrombocytopenia | Bleeding risk |
| Tumor lysis syndrome | Treated with kidney flushing and allopurinol |
| Mucositis | Mouth lining atrophies and ulcerates |

> Chemotherapy toxicity falls on fast-dividing tissues: marrow, gut, and hair.

Library: Targeted therapy and antibody naming
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Treatment

## How targeted therapy differs from chemotherapy

- Acts on a specific molecular target linked to the cancer
- Deliberately designed to interact with that target
- Usually cytostatic rather than cytotoxic

## Antibody names

| Type | Human share | Suffix |
|---|---|---|
| Murine | 0% | -omab |
| Chimeric | 65% | -ximab |
| Humanized | Over 90% | -zumab |
| Human | 100% | -umab |

## Examples from the course

| Drug | Target | Use |
|---|---|---|
| Trastuzumab | HER2 | HER2-positive breast cancer |
| Rituximab | CD20 | B-cell lymphomas (part of R-CHOP) |
| Imatinib | BCR-ABL | CML |
| Ribociclib | CDK4/6 | Cancers with active cyclin D |

> Targeted therapies are usually cytostatic; chemotherapy is cytotoxic.

Library: Hormone therapy and immunotherapy
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Foundations of Cancer Treatment

## Hormone therapy

- Blocks the hormones that hormone-responsive cancers (breast, prostate, endometrium) need to grow
- Tamoxifen blocks the estrogen receptor in ER-positive breast cancer; side effects include hot flashes and higher risk of DVT and endometrial cancer

## Immunotherapy

| Type | How it works |
|---|---|
| Immune checkpoint inhibitors | Block checkpoints so immune cells respond more strongly; side effects look autoimmune (add "-itis" to any organ) |
| T-cell transfer | The patient's best tumor-fighting T cells are selected, expanded, and returned |
| Monoclonal antibodies | Bind targets on cancer cells and mark them for destruction |
| Vaccines | Raised against antigens on the cancer cells |
| Immune system modulators | Boost the overall immune response |

> Checkpoint inhibitor side effects are autoimmune: any organ plus "-itis."

Library: Cell cycle control in cancer
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Cancer Survival Mechanisms

## Key findings

| Phase | Events |
|---|---|
| G1 | Protein, RNA, and lipid synthesis; no chromosome replication |
| S | DNA replication |
| G2 | Duplicated chromosomes checked for errors and repaired |

- p53 is the master regulator of the cell cycle and survival; as a transcription factor it induces p21, which blocks G1 progression, and it can also trigger apoptosis
- Rb binds and inactivates E2F, the transcription factor that drives cell cycle progression
- Mutations in Rb and p53 carry the worst outcomes
- Relapsed or progressing tumors often show mutated Rb, high cyclin E, or mutated p53

> Loss of Rb and p53 carries the worst outcomes and drives therapy resistance.

Library: Apoptosis pathways and PARP inhibitors
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Cancer Survival Mechanisms

## Pathways

| Pathway | Sequence |
|---|---|
| Intrinsic (mitochondrial) | Bax and other pro-apoptotic proteins release cytochrome c → caspase-9 → caspase-3 → PARP cleavage → cell death |
| Extrinsic | Death ligand binds death receptor → caspase-8 → caspase-3 → PARP cleavage → cell death |

- The intrinsic pathway is balanced by anti-apoptotic Bcl-2 and pro-apoptotic Bax
- Cleavage of PARP marks cell death

## PARP inhibitors

PARP helps repair DNA damage. Inhibiting it makes it harder for cancer cells to repair their genomes, pushing them toward apoptosis. PARP inhibitors are used in hereditary breast and ovarian cancer (BRCA1/2).

> Both apoptosis pathways converge on caspase-3 and PARP cleavage.

Library: Telomerase and replicative immortality
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Cancer Survival Mechanisms

## Key findings

- Telomeres protect chromosome ends from damage and fusion
- Losing telomeres usually stops cell growth or triggers apoptosis
- Telomerase (a reverse transcriptase) is overexpressed in most cancer cells, making them immortal
- Telomerase can serve as a molecular marker of cancer

> Most cancers overexpress telomerase, which makes them immortal.

Library: Warburg effect and cancer metabolism
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Cancer Survival Mechanisms

## Warburg effect

- Cancer cells prefer glycolysis over the citric acid cycle, even though it makes less ATP, because it's fast
- The hypoxic tumor core makes reliance on oxidative phosphorylation a liability, and hypoxia promotes resistance to chemotherapy and radiation
- Tumors reduce pyruvate entry into mitochondria and lower pyruvate dehydrogenase activity
- The Warburg effect helps detect tumors and metastases on imaging

## Altered citric acid cycle

- The cycle is often truncated and runs backward, fed by glutamine addiction
- Alpha-ketoglutarate → isocitrate → citrate, and citrate feeds fatty acid synthesis for new membranes
- Mutant isocitrate dehydrogenase turns alpha-ketoglutarate into 2-hydroxyglutarate (2-HG), which increases methylation of tumor suppressors

## Cachexia

A multifactorial metabolic syndrome in about 80% of cancer patients: involuntary weight loss, anorexia, muscle loss (sarcopenia), and fatigue, driven by tumor-induced inflammatory cytokines such as TNF-alpha.

> Mutant IDH makes 2-hydroxyglutarate, which silences tumor suppressors by methylation.

Library: Angiogenesis and metastasis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Cancer Survival Mechanisms

## Angiogenesis

- Growth of new vessels from existing vasculature
- The angiogenic switch: tumors secrete activators and inhibitors into the microenvironment
- VEGF binds its receptors and stimulates capillary formation
- Anti-angiogenic therapy often fails because many pathways can compensate; when one drug fails, the next needs a different target

## Steps of metastasis

1. Angiogenic switch and primary tumor growth
2. Loss of contact inhibition and breakdown of the basement membrane
3. Intravasation and survival in the circulation (most cells don't survive)
4. Extravasation into distant organs, which depends on the tissue of origin
5. Metastatic colonization and clinical detection

- Metastatic cancer isn't curable, but it can be managed with chemotherapy, radiation, anti-angiogenic drugs, immunotherapy, and targeted therapy

> VEGF drives the angiogenic switch that lets a tumor grow and spread.

Library: Cancer immunoediting and immune checkpoints
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Cancer Survival Mechanisms

## Immunoediting

| Phase | What happens |
|---|---|
| Elimination | Immune surveillance destroys tumor cells |
| Equilibrium | The immune system selects tumor variants better able to survive attack |
| Escape | The sculpted tumor grows uncontrolled in an immunocompetent host |

- The immune system fails because tumor antigens look like self, and T cells can be kept out of the tumor

## Checkpoints

| Checkpoint | Mechanism | Drug |
|---|---|---|
| CTLA-4 | An off switch on T cells | Anti-CTLA-4 antibodies |
| PD-1/PD-L1 | PD-L1 on tumor cells binds PD-1 on T cells and suppresses them | Anti-PD-1 antibodies |

> PD-L1 on tumor cells binds PD-1 on T cells to shut them down.

Library: Personalized and stratified medicine
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Individualizing Care of Cancer Patients (Personalized Medicine)

## Why personalize

Tailoring treatment gives better efficacy, individualized dosing, fewer adverse effects than chemotherapy, and better prognosis.

## Tools

- Next-generation sequencing covers 20,000-25,000 genes (about 1% of the genome) with a 2-week turnaround
- Cytogenetic classification replaced morphology alone for prognosis, since translocations and unfavorable genetics can be seen

## Stratified medicine

- Patients are grouped by features that predict response
- Immunotherapy response depends on how much of a target, such as PD-L1, the tumor expresses, and individuals vary within each group

## Treatment selection

- Each cancer is really many diseases defined by driver mutations; an EGFR-mutant tumor is treated differently from an ALK-driven one
- Targeted therapy can reach response rates up to 80% with much less toxicity than traditional chemotherapy
- Breast cancers are tested for hormone receptors, and after surgery, genetic relapse-risk testing decides who needs chemotherapy

> Driver mutations such as EGFR or ALK decide which targeted drug a patient gets.

Library: Pharmacogenomics in cancer care
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 5: Foundations of Cancer Biology and Cancer Genetics / Individualizing Care of Cancer Patients (Personalized Medicine)

## Key findings
