| Reoviridae | Segmented dsRNA | Rotavirus: the most common cause of infant diarrhea and #1 cause of fatal childhood diarrhea worldwide |
| Retroviridae | (+) RNA copied to DNA that integrates | HIV, HTLV; oncogenic potential |
| Coronaviridae | (+), enveloped, helical | SARS-CoV-2; binds ACE2 |
| Flaviviridae | (+), enveloped | HCV, West Nile, yellow fever, dengue |
| Picornaviridae | (+), naked | HAV, polio, rhinovirus, coxsackievirus |
| Caliciviridae | (+), naked | Norovirus (Norwalk): adult epidemic gastroenteritis |
| Togaviridae | (+), enveloped | Rubella, equine encephalitis viruses |
| Paramyxoviridae | (-), enveloped | RSV, measles, mumps, parainfluenza |
| Rhabdoviridae | (-), enveloped | Rabies |
| Bunyaviridae | (-), enveloped | Hantavirus |

> Antigenic shift (reassortment of influenza's segments) causes pandemics; drift causes yearly outbreaks.

Library: Viral spread and course of infection
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Viral Structure and Pathogenesis

## Spread

- Portals of entry: broken skin, mucosa, inhalation
- Replication happens in cells that have the receptor and the machinery to make virus
- Symptoms accompany replication at the primary site
- Spread to other sites is through blood or lymphatics; viremia is virus in the bloodstream

## Course

| Stage | What happens |
|---|---|
| Acquisition | Infection starts at the primary site and triggers innate defenses |
| Incubation | Virus amplifies and may spread to a secondary site |
| Prodrome | Nonspecific symptoms: fever, chills, body aches |
| Specific illness | Characteristic findings from tissue damage and the immune response |
| Contagion | Virus is released from tissue |
| Convalescence | Resolution, or persistent or chronic disease |

> The prodrome is the stage of nonspecific symptoms before the characteristic illness.

Library: Viral cytopathic effects and inclusion bodies
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Viral Structure and Pathogenesis

## Key findings

| Finding | Description | Virus |
|---|---|---|
| Syncytia | Viral glycoprotein on the cell surface fuses cells into multinucleated giant cells, aiding spread and hiding the virus | SARS-CoV-2, RSV |
| Chromatin margination | Chromatin pushed to the nuclear edge | Parvovirus B19 |
| Negri bodies | Eosinophilic cytoplasmic inclusions in pyramidal neurons | Rabies |
| Owl's eye inclusions | Basophilic nuclear inclusions | CMV |
| Cowdry bodies | Eosinophilic nuclear inclusions | HSV, VZV |
| Koilocytosis | Cytoplasmic vacuolization of squamous cells | HPV |

- Inclusion bodies are sites of viral replication or capsids, and their staining pattern can be diagnostic

> Owl's eye inclusions mean CMV; Negri bodies mean rabies.

Library: SARS-CoV-2 pathogenesis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Viral Structure and Pathogenesis

## Key findings

- SARS-CoV-2 binds ACE2, a peptidase that breaks down angiotensin II
- Binding causes loss of ACE2 function, so angiotensin II rises
- Excess angiotensin II causes vasoconstriction and coagulopathy
- RIG-I-like receptors sensing viral RNA are part of the normal response
- Remdesivir, an adenosine analog chain terminator, is used for COVID-19
- The COVID-19 vaccine induces neutralizing antibodies that block the S protein from its receptor

> Losing ACE2 function raises angiotensin II, driving vasoconstriction and clotting.

Library: Hepatitis C antivirals
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Viral Structure and Pathogenesis

## Hepatitis C

HCV is a (+) RNA flavivirus spread parenterally and sexually. Acute infection is usually subclinical, about 80% become chronic, and it's associated with cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma.

## Drugs

| Drug | Target |
|---|---|
| Glecaprevir | NS3/4A protease |
| Pibrentasvir | NS5A |
| Sofosbuvir | NS5B (nucleoside analog) |
| Velpatasvir | NS5A |
| Ribavirin | Used for HCV and RSV (target not listed) |

> Glecaprevir targets NS3/4A, pibrentasvir and velpatasvir target NS5A, and sofosbuvir targets NS5B.

Library: Herpesvirus structure, subfamilies, and latency
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Structure

Herpesviruses are dsDNA viruses with four parts: an outer lipid envelope, a tegument of proteins that aid replication, an icosahedral nucleocapsid, and a core of linear double-stranded DNA.

## Subfamilies

| Subfamily | Viruses |
|---|---|
| Alpha | HSV-1, HSV-2, VZV |
| Beta | CMV, HHV-6, HHV-7 |
| Gamma | EBV, HHV-8 |

## Latency and reactivation

- In latency, no infectious virions are made
- Viral and host factors, including tegument proteins, trigger reactivation, leading to viral spread and symptoms

## Pathogenesis

- Tissue destruction of skin, mucosa, or organs (CMV, HSV, VZV)
- Immunopathologic effects
- Neoplastic transformation, directly or by altering immune surveillance

> Alpha herpesviruses (HSV-1, HSV-2, VZV) establish latency in sensory ganglia.

Library: Herpesvirus lab diagnosis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Key findings

| Method | Key points |
|---|---|
| Tissue or cell culture | Used for HSV, VZV, and CMV |
| Antigen detection | ELISA or immunofluorescence |
| Nucleic acid detection | PCR detects the viral genome |
| Serology | IgM means acute infection; IgG means prior exposure |
| Histopathology | Immunohistochemistry; a Tzanck smear showing multinucleated giant cells suggests infection |

> IgM points to acute infection; IgG points to past exposure.

Library: HSV-1 infection
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Primary infection

- HSV is neurotropic and infects the skin, eye, mucous membranes, and nervous system
- HSV-1 is usually acquired in childhood through saliva and is mostly asymptomatic; incubation is 2-12 days
- Gingivostomatitis (painful blisters and ulcers of the lips, gums, and mouth) in children; exudative pharyngitis in adults
- Resolves in 10-14 days but becomes latent in the sensory (trigeminal) ganglia

## Other manifestations

- Herpetic whitlow: painful finger infection spread from an oral lesion, often mistaken for a bacterial infection
- Herpes gladiatorum: in wrestlers, anywhere on the skin
- Genital HSV-1: uncommon and milder than HSV-2

## Recurrence

Reactivation travels from the neuron down the axon to the skin, causing cold sores (herpes labialis). Triggers include fever, stress, sun, trauma, lowered immunity, and steroids.

> HSV-1 hides in the trigeminal ganglion and reactivates as cold sores.

Library: HSV-2 and genital herpes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Key findings

- Painful grouped vesicles on a red base that progress to pustules and ulcers, usually on the external genitalia
- A first episode can come with fever and lymphadenopathy
- Spread by sex and perinatally; latent in the sacral ganglia
- 88% recur within a year, more often in immunocompromised patients
- Viral shedding, including prolonged asymptomatic shedding, spreads infection to partners
- Viral meningitis is more common with HSV-2 than HSV-1

## Treatment

Acyclovir, famciclovir, or valacyclovir. Suppressive therapy reduces recurrences but may not stop shedding.

> Asymptomatic viral shedding spreads genital herpes, and suppressive therapy doesn't fully stop it.

Library: HSV complications
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Key findings

| Complication | Key points |
|---|---|
| Herpes encephalitis | HSV-1 reactivation from the trigeminal ganglion; temporal lobe; fever, behavior change, seizures; treat with acyclovir |
| Neonatal herpes | Meningoencephalitis, disseminated or localized |
| Ocular herpes | Keratitis or retinitis |
| Immunocompromised reactivation | Esophagitis and other organ involvement |
| Eczema herpeticum | HSV in skin with active disease such as eczema |

> Fever, behavior change, and seizures with a temporal lobe lesion point to HSV encephalitis.

Library: Herpes antivirals
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Mechanism of acyclovir

1. Viral thymidine kinase adds the first phosphate to acyclovir
2. Human kinases add the other two
3. Acyclovir triphosphate is built into viral DNA and terminates the chain

Because the first step needs viral thymidine kinase, these drugs don't work against viruses that lack it.

## Drugs

| Drug | Use | Key toxicity |
|---|---|---|
| Acyclovir, valacyclovir (prodrug), famciclovir | HSV-1, HSV-2, VZV | Agitation, delirium, hallucinations, myoclonus |
| Ganciclovir, valganciclovir | CMV | Bone marrow suppression (ganciclovir); nephrotoxicity (valganciclovir); crystalluria |
| Cidofovir | Resistant HSV, VZV, CMV | Nephrotoxicity (dose-limiting), Fanconi-type syndrome |
| Foscarnet | Resistant HSV, VZV, CMV | Nephrotoxicity, acute tubular necrosis |
| Letermovir | Prevents CMV reactivation after bone marrow transplant | |

- Probenecid is also on the course's herpes medication list

> Acyclovir needs viral thymidine kinase for its first phosphorylation.

Library: Varicella (chickenpox)
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Key findings

- Primary VZV infection, usually in children and often more serious in adults
- Spread by respiratory droplets or contact with active lesions
- Itchy rash that starts on the trunk and moves through vesicle, pustule, and crusted ulcer
- Contagious until all lesions have crusted
- Disseminated varicella occurs in immunocompromised patients
- Latent in the dorsal root or trigeminal ganglia afterward

## Treatment and prevention

- Neonates: varicella zoster immune globulin (VZIG) plus acyclovir
- Adults: acyclovir
- Prevention: VARIVAX, the childhood vaccine

> Chickenpox is contagious until every lesion has crusted.

Library: Herpes zoster (shingles)
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Key findings

- Reactivation of latent VZV from a sensory ganglion
- Painful, burning dermatomal vesicular rash, most often thoracic or lumbar
- Disseminated zoster occurs in immunocompromised patients

## Post-herpetic neuralgia

Pain that persists in the dermatome after the rash heals, common in the elderly and probably from nerve damage. Early treatment may reduce how often it happens and how long it lasts.

## Treatment and prevention

- Treatment: famciclovir or valacyclovir
- Prevention: Shingrix, a two-dose vaccine series

> Early antiviral treatment of shingles may reduce post-herpetic neuralgia.

Library: EBV and infectious mononucleosis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Key findings

- Very common, mostly acquired in childhood and usually asymptomatic
- Spread by saliva through close contact with an asymptomatic shedder
- Replicates in oropharyngeal epithelium and infects B cells through CD21, where it becomes latent

## Infectious mononucleosis

- Ranges from asymptomatic to life-threatening
- Fever, pharyngitis, lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, malaise, fatigue
- Mild leukocytosis with relative neutropenia; elevated liver enzymes
- Atypical lymphocytosis (over 10%) made up of reactive T cells (Downey cells)
- Positive heterophile antibody (Monospot) test

## Treatment

Rest and no contact sports; steroids may help some complications.

> Mono has a positive Monospot and atypical T-lymphocytes.

Library: CMV infection
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Key findings

| Presentation | Key points |
|---|---|
| CMV mononucleosis | Healthy young adults with fever, malaise, adenopathy, atypical lymphocytes, but a negative heterophile test and usually no sore throat |
| Congenital (cytomegalic inclusion disease) | Primary maternal infection; a TORCH infection: hearing loss, microcephaly, chorioretinitis, periventricular calcifications, hepatosplenomegaly with jaundice |
| Perinatal | Acquired at birth or through breast milk |
| Opportunistic | Reactivation or primary infection in immunosuppressed patients; fever, leukopenia, organ inflammation, transplant graft loss |

- Spread through close contact with secretions, transfusions, transplants, and sex
- Latent in mononuclear cells; owl's eye inclusions

## Diagnosis and treatment

- Positive CMV IgM suggests active disease; quantitative blood PCR tracks the virus and predicts reactivation
- Treatment: ganciclovir or valganciclovir; letermovir prevents reactivation after bone marrow transplant

> CMV mono looks like EBV mono but has a negative heterophile test.

Library: HHV-6, HHV-7, and roseola
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Herpes Virus Infections

## Key findings

| Virus | Key points |
|---|---|
| HHV-6 (variants 6A and 6B) | Spread by saliva, blood, tissue, and genital secretions; over 80% are seropositive by age 2; causes exanthem subitum (roseola infantum) and febrile illness without rash |
| HHV-7 | Acquired in childhood through saliva; similar illness with exanthem subitum, fever, and seizures or encephalitis |

## Roseola

High fever for several days, which can cause febrile seizures, followed by a diffuse macular rash that starts on the trunk and spreads ("fever first, rash later").

> Roseola is high fever first, then a rash.

Library: HIV entry: CD4, CCR5, and CXCR4
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## CD4

- CD4 is a transmembrane glycoprotein on all helper T cells that helps them bind MHC class II
- HIV binds CD4, but CD4 alone allows binding, not entry
- The primary target of HIV is the memory T cell

## Coreceptors and fusion

- HIV also needs a coreceptor: CCR5 (CCR5-tropic strains), CXCR4 (CXCR4-tropic), or either (dual/mixed tropic)
- Binding changes the shape of gp120 and gp41, and gp41 exposes a hydrophobic domain that fuses the viral and cell membranes

## CCR5 delta-32

- CCR5 normally binds pro-inflammatory cytokines
- The delta-32 mutation truncates the receptor without harming the inflammatory response
- Homozygotes are resistant to HIV; heterozygotes can be infected but may progress more slowly
- Homozygotes have a higher risk of symptomatic West Nile virus infection

> CCR5 delta-32 homozygotes are resistant to CCR5-tropic HIV.

Library: HIV elite controllers
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## Key findings

- About 1.5-2% of people with HIV
- Keep viral loads undetectable for at least 12 months without treatment
- HIV RNA may be detectable but stays under 200 copies/mL and always under 500 copies/mL
- CD4 count stays above 500 cells/microliter
- CCR5 delta-32 heterozygosity doesn't fully explain it, and they can still transmit the virus
- It isn't known whether treatment benefits them

> Elite controllers keep HIV suppressed without treatment but can still transmit it.

Library: AIDS and opportunistic infections by CD4 count
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## AIDS

Most HIV illness comes from immunosuppression, and the worse the CD4 decline, the worse the immunosuppression. AIDS is defined by a CD4 count under 200, an opportunistic infection, or an AIDS-defining malignancy.

## Infections by CD4 count

| CD4 count | Conditions |
|---|---|
| Under 500 | Oral thrush (Candida), oral hairy leukoplakia (EBV), Kaposi sarcoma (HHV-8), HPV squamous cell carcinoma, reactivated tuberculosis |
| Under 200 | Pneumocystis pneumonia, histoplasmosis, PML (JC virus), HIV nephropathy and dementia |
| Under 100 | Candida esophagitis, CMV (colitis, retinitis, esophagitis, encephalitis, pneumonitis), cryptococcal meningitis, Cryptosporidium, toxoplasmosis, EBV CNS lymphoma, bacillary angiomatosis (Bartonella) |
| Under 50 | Disseminated Mycobacterium avium complex |

## Skin

Seborrheic dermatitis, fungal and viral skin infections, parasites, and Kaposi sarcoma.

> Pneumocystis pneumonia appears once CD4 falls below 200.

Library: HIV-associated candidiasis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## Key findings

Candidiasis (usually Candida albicans) is often the first sign of immunosuppression, and dentists are among the most common people to identify HIV.

| Site | Findings |
|---|---|
| Mouth: pseudomembranous thrush | Most common; scrapable white plaques never limited to the surface of the tongue |
| Mouth: erythematous | Can be painful; often glossitis |
| Mouth: angular cheilitis | Mildly painful, sometimes ulcerated corners of the mouth |
| Esophagus (invasive) | Dysphagia and odynophagia; CD4 under 100 |
| Vagina (recurrent) | Itching, mild pain, white plaques |

## Look-alike

Oral hairy leukoplakia (EBV) is a white plaque on the lateral tongue that can't be scraped off.

## Treatment

Oral imidazoles such as fluconazole.

> Thrush scrapes off; oral hairy leukoplakia on the lateral tongue doesn't.

Library: Pneumocystis pneumonia
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## Key findings

- Pneumocystis jirovecii is a ubiquitous fungus with protozoal features, acquired in childhood; disease usually comes from reactivation
- CD4 under 200 (or under 14%)
- Subacute progressive dry cough, dyspnea, fever, fatigue
- Exam: tachypnea with clear lungs
- Chest imaging: diffuse interstitial infiltrates or ground-glass opacities
- Arterial blood gas: hypoxia, or desaturation with exertion; a cheap and sensitive test
- Diagnosis: bronchoalveolar lavage, biopsy, or induced sputum, showing foamy intra-alveolar fluid with trophs and cysts

## Treatment

- Supportive care with oxygen or intubation
- TMP-SMX for 21 days
- Add prednisone when room-air PaO2 is under 70 mm Hg (or the A-a gradient is over 35), to blunt the inflammatory response and avoid ventilation
- Prophylaxis: TMP-SMX when CD4 is under 200

> Add steroids to TMP-SMX for PCP when room-air PaO2 is under 70 mm Hg.

Library: Mycobacterium avium complex in HIV
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## Key findings

- Disseminated infection when CD4 is under 50
- Found in soil and water
- Nonspecific systemic illness: fever, night sweats, weight loss, malaise, anemia, lymphadenopathy, heavy diarrhea, hepatosplenomegaly, high alkaline phosphatase

## Treatment

- Treating the HIV is central
- Clarithromycin or azithromycin plus ethambutol, adding rifabutin if severe
- Prophylaxis: azithromycin or clarithromycin

> Disseminated MAC appears when CD4 falls below 50.

Library: Cryptococcal meningitis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## Key findings

- Cryptococcus neoformans, an encapsulated yeast; CD4 under 100
- Subacute headache, fever, and confusion
- CSF: lymphocytic pleocytosis, slightly low glucose, opening pressure usually over 20 cm
- India ink stain and cryptococcal antigen confirm the diagnosis

## Treatment

| Phase | Drugs |
|---|---|
| Induction | Amphotericin B for two weeks, with or without flucytosine (bone marrow toxic) |
| Consolidation | High-dose fluconazole |
| Maintenance | Oral fluconazole |

> The most common cause of death is failing to recognize and treat high CSF pressure.

Library: Cerebral toxoplasmosis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## Key findings

- Toxoplasma gondii, a ubiquitous parasite acquired from cysts (cat litter, undercooked meat); CD4 under 100
- Focal encephalitis: headache, confusion, focal deficits, seizures
- Predilection for the basal ganglia
- MRI is more sensitive and usually shows multiple ring-enhancing lesions with edema

## Diagnosis

Usually clinical. A positive toxoplasma IgG with a consistent presentation and response to treatment supports a presumptive diagnosis; a definitive diagnosis needs the organism.

## Treatment and prophylaxis

- Pyrimethamine plus sulfadiazine (with leucovorin)
- Prophylaxis: TMP-SMX

> Multiple ring-enhancing lesions in the basal ganglia point to toxoplasmosis.

Library: CNS lymphoma and PML in HIV
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## Key findings

| Condition | Cause | Findings | Diagnosis | Treatment |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CNS lymphoma | Mostly EBV; the first lymphoma considered HIV-related | Headache, seizures, focal deficits; single or multiple enhancing lesions with edema, which may be solitary | CSF PCR for EBV | Radiation |
| Progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML) | JC polyomavirus; affects only white matter | Gait disturbance, confusion, homonymous hemianopia; non-enhancing demyelination on MRI | CSF PCR for JC virus | Treat the underlying HIV |

> PML is JC virus demyelination of white matter, with non-enhancing lesions.

Library: CMV retinitis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## Key findings

- Common with CMV infection in AIDS (CD4 under 100)
- Floaters, blurred vision, and scotomata; can lead to vision loss
- Cotton-wool spots and hemorrhage on fundoscopy
- Diagnosed by an ophthalmologist

## Treatment

- Ganciclovir (bone marrow toxic), a ganciclovir retinal implant, or oral valganciclovir (nephrotoxic)
- Treating the underlying HIV

> CMV retinitis presents with floaters and scotomata when CD4 is under 100.

Library: HIV-associated diarrhea and Cryptosporidium
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Pathogenesis

## Causes

Cryptosporidium, Mycobacterium avium complex, Giardia, and amoeba.

## Cryptosporidium

- A parasite found in water supplies; CD4 under 100
- Large-volume, chronic watery diarrhea
- Diagnosis: modified acid-fast stain of stool showing oocysts, or a GI pathogen panel
- Treatment: treat the HIV to restore immunity; nitazoxanide for 14 days

> Acid-fast oocysts in stool point to Cryptosporidium.

Library: How viruses cause cancer
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Oncogenic Viruses

## Key findings

Persistent viral infection can drive uncontrolled growth and malignant transformation.

| Mechanism | Example |
|---|---|
| Viral insertion turns a proto-oncogene into an oncogene | EBV LMP-1 |
| Viral proteins block tumor suppressors | HPV E6 (p53) and E7 (Rb) |
| Viral anti-apoptosis proteins | HHV-8 BCL2 and LANA-1 |

| Virus | Cancer |
|---|---|
| EBV | Burkitt, Hodgkin, and non-Hodgkin lymphoma; nasopharyngeal carcinoma; CNS lymphoma |
| HPV 16, 18 | Cervical and anogenital carcinoma |
| HHV-8 | Kaposi sarcoma |
| HBV | Hepatocellular carcinoma |
| HTLV-1 | Adult T-cell leukemia |

> Oncogenic viruses either activate oncogenes or disable tumor suppressors.

Library: EBV oncogenesis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Oncogenic Viruses

## Infection

EBV is an enveloped dsDNA herpesvirus spread by saliva. It starts in the oropharynx, reaches B cells through viremia, and stays latent in B cells. Cytotoxic T cells (Downey cells) are activated to kill infected B cells.

## Viral proteins

| Protein | Effect |
|---|---|
| LMP-1 | Oncogene that acts like a constitutively active CD40 receptor, driving B cell growth and activating BCL2 to block apoptosis |
| EBNA-2 | Activates host genes such as cyclin D and immortalizes B cells |
| vIL-10 | Stops macrophages from activating the T cells that would control transformed B cells |

> LMP-1 acts like a permanently active CD40 and turns on BCL2.

Library: Burkitt lymphoma and EBV
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Oncogenic Viruses

## Key findings

- B cell lymphoma, common along the malaria belt; more than 90% of African tumors carry the EBV genome
- EBV acts as a polyclonal B cell mitogen that sets the stage for the t(8;14) translocation, which puts MYC under the immunoglobulin heavy chain promoter
- Starry sky pattern on histology; CD10 positive; Ki-67 near 100%

| Form | Presentation |
|---|---|
| Endemic (African) | Jaw mass; EBV |
| Sporadic | Abdominal (ileocecal) mass |
| HIV-associated | |

> Burkitt lymphoma is t(8;14) MYC activation, with a starry sky pattern.

Library: HPV oncogenesis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Oncogenic Viruses

## Key findings

- Non-enveloped, circular dsDNA virus that infects squamous epithelium
- Hallmark: koilocytosis (cytoplasmic vacuolization)
- Viral DNA integrates into the host genome, leading to malignant transformation
- E6 inactivates p53, and E7 inactivates Rb

| Risk | Types | Disease |
|---|---|---|
| Low | 6, 11 | Anogenital warts (condylomata acuminata, cauliflower-like lesions) |
| High | 16, 18 | 70% of cervical carcinoma; other anogenital cancers |

## Prevention

The HPV vaccine protects against types 6, 11, 16, and 18.

> HPV E6 degrades p53 and E7 inactivates Rb.

Library: Kaposi sarcoma herpesvirus (HHV-8)
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Oncogenic Viruses

## Key findings

- Spread sexually or through organ transplants; primary infection is asymptomatic
- Latent in endothelial spindle cells; disproportionately affects men who have sex with men with HIV
- The virus carries human-like genes: anti-apoptosis proteins (BCL2), an Rb inhibitor (LANA-1), and growth factors (IL-6, IL-8) for B cells and spindle cells
- Uncontrolled spindle cell growth causes Kaposi sarcoma: violaceous raised plaques or nodules made of sheets of proliferating spindle cells, often in the mouth and lymph nodes, with visceral involvement in about half
- Also causes Castleman disease

## Treatment

Antiretrovirals alone for early disease (stage T0), plus liposomal doxorubicin for more advanced disease (stage T1).

> Kaposi sarcoma is HHV-8-driven proliferation of endothelial spindle cells.

Library: Hepatitis B and hepatocellular carcinoma
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Oncogenic Viruses

## Key findings

- HBV causes about 80% of hepatocellular carcinoma
- Enveloped dsDNA hepadnavirus spread by blood, sex, and shared needles
- Causes hepatitis, cirrhosis, and hepatocellular carcinoma
- Replicates through an RNA intermediate copied back to DNA by reverse transcriptase, so lamivudine works against it

> HBV causes about 80% of hepatocellular carcinoma.

Library: HIV transmission
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Epidemiology, Treatment, and Prevention

## Key findings

- HIV is transmitted through sex, blood transfusion, shared needles and syringes, pregnancy, breastfeeding, and organ transplant
- Primary infection can cause a mononucleosis-like illness
- As disease progresses, the virus kills more CD4 T cells, viral replication rises, and the gut barrier weakens

> Primary HIV infection can look like mononucleosis.

Library: HIV entry inhibitors: maraviroc and enfuvirtide
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Epidemiology, Treatment, and Prevention

## Key findings

| Drug | Class | Mechanism | Key points |
|---|---|---|---|
| Maraviroc | CCR5 antagonist | Blocks the CCR5 coreceptor | Needs viral tropism testing first; rarely used now; upper respiratory infections |
| Enfuvirtide | Fusion inhibitor | Mimics the C-helix of gp41 and blocks the shape change needed for fusion | Used for multidrug-resistant HIV; injection site reactions, hypersensitivity, hypotension, GI upset, itching, myalgia |

> Maraviroc only works on CCR5-tropic virus, so tropism testing comes first.

Library: NRTIs and NNRTIs
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Epidemiology, Treatment, and Prevention

## NRTIs

Nucleoside/nucleotide reverse transcriptase inhibitors act as chain terminators.

| Drug | Key toxicity |
|---|---|
| Abacavir (ABC) | Hypersensitivity; screen for HLA-B*57:01 first |
| Emtricitabine (FTC) | Few side effects |
| Lamivudine (3TC) | Few side effects; also treats HBV |
| Tenofovir (TDF) | Bone density loss and Fanconi syndrome; tenofovir alafenamide (TAF) was developed to reduce these |
| Zidovudine (AZT) | Bone marrow suppression, mitochondrial toxicity |

- Didanosine and stavudine are also on the course's NRTI list

## NNRTIs

Non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors are rarely used now. They cause rashes, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, and hepatitis ("Stevens-Johnson had a weird rash and gave me hepatitis"). Examples include efavirenz, delavirdine, and nevirapine.

> Screen for HLA-B*57:01 before giving abacavir.

Library: Integrase and protease inhibitors
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Epidemiology, Treatment, and Prevention

## Integrase strand transfer inhibitors (INSTIs, "-tegravir")

- Block insertion of HIV DNA into the host genome, leaving circular, non-integrated episomes
- Best tolerated class with the fewest side effects; resistance develops slowly

| Drug | Combination |
|---|---|
| Dolutegravir (DTG) | Triumeq: dolutegravir + abacavir + lamivudine |
| Bictegravir (BIC) | Biktarvy: bictegravir + tenofovir alafenamide + emtricitabine |
| Raltegravir | |

## Protease inhibitors ("-navir")

- Viral protease lets new virions mature, so blocking it stops maturation
- Side effects are mostly GI
- Ritonavir is an example

> Integrase inhibitors are the best-tolerated HIV class and the base of two-class therapy.

Library: HIV treatment strategy and resistance
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / HIV Epidemiology, Treatment, and Prevention

## Management

- Start treatment as soon as possible, regardless of CD4 count
- Two-class therapy: an integrase inhibitor plus one or two NRTIs (for example, dolutegravir plus lamivudine)

## Resistance mutations

| Mutation | Resistance to |
|---|---|
| K103N | NNRTIs |
| TAMs (thymidine analog mutations in reverse transcriptase) | NRTIs zidovudine and stavudine |
| M184V | NRTIs lamivudine and emtricitabine |

> M184V causes resistance to lamivudine and emtricitabine.

Library: Mechanisms of cell injury
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Pathology of Cell Injury and Death

## Key findings

The main targets of cell injury are the mitochondria, the cell membrane, the cytoskeleton, and DNA and protein synthesis.

1. Mitochondrial damage lowers ATP production
2. Membrane pumps fail
3. Intracellular free calcium rises and directly damages membranes and mitochondria
4. Reactive oxygen species form and cause more membrane, DNA, and mitochondrial damage

> Rising intracellular calcium drives much of the damage in cell injury.

Library: Reversible vs. irreversible cell injury
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Pathology of Cell Injury and Death

## Key findings

| Feature | Reversible | Irreversible |
|---|---|---|
| Cause | Mild stimulus; cell recovers when it's removed | Severe or prolonged stimulus with ATP depletion and membrane damage |
| Electron microscopy | Ribosomes detach from rough ER; mitochondrial cristae preserved (the earliest changes) | Severe mitochondrial swelling with destroyed cristae; extensive membrane damage; lysosomal enzymes leak and digest the cell |
| Light microscopy | Cellular swelling, hydropic degeneration (water droplets), fatty change (lipid droplets) | Nuclear changes and deeply eosinophilic cytoplasm from denatured proteins |

## Nuclear changes of necrosis

| Change | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Pyknosis | Shrinkage and condensation into a small, dense nucleus |
| Karyorrhexis | Fragmentation of the nucleus |
| Karyolysis | Dissolution of the nucleus |

- In irreversible injury, calcium influx and release of enzymes into the tissue lead to necrosis

> Pyknosis, karyorrhexis, and karyolysis mark irreversible injury.

Library: Types of necrosis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Pathology of Cell Injury and Death

## Key findings

| Type | Features | Typical setting |
|---|---|---|
| Coagulative | Cell outlines and architecture preserved early; protein denaturation makes cells deeply eosinophilic | Ischemia or infarction of heart, kidney, liver, spleen (not brain) |
| Liquefactive | Enzyme digestion outpaces denaturation; architecture lost, tissue liquefies | Ischemia of the brain |
| Caseous | Soft, white, "cream cheese" material; amorphous with no architecture | Tuberculosis |
| Enzymatic fat | Chalky, pale foci in fat around the pancreas from released enzymes | Pancreatitis from alcohol or duct obstruction |
| Fibrinoid | Fibrin deposits in damaged arterial walls | Vasculitis, hypertension |

> Ischemia causes coagulative necrosis everywhere except the brain, where it's liquefactive.

Library: Antifungal drugs at a glance
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Antifungal Drugs

## Key findings

Two of the three core antifungals act on ergosterol in the fungal cell membrane (one binds it and one blocks its synthesis), and the third becomes 5-FU and disrupts fungal DNA synthesis.

| Drug | Target | Main uses | Key toxicity |
|---|---|---|---|
| Amphotericin B | Binds ergosterol and tears holes in the membrane | Cryptococcal meningitis, systemic fungal infections | Fever, chills, hypotension, nephrotoxicity, arrhythmias, anemia, IV phlebitis |
| Flucytosine | Becomes 5-FU and inhibits thymidylate synthase | Cryptococcal meningitis, systemic fungal infections (with amphotericin B) | Bone marrow suppression |
| Fluconazole | Inhibits the CYP450 step from lanosterol to ergosterol | Candida albicans, cryptococcal meningitis | Testosterone synthesis inhibition, liver dysfunction |

## Other agents in the course list

- Nystatin and itraconazole are listed without further detail
- Itraconazole is paired with TMP-SMX as prophylaxis in chronic granulomatous disease

> Amphotericin B binds ergosterol, while fluconazole blocks ergosterol synthesis.

Library: Amphotericin B
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Antifungal Drugs

## Mechanism

Amphotericin B binds ergosterol in the fungal cell membrane and tears holes in it.

## Uses

- Cryptococcal meningitis, where it's given with flucytosine
- Systemic fungal infections

## Toxicity

Students remember it as "amphoterrible" because the side effects are broad:

- Fever, chills, and hypotension with infusions
- Nephrotoxicity
- Arrhythmias
- Anemia
- IV phlebitis at the infusion site

> Amphotericin B pokes holes in the fungal membrane by binding ergosterol, and nephrotoxicity is its signature toxicity.

Library: Flucytosine
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Antifungal Drugs

## Mechanism

Flucytosine is a precursor of 5-fluorouracil (5-FU). Inside the fungus it becomes 5-FU, which inhibits thymidylate synthase and blocks DNA synthesis.

## Uses

- Cryptococcal meningitis
- Systemic fungal infections
- Always used in combination with amphotericin B

## Toxicity

- Bone marrow suppression

> Flucytosine works through 5-FU, so it shares 5-FU's marrow toxicity.

Library: Fluconazole
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 8: Foundations of Microbiology I / Antifungal Drugs

## Mechanism

Fluconazole inhibits ergosterol synthesis by blocking the cytochrome P450 enzyme that converts lanosterol to ergosterol.

## Uses

- Candida albicans
- Cryptococcal meningitis, following amphotericin B and flucytosine

## Toxicity

Because it inhibits CYP450 enzymes, fluconazole also affects human steroid and drug metabolism:

- Inhibits testosterone synthesis
- Liver dysfunction

> Fluconazole's CYP450 inhibition explains both its antifungal action and its effects on testosterone and the liver.

Library: Staphylococcus aureus
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Cocci

## Key findings

- Gram-positive cocci, coagulase positive, beta-hemolytic
- Catalase positive, which is why it's a classic infection in chronic granulomatous disease

## Diseases

| Disease | Cause |
|---|---|
| Skin infections (furuncles, cellulitis), including MRSA | Local invasion |
| Staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome (Ritter's disease) | Exfoliative toxins |
| Toxic shock syndrome | TSST-1, a superantigen that binds MHC II and raises IL-2 |
| Food poisoning | Preformed toxin in foods such as mayonnaise and meats |
| Osteomyelitis | Also a leading cause in IV drug users |

## Treatment clues

- MSSA: antistaphylococcal penicillins (nafcillin, oxacillin, dicloxacillin) or cefazolin
- MRSA, in order: vancomycin, then ceftaroline, then daptomycin

> Toxic shock syndrome comes from TSST-1, a superantigen that binds MHC II.

Library: Coagulase-negative staphylococci
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Cocci

## Key findings

| Organism | Coagulase | Classic infection | Clue |
|---|---|---|---|
| Staphylococcus saprophyticus | Negative | Urinary tract infections | Urease positive |
| Staphylococcus epidermidis | Negative | Bacteremia in neutropenic patients | Prosthetic devices; urease positive |

Both are gram-positive cocci that are separated from S. aureus by a negative coagulase test.

> S. epidermidis infects prosthetic devices, and S. saprophyticus causes UTIs.

Library: Streptococcus pyogenes (group A strep)
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Cocci

## Key findings

- Gram-positive cocci, beta-hemolytic, Lancefield group A
- M protein is the major virulence factor

## Suppurative diseases

- Pharyngitis
- Erysipelas, cellulitis, and lymphangitis
- Scarlet fever: strawberry tongue, Pastia's lines, and a blanching rash

## Immune-mediated sequelae

- Rheumatic fever, with damage driven by Th1 cells
- Glomerulonephritis, with hump-like deposits of antigens and immune complexes
- Sydenham chorea

## Treatment

Penicillin G covers Streptococcus pyogenes.

> M protein is group A strep's main virulence factor, and rheumatic fever is a Th1-driven complication.

Library: Streptococcus agalactiae (group B strep)
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Cocci

## Key findings

- Gram-positive cocci, Lancefield group B, encapsulated
- Weakly beta- or alpha-hemolytic
- Colonizes the GI and GU tracts
- CAMP test positive: an arrowhead-shaped zone of hemolysis

## Diseases

- Neonatal sepsis, transmitted to babies at birth and helped by the hemolytic pigment granadaene
- The first cause on the course's meningitis list, ahead of Listeria and E. coli
- A concern in pregnancy and for patients without a functioning spleen

## Treatment

Penicillin G covers Streptococcus agalactiae.

> Group B strep passes to the baby at birth and is the leading cause of neonatal sepsis and meningitis.

Library: Streptococcus pneumoniae and viridans streptococci
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Cocci

## Key findings

Both are alpha-hemolytic gram-positive cocci. Optochin separates them because S. pneumoniae shows a zone of inhibition around an optochin disk.

| Feature | S. pneumoniae | Viridans streptococci |
|---|---|---|
| Classic disease | Pneumonia | Dental caries, infective endocarditis |
| Key mechanism | Pneumolysin makes pores and lowers ciliary motility | Sugar-driven local acidification and enamel demineralization |
| Optochin | Zone of inhibition (sensitive) | Not listed |

## Risk groups

S. pneumoniae is the top encapsulated organism threatening asplenic and sickle cell patients.

> S. pneumoniae is alpha-hemolytic and optochin sensitive.

Library: Enterococcus
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Cocci

## Key findings

- E. faecalis and E. faecium (group D strep)
- Gram-positive cocci, gamma-hemolytic (no hemolysis)
- Grows in 6.5% NaCl (turbidity)
- Turns black on agar by reacting with iron
- Lives in the GI, GU, and biliary tracts

## Treatment clues

- Aminopenicillins such as ampicillin cover Enterococcus faecalis
- First-generation cephalosporins have no activity against Enterococcus
- Imipenem and meropenem cover E. faecalis, but ertapenem doesn't
- Linezolid covers resistant enterococci

> Enterococcus grows in 6.5% salt and isn't covered by cephalosporins.

Library: Hemolysis patterns of gram-positive cocci
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Cocci

## Key findings

| Hemolysis | Organisms | Appearance |
|---|---|---|
| Beta | S. pyogenes, S. agalactiae (weakly), S. aureus, and among bacilli Listeria | Complete clearing |
| Alpha | S. pneumoniae, viridans streptococci | Partial, green |
| Gamma | Enterococcus | None |

## Tests that finish the identification

- Coagulase separates S. aureus (positive) from S. epidermidis and S. saprophyticus (negative)
- CAMP test identifies group B strep
- Optochin sensitivity identifies S. pneumoniae
- Growth in 6.5% NaCl identifies Enterococcus

> Alpha-hemolytic cocci are split by optochin: S. pneumoniae is sensitive.

Library: Bacillus anthracis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Bacilli

## Key findings

- Large gram-positive rod, non-motile and non-hemolytic
- Spore forming, with central spores ("bamboo" chains) and medusa head colonies
- Found in contaminated livestock, soil, and feed

## Toxins

Protective antigen (PA) delivers edema toxin (ET) and lethal toxin (LT), which inhibit the MAPK pathway. Edema toxin raises cAMP and lowers ATP, causing fluid shifts, while lethal toxin causes cell leakage and lysis. B. anthracis and B. pertussis both work by raising cAMP.

## Forms of anthrax

| Form | Key features |
|---|---|
| Cutaneous | Malignant pustule, painful adenopathy after contact with infected animal tissue |
| Inhalational | Biphasic respiratory illness with a widened mediastinum |
| Gastrointestinal | Intestinal ulcers, mesenteric adenopathy, bloody diarrhea, sepsis |
| Injectional | Cellulitis, abscess, necrotizing fasciitis, progressing to respiratory failure, meningitis, and shock |

## Treatment clue

Ciprofloxacin is listed for B. anthracis.

> Inhalational anthrax classically shows a widened mediastinum.

Library: Bacillus cereus
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Bacilli

## Key findings

- Large motile gram-positive rod, beta-hemolytic, spore forming
- Found in soil

## Food poisoning from reheated rice

| Toxin | Effect | Illness |
|---|---|---|
| Heat-stable | Raises cGMP | Vomiting |
| Heat-labile | Raises cAMP | Diarrhea |

## Eye infections

B. cereus causes rapid eye toxicity and post-traumatic endophthalmitis.

> Fried or reheated rice plus vomiting points to B. cereus's heat-stable toxin.

Library: Clostridium perfringens
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Bacilli

## Key findings

- Gram-positive rod, obligate anaerobe like other clostridia
- Alpha toxin (lecithinase) produces zoned hemolysis on blood agar: beta-hemolysis inside, alpha-hemolysis outside

## Diseases

- Gas gangrene
- Food poisoning, linked to contaminated pork
- Necrotizing enterocolitis: abdominal pain, vomiting, and bloody diarrhea; in premature infants it's tied to reduced peristalsis and low microbial diversity and can be fatal

> C. perfringens alpha toxin is a lecithinase that causes double-zone hemolysis and gas gangrene.

Library: Tetanus vs. botulism
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Bacilli

## Key findings

Both are large motile gram-positive rods and obligate anaerobes with toxins that act at the synapse, but they cause opposite paralysis.

| Feature | Clostridium tetani | Clostridium botulinum |
|---|---|---|
| Toxin action | Blocks glycine and GABA release | Blocks acetylcholine release |
| Paralysis | Spastic | Flaccid |
| Signs | Risus sardonicus, spasms, asphyxiation or pneumonia | Flaccid paralysis; floppy baby syndrome in infants |
| Source | Soil | Canned food, honey, corn syrup; spores in soil and water |
| Lab clue | Tennis racket shape; raised colonies with irregular edges | Infant botulism: positive stool culture and toxin assay |

> Tetanus toxin causes spastic paralysis, while botulinum toxin causes flaccid paralysis.

Library: Clostridioides difficile
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Bacilli

## Key findings

- Gram-positive, spore-forming rod
- Overgrows after antibiotics disrupt the normal flora, especially fluoroquinolones and clindamycin
- Spread in healthcare settings

## Disease

- Diarrhea
- Pseudomembranous colitis
- Toxic megacolon

## Treatment clue

Metronidazole is listed for C. difficile. Clindamycin causes C. difficile colitis in about 10% of patients.

> Recent clindamycin or a fluoroquinolone followed by diarrhea suggests C. difficile.

Library: Corynebacterium diphtheriae
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Bacilli

## Key findings

- Gram-positive rod spread by touch and respiratory secretions
- Diphtheria toxin inactivates elongation factor 2 by ADP-ribosylation, the same mechanism as Pseudomonas exotoxin A
- Black colonies on blood agar; Loeffler's medium shows metachromatic granules

## Disease

- Pharyngitis progressing to palatal paralysis and lymphadenopathy ("bull neck")
- Myocarditis and cranial neuropathy
- Necrotic cutaneous infection, with outbreaks in homeless populations

## Treatment and prevention

- Penicillin or erythromycin plus diphtheria antitoxin
- Prevention with the Tdap vaccine

> Diphtheria toxin ADP-ribosylates elongation factor 2 and stops protein synthesis.

Library: Listeria monocytogenes
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Positive Bacilli

## Key findings

- Small aerobic gram-positive rod, beta-hemolytic, catalase positive
- Tumbling motility and actin rockets; umbrella-shaped growth in agar
- Found in water, plants, and animals; spread by unpasteurized milk and deli meats

## Disease

- Flu-like illness
- Puerperal (childbed) fever and sepsis in pregnancy
- Neonatal sepsis and meningitis, second on the course's meningitis list after group B strep

## Treatment

Ampicillin is the drug linked to Listeria.

> Listeria in pregnancy or a newborn is treated with ampicillin.

Library: Enterobacteriaceae
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Negative Bacilli

## Key findings

- Gram-negative rods and facultative anaerobes
- All ferment glucose and reduce nitrate to nitrite
- None have cytochrome c oxidase (oxidase negative)
- Members include Salmonella typhi, Yersinia pestis, E. coli, Shigella dysenteriae, Proteus, Serratia, Enterobacter, and Klebsiella pneumoniae

## Endotoxin

Endotoxin is the major pathogenic factor in serious illness. It rises during rapid growth and bacterial lysis and drives septic shock.

## Lab clue

MacConkey agar selects for these organisms because bile salts and crystal violet block gram-positive growth, and lactose fermenters turn red.

> Enterobacteriaceae ferment glucose, reduce nitrate, and are oxidase negative.

Library: E. coli, Shigella, and Salmonella typhi
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Negative Bacilli

## Key findings

| Organism | Motility | Disease | Mechanism or clue |
|---|---|---|---|
| Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC) | Motile | Watery (osmotic) diarrhea | Heat-labile and heat-stable toxins |
| EHEC | Motile | Foodborne illness | Undercooked meat |
| Shigella | Non-motile | Bloody diarrhea, HUS, MAHA | Shiga toxin |
| Salmonella typhi | Not listed | Typhoid fever | Osteomyelitis in sickle cell disease |

## Related points

- E. coli is third on the course's meningitis list, after group B strep and Listeria
- Ciprofloxacin is listed for S. typhi and GI infections

> Shiga toxin causes bloody diarrhea and HUS.

Library: Klebsiella pneumoniae and Proteus mirabilis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Negative Bacilli

## Key findings

| Feature | Klebsiella pneumoniae | Proteus mirabilis |
|---|---|---|
| Motility | Non-motile | Swarming motility |
| Capsule | Thick capsule | Not listed |
| Urease | Positive | Positive |
| Oxidase | Negative | Negative |
| Classic clue | Threat to asplenic and sickle cell patients | Kidney stones; agar looks like a target with ripples |

Both are Enterobacteriaceae, so both ferment glucose and are oxidase negative.

> Proteus is urease positive and swarms, which explains its link to kidney stones.

Library: Pseudomonas aeruginosa
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Negative Bacilli

## Key findings

- Gram-negative rod, obligate aerobe, oxidase positive
- Blue-green pigment with a grape-like odor
- Found in soil and water
- Classic in diabetics, burn patients, and IV drug users; a cause of osteomyelitis
- Catalase positive, so it's a risk in chronic granulomatous disease (Burkholderia cepacia)

## Virulence factors

| Factor | Effect |
|---|---|
| Elastase and alkaline protease | Destroy the cornea |
| Exotoxin A | Inactivates elongation factor 2 by ADP-ribosylation |
| Exoenzyme S | Impairs phagocytosis |

## Antipseudomonal drugs

Gentamicin, piperacillin, piperacillin-tazobactam, cefepime, ceftazidime, imipenem, meropenem, ciprofloxacin, and aztreonam for penicillin-allergic patients.

> Pseudomonas exotoxin A and diphtheria toxin both ADP-ribosylate elongation factor 2.

Library: Anaerobic gram-negative bacilli
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Negative Bacilli

## Key findings

- Bacteroidetes are gram-negative anaerobes that use nitrate as the final electron acceptor
- They contain endotoxin and make beta-lactamase
- They need anaerobic culture methods, such as thioglycolate broth or an anaerobic jar

## Clues to an anaerobic infection

- Infection near a mucosal surface
- Mixed flora on Gram stain
- Foul-smelling (malodorous) infection
- Gas in the tissue

## Drugs with anaerobic coverage

Metronidazole, clindamycin, amoxicillin-clavulanate, and moxifloxacin. Aztreonam and first-generation cephalosporins have no anaerobic activity.

> Mixed flora near a mucosal surface with a foul smell suggests anaerobes.

Library: Neisseria meningitidis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Negative Cocci and Coccobacilli

## Key findings

- Gram-negative diplococcus that ferments glucose and maltose
- Virulence: capsule, endotoxin (LOS, with lipid A as the toxic part), and pili
- Grows on chocolate agar (Thayer-Martin)
- A major threat to asplenic and sickle cell patients

## Disease

- Meningitis
- Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome: DIC and shock

## Treatment and prevention

| Situation | Approach |
|---|---|
| Treatment | Ceftriaxone |
| Routine vaccine | Quadrivalent polysaccharide-protein conjugate (A, C, Y, W); pentavalent (A, B, C, Y, W) |
| Serogroup B vaccine | Ages 18 to 23 for short-term protection, such as military recruits and college students |
| Close contacts | Chemoprophylaxis with rifampin (a strong CYP450 inducer), ciprofloxacin, or azithromycin |

> Meningococcus ferments maltose, and close contacts get chemoprophylaxis.

Library: Neisseria gonorrhoeae
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Negative Cocci and Coccobacilli

## Key findings

- Gram-negative diplococcus that ferments glucose only (not maltose)
- Virulence: LOS and pili, with no capsule
- Grows on chocolate agar
- Causes gonococcal conjunctivitis in newborns (ophthalmia neonatorum)

## Treatment

Parenteral ceftriaxone.

## Resistance mechanisms

| Mechanism | Drugs affected |
|---|---|
| Efflux pumps | Tetracyclines |
| PenA mutations change penicillin-binding protein affinity | Beta-lactams |
| PenB porin changes | Beta-lactams |
| DNA gyrase changes | Fluoroquinolones |

> Gonococcus has no capsule and ferments glucose but not maltose, unlike meningococcus.

Library: Haemophilus influenzae
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Negative Cocci and Coccobacilli

## Key findings

- Pleomorphic gram-negative coccobacillus with a capsule and LOS
- Needs factors X and V; grows on chocolate agar
- Type b threatens asplenic and sickle cell patients

## Diseases

- Otitis media, sinusitis, bronchitis, and pneumonia
- Epiglottitis, with the 4 Ds: dysphagia, dysphonia, drooling, and distress
- Meningitis

## Diagnosis and treatment

| Situation | Approach |
|---|---|
| Diagnosis | Culture showing X and V factor dependence; antigen detection |
| Otitis media and sinusitis | Ampicillin, amoxicillin-clavulanate, or TMP-SMX |
| Meningitis | Third-generation cephalosporin |
| Close contacts | Rifampin prophylaxis |
| Prevention | Hib vaccine |

> H. influenzae needs factors X and V, and meningitis is treated with a third-generation cephalosporin.

Library: Bordetella pertussis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Negative Cocci and Coccobacilli

## Key findings

- Encapsulated gram-negative coccobacillus, oxidase positive
- Spread by aerosol; doesn't invade tissue
- Toxin ADP-ribosylates Gi, raising cAMP (the same cAMP effect as B. anthracis edema toxin)
- Very high white count with about 50% lymphocytes
- Cultured on Regan-Lowe medium

## Stages

| Stage | Features |
|---|---|
| Catarrhal | Nonspecific flu-like symptoms |
| Paroxysmal | Spasms of cough, cyanosis, vomiting, whoop, rales, syncope |
| Convalescent | Spasms resolve over 1 to 3 months |

## Treatment and prevention

Erythromycin for treatment and Tdap for prevention.

> Pertussis toxin ADP-ribosylates Gi, and the whoop comes in the paroxysmal stage.

Library: Moraxella catarrhalis and Kingella kingae
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Gram-Negative Cocci and Coccobacilli

## Key findings

| Feature | Moraxella catarrhalis | Kingella kingae |
|---|---|---|
| Shape | Gram-negative diplococcus | Gram-negative coccobacillus |
| Oxidase | Positive | Positive |
| Virulence | LOS (lipid A is the toxic part); doesn't use carbohydrates | Pili, capsule, toxin |
| Diseases | Otitis media, pneumonia | Septic arthritis in toddlers, endocarditis, osteoarticular infection |
| Lab clue | Grows on chocolate agar | PCR-based assays |

> Septic arthritis in a toddler points to Kingella kingae.

Library: Mycobacteria overview
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Mycobacteria and Nocardia

## Key findings

- Mycobacteria are acid-fast bacilli
- The group includes M. tuberculosis, M. leprae, and the nontuberculous mycobacteria (NTM)
- M. tuberculosis is an obligate aerobe

## Latent tuberculosis and TNF inhibitors

Latent M. tuberculosis can reactivate when a patient starts a TNF-alpha inhibitor such as infliximab, adalimumab, or etanercept. A PPD or IGRA is done before starting these drugs.

## HIV

HIV raises the risk of reactivating latent tuberculosis even when the CD4 count is under 500.

> Screen for latent TB with a PPD or IGRA before starting a TNF-alpha inhibitor.

Library: Nontuberculous mycobacteria
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Mycobacteria and Nocardia

## Key findings

- Cause pulmonary infections, disseminated disease, and granulomatous inflammation
- Disseminated NTM occurs with AIDS (CD4 under 50) and with genetic defects in IFN-gamma and IL-12
- The most common NTM in HIV-negative patients are MAC, M. abscessus, and M. kansasii

## Growth and pigment groups

| Organism | Growth | Pigment | Key clue |
|---|---|---|---|
| M. kansasii | Slow | Photochromogen | TB-like lung disease with cavitation and upper lobe predilection; isolated from tap water |
| M. marinum | Slow | Photochromogen | |
| M. scrofulaceum | Slow | Scotochromogen | |
| M. gordonae | Slow | Scotochromogen | |
| M. avium complex (MAC) | Slow | Non-chromogen | Disseminated disease in AIDS with CD4 under 50; soil and water |
| M. abscessus | Rapid | Non-pigmented | Resembles MAC disease; soil, dust, water, animals |

Other rapid growers are M. chelonae and M. fortuitum.

## Treatment clues

- MAC treatment is clarithromycin or azithromycin plus ethambutol, adding rifabutin if severe; prophylaxis uses azithromycin, clarithromycin, or rifabutin
- Rifabutin and amikacin are listed for NTM infections

> M. kansasii mimics tuberculosis, with upper lobe cavitation.

Library: Leprosy: tuberculoid vs. lepromatous
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Mycobacteria and Nocardia

## Key findings

- Caused by Mycobacterium leprae, which doesn't grow in culture
- Found in armadillos
- The form of disease depends on the T helper response

| Feature | Tuberculoid (paucibacillary) | Lepromatous (multibacillary) |
|---|---|---|
| Immune response | Strong Th1 (IL-2, IFN-gamma) | Weak Th1, strong Th2 |
| Pathology | Granulomatous infection with few organisms | "Globi" of acid-fast bacilli |
| Clinical clue | Nerve damage, autoamputation of fingers or toes | Widespread bacilli |

## Treatment

Triple-drug regimen of dapsone, rifampin, and clofazimine.

> A strong Th1 response gives tuberculoid leprosy; a Th2-skewed response gives lepromatous leprosy.

Library: Nocardia
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Mycobacteria and Nocardia

## Key findings

- An actinomycete; Nocardia asteroides is the main species
- Gram-positive, weakly acid-fast, with filamentous branching aerial hyphae
- Obligate aerobe found in soil
- Catalase positive (a CGD risk) and urease positive
- Prevents phagosome acidification and phagolysosome fusion
- Doesn't form granulomas

## Disease

Mostly an opportunistic infection: respiratory infection in immunocompromised patients that can spread from the lungs to the CNS as a brain abscess.

## Treatment

TMP-SMX is listed for Nocardia; linezolid also covers it.

> Lung infection plus a brain abscess in an immunocompromised patient suggests Nocardia.

Library: Antimycobacterial drugs
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Mycobacteria and Nocardia

## Key findings

| Drug | Mechanism or use | Toxicity |
|---|---|---|
| Rifampin | Inhibits bacterial DNA-dependent RNA polymerase; TB and leprosy | Red body fluids, hepatotoxicity, CYP inducer |
| Isoniazid | TB | Sideroblastic anemia from B6 deficiency (B6 is the ALAS cofactor) |
| Pyrazinamide | TB | Not listed |
| Ethambutol | Inhibits linking of the mycobacterial cell wall; TB and MAC | Hyperuricemia and gout |

## Related regimens

- MAC: clarithromycin or azithromycin plus ethambutol, adding rifabutin if severe
- Leprosy: dapsone, rifampin, and clofazimine

> Isoniazid causes B6 deficiency, which can cause sideroblastic anemia.

Library: Brucellosis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Zoonotic, Tick-Borne, and Intracellular Infections

## Key findings

- Brucella is a gram-negative coccobacillus
- Spread by contact with infected animals, animal-derived food, inhalation, or skin abrasions
- Replicates after the Brucella-containing vacuole fuses with the endosome and ER

## Clinical features

| Form | Features |
|---|---|
| Brucellosis | Spiking, undulating or relapsing fever with chills, night sweats, hepatosplenomegaly, malodorous sweat; non-erosive peripheral arthritis, sacroiliitis, spondylitis |
| Neurobrucellosis | Cranial nerve palsies, personality change, depression, meningoencephalitis, brain abscess and granuloma |

## Diagnosis and treatment

- Blood and bone marrow culture, Rose Bengal test
- Doxycycline and gentamicin, or doxycycline, rifampin, and ceftriaxone

> Undulating fever with bone and joint involvement after animal exposure suggests brucellosis.

Library: Rocky Mountain spotted fever
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Zoonotic, Tick-Borne, and Intracellular Infections

## Key findings

- Caused by Rickettsia, obligate intracellular bacteria that grow freely in the host cell cytoplasm
- Spread by ticks, mostly in spring and summer
- Delay in diagnosis raises the fatality rate

## Clinical features

- Nonspecific early symptoms that progress to systemic, life-threatening disease
- Rash of blanching macules, papules, petechiae, and purpura that starts at the wrists and ankles and spreads to the trunk
- High white count, high ALT and AST, thrombocytopenia, and CSF pleocytosis

## Diagnosis and treatment

- Indirect or direct fluorescent antibody testing
- Doxycycline

> A rash that starts on the wrists and ankles after a tick bite points to Rocky Mountain spotted fever.

Library: Ehrlichiosis and anaplasmosis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Zoonotic, Tick-Borne, and Intracellular Infections

## Key findings

Both are Anaplasmataceae, obligate intracellular bacteria that replicate inside host cell vacuoles.

| Feature | Ehrlichia chaffeensis | Anaplasma phagocytophilum |
|---|---|---|
| Disease | Human monocytic ehrlichiosis | Human granulocytic anaplasmosis |
| Vasculitis | Rare | Rare |
| Rash | About one-third (maculopapular, petechial) | About 10% |
| Other | Nonspecific symptoms | Fewer GI symptoms; mild anemia, leukopenia, high bands, low platelets |
| Treatment | Doxycycline | Doxycycline |

> Rickettsia grows free in the cytoplasm, while Ehrlichia and Anaplasma grow inside vacuoles.

Library: Q fever (Coxiella burnetii)
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Zoonotic, Tick-Borne, and Intracellular Infections

## Key findings

- Coxiella burnetii is a gram-negative coccobacillus
- Spread by infected aerosols in dust, infected materials, raw milk, and ticks; slaughterhouse (abattoir) workers are at risk

## Clinical features

| Phase | Features |
|---|---|
| Acute | Flu-like illness, pneumonia with rounded densities or halo signs, crackles, dyspnea, relative bradycardia; high CRP, ALT, and AST with a low white count |
| Chronic | Endocarditis, myocarditis, hepatosplenomegaly |
| Rare | Meningitis, meningoencephalitis |

## Diagnosis and treatment

- PET/CT scan
- No heart disease: doxycycline
- Heart disease: doxycycline plus hydroxychloroquine

> An abattoir worker with pneumonia and relative bradycardia suggests Q fever.

Library: Leptospirosis
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Zoonotic, Tick-Borne, and Intracellular Infections

## Key findings

- Leptospira is a highly motile aerobic spirochete
- Spread by rats and contaminated food, soil, and water; enters through breaks in the skin
- Epidemic in Southeast Asia, Latin America, the Caribbean, and sub-Saharan Africa

## Weil's disease

Often biphasic, with a leptospiremic phase followed by an immune phase with IgM antibodies. Features include jaundice, conjunctival suffusion, flu-like symptoms, and pulmonary hemorrhage.

## Diagnosis and treatment

- Darkfield microscopy, culture, microscopic agglutination test
- Mild illness: doxycycline
- Severe illness: penicillin G or ceftriaxone

> Jaundice with conjunctival suffusion after rat or water exposure suggests leptospirosis.

Library: Psittacosis (Chlamydophila psittaci)
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Zoonotic, Tick-Borne, and Intracellular Infections

## Key findings

- Carried by parrots and other birds
- Occupational risk for poultry farm workers, veterinarians, pet store workers, and bird owners

## Clinical features

Fever, chills, headache, dry cough, sore throat, diarrhea, a facial rash (Horder spots), and lobar pneumonia, with many neutrophils on sputum Gram stain.

## Treatment

Doxycycline, or azithromycin for children.

> Pneumonia in a bird owner points to psittacosis.

Library: Beta-lactam antibiotics overview
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Beta-Lactam Antibiotics

## Mechanism

Beta-lactams bind penicillin-binding proteins (PBPs) and inhibit transpeptidation, the cross-linking of peptidoglycan. Without cell wall synthesis, the bacteria lyse.

## Classes

- Penicillins
- Cephalosporins
- Carbapenems
- Monobactams (aztreonam)

## Related points

- Penicillin can trigger warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia
- Beta-lactamase inhibitors (clavulanate, sulbactam, tazobactam, and others) protect a penicillin from bacterial beta-lactamases
- Gonococcal PenA mutations change PBP affinity for beta-lactams

> Beta-lactams bind PBPs and block peptidoglycan cross-linking.

Library: Penicillins
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Beta-Lactam Antibiotics

## Key findings

| Group | Drugs | Coverage |
|---|---|---|
| Natural | Penicillin G, penicillin V | Gram-positives except Staph: S. pyogenes, S. agalactiae, S. pneumoniae, actinomycosis, syphilis |
| Aminopenicillins | Ampicillin, amoxicillin | Also Enterococcus faecalis and Listeria |
| Antistaphylococcal | Nafcillin, oxacillin, dicloxacillin | MSSA; not MRSA or MRSE |
| Advanced | Piperacillin | Gram-positives and gram-negatives except Staph |

## Beta-lactamase inhibitor combinations

| Combination | Notable use |
|---|---|
| Amoxicillin-clavulanate (oral) | Anaerobes |
| Ampicillin-sulbactam (IV) | Acinetobacter |
| Piperacillin-tazobactam | Gram-negatives, including Pseudomonas |

## Key side effect

Ampicillin or amoxicillin given during EBV mononucleosis causes a maculopapular rash.

> Antistaphylococcal penicillins treat MSSA but not MRSA.

Library: Cephalosporins by generation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Beta-Lactam Antibiotics

## Key findings

All cephalosporins add gram-negative coverage compared with early penicillins.

| Generation | Drugs | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| 1st | Cefazolin, cephalexin, cefadroxil | Gram-positives (cefazolin for MSSA); no MRSA, MRSE, Enterococcus, or anaerobes; not Pseudomonas |
| 2nd | Cefoxitin, cefuroxime | Surgical prophylaxis |
| 3rd | Ceftriaxone, ceftazidime, cefpodoxime, cefdinir | Ceftazidime covers Pseudomonas; ceftriaxone covers invasive Salmonella in severe pediatric malaria |
| 4th | Cefepime | Covers Pseudomonas; a zwitterion |
| 5th | Ceftaroline | Anti-MRSA activity |
| 6th | Cefiderocol | Iron chelation |

## Side effects

Hypersensitivity and disulfiram-like reactions are listed with the first generation.

> Ceftaroline is the cephalosporin with MRSA activity.

Library: Carbapenems and aztreonam
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Beta-Lactam Antibiotics

## Carbapenems

- Ertapenem, imipenem, and meropenem
- Broad spectrum, used for highly resistant or life-threatening infections
- Imipenem and meropenem cover Acinetobacter, Pseudomonas, and E. faecalis
- Ertapenem lacks that coverage (no Acinetobacter, Pseudomonas, or Enterococcus)
- Side effects: seizures and GI upset

## Aztreonam (monobactam)

- The beta-lactam for patients with a beta-lactam or penicillin allergy
- An antipseudomonal option for penicillin-allergic patients
- Active against gram-negative rods and resists beta-lactamases
- No activity against gram-positives or anaerobes

> Ertapenem is the carbapenem that doesn't cover Pseudomonas, Acinetobacter, or Enterococcus.

Library: Drug choices for MRSA and Pseudomonas
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Beta-Lactam Antibiotics

## MRSA, in the course's order

1. Vancomycin
2. Ceftaroline
3. Daptomycin

Linezolid, doxycycline, delafloxacin, and TMP-SMX also have MRSA activity.

## Pseudomonas

- Aminoglycosides: gentamicin, tobramycin, amikacin
- Piperacillin and piperacillin-tazobactam
- Ceftazidime and cefepime
- Imipenem and meropenem (not ertapenem)
- Ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin, and delafloxacin (not moxifloxacin)
- Aztreonam for penicillin-allergic patients

> For MRSA, the course's order is vancomycin, then ceftaroline, then daptomycin.

Library: Vancomycin
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Other Cell Wall and Membrane Antibiotics

## Mechanism

Vancomycin inhibits peptidoglycan synthesis by binding the D-Ala-D-Ala site and preventing polymerization.

## Uses

- MRSA
- Gram-positive coverage only

## Side effects

- Vancomycin infusion syndrome ("red man syndrome"), flushing related to the infusion rate
- Nephrotoxicity
- Ototoxicity

## Monitoring

Vancomycin has a narrow therapeutic index, so levels are monitored.

> Vancomycin binds D-Ala-D-Ala and is the first-line drug for MRSA.

Library: Daptomycin
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Other Cell Wall and Membrane Antibiotics

## Mechanism

Daptomycin forms cation channels in the membrane that release potassium and cause rapid depolarization.

## Uses

- Gram-positives only, including Staph aureus and MRSA
- Skin infections
- Can't be used for pneumonia because lung surfactant inactivates it

## Side effects

Myopathy and rhabdomyolysis.

> Daptomycin is inactivated by surfactant, so it can't treat pneumonia.

Library: Fosfomycin and polymyxins
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Other Cell Wall and Membrane Antibiotics

## Key findings

| Drug | Target | Key point |
|---|---|---|
| Fosfomycin | Cell wall | The oral form is only effective for urinary tract infections |
| Polymyxins | Cell membrane | Detergent-like mechanism |

> Oral fosfomycin is only useful for UTIs.

Library: Aminoglycosides
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Protein Synthesis Inhibitors

## Mechanism

Aminoglycosides bind the 30S ribosomal subunit and interfere with the growing peptide chain, causing mRNA misreads, nonsense proteins, and a defective cell membrane.

## Drugs and coverage

| Drug | Coverage |
|---|---|
| Gentamicin | Enterobacteriaceae, Pseudomonas |
| Tobramycin | Enterobacterales, Pseudomonas |
| Amikacin | Enterobacterales, Pseudomonas, nontuberculous mycobacteria |
| Plazomicin, neomycin, streptomycin | Other members of the class |

All aminoglycosides are used for urinary tract infections.

## Side effects

Nephrotoxicity, ototoxicity, vertigo, and neuromuscular blockade. Avoid in pregnancy.

> Aminoglycosides are 30S inhibitors that cause nephrotoxicity and ototoxicity.

Library: Tetracyclines
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Protein Synthesis Inhibitors

## Mechanism

Tetracyclines prevent tRNA from binding the 30S ribosome during elongation.

## Doxycycline uses

- Lyme disease
- Mycoplasma, Chlamydia, and other atypical organisms
- Staph aureus, including MRSA
- Rickettsial infections, brucellosis, Q fever, leptospirosis, and psittacosis
- Syphilis and protozoa

## Side effects and cautions

- Photosensitivity
- Tooth discoloration, so not used in children
- Contraindicated in pregnancy
- GI distress and pill esophagitis
- Chelates metals, so it shouldn't be taken with milk

> Doxycycline chelates calcium, stains teeth, and causes photosensitivity.

Library: Macrolides
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Protein Synthesis Inhibitors

## Mechanism

Macrolides (azithromycin, clarithromycin, erythromycin) bind the 50S subunit and inhibit transpeptidation and translocation during elongation.

## Uses

- Gram-positive cocci
- Atypical organisms such as Mycoplasma pneumoniae and Chlamydia
- Nontuberculous mycobacteria and MAC (treatment and prophylaxis)
- Some STIs
- Mild babesiosis, with atovaquone
- Erythromycin for pertussis and diphtheria

## Side effects

Prolonged QT interval and GI upset.

> Macrolides are 50S inhibitors that prolong the QT interval.

Library: Clindamycin and linezolid
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / Protein Synthesis Inhibitors

## Key findings

Both bind the 50S ribosomal subunit.

| Feature | Clindamycin | Linezolid |
|---|---|---|
| Class | Lincosamide | Oxazolidinone |
| Mechanism | Blocks peptide bond formation | Blocks translation |
| Coverage | Staph, Strep, anaerobes, toxoplasmosis, pneumocystosis; severe babesiosis with quinine | Resistant gram-positives (Staph, Strep, Enterococcus), mycobacteria, Nocardia |
| Key toxicity | Diarrhea and C. difficile colitis (about 10%) | Marrow suppression (thrombocytopenia by 2 weeks), peripheral and optic neuropathy (by 4 weeks), lactic acidosis, serotonin syndrome, rhabdomyolysis |

Data on clindamycin's efficacy in necrotizing fasciitis are limited.

> Clindamycin's classic complication is C. difficile colitis, and linezolid's is marrow suppression.

Library: Fluoroquinolones
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / DNA and RNA Synthesis Inhibitors

## Mechanism

Fluoroquinolones inhibit DNA gyrase (topoisomerase II).

## Coverage by drug

| Drug | Coverage | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Ciprofloxacin | Enterobacterales, Pseudomonas; GI infections, B. anthracis, S. typhi | Poor gram-positive coverage |
| Levofloxacin | Enterobacterales, Pseudomonas, Strep | Long half-life |
| Moxifloxacin | Enterobacterales, Strep, anaerobes | No Pseudomonas; no urinary penetration |
| Delafloxacin | Enterobacterales, Pseudomonas, gram-positives including MRSA, anaerobes | |

## Side effects

Tendonitis and tendon rupture, prolonged QT, C. difficile infection, confusion, and GI upset.

> Fluoroquinolones inhibit DNA gyrase and can rupture tendons.

Library: Metronidazole
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / DNA and RNA Synthesis Inhibitors

## Mechanism

Metronidazole, a nitroimidazole, diffuses into the organism and is reduced in its cytoplasm. Electrons transfer to the nitro group, which breaks DNA strands.

## Uses

- Anaerobes and intra-abdominal infections
- Bacterial vaginosis
- Protozoal infections
- C. difficile

## Side effects

- Disulfiram-like reaction, so it can't be given to patients taking disulfiram
- Peripheral neuropathy
- Gynecomastia

> Metronidazole covers anaerobes and causes a disulfiram-like reaction.

Library: Rifamycins
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / DNA and RNA Synthesis Inhibitors

## Mechanism

Rifamycins inhibit bacterial DNA-dependent RNA polymerase, stopping transcription.

## Drugs

| Drug | Uses | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Rifampin | TB, leprosy; prophylaxis for N. meningitidis and H. influenzae contacts | Never used alone; penetrates biofilms |
| Rifabutin | Nontuberculous mycobacteria | Somewhat better drug interaction profile |
| Rifaximin | GI use only; hepatic encephalopathy | Poorly absorbed |
| Rifapentine | Listed without detail | |

## Class side effects

Red-orange body fluids, hepatotoxicity, and flu-like symptoms. Rifamycins induce CYP enzymes, which causes drug interactions.

> Rifampin induces CYP450, turns body fluids red, and is never used alone.

Library: TMP-SMX (trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole)
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II / DNA and RNA Synthesis Inhibitors

## Mechanism

TMP-SMX blocks folate synthesis at two steps, dihydropteroate synthase (DHPS) and dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR), which stops thymidine synthesis and DNA replication.

## Uses

- Simple urinary tract infections
- MRSA and other gram-positives, plus gram-negatives
- Pneumocystis treatment, and prophylaxis when the CD4 count is under 200
- Nocardia and Stenotrophomonas
- Prophylaxis in chronic granulomatous disease, with itraconazole

## Side effects

- Rash progressing to Stevens-Johnson syndrome
- Bone marrow suppression
- Nephrotoxicity
- Hemolysis in G6PD deficiency
- Hyperkalemia and hyponatremia

> TMP-SMX blocks DHPS and DHFR and is the drug for Pneumocystis and Nocardia.

Library: Bacteria grouped by clinical clue
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II

## Key findings

| Clue | Organisms |
|---|---|
| Asplenia or sickle cell disease | S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae type b, N. meningitidis, Salmonella (osteomyelitis), Klebsiella, Capnocytophaga (dog bites), B. pertussis, group B strep |
| Catalase positive (CGD risk) | S. aureus, Nocardia, Burkholderia cepacia, Listeria, Serratia, E. coli |
| Meningitis (course list, in order) | Group B strep, Listeria, E. coli |
| Pregnancy | Listeria, Treponema pallidum, group B strep |
| Foodborne | S. aureus (mayonnaise, meats), Listeria (deli meats, unpasteurized milk), B. cereus (reheated rice), C. perfringens, EHEC (undercooked meat) |
| Found in soil | Nocardia, B. cereus, C. tetani |
| Spore forming | B. anthracis, B. cereus, C. difficile |
| Obligate aerobes | M. tuberculosis, Nocardia, Pseudomonas |
| Osteomyelitis | S. aureus, Pseudomonas, Salmonella typhi |
| Diabetics, burns, IV drug use | Pseudomonas (and S. aureus in IV drug use) |
| Urease positive | S. saprophyticus, S. epidermidis, H. pylori, Nocardia, Klebsiella |
| ADP-ribosylation | Diphtheria toxin, Pseudomonas exotoxin A |
| Raise cAMP | B. anthracis, B. pertussis |

> Asplenic patients are most threatened by encapsulated organisms such as S. pneumoniae.

Library: Bacterial culture media and lab tests
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 9: Foundations of Microbiology II

## Key findings

| Test or medium | What it shows |
|---|---|
| MacConkey agar | Selects gram-negatives (bile salts and crystal violet block gram-positives); lactose fermenters drop the pH below 6.8 and turn red |
| Chocolate agar | Grows N. meningitidis (Thayer-Martin), N. gonorrhoeae, Moraxella, and H. influenzae (needs factors X and V) |
| Loeffler's medium | C. diphtheriae with metachromatic granules |
| Regan-Lowe medium | B. pertussis |
| Anaerobic cultures | Thioglycolate broth, anaerobic jar with blood agar, cooked chopped meat broth |
| Susceptibility testing | Broth dilution, E-test, agar diffusion, mass spectrometry |

## Hemolysis and identification tests

- CAMP test: group B strep
- Optochin sensitivity: S. pneumoniae
- Growth in 6.5% NaCl: Enterococcus
- Coagulase: S. aureus

> On MacConkey agar, lactose fermenters turn red.

Library: Sperm structure and capacitation
Folder: Foundations of Medicine / Week 10: Foundations of Early Development / Fertilization and Implantation

## Sperm structure

| Part | Function |
|---|---|
| Acrosome | Sac of digestive enzymes needed to get through the zona pellucida |
| Nucleus | Tightly condensed, inactive chromatin, which allows efficient movement |
| Mitochondrial sheath | Sits in the mid-piece and powers the flagellum |
| Flagellum | Runs through the mid-piece and tail |

## Capacitation

Sperm become more capable of motility and fertilization after they enter the vagina.

1. Sperm start out with cholesterol-rich plasma membranes, which limit motility and the ability to activate the ZP3 receptor
2. Cervical and uterine secretions remove the cholesterol
3. Calcium flows into the sperm cytoplasm and hyperactivates motility

> Capacitation removes membrane cholesterol so calcium can hyperactivate sperm motility.
