| Insertion | Distal attachment | Movable point |

> Muscle tissue does primarily one thing: it produces tension, a force.

Library: Muscle tissue characteristics
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System

## Key features

| Property | Definition |
|---|---|
| Irritability | Ability to respond to stimulation |
| Contractility | Ability to shorten when it receives sufficient stimulation |
| Extensibility | Ability to lengthen or stretch beyond the resting length |
| Elasticity | Ability of the muscle fiber to return to its resting length once the stretch is removed |

## Irritability

- In muscle, the stimulation comes from a motor neuron releasing a neurotransmitter
- Skeletal muscle is one of the most sensitive and responsive tissues in the body
- As an excitable tissue, it can be recruited quickly with significant control for producing movement

## Contractility

- Some muscles can shorten 50 to 70% of their resting length, with an average of 57%
- How far a muscle can shorten is limited by the physical confinement of the body
- The sartorius (anterior thigh) could shorten more than half its length only if detached from the body; in vivo, the hip and knee joints and the length of the femur limit it

## Extensibility

- The muscle can't produce its own elongation; another muscle (the antagonist) or an external force has to do it, as in stretching
- The connective tissue surrounding and within the muscle determines how extensible it is

## Elasticity

- Determined by the connective tissue in the muscle rather than the fibrils themselves
- Elasticity increases muscular power when a concentric action is preceded by a stretch
- Extensibility and elasticity together act as a protective mechanism that maintains the muscle's integrity

> A muscle can't lengthen itself; extensibility depends on an antagonist muscle or an external force.

Library: Functional compartments of the limbs
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System

![Cross section of the right leg (anterosuperior view) labeled skin, subcutaneous tissue (superficial fascia), deep fascia (outer circumferential layer), intermuscular septa, investing fascia of muscle, deep fascia blended with periosteum of bone, tibia, fibula, interosseous membrane, neurovascular sheath, superficial vein, cutaneous nerve, dorsiflexor muscles (anterior compartment), evertor muscles (lateral compartment), plantar flexor muscles (posterior compartment), and long flexor muscles of foot and ankle](library/images/msk-week-13/intro-leg-compartments-cross-section.jpg)

## Identify it

- Deep fascia forms an outer circumferential layer under the subcutaneous tissue
- Intermuscular septa run inward from it and divide the muscles into compartments
- Investing fascia surrounds individual muscles
- In the leg, the dorsiflexor muscles fill the anterior compartment, the evertor muscles the lateral compartment, and the plantar flexor muscles the posterior compartment, with the long flexor muscles of the foot and ankle deep to them
- Where deep fascia meets the tibia, it blends with the periosteum

## Key features

- A fascial compartment is a section of the body containing muscles and nerves surrounded by deep fascia
- Compartments are most often seen in the limbs, where the muscles in a compartment have similar functions
- Muscles in a compartment are usually innervated by the same nerve
- Thick sheets of deep fascia called intermuscular septa separate the compartments
- In living people, fascia creates planes that are potential spaces between adjacent structures, and these planes matter in surgical procedures and in the spreading or containment of infections

> Muscles in one compartment usually share a similar function and the same nerve supply.

Library: Isometric, concentric, and eccentric contractions
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System

![Two figures of muscle contraction types: a top-down view of the shoulder showing isometric, concentric, and eccentric activation against an external force, and an arm holding a dumbbell still (isometric, no movement), lifting it (concentric, movement toward the shoulder), and lowering it (eccentric, movement away)](library/images/msk-week-13/intro-contraction-types.jpg)

## Key features

| Type | What the muscle does | Torque balance at the joint |
|---|---|---|
| Isometric | Activated, but no movement | Internal torque equals external torque |
| Concentric | Activated and shortens | Internal torque exceeds the opposing external torque |
| Eccentric | Activated, producing force while being elongated | External torque exceeds internal torque |

## Word roots

- Isometric: Greek isos (equal) and metron (measure or length)
- Concentric: coming to the center
- Eccentric: away from the center

## Identify it

- Isometric: the arm holds the dumbbell in place with no movement
- Concentric: the forearm moves toward the shoulder as the muscle shortens
- Eccentric: the forearm moves away as the active muscle lengthens

The notes show the same idea at the shoulder against gravity. The deltoid holding the arm in an abducted position with no movement is isometric. Raising the arm in abduction, with muscle force exceeding gravity, is concentric. Lowering the arm in adduction as the deltoid lengthens and gravity outweighs the muscle's force is eccentric.

> In an eccentric contraction the muscle is active and producing force while it lengthens, because external torque exceeds internal torque.

Library: A muscle's action at a joint
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System

![Three shoulder diagrams with a red arrow showing a muscle's line of pull and a black line from the joint axis: frontal plane, posterior view, producing adduction; horizontal plane, superior view, producing external rotation; sagittal plane, lateral view, producing extension](library/images/msk-week-13/intro-muscle-action-shoulder-planes.jpg)

## Key features

- A muscle's action is its potential to cause a torque about an axis in a particular plane
- Actions are defined by movement in reference to the anatomical position
- Actions are defined by a concentric muscle action
- To find the action, determine where the muscle's line of pull lies with respect to the joint's axis of rotation
- A muscle may have other actions as the joint angle or position changes

## Identify it

- Frontal plane (posterior view): the line of pull produces adduction
- Horizontal plane (superior view): the line of pull produces external rotation
- Sagittal plane (lateral view): the line of pull produces extension
- In each panel, the short black line runs from the joint's axis to the line of pull, which is the moment arm (the distance of the line of action from the joint axis)

The three panels show one joint, the shoulder, moving in three different planes, so the plane has to be named along with the movement. Because the line of pull and its distance from the axis shift as the joint moves, a muscle's action can change with joint angle or position.

> A muscle's action is the movement it causes from anatomical position during a concentric action, set by its line of pull relative to the joint's axis of rotation.

Library: Muscle roles: agonists, antagonists, and synergists
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System

![Arm muscles during elbow flexion, with two muscles labeled agonists and one labeled antagonist, and during elbow extension, with the labels reversed; a second panel shows the trapezius and serratus anterior with arrows along their lines of pull](library/images/msk-week-13/intro-agonist-antagonist-elbow.jpg)

## Key features

| Role | Definition | Lecture example |
|---|---|---|
| Agonist (prime mover) | Muscle or muscle group that executes a particular movement | Quadriceps femoris in knee extension |
| Antagonist | Muscle or muscle group that creates movement opposite of the agonists | Hamstrings in knee extension |
| Synergist | Muscles acting cooperatively to produce a particular movement | Act as neutralizers or stabilizers |

## Synergists

- Neutralizers prevent an undesired action of one of the movers. In scapular retraction, the trapezius retracts and upward rotates the scapula while the rhomboids retract and downward rotate it, so the rotational parts cancel out.
- Stabilizers steady or support a body part against another muscle's action. The gluteus medius prevents pelvic tilting during walking, running, or balancing on one leg.

## Identify it

- Elbow flexion: the two muscles labeled agonists shorten to bend the elbow while the muscle on the other side is the antagonist
- Elbow extension: the same muscles swap labels, so the former antagonist is now the agonist
- The trapezius and serratus anterior panel shows arrows along each muscle's line of pull; the slide doesn't label a role for it

> Roles depend on the movement: the agonists for elbow flexion become the antagonists for elbow extension.

Library: Identifying muscle roles from movement
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System

![Two drawings of an arm holding a 10 kg weight: on the left the forearm moves up and the biceps brachii is labeled contracting muscle (concentric) with a relaxed muscle on the other side; on the right the forearm moves down and the biceps contraction type and the relaxed muscle are left as question marks](library/images/msk-week-13/intro-muscle-role-biceps-weight.jpg)

## Key features

To decide what a muscle is doing during an observed movement, the lecture says to ask what the motive force is. In other words, figure out whether the muscle is creating the movement or controlling a movement that something else is causing.

- Left figure: the forearm moves up as the weight is lifted, the biceps brachii is the contracting muscle and works concentrically, and the muscle on the opposite side is relaxed
- Right figure: the forearm moves down with the same weight, and the slide leaves the biceps' contraction type and the role of the relaxed muscle as questions

## Working it out

The biceps is still labeled the contracting muscle in the right figure, but the forearm is moving away from the shoulder, so the biceps is being elongated while active. By the lecture's definitions, that's an eccentric action, where the external torque from the weight exceeds the muscle's internal torque. The motive force for the downward movement is the load, and the biceps controls it.

| Observed movement | Motive force | Biceps action |
|---|---|---|
| Weight lifted | Biceps | Concentric |
| Weight lowered | Weight (external torque) | Eccentric |

> To assign a muscle's role, first ask what the motive force is.

Library: Muscle strength: force and moment arm
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System

![Line drawings of the elbow at five joint angles showing the muscle's line of pull and its moment arm changing through the range of motion, and a drawing of two forearm muscles with different attachment points on the forearm](library/images/msk-week-13/intro-moment-arm-attachment.jpg)

## Key features

From a biomechanical perspective, strength is the product of the force (tension) a muscle produces and the moment it creates about the joint, so strength (torque) equals muscle force times moment arm.

Factors that influence muscle force (covered in later lectures on muscle structure, function, and physiology):

- Muscle size and architecture
- Muscle fiber types
- Motor unit recruitment and rate coding
- Length-tension relationship
- Force-velocity relationship

Factors that influence torque are muscle force and the moment arm, which depends on the angle and the point of muscle attachment.

## Angle of muscle attachment

- The closer the insertion angle is to 90 degrees, the larger the moment arm (the distance of the muscle's line of action from the joint axis)
- Most muscles have insertion angles under 45 degrees, so they aren't very efficient at producing torque
- Insertion angles change through the range of motion (ROM), so a muscle's torque output (strength) changes through the ROM
- We generally can't alter insertion angles; there's some evidence they may change with hypertrophy, but how much and with what effect isn't known
- Differences in strength between people may be partly due to differences in insertion angles

## Point of muscle attachment

- In theory, the farther from the joint a muscle attaches, the larger the moment arm can potentially be
- Because body segments are long, a farther attachment forces a smaller insertion angle, so this principle doesn't usually hold for muscle
- Attachment sites can't be altered, and differences in them may partly explain strength differences between people

> Strength (torque) = muscle force x moment arm, and the moment arm is largest when the insertion angle is near 90 degrees.

Library: Musculoskeletal lever classes
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Introduction to the Musculoskeletal System

![Three lever classes with body examples: class 1 with the fulcrum between effort and load, shown as the neck extensor muscles balancing the center of gravity of the head; class 2 with the load between fulcrum and effort, shown as the triceps surae (calf) muscles lifting body weight with the fulcrum under the front of the foot; class 3 with the effort between fulcrum and load, shown as the biceps brachii lifting the center of gravity of the forearm with the fulcrum at the elbow](library/images/msk-week-13/intro-lever-classes.jpg)

## Key features

Internal and external forces produce torques through a system of levers. Freely movable (synovial) joints form the fulcrums about which the rigid bones articulate, outside forces such as weights act as loads on the bones, and skeletal muscles produce tension as efforts that pull on the bones. With fulcrum, load, and effort, there are three possible arrangements, called lever classes.

| Class | Arrangement | Body example | Mechanical advantage |
|---|---|---|---|
| Class 1 | Fulcrum between effort and load | Neck extensors and head | Possible if the load is closer to the fulcrum |
| Class 2 | Load between fulcrum and effort | Calf muscles lifting body weight | Always gained |
| Class 3 | Effort between fulcrum and load | Biceps brachii lifting the forearm | Cannot be gained |

## Properties

- Class 1: the load can move a smaller or greater distance, and slower or faster, than the effort depending on their distances from the fulcrum; the effort force can be less than the load if the effort is farther from the fulcrum
- Class 2: the load moves a smaller distance and more slowly than the effort; the effort force is less than the load, so a mechanical advantage is gained
- Class 3: the load moves a greater distance and faster than the effort; the effort force can't be less than the load, so no mechanical advantage is gained
- Most joint articulations in the body are class 3 levers

> Most joints are class 3 levers: the load moves farther and faster than the effort, but no mechanical advantage is gained.

Library: CNS and PNS divisions
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Flowchart of the nervous system: central nervous system (brain, spinal cord) and peripheral nervous system (cranial nerves, spinal nerves, ganglia); the PNS splits into sensory (afferent) and motor (efferent) divisions, each somatic or visceral, and visceral motor (autonomic nervous system) splits into enteric, sympathetic, and parasympathetic divisions; inset body figure labels brain, spinal cord, cranial nerves, and spinal nerves](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-nervous-system-divisions-cns-pns.jpg)

## Key features

| Division | Components | Role |
|---|---|---|
| Central nervous system (CNS) | Brain, spinal cord | Integrates and coordinates incoming and outgoing signals; higher mental functions |
| Peripheral nervous system (PNS) | Cranial nerves, spinal nerves, ganglia | Nerve fibers and cell bodies outside the CNS |

- 12 pairs of cranial nerves exit the cranial cavity
- 31 pairs of spinal nerves exit the vertebral column through the intervertebral (IV) foramina
- A ganglion is a collection of nerve cell bodies outside the CNS, and ganglia can be sensory or autonomic

## Functional divisions of the PNS

- Sensory (afferent) division, with somatic and visceral parts
- Motor (efferent) division, with somatic and visceral parts
- Visceral motor is the autonomic nervous system (ANS), made of the enteric nervous system, the sympathetic division, and the parasympathetic division

## Terms

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Afferent | Towards the CNS |
| Efferent | Away from the CNS |
| Somatic | Body wall, limbs |
| Visceral | Organs/viscera |

> Afferent means toward the CNS (sensory) and efferent means away from the CNS (motor).

Library: Neuron structure and structural types
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Diagram of two connected neurons labeling dendrites, cell body (soma), axon, myelin sheath, axon terminals, and a boxed synapse, with arrows showing the direction of impulse flow](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-neuron-parts-diagram.jpg)

## Key features

The neuron is the structural and functional unit of the nervous system, specialized for rapid communication.

- **Cell body (soma)** holds the nucleus
- **Dendrites** carry impulses toward the cell body and pick up stimuli from other neurons
- **Axon** conducts impulses away from the cell body to other neurons and ends in axon terminals
- **Myelin sheath** surrounds some axons and increases conduction velocity
- **Synapses** are points of contact between neurons, where communication happens by release of neurotransmitters

In the figure, arrows show impulses entering through the dendrites, passing through the cell body, and running down the axon to the axon terminals, which synapse on the next neuron.

## Structural classification

![Unlabeled drawings of three neuron shapes in the slide's bullet order, top to bottom: multipolar (many dendrites and one myelinated axon), bipolar (one dendrite and one axon on opposite sides of the cell body), and pseudounipolar (round cell body on a short stalk joined to one long process)](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-neuron-structural-types.jpg)

| Type | Processes | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Multipolar | Several dendrites and a single axon | Motor neurons and interneurons; most common type (~99%) |
| Bipolar | A single dendrite and a single axon arise from the cell body | Special sensory neurons (retina, olfactory epithelium) |
| Pseudounipolar | Cell body attached to a central and a peripheral process by a single short axon | Sensory neurons |

> About 99% of neurons are multipolar (motor neurons and interneurons), while sensory neurons are pseudounipolar.

Library: Functional neuron types: sensory, interneuron, motor
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Diagram of a pin stimulus at a skin receptor, a sensory neuron with its cell body in the dorsal root ganglion, a relay neuron in the spinal cord gray matter, and a motor neuron leaving through the ventral root to an effector muscle](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-sensory-relay-motor-neurons.jpg)

## Key features

Neurons are also grouped by the direction they carry signals.

| Type | Direction | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Sensory (afferent) | Periphery to CNS | Touch, temperature, proprioception, vibration, pressure, pain |
| Interneuron | Restricted to the CNS | Sits between sensory and motor neurons for integration; excitatory or inhibitory |
| Motor (efferent) | CNS to target/effector organ | Targets are muscle tissue and glands |

This lines up with the structural types: sensory neurons are pseudounipolar, and motor neurons and interneurons are multipolar.

## Identify it

- The sensory neuron's cell body sits in the dorsal root ganglion, outside the cord
- The neuron between the sensory and motor neurons (labeled relay neuron on the slide) is in the gray matter of the cord, which fits interneurons being restricted to the CNS
- The motor neuron's cell body is in the cord, and its axon leaves through the ventral root to reach the muscle

> Sensory neurons carry signals from the periphery to the CNS, interneurons stay within the CNS, and motor neurons carry signals from the CNS to muscles and glands.

Library: Glial cell types by division (CNS vs PNS)
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Drawings of glial cells: (a) astrocyte with processes touching a capillary and a neuron, (b) microglial cell among neurons, (c) ependymal cells lining a fluid-filled cavity over brain or spinal cord tissue, (d) oligodendrocyte whose processes form myelin sheaths around several nerve fibers, (e) sensory neuron with Schwann cells forming the myelin sheath along the nerve fiber and satellite cells around the cell body](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-glial-cells-cns-pns.jpg)

## Key features

| Division | Glial cells |
|---|---|
| CNS | Astrocytes, microglia, ependymal cells, oligodendrocytes |
| PNS | Satellite cells, Schwann cells |

## Identify it

- **Astrocyte**: star-shaped cell with many processes reaching a capillary and a neuron
- **Microglial cell**: small cell with fine branching processes lying among neurons
- **Ependymal cells**: a sheet of cells lining a fluid-filled cavity of the brain or spinal cord
- **Oligodendrocyte**: one cell body with processes wrapping myelin around several nerve fibers
- **Schwann cells**: a chain of cells forming the myelin sheath along a peripheral nerve fiber
- **Satellite cells**: small cells surrounding the cell body of a sensory neuron

The slide lists four CNS glia and two PNS glia. Both PNS types show up on the same sensory neuron in panel (e), with satellite cells around the cell body and Schwann cells along the fiber.

> In the figure, oligodendrocytes (CNS) wrap myelin around several nerve fibers, while Schwann cells (PNS) form the myelin sheath along a peripheral nerve fiber.

Library: Gray and white matter in brain and cord sections
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Neuron drawing split by a dashed line into its cell body (gray matter: neuronal cell bodies and interneurons) and its myelinated axon (white matter: myelinated axons), above gross photos of a coronal brain section, an axial brain section, and an axial cross-section of the spinal cord in the vertebral canal](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-gray-white-matter-brain-cord-sections.jpg)

## Key features

The CNS is built from two kinds of tissue that look different in cut sections.

| Feature | Gray matter | White matter |
|---|---|---|
| Contents | Neuronal cell bodies and interneurons | Myelinated axons (nerve fibers) |
| Color | Gray | White because of the myelin |
| Position in spinal cord | Central, "H-shaped" on transverse section | Peripheral |
| Function in cord | Cell bodies of motor neurons and neurons receiving sensory input | Fibers traveling to and from the brain |

## Identify it

- In the coronal and axial brain photos, darker gray matter and paler white matter can be told apart by color
- In the axial spinal cord photo, a darker butterfly ("H") of gray matter sits in the middle and is surrounded by pale white matter

The same split runs through the terminology. Groups of cell bodies in gray matter are called nuclei or columns, and bundles of axons in white matter are tracts, fasciculi, and funiculi.

> In the spinal cord, gray matter is central and H-shaped, and white matter (myelinated axons) surrounds it.

Library: Neuroanatomy terms: nucleus, column, tract, ganglion, nerve
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

## Key features

The same kind of structure gets a different name depending on whether it is in the CNS or the PNS.

| Term | Location | Description | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nucleus (nuclei) | CNS | A group of functionally related neuronal cell bodies | Caudate nucleus (basal nuclei/ganglia) |
| Column | CNS (spinal cord) | Functionally related neuronal cell bodies forming a longitudinal column | Intermediolateral cell column |
| Tract, fasciculus, lemniscus | CNS | A bundle of parallel axons | Corticospinal tract, fasciculus gracilis, medial lemniscus |
| Funiculus (funiculi) | CNS | A group of several parallel tracts or fasciculi | Anterior, posterior, and lateral funiculi of the spinal cord |
| Ganglion (ganglia) | PNS | A group of neuronal cell bodies in a peripheral nerve or root | Dorsal root ganglion |
| Nerve, ramus, root | PNS | A bundle of parallel axons | Sciatic nerve, ventral root of a spinal nerve, gray and white rami |

## Pairs to keep straight

- Cell bodies: nucleus or column in the CNS, ganglion in the PNS
- Axon bundles: tract, fasciculus, or lemniscus in the CNS, nerve, ramus, or root in the PNS
- A funiculus is bigger than a tract because it groups several tracts together

> A group of cell bodies is a nucleus in the CNS but a ganglion in the PNS, and a bundle of axons is a tract in the CNS but a nerve in the PNS.

Library: Spinal cord extent and position in the vertebral canal
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Posterior view of the trunk with the spinal cord and spinal nerves exposed, with a blue arrow at the top of the cord near the foramen magnum and a purple arrow at the lower end of the cord near the L1/L2 level, below which the nerve roots continue down the vertebral canal](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-cord-extent-posterior-view.jpg)

## Key features

- Part of the CNS, housed and protected within the vertebral canal
- Continuous with the brainstem at the foramen magnum
- Ends at the L1/L2 vertebral level in adults
- In adults the cord is shorter than the vertebral column and occupies only the superior 2/3 of the spinal canal
- A major reflex center and the conduction pathway between the body and the brain, with ascending tracts carrying sensory signals to the brain and descending tracts carrying motor signals to the periphery

![Axial section through a vertebra with a boxed close-up of the spinal cord inside the vertebral canal, surrounded by its coverings and epidural fat, oriented anterior (top) and posterior (bottom)](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-cord-in-vertebral-canal-axial.jpg)

## Spinal cord trauma

Damage to the cord can come from direct injury to the cord itself or indirectly from damage to surrounding bones, tissues, or blood vessels (bleeding, swelling, inflammation, or fluid accumulation).

| Injury | Result below the level of injury |
|---|---|
| Complete | Neural messages can't be relayed, causing paralysis |
| Incomplete | Some movement and sensation may still be possible |

Extensive physical therapy, occupational therapy, and other rehabilitation are often needed after the injury heals.

> The adult spinal cord runs from the foramen magnum (continuous with the brainstem) to the L1/L2 vertebral level.

Library: Cervical and lumbosacral enlargements
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Posterior view of a dissected spinal cord in a cadaver, with brackets marking the cervical enlargement (C5-T1 spinal cord segments, innervation for upper limbs) and the lumbosacral enlargement (L1-S3 spinal cord segments, innervation for lower limbs); laminae and spinous processes removed](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-cervical-lumbosacral-enlargements-cadaver.jpg)

## Identify it

- The cord's diameter isn't uniform along its length
- Two widened stretches: one in the lower neck region and one just above the tapering lower end of the cord
- Below the lumbosacral enlargement the cord tapers into the conus medullaris

## Key features

| Enlargement | Segments (slides) | Segments (handout) | Supplies |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cervical | C5-T1 | C4-T1 | Upper limbs (brachial plexus) |
| Lumbosacral | L1-S3 | T11-S1 | Lower limbs (lumbar and sacral plexuses) |

Both enlargements are widened stretches of the cord itself, one in the cervical cord and one in the lower cord just above the conus medullaris. The spinal nerves that arise there feed the plexuses for the limbs.

The slides and the lecture handout give different segment ranges for both enlargements, so check which one your course expects. Both sources agree on what each enlargement supplies.

> The cervical enlargement gives rise to the nerves of the upper limbs (brachial plexus), and the lumbosacral enlargement gives rise to the nerves of the lower limbs (lumbar and sacral plexuses).

Library: Conus medullaris, filum terminale, and cauda equina
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Two posterior-view cadaver photos of the inferior end of the spinal cord: left, forceps spread nerve roots to show the conus medullaris and the thin filum terminale internum running down the middle; right, the bundle of nerve roots in the lower vertebral canal labeled cauda equina](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-conus-medullaris-filum-terminale-cauda-equina.jpg)

## Identify it

- **Conus medullaris**: the tapering inferior end of the cord, just inferior to the lumbosacral enlargement
- **Filum terminale internum**: a single thin strand running down from the tip of the conus in the midline, surrounded by nerve roots
- **Cauda equina**: the loose bundle of lumbar and sacral nerve roots below the end of the cord ("horse tail")

## Key features

- The filum terminale is a slender anchoring filament of connective tissue from the tip of the conus, a vestigial remnant of the caudal part of the spinal cord
- Lower lumbar and sacral roots must keep descending past the end of the cord (L1/L2) to reach their IV foramina, which forms the cauda equina

| Part | Covering | Anchoring |
|---|---|---|
| Filum terminale internum | Continuation of pia mater; surrounded by cauda equina roots | Superior part |
| Filum terminale externum | Surrounded by dura mater | Attaches to the coccyx |

## Cauda equina syndrome

Compression or damage to the cauda equina. It's rare but serious and a surgical emergency, and severity depends on how much compression there is and which roots are involved. Red flag symptoms:

- Urinary retention
- Urinary and/or fecal incontinence
- Saddle anesthesia (sensory disturbance of the anus, genitals, and gluteal region)
- Weakness or paralysis, usually of more than one nerve root
- Pain in the back and/or legs
- Sexual dysfunction

> Cauda equina syndrome is a surgical emergency; red flags include urinary retention, incontinence, and saddle anesthesia.

Library: Longitudinal grooves of the spinal cord
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Three-dimensional drawing of a spinal cord segment with rootlets, labeling the posterior median sulcus, posterior intermediate sulcus, posterolateral sulcus, central canal, anterior median fissure, and anterolateral sulcus, with a dorsal-ventral orientation arrow](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-cord-longitudinal-grooves.jpg)

## Identify it

| Groove | Surface | Clue |
|---|---|---|
| Anterior median fissure | Ventral midline | Deep cleft splitting the front of the cord |
| Anterolateral sulcus | Ventral, off midline | Line where the ventral rootlets attach in the figure |
| Posterior median sulcus | Dorsal midline | Midline groove on the back of the cord |
| Posterior intermediate sulcus | Dorsal, just lateral to midline | Between the posterior median and posterolateral sulci |
| Posterolateral sulcus | Dorsal, most lateral | Most lateral of the three dorsal grooves |

- The central canal is the small opening in the center of the gray matter
- Orientation tip: the deep fissure is anterior (ventral), and the three paired or midline sulci on the other side are posterior (dorsal)

## Why they matter

The grooves are landmarks for the vessels on the cord surface covered later in the lecture. The anterior spinal artery runs in the anterior median fissure, and the posterior spinal arteries run along the posterolateral sulci.

> The anterior spinal artery lies in the anterior median fissure, and the paired posterior spinal arteries lie along the posterolateral sulci.

Library: Spinal cord horns and their neurons
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Three-dimensional spinal cord segment with the horns of the gray matter outlined on one side: ventral horn (large blue-gray outline), lateral horn (small green outline), and dorsal horn (purple outline), beside slide text listing each horn's neurons](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-cord-gray-matter-horns.jpg)

## Identify it

- Gray matter is central and "H-shaped" on cross-section and is divided into horns
- **Ventral (anterior) horn**: the large, broad front projection
- **Dorsal (posterior) horn**: the narrower projection reaching toward the back of the cord
- **Lateral horn**: a small side projection between the dorsal and ventral horns

## Key features

| Horn | Contents | Segments |
|---|---|---|
| Ventral | Cell bodies of motor (somatic efferent) neurons, the lower motor neurons; their axons form the anterior root | All |
| Dorsal | Axons of sensory (somatic afferent and visceral afferent) neurons, and neurons that receive impulses from sensory neurons in the dorsal root ganglia | All |
| Lateral | Cell bodies of motor (visceral efferent) neurons: preganglionic sympathetic and parasympathetic neurons | T1-L2 and S2-S4 (slides) |

The handout describes the lateral horn only in the thoracic and upper lumbar cord (T1-L2(3)) with sympathetic neurons, while the slide adds S2-S4 for preganglionic parasympathetic neurons.

> Ventral horn cell bodies are the lower motor neurons, and lateral horn cell bodies are preganglionic autonomic (visceral efferent) neurons.

Library: Spinal cord funiculi and white commissure
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Three-dimensional spinal cord segment with the white matter regions outlined: anterior funiculus (orange), posterior funiculus (pink), lateral funiculus (blue), and anterior white commissure (small red bar near the central canal), beside slide text listing their tracts](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-cord-funiculi-white-commissure.jpg)

## Identify it

- White matter is peripheral, surrounding the gray matter
- **Anterior funiculus**: between the anterior median fissure and the ventral rootlets
- **Lateral funiculus**: the side of the cord, between the ventral and dorsal roots
- **Posterior funiculus**: between the dorsal horn and the posterior midline
- **Anterior white commissure**: a thin band crossing the midline in front of the central canal

## Key features

White matter is made of myelinated nerve fibers (axons) traveling to and from the brain, and it looks white because of the myelin. A funiculus (column) is a group of several parallel tracts or fasciculi (axons).

| Region | Contents |
|---|---|
| Anterior funiculus | Ascending sensory and descending motor tracts |
| Posterior funiculus | Ascending sensory tracts only |
| Lateral funiculus | Ascending sensory and descending motor tracts |
| Anterior white commissure | Crossing fibers from several tracts |

> The posterior funiculus carries only ascending sensory tracts, while the anterior and lateral funiculi carry both ascending sensory and descending motor tracts.

Library: Ascending vs descending tract neuron chains
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Coronal brain and brainstem with a spinal cord cross-section showing an ascending spinal pathway (1st order neuron in blue entering the cord, 2nd order neuron in orange crossing and ascending, 3rd order neuron in magenta reaching the cortex) and a descending spinal pathway (upper motor neuron in green from cortex to cord, lower motor neuron in red leaving the cord, interneuron in brown), with a color legend](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-ascending-descending-tract-neurons.jpg)

## Key features

| Feature | Ascending tracts | Descending tracts |
|---|---|---|
| Carry | Sensory stimuli from the periphery to the brain | Motor output from the brain to the periphery |
| Number of neurons | Three | Two |
| Neurons | 1st, 2nd, and 3rd order neurons | Upper motor neuron, lower motor neuron |

## Upper vs lower motor neurons

- **Upper motor neurons** start in the brain, travel down to the brainstem or spinal cord, and synapse on lower motor neurons
- **Lower motor neurons** carry the signal from the upper motor neurons to the target muscle or gland
- Lower motor neuron cell bodies are in the ventral (anterior) horn, and their axons leave through the anterior roots of spinal nerves or through cranial nerves

## Identify it

- In the figure, the ascending chain enters the cord from the periphery (blue), crosses and rises in the cord (orange), and ends in the cortex (magenta)
- The descending chain runs from the cortex down the cord (green) and leaves the ventral horn to the periphery (red)

> Ascending sensory pathways use three neurons (1st, 2nd, 3rd order), and descending motor pathways use two (upper and lower motor neuron).

Library: Cord regions by white-to-gray matter ratio
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Four stained spinal cord cross-sections with the gray matter outlined and tinted pink and the white matter dark: cervical, thoracic, lumbar, and sacral levels](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-white-gray-matter-ratio-by-cord-region.jpg)

## Identify it

The slide compares the amount of white matter to gray matter at the four cord regions. Reading the figure:

| Region | Overall shape | Gray vs white |
|---|---|---|
| Cervical | Wide, oval section | A lot of white matter surrounding a broad H of gray matter |
| Thoracic | Smaller, round section | Thin, slender gray horns with a small lateral projection; white matter dominates |
| Lumbar | Round section | Large, bulky gray horns; less white matter than cervical |
| Sacral | Small section | Gray matter fills most of the section; only a thin rim of white matter |

- Gray matter in this figure is outlined in pink, and white matter is the darker surrounding tissue
- The small side projection seen at the thoracic level fits the lateral horn, which the lecture places in T1-L2

## Key features

- White matter is made of myelinated axons traveling to and from the brain
- Gray matter holds neuronal cell bodies, divided into ventral, dorsal, and (at some levels) lateral horns

> Moving down the cord from cervical to sacral, the proportion of white matter shrinks and gray matter takes up more of the section.

Library: Spinal nerve formation: rootlets, roots, and spinal nerve
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Anterior view of two spinal cord segments with dorsal rootlets, ventral rootlets, dorsal root, ventral root, dorsal root ganglion, spinal nerve, dorsal ramus, and ventral ramus labeled; the cut top surface marks the dorsal/posterior horn, lateral horn, and ventral/anterior horn; a dotted line separates the two segments](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-nerve-rootlets-roots-rami-anterior.jpg)

## Identify it

- Many small rootlets leave the front and back of the cord and merge into one ventral root and one dorsal root
- The dorsal root has a swelling, the dorsal root (spinal) ganglion
- Ventral and dorsal roots join to form the spinal nerve, which then splits into a dorsal and a ventral ramus

![Spinal cord segment tracing a motor neuron (magenta cell body in the ventral horn, axon out through the ventral rootlets and ventral root) and a sensory neuron (blue cell body in the dorsal root ganglion, process through the dorsal root into the cord), both running in the spinal nerve, dorsal ramus, and ventral ramus](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-nerve-motor-sensory-cell-bodies.jpg)

## Key features

| Structure | Fibers | Cell bodies |
|---|---|---|
| Ventral (anterior) rootlets and root | Motor (efferent) axons, plus presynaptic autonomic fibers | Anterior horn of the gray matter |
| Dorsal (posterior) rootlets and root | Sensory (afferent) axons/processes | Dorsal root (spinal) ganglion |
| Spinal nerve | Mixed: motor and sensory | None |
| Dorsal and ventral rami | Mixed: motor and sensory | None |

- The spinal nerve lies within the intervertebral (IV) foramen
- Each pair of spinal nerves represents one spinal cord segment

> Dorsal roots are sensory with cell bodies in the dorsal root ganglion, ventral roots are motor with cell bodies in the ventral horn, and the spinal nerve and both rami are mixed.

Library: Dorsal and ventral rami of a spinal nerve
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Superior view of an axial section through a thoracic vertebra and back muscles, labeling the medial branch of the dorsal ramus, lateral branch of the dorsal ramus, dorsal ramus, ventral ramus, spinal nerve beside the DRG, rami communicantes, and sympathetic trunk](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-dorsal-ventral-rami-superior-view.jpg)

## Identify it

- The spinal nerve is the short trunk just past the dorsal root ganglion (DRG)
- The **dorsal ramus** turns backward into the back muscles and divides into medial and lateral branches
- The **ventral ramus** continues forward and laterally around the body wall
- The **rami communicantes** are small connections between the ventral ramus and the sympathetic trunk beside the vertebral body

## Key features

| Branch | Supplies | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Dorsal (posterior) ramus | Skin of the back and the deep (intrinsic) back muscles | Medial and lateral branches |
| Ventral (anterior) ramus | Limbs and the rest of the trunk | Most merge with one or more adjacent rami to form major nerve plexuses |
| Rami communicantes | Carry pre- and post-ganglionic sympathetic fibers | Connect to the sympathetic trunk |

- Both rami carry motor and sensory fibers

> Dorsal rami supply the skin and deep muscles of the back, while ventral rami supply the limbs and the rest of the trunk, mostly by forming plexuses.

Library: Spinal cord segments and the 31 spinal nerve pairs
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Lateral diagram of the vertebral column and spinal cord with spinal nerves color-coded (cervical green, thoracic blue, lumbar purple, sacral and coccygeal red), labeling vertebrae C1 to coccyx, the cervical enlargement, lumbosacral enlargement, conus medullaris, cauda equina, filum terminale internum, filum terminale externum, termination of the dural sac, sacrum, coccygeal nerve, and coccyx](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-cord-segments-spinal-nerves.jpg)

## Key features

- The cord is divided into regions, and each region consists of segments
- Each segment gives rise to a pair of spinal nerves, so there are 31 pairs
- Each nerve is named by a letter (region) and a number
- Segments are cord levels, not vertebral levels, and because the adult cord is shorter than the vertebral column, the lower segments sit well above the vertebrae with the same number

| Region | Vertebrae | Spinal nerve pairs |
|---|---|---|
| Cervical | 7 | 8 (C1-C8) |
| Thoracic | 12 | 12 (T1-T12) |
| Lumbar | 5 | 5 (L1-L5) |
| Sacral | 5 fused | 5 (S1-S5) |
| Coccygeal | 4 fused | 1 (Co1) |

## Identify it

- The cervical region has one more nerve than vertebrae, because C1 exits above the C1 vertebra and C8 exits below C7
- Below the conus medullaris, the lumbar and sacral roots run down together as the cauda equina
- The dural sac ends in the sacrum, and the filum terminale externum continues on to the coccyx

> There are 31 pairs of spinal nerves: 8 cervical, 12 thoracic, 5 lumbar, 5 sacral, and 1 coccygeal.

Library: Spinal nerve exit points through the vertebral column
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Lateral diagram of the cervical and thoracic spinal cord and vertebrae with circled labels for the C1 spinal nerve (exits above C1 vertebra) and the C8 spinal nerve (exits below C7 vertebra), plus labeled cervical enlargement, lumbosacral enlargement, conus medullaris, and nerves T1 to T12](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-cervical-thoracic-nerve-exit-c1-c8.jpg)

## Key features

| Nerves | Exit from the vertebral canal |
|---|---|
| C1-C7 | IV foramen superior to the corresponding vertebra |
| C8 | IV foramen inferior to the C7 vertebra (between C7 and T1) |
| T1-T12 | IV foramen inferior to the corresponding vertebra |
| L1-L5 | IV foramen inferior to the corresponding vertebra |
| S1-S4 | Anterior sacral foramina |
| S5 | Sacral hiatus |
| Co1 | Sacral hiatus |

## Segment vs vertebral level

- Cervical cord segments stay close to their corresponding vertebrae
- Below the cervical region, spinal nerves leave by passing inferior to the pedicles of their corresponding vertebrae
- The distance between a cord segment and the IV foramen its nerves use grows as the cord descends
- Because the cord ends at L1 or L2 in adults, the lower lumbar and sacral roots have to descend inside the canal to reach their foramina, forming the cauda equina

> C1-C7 exit above their same-numbered vertebrae, C8 exits between C7 and T1, and every spinal nerve below that exits beneath its same-numbered vertebra.

Library: Spinal cord length vs vertebral column by age
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Four lateral drawings of the lower spinal cord, meninges, and vertebrae at 8 weeks, 24 weeks, newborn, and adult; labels include body of vertebra, dura mater, pia mater, arachnoid, root of first sacral nerve, dorsal root ganglion, conus medullaris, filum terminale, cerebrospinal fluid in subarachnoid space, root of first sacral nerve (part of cauda equina), and end of sac of arachnoid and dura at S2](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-cord-length-development-stages.jpg)

## Key features

- In the embryo the cord fills the full length of the spinal canal, so each cord segment sits at the level of its corresponding vertebra
- During fetal development the vertebral column grows faster than the spinal cord
- As a result, the cord's tip ends up higher and higher relative to the vertebrae, and roots have to run downward to their foramina

| Stage | Inferior end of the cord |
|---|---|
| Embryo (8 weeks in figure) | Full length of the canal |
| Birth | Tip of the conus medullaris at L4-L5 |
| Children | L3 |
| Adults | L1 or L2; cord occupies the superior 2/3 of the canal |

## Identify it

- At 8 weeks the root of the first sacral nerve leaves horizontally at S1, level with its segment
- In the adult the same S1 root runs a long way down inside the sac as part of the cauda equina
- The arachnoid and dura sac ends at S2 in the adult, well below the end of the cord, with CSF filling the space between

> The tip of the conus medullaris is at L4-L5 at birth, L3 in children, and L1/L2 in adults, because the vertebral column outgrows the cord.

Library: Disc herniation and nerve root compression
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Posterior view of the lower lumbar vertebrae and sacrum with the dural sac opened, showing L4 and L5 nerve roots exiting below their vertebrae and dashed ovals marking lateral protrusions of the L4/5 and L5/S1 discs pressing on the descending L5 and S1 roots; caption explains that an L4/5 protrusion affects L5 roots and an L5/S1 protrusion affects S1 roots](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-disc-herniation-root-compression-diagram.jpg)

## Key features

- Disc herniation is displacement of the nucleus pulposus from its normal position, into or through the anulus fibrosus
- Most common in the lumbosacral region, with ~95% at L4-L5 or L5-S1
- A herniation lateral to the posterior longitudinal ligament (posterolateral) can compress nerve roots, and a more direct posterior herniation can compress the spinal cord itself
- General rule: a protruding disc usually compresses the nerve root numbered one inferior to the disc
- Disc protrusions are also frequent in the cervical region, and because cervical nerves exit superior to their same-numbered vertebrae, the numbering rule still works
- Cervical herniations cause pain in the neck, shoulder, arm, and hand

| Herniated disc | Root compressed |
|---|---|
| L4/L5 | L5 |
| L5/S1 | S1 |
| C5/C6 (one of the most common cervical) | C6 |
| C6/C7 (one of the most common cervical) | C7 |

![Sagittal T2-weighted MRI of the lumbar spine with the L4, L5, and S1 vertebral bodies labeled, shown on the disc herniation slide](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-disc-herniation-sagittal-t2-mri.jpg)

> A herniated disc usually compresses the nerve root numbered one below it, so an L4-L5 herniation compresses the L5 root.

Library: Radiculopathy (pinched nerve)
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

## Key features

A radiculopathy happens when a compressed or inflamed spinal nerve root causes neurological deficits. It's commonly called a "pinched nerve."

- Deficits include problems with reflexes, numbness, tingling, and/or weakness
- Symptoms depend on which nerves are affected
- Compression of the root usually happens at the intervertebral foramen

## Causes listed in the lecture

- Degenerative disc disease
- Osteoarthritis
- Facet joint degeneration or hypertrophy
- Ligamentous hypertrophy
- Spondylolisthesis
- A combination of these factors

## Why the lower lumbar nerves are vulnerable

The lumbar spinal nerves get bigger from superior to inferior, while the lumbar IV foramina get smaller. The L5 spinal nerves are the largest and pass through the smallest foramina, so the more inferior nerves are more vulnerable to compression.

| Feature | Upper lumbar | Lower lumbar (L5) |
|---|---|---|
| Spinal nerve size | Smaller | Largest |
| IV foramen diameter | Larger | Smallest |
| Risk of compression | Lower | Highest |

> Radiculopathy is a compressed or inflamed nerve root, usually at the IV foramen, and L5 is most vulnerable because it's the largest lumbar nerve passing through the smallest foramen.

Library: Dermatomes and landmark levels
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Dermatome maps: a figure bent forward on all fours showing dermatome bands running along the body, and anterior and posterior full-body maps (Keegan and Garrett) labeling dermatomes from C2 to S5, with the face supplied by the trigeminal nerve (CN V) and a note that adjacent dermatomes actually overlap considerably](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-dermatome-map-anterior-posterior.jpg)

## Key features

- A dermatome is a unilateral area of skin supplied by a single spinal nerve (by the sensory fibers of that nerve)
- Dermatomes are segmental, matching the segment that gives rise to the spinal nerve
- The map's note says adjacent dermatomes overlap considerably even though they're drawn as distinct bands

## Landmarks

| Dermatome | Landmark |
|---|---|
| T4 | Nipple |
| T7 | Xiphoid process of sternum |
| T10 | Umbilicus |

## Identify it

- On the trunk, dermatomes run as horizontal bands (T1 to T12 in blue on the map)
- On the limbs, dermatomes run as long strips down the arm (C5 to T1) and leg (L1 to S2)
- The face is supplied by the trigeminal nerve (CN V) rather than a spinal nerve, and C2 begins at the back of the head
- The bent-forward figure shows how the bands line up when the body is in a four-legged posture

> T4 is at the nipple, T7 at the xiphoid process, and T10 at the umbilicus.

Library: Dermatomes vs peripheral nerve cutaneous fields
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Two side-by-side diagrams: left, three spinal cord segments sending three separate segmental (spinal) nerves to three overlapping dermatomes, as in the skin of the trunk; right, three spinal nerves combining through a plexus into one peripheral nerve whose area of distribution (dotted line) contains three overlapping dermatomes, as in the skin of the limbs](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-dermatomal-vs-peripheral-nerve-distribution.jpg)

## Key features

| Feature | Dermatome | Peripheral nerve field |
|---|---|---|
| Supplied by | A single spinal nerve | A named peripheral nerve |
| Pattern | Segmental | Multisegmental |
| Where it dominates | Skin of the trunk | Skin of the limbs |
| Origin | One cord segment | Branches of major plexuses formed by merging, exchange, and redistribution of fibers from ventral rami |

On the trunk, three cord segments send three separate spinal nerves, each to its own dermatome band, and neighboring bands overlap. In the limbs, the same three spinal nerves first mix in a plexus and leave as one peripheral nerve, so the skin supplied by that one peripheral nerve contains parts of three overlapping dermatomes. The limb maps below show peripheral nerve territories, which can be compared with the strips on the dermatome map.

## Examples from the lecture

- Lateral antebrachial cutaneous nerve (C5-C7)
- Lateral femoral cutaneous nerve (L2, L3)

![Anterior (palmar) and posterior (dorsal) views of the upper limb with cutaneous nerve territories color-coded, including supraclavicular nerves (C3, 4), superior and inferior lateral brachial cutaneous nerves, intercostobrachial and medial brachial cutaneous nerves, medial antebrachial cutaneous nerve, lateral antebrachial cutaneous nerve (C5, 6 (7)), posterior antebrachial cutaneous nerve, and radial, ulnar (C8, T1), and median nerve branches in the hand](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-upper-limb-cutaneous-nerves.jpg)

![Anterior and posterior views of the lower limb with cutaneous territories color-coded, labeling the lateral femoral cutaneous nerve, anterior cutaneous branches of the femoral nerve, infrapatellar and medial crural cutaneous branches of the saphenous nerve, posterior femoral cutaneous nerve, and sciatic branches (lateral and medial sural cutaneous, superficial fibular, sural, medial calcaneal branches of tibial nerve)](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-lower-limb-cutaneous-nerves.jpg)

> A dermatome is the skin of one spinal nerve, while a peripheral nerve field carries fibers from several spinal nerves combined through a plexus.

Library: Shingles and the dermatome pattern
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Photo of a red rash of grouped patches in a horizontal band across one side of the lower trunk, above a dermatome map of the trunk labeling bands T5 to T12 and L1, with C5 and C8 on the arm](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-shingles-dermatome-rash.jpg)

## Identify it

- A skin lesion confined to a single dermatome, so on the trunk it follows one horizontal band on one side
- Skin becomes red, and vesicular eruptions appear
- Symptoms include itchiness, tingling, and sharp burning pain

## Key features

- Shingles (herpes zoster) is caused by reactivation of varicella-zoster virus (VZV)
- After a previous varicella (chickenpox) infection, the virus stays dormant in sensory ganglia of cranial nerves or in dorsal root ganglia
- On reactivation it spreads to the skin through sensory neurons, which is why the painful rash has a well-defined dermatomal distribution
- Commonly occurs in adults and the elderly

## Connection to spinal nerve anatomy

Each dorsal root ganglion holds the sensory cell bodies for one spinal nerve, and one spinal nerve supplies one dermatome. Virus waking up in one ganglion therefore shows up in one dermatome.

> Shingles is reactivated varicella-zoster virus from a dorsal root or cranial sensory ganglion, causing a painful vesicular rash limited to one dermatome.

Library: General sensory vs somatic motor fibers
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Side-by-side drawings of (A) a multipolar motor neuron with dendrites, cell body, nucleus, axon with a collateral branch, Schwann cell nuclei, nodes of the myelin sheath, and peripheral axon terminals at an effector organ, and (B) a pseudounipolar sensory neuron with a cell body on a common stem for the peripheral and central processes, Schwann cells, nodes, and central axon terminals; a dashed line separates CNS from PNS and arrows show direction of conduction](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-multipolar-motor-vs-pseudounipolar-sensory.jpg)

## Key features

| Feature | General sensory (afferent) fibers | Somatic motor (efferent) fibers |
|---|---|---|
| Carry | Pain, temperature, touch, pressure, proprioception from the body to the CNS | Impulses to skeletal (voluntary) muscle |
| Neuron type | Pseudounipolar | Multipolar (lower motor neuron) |
| Cell body | Outside the CNS: spinal (dorsal root) ganglia or sensory ganglia of cranial nerves | Gray matter of the spinal cord (anterior horn) |
| Route | Central process enters the CNS through the posterior root | Axon leaves through the anterior root or a cranial nerve |

## Identify it

- **Pseudounipolar sensory neuron**: a short common stem leaves the cell body and splits into a peripheral process (from the receptor organ toward the cell body) and a central process (from the cell body into the CNS)
- **Multipolar motor neuron**: two or more dendrites and one axon, which may give off one or more collateral branches; it controls skeletal muscle and the ANS
- A single lower motor neuron spans the distance from the spinal cord to the target muscle, and the upper motor neuron connects its synapse in the cord to the brain

> General sensory fibers are pseudounipolar neurons with cell bodies in sensory ganglia outside the CNS, while somatic motor fibers are multipolar lower motor neurons with cell bodies in the anterior horn.

Library: Myotomes
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

## Key features

A myotome is a group of muscles innervated by a single spinal nerve (by the motor fibers of that nerve). Because ventral rami merge into plexuses, a named nerve can carry fibers from more than one spinal nerve, and one spinal nerve can contribute to a movement through a named nerve.

## Worked example from the lecture

1. The C5 spinal nerve contributes to the axillary nerve of the brachial plexus
2. The axillary nerve innervates the deltoid muscle
3. The deltoid abducts the arm
4. So C5 contributes to shoulder abduction

## Myotome table

| Myotome | Action |
|---|---|
| C5 | Shoulder abduction and external rotation; elbow flexion |
| C6 | Wrist extension |
| C7 | Elbow extensors and wrist flexion |
| C8 | Thumb extension and finger flexion |
| T1 | Finger abduction |
| L1/L2 | Hip flexion |
| L3 | Knee extension |
| L4/L5 | Ankle dorsiflexion |
| L5 | Great toe extension |
| S1 | Ankle plantar flexion |
| S4 | Bladder and rectum motor supply |

> C5 contributes to shoulder abduction (through the axillary nerve to the deltoid), and S1 to ankle plantar flexion.

Library: Reflex arc
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Diagram of somatic and visceral pathways: a hand touching a hot iron (1) sends a general sensory fiber (2, dashed) through the spinal (sensory) ganglion and posterior horn into the spinal cord (3), and a somatic motor fiber (4, solid) from the anterior horn runs out the mixed spinal nerve to the arm muscle (5); visceral sympathetic and parasympathetic pathways to the heart, lateral horn, gray and white rami communicantes, sympathetic ganglion, and fiber-type legends are also shown](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-reflex-arc-somatic-visceral-pathways.jpg)

## Key features

- A reflex is a rapid, involuntary movement in response to a stimulus
- A reflex arc is the neural pathway that carries sensory information from the receptor to the spinal cord (via a sensory neuron) and then carries the response generated by the cord to the effector organ (via a motor neuron)

## Parts of the reflex arc (numbers match the figure)

1. Sensory stimulus and receptor (hand on the hot iron)
2. Sensory neuron (dashed general sensory fiber, cell body in the spinal ganglion)
3. Spinal cord
4. Motor neuron (solid somatic motor fiber from the anterior horn)
5. Target/effector organ (arm muscle)

## Myotatic reflex

The myotatic reflex (stretch reflex, deep tendon reflex) is muscle contraction in response to stretching within the muscle, for example by striking its tendon.

- Biceps reflex
- Brachioradialis reflex
- Patellar reflex

> A reflex arc runs receptor, sensory neuron, spinal cord, motor neuron, effector, and the myotatic (deep tendon) reflex is the classic example tested with the biceps, brachioradialis, and patellar reflexes.

Library: Spinal meninges: dura, arachnoid, and pia
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Drawing of the spinal cord with its coverings peeled back in layers, labeling the pia mater on the cord surface, the thin arachnoid mater, and the outer dura mater extending as sleeves along the spinal nerves](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-meninges-layers-drawing.jpg)

## Key features

The spinal meninges surround, support, and protect the spinal cord and nerve roots in the vertebral canal.

| Layer | Description | Notable features |
|---|---|---|
| Dura mater ("tough mother") | Tough outermost layer of thick connective tissue | Forms the dural (thecal) sac; separated from bone by the epidural space |
| Arachnoid mater | Delicate, avascular middle layer | Sends trabeculae across the subarachnoid space to the pia; held against the dura by CSF pressure, not attached to it |
| Pia mater | Thin innermost layer directly covering the cord, roots, and vessels | Forms the denticulate ligaments and the filum terminale internum |

## Dural sac

- Continuous with the cranial dura mater at the foramen magnum
- Extends inferiorly to the S2 vertebral level
- Pierced by spinal nerves, and the dura surrounds each pair of nerve roots (dural root sheath)
- Anchored to the coccyx by the filum terminale externum

![Drawing of a spinal cord segment in a vertebra showing the layered coverings: pia mater on the cord, arachnoid mater with the subarachnoid space, dura mater with a dural root sheath around the spinal nerve, and epidural fat; also labels the white and gray matter, posterior rootlets, posterior and anterior roots, spinal ganglion, spinal nerve, and posterior and anterior rami](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-nerve-roots-meningeal-coverings.jpg)

## Pia mater extensions

- **Denticulate ligaments**: tooth-like extensions of pia between the anterior and posterior roots that anchor the cord in the dural sac
- **Filum terminale internum**: continuation of pia from the tip of the conus medullaris

![Cadaver photo of the opened vertebral canal with the dura mater reflected, labeling the pia mater on the cord, the denticulate ligaments, and the arachnoid mater, with an inset of the conus region labeling the filum terminale internum](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-meninges-cadaver-denticulate-ligaments.jpg)

> The denticulate ligaments and the filum terminale internum are both extensions of the pia mater that anchor the cord.

Library: Spinal meningeal spaces and the lumbar cistern
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Axial section of a vertebra and spinal cord labeling the epidural space (with veins and fat), dura mater, arachnoid mater and subarachnoid space, and pia mater on the cord surface](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-meninges-spaces-axial.jpg)

## Key features

| Space | Location | Type | Contents |
|---|---|---|---|
| Epidural | Between bone and dura (superficial to dura) | Actual | Epidural fat and the internal vertebral venous plexus |
| Subdural | Between dura and arachnoid | Potential | None normally |
| Subarachnoid | Between arachnoid and pia | Actual | Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) and the vasculature of the cord |

- The epidural space extends from the foramen magnum to the sacral hiatus
- Cancer can metastasize to the spinal cord through the vasculature in the epidural space

## Identify it

- The epidural space is the outermost layer, with fat and veins packed against the bone
- The dura mater is the first membrane inside it
- The subarachnoid space is the dark band between the arachnoid and the cord, crossed by thin trabeculae
- The pia mater lies directly on the cord surface

## Lumbar cistern

- An enlargement of the subarachnoid space between the end of the spinal cord (L2 vertebral level) and the end of the dural sac (S2 vertebral level)
- Holds the cauda equina bathed in CSF
- It's the target for a lumbar puncture

![Posterolateral view of the lower thoracic, lumbar, and sacral vertebral column with the dural sac opened, labeling the spinal cord, dural sac, arachnoid mater, subarachnoid space, conus medullaris near L1/L2, cauda equina in the lumbar cistern, filum terminale internum, spinal sensory ganglia in dural sleeves, sacrum, filum terminale externum, coccyx, and nerves T10 to S5](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-dural-sac-lumbar-cistern-posterolateral.jpg)

> The lumbar cistern is the CSF-filled subarachnoid space from the end of the cord (L2) to the end of the dural sac (S2), containing the cauda equina.

Library: Lumbar puncture needle path
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Patient lying on her side with the trunk flexed while a needle is inserted in the lower back, above a sagittal drawing of the lumbar spine (L3 to L5) tracing the needle through skin, subcutaneous layer, back muscles, supraspinous ligament, interspinous ligament, ligamentum flavum, epidural space, and dura mater and arachnoid into the subarachnoid space containing the cauda equina](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-lumbar-puncture-position-needle-path.jpg)

## Key features

- Withdrawal of CSF from the lumbar cistern, important for evaluating CNS disorders
- Level: ~L4/L5 on the slide; the handout says usually L3/L4 or L4/L5
- At these levels there's no danger of damaging the spinal cord, which ends at L1/L2
- Landmark: a line between the tops of the iliac crests (supracristal or intercristal plane) usually passes through the L4 spinous process
- Position: patient on the side with the trunk flexed, which spreads the laminae and spinous processes and stretches the ligamenta flava so the needle goes in more easily
- The needle goes in the midline between the spinous processes
- Not performed when intracranial pressure is increased

## Layers the needle passes through

1. Skin
2. Subcutaneous tissue
3. Muscle
4. Supraspinous ligament
5. Interspinous ligament
6. Ligamentum flavum
7. Epidural space
8. Dura mater
9. Arachnoid mater
10. Subarachnoid space (lumbar cistern)

> A lumbar puncture enters the lumbar cistern at about L4/L5, using the iliac crests to find L4, and the last layers crossed are ligamentum flavum, epidural space, dura, and arachnoid.

Library: Epidural anesthesia vs lumbar puncture
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Sagittal drawing of the lumbar spine and sacrum with the cauda equina in the dural sac, showing needles entering between lumbar spinous processes and one needle entering the sacral canal through the sacral hiatus; blue arrows point to one lumbar needle and to the sacral needle, and a red X marks a second lumbar needle](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-epidural-anesthesia-needle-sites.jpg)

## Key features

- An epidural block injects medication into the epidural space to bathe and block sensory and motor nerve roots
- The anesthetic acts on spinal nerve roots after they leave the dural sac
- A lumbar epidural is usually given at L3/L4 or L4/L5, the same site as a lumbar puncture
- Anesthetic can also go into the sacral canal through the sacral hiatus or through the posterior sacral foramina
- How far it spreads (and how many nerves it affects) depends on the amount injected and the patient's position
- Indications: obstetrical anesthesia during labor, and surgical anesthesia for thoracic, intraabdominal, or spine surgery

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Lumbar puncture | Epidural block |
|---|---|---|
| Space entered | Subarachnoid space (lumbar cistern) | Epidural space |
| Crosses the dura? | Yes, through dura and arachnoid | No, stops superficial to the dura |
| Purpose | Withdraw CSF to evaluate CNS disorders | Inject anesthetic to block nerve roots |
| Usual level | L3/L4 or L4/L5 | L3/L4 or L4/L5, or the sacral hiatus |

> A lumbar puncture goes through the dura into the subarachnoid space to sample CSF, while an epidural block stops in the epidural space outside the dura to anesthetize nerve roots.

Library: Anterior and posterior spinal arteries
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Anterior and posterior views of the spinal cord and its arteries labeling the right and left vertebral arteries, the anterior spinal artery, the left and right posterior spinal arteries, and the artery of Adamkiewicz joining the anterior spinal artery in the lower thoracic region](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-arteries-artery-of-adamkiewicz.jpg)

## Key features

Three longitudinal spinal arteries supply the cord: one anterior and two posterior.

| Artery | Number | Origin | Course | Supplies |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Anterior spinal artery | Unpaired | Union of branches from the vertebral arteries | Anterior median fissure | Anterior 2/3 of the cord |
| Posterior spinal arteries | Paired | Left and right vertebral arteries | Posterolateral sulci | Posterior 1/3 of the cord |

![Axial section of the spinal cord showing the posterior spinal artery territory (dorsal, shaded green) and the anterior spinal artery territory (ventral), with the posterior spinal arteries, anterior spinal artery, and the spinal arterial plexus around the cord labeled](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-artery-territories-axial.jpg)

## Identify it

- On the cross-section, the posterior territory covers the dorsal part of the cord, and the anterior territory covers everything ventral to it
- A ring of vessels (spinal arterial plexus) connects the anterior and posterior spinal arteries around the cord

## Clinical significance

Deficient blood supply (ischemia) to the cord affects its function and can cause muscle weakness and paralysis. Fractures, dislocations, and obstructive arterial disease can interfere with the blood supply from the spinal and medullary arteries.

> The unpaired anterior spinal artery supplies the anterior 2/3 of the cord, and the paired posterior spinal arteries supply the posterior 1/3.

Library: Artery of Adamkiewicz and segmental medullary arteries
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Three-dimensional drawing of a spinal cord segment with its arteries, labeling the paired posterior spinal arteries, the anterior spinal artery, and segmental medullary arteries joining them](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-cord-arteries-3d.jpg)

## Key features

The longitudinal spinal arteries alone aren't enough for the lower cord, so segmental vessels reinforce them.

- Segmental medullary and radicular arteries reinforce circulation to the inferior spinal cord
- They're branches of the ascending cervical, deep cervical, vertebral, posterior intercostal, and lumbar arteries (the handout also lists lateral sacral arteries)
- Segmental medullary arteries (anterior and posterior) are small, enter through the IV foramina at irregular levels, and cluster around the cervical and lumbosacral enlargements, where the need for blood is greatest

## Artery of Adamkiewicz

| Feature | Detail |
|---|---|
| Full name | Greater (great) anterior segmental medullary artery |
| Size | Largest anterior segmental medullary artery |
| Origin | Spinal branch of an inferior intercostal or upper lumbar artery |
| Side | Left side in ~65% of people |
| Territory | Reinforces circulation to the inferior 2/3 of the cord and the lumbosacral enlargement |

- It's the major blood supply to the lumbar and sacral cord
- It's at risk during some procedures and surgeries, for example surgical treatment of an aortic aneurysm
- Damage or obstruction can cause bilateral lower extremity weakness or paralysis and loss of urinary and fecal continence

> Injury to the artery of Adamkiewicz (for example during aortic aneurysm repair) can cause bilateral leg weakness or paralysis and loss of bladder and bowel continence.

Library: Spinal cord veins and Batson's plexus
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Overview of the Nervous System and Spinal Cord

![Drawing of a spinal cord segment labeling the anterior spinal vein, posterior spinal vein, and medullary veins, beside a vertebra with the internal vertebral venous plexuses labeled](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-spinal-cord-veins-internal-vertebral-plexus.jpg)

## Venous drainage

1. Anterior and posterior spinal veins run along the cord
2. Anterior and posterior medullary and radicular veins drain them
3. These drain into the internal vertebral (epidural) venous plexuses in the epidural space
4. The internal plexuses communicate with the external vertebral venous plexuses on the outer surface of the vertebrae

## Batson's plexus

The anterior and posterior internal vertebral (epidural) venous plexuses are called Batson's plexus. It's valveless and can communicate with:

- Dural venous sinuses and vertebral veins in the cranium
- External vertebral venous plexuses
- Thoracic, abdominal, and deep pelvic veins (for example the prostatic venous plexus)

Because it has no valves and connects all these regions, it serves as a route for spread of cancer and infection. For example, prostate cancer can metastasize through the plexus to all regions of the spine, the meninges, and the brain.

![Unlabeled drawings used with Batson's plexus: posterior view of the lumbar vertebral canal and sacrum opened to show the internal vertebral venous plexus, the cranial cavity floor with dural venous sinuses, and a sagittal pelvis with pelvic veins](library/images/msk-week-13/nssc-vertebral-venous-plexus-batson.jpg)

> Batson's plexus (internal vertebral venous plexus) is valveless, so it can carry cancer, such as prostate cancer, and infection to the spine and brain.

Library: Nervous tissue organization: neurons, glia, and neuropil
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Body diagram of the nervous system labeling the central nervous system (brain, spinal cord) and the peripheral nervous system (ganglion, nerve)](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-cns-pns-overview-diagram.jpg)

## Key features

- The CNS is all parts of the brain plus the spinal cord
- The PNS is the nerves, which run between the CNS and other tissues, plus nerve relay stations called ganglia
- PNS neurons and glia lie partly or entirely outside the brain and spinal cord
- The PNS has three subdivisions: sensorimotor, autonomic, and enteric

| Accumulation of | PNS | CNS |
|---|---|---|
| Neuron cell bodies | Ganglion | Nucleus |
| Axons | Nerve | Tract |

Gray matter and white matter are special CNS terms.

![Stained section of nervous tissue with arrows to neurons (one nucleolus marked in red), glia (support cells), and the neuropil between them](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-neurons-glia-neuropil.jpg)

## Identify it

- Neurons are the largest cells, with a cell body that sends out processes and a nucleus with a visible nucleolus
- Glia are smaller support cells scattered among the neurons
- Neuropil is the tangled background of neuronal and glial processes with no apparent organization

## Function

- Neurons carry out the receptive, integrative, and motor functions of the nervous system
- Glia support the neurons
- Neuropil is the space between neuronal and glial cell bodies, made of dendrites, axons, synapses, glial cell processes, and microvasculature
- Neuropil is present in both white and gray matter

> Cell bodies cluster in a ganglion (PNS) or nucleus (CNS); axons bundle into a nerve (PNS) or tract (CNS).

Library: Neuron compartments: cell body, dendrites, axon, and terminal
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

Under the microscope a neuron has four compartments: the cell body (soma or perikaryon), a variable number of dendrites, a single axon, and a synaptic terminus.

![Diagram of a multipolar neuron labeled synapses, nucleus with the nucleolus marked in red, perikaryon, axon hillock, dendrite, axon, and terminal boutons](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-neuron-structure-diagram.jpg)

## Key features

- The cell body is 4 to 100 µm across, with a euchromatic nucleus and a prominent nucleolus
- It holds most of the cell's membrane organelles, including rough ER (called Nissl substance in neurons), Golgi, lysosomes, and SER, so it's the neuron's metabolic center and main site of protein synthesis
- Neurons make a lot of protein but export little of it; most meets internal needs
- Dendrites are extensions of the cell body that receive stimuli from other neurons, and they have only limited protein synthesis
- The axon is a single process that conducts impulses to other neurons, and it has no biosynthetic capability
- The axon leaves the cell body at the cone-shaped axon hillock
- The initial segment lies between the hillock and the start of the myelin sheath. It has a high density of voltage-sensitive ion channels, and the action potential starts here and typically travels away from the cell body.
- At the terminal, one axon may branch to contact as many as 10,000 other neurons
- Synapses pass impulses to another neuron or to other cells like muscle fibers or glands

![Light micrograph of a neuron labeled dendrites, cell body, supporting cells, and axon](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-neuron-micrograph-labeled.jpg)

## Identify it

- The cell body is the large, dark central mass with a round nucleus and dark nucleolus
- Several dendrites branch off the cell body
- One axon leaves the cell body as a single process
- Supporting cells show up as small dark nuclei around the neuron

> The action potential starts at the axon's initial segment, just past the axon hillock, which has a high density of voltage-sensitive ion channels.

Library: Nissl bodies and the axon hillock
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Ultrastructure diagram of a neuron cell body labeling dendrite, synapse, nucleolus, nucleus, rough endoplasmic reticulum (Nissl body), Golgi apparatus, mitochondrion, intermediate filaments (neurofilaments), axon hillock, microtubule, and axon](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-neuron-ultrastructure-nissl.jpg)

## Key features

- Nissl bodies are large aggregations of rough endoplasmic reticulum in the neuron
- Nissl bodies are present in the cell body and dendrites but not in the axon hillock or the axon
- The axon hillock is the cone-shaped region where the axon starts. It has microtubules but little Nissl substance, so it looks like a pale-staining region in histological sections.
- Hillock cytoplasm is dominated by cytoskeleton (microtubules and neurofilaments) plus membrane organelles headed into the axon

![Two H&E micrographs of neurons with key: (a) N on two neuron cell bodies; (b) one neuron with S on Nissl bodies, N on the neuron, A on the axon, D on a dendrite, and P on neuropil](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-neurons-he-nissl-axon-dendrite.jpg)

Key: N, neurons; S, Nissl body; A, axon; D, dendrites; P, neuropil.

## Identify it

- Neurons (N) are large cell bodies with a round nucleus and prominent nucleolus
- Nissl bodies (S) are dark purple clumps in the cell body cytoplasm
- The axon (A) is a tapering, paler process with no Nissl clumps
- Dendrites (D) are processes that leave the cell body and do contain Nissl bodies
