- Neuropil (P) is the pink, finely textured background between the cell bodies

> Nissl bodies (clumped rough ER) fill the cell body and dendrites but are absent from the axon hillock and axon.

Library: Dendrites and dendritic spines
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Black-stained stellate neuron with many dendrites radiating from the cell body; DS marks a dendritic spine and a red label marks the axon](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-dendritic-spines.jpg)

## Identify it

- Many dendrites radiate from a single stellate (star-shaped) neuron
- Each dendrite carries many tiny bumps along its surface, the dendritic spines (DS)
- The axon is labeled as one thin process leaving the cell body

## Key features

- Dendrites are tapering processes whose branching ranges from simple to elaborate
- Spines are about 2 µm long and are postsynaptic specializations, the sites of synapses with other neurons
- Dendrites are the neuron's receiving domain. Their membranes have receptors that bind transmitters released by input cells, so dendrites are postsynaptic.
- Neurotransmitters cause a local graded electrical response in the dendrite, not an action potential
- The shape of the dendritic arbor is characteristic of each neuron subset and is one of the main features used to classify neurons

> Dendritic spines are the sites of synapses with other neurons, and dendrites respond with graded potentials rather than action potentials.

Library: Neuron shapes: multipolar, bipolar, and pseudo-unipolar
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

Neurons are classified by the number and arrangement of their processes.

![Diagrams of multipolar neurons (dendrites, cell body, axon), a bipolar neuron (dendrite, cell body, axon), and a pseudo-unipolar neuron (dendrites, cell body, short single process, peripheral process, central process, axon), with red input and blue output arrows](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-neuron-types-diagram.jpg)

![Line drawings comparing unipolar, bipolar, pseudounipolar, and multipolar neurons](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-neuron-types-drawing-unipolar.jpg)

## Identify it

- Multipolar: a cell body with many branching dendrites plus one axon
- Bipolar: the cell body sits between one dendrite on one side and one axon on the other
- Pseudo-unipolar: the cell body hangs off a short single process that joins a long axon with a peripheral and a central process
- Unipolar: a single process
- In the diagrams, input (red arrows) comes in through dendrites and output (blue arrows) leaves through the axon

## Look-alikes

| Type | Processes | Where found |
|---|---|---|
| Multipolar | Two or more dendrites, one axon | Most common; throughout the CNS (intermediate, integratory, and motor neurons), e.g., cortical pyramidal cells |
| Bipolar | One dendrite, one axon | Receptor neurons for smell, sight, and balance, e.g., retinal bipolar cells |
| Pseudo-unipolar | One process that splits into a dendrite and an axon | Dorsal root and cranial nerve ganglia (somatic and visceral sensory ganglion cells) |
| Unipolar | One process | Very common in invertebrates |

Pseudo-unipolar neurons are often mixed up with unipolar neurons, which are more common in invertebrates. Later in the lecture, anterior horn motor neurons and autonomic ganglion cells are multipolar, and dorsal root ganglion cells are pseudo-unipolar.

> Pseudo-unipolar neurons (one process that splits into a dendrite and an axon) are the sensory neurons of dorsal root and cranial nerve ganglia.

Library: Axonal transport: anterograde vs. retrograde
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Diagram of axonal transport along microtubules: kinesin and dynein/dynactin/activator motors carry synaptic vesicles, mitochondria, and degradative organelles between the cell body and presynaptic sites, with anterograde and retrograde arrows](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-axonal-transport-diagram.jpg)

Axonal transport is required for the normal function, maintenance, and growth of neurons. The cell body makes most of the neuron's protein, and the axon has no biosynthetic capability of its own.

## Key features

| Direction | Moves material | Cargo examples |
|---|---|---|
| Anterograde | From the soma toward the axon | Mitochondria, enzymes, synaptic vesicles, Ca ions |
| Retrograde | From the axon toward the soma | Used synaptic vesicles, damaged mitochondria, growth factors, some pathogens |

- Transport runs along microtubules, shown with plus and minus ends in the diagram
- In the diagram, kinesin carries cargo in the anterograde direction and dynein (with dynactin and an activator) carries cargo in the retrograde direction
- The diagram's legend shows mitochondria, Golgi apparatus, synaptic vesicles, and degradative organelles as cargo
- The learning objective pairs anterograde transport with secretory vesicles and retrograde transport with growth factors

> Anterograde transport moves synaptic vesicles and mitochondria from the soma toward the axon; retrograde transport brings growth factors, used vesicles, damaged mitochondria, and some pathogens back to the soma.

Library: Synapse types: contact sites and electrical vs. chemical
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

A signal is received by dendrites and passed along the axon to another neuron, a muscle, or a gland. The axon ends at a bulbous swelling called the terminal bouton.

![Diagram of three synapse types between neurons: axodendritic, axosomatic, and axoaxonic, with dendrites, soma (cell body), and axon labeled](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-synapse-contact-types.jpg)

## Key features

- Axodendritic: axon to dendrite
- Axosomatic: axon to cell body
- Axoaxonic: axon to axon
- Synapses form between two neurons or between a neuron and a target cell such as a muscle cell

![Diagram comparing an electrical synapse (gap junction, flow of ions into the receiving cell) with a chemical synapse (synaptic vesicles, neurotransmitters, ion channels on the receiving cell)](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-electrical-vs-chemical-synapse.jpg)

## Identify it

- Electrical synapse: the two cell membranes are linked by gap junction channels, and ions flow straight through into the receiving cell
- Chemical synapse: the terminal holds synaptic vesicles full of neurotransmitter, a gap separates it from the receiving cell, and ion channels on the receiving cell open when transmitter binds
- In the contact-site diagram, follow the axon of the first neuron to see whether it ends on a dendrite, the soma, or another axon

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Electrical synapse | Chemical synapse |
|---|---|---|
| Junction | Gap junction | Synaptic cleft |
| How the signal crosses | Ions flow directly between cells | Neurotransmitter released by the presynaptic neuron acts on the postsynaptic cell |
| Direction and speed | Bidirectional, very rapid | Presynaptic to postsynaptic |
| How common | Rare, between two neurons | Common |

> Electrical synapses pass ions directly through gap junctions (bidirectional and very rapid); chemical synapses release neurotransmitter into a synaptic cleft.

Library: Chemical synapse structure
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Chemical synapse diagram (synaptic vesicle, neurotransmitters, voltage-gated Ca++ channel, neurotransmitter re-uptake pump, neurotransmitter receptors, post-synaptic density, axon terminal, synaptic cleft, dendritic spine) beside an electron micrograph labeling synaptic vesicles within the axon and the post-synaptic cell with neurotransmitter receptors](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-chemical-synapse-diagram-em.jpg)

![Electron micrograph of a synapse labeling the terminal bouton, synaptic vesicles, synaptic cleft, and neuronal cell body](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-synapse-em-terminal-bouton.jpg)

## Identify it

- The presynaptic terminal (terminal bouton) is packed with small, round, pale synaptic vesicles
- A narrow synaptic cleft separates the terminal from the postsynaptic cell
- The postsynaptic membrane faces the vesicle-filled terminal and carries the neurotransmitter receptors

## Key features

- Synaptic vesicles release neurotransmitter by exocytosis into the cleft, and it's taken up by the postsynaptic membrane of the receiving cell
- Presynaptic compartment: electron-lucent vesicles 40 to 60 nm across holding classical non-peptide transmitters (acetylcholine, glutamate, GABA, glycine, norepinephrine, epinephrine, serotonin, ATP)
- Vesicles docked on the membrane facing the cleft mark the active zone
- Some terminals also have larger dense core vesicles (250 to 400 nm) holding peptide transmitters, similar to endocrine secretory granules
- Synaptic cleft: 15 to 30 nm wide, wider at the neuromuscular junction. Transmitter crosses by diffusion, and its effect ends by reuptake into the presynaptic terminal, breakdown in the cleft, or diffusion out of the cleft.
- Extracellular matrix in the cleft lines up the pre- and postsynaptic membranes
- Postsynaptic compartment: ionotropic receptors (ligand-gated ion channels) and G protein-coupled receptors, with excitatory (depolarizing) or inhibitory (hyperpolarizing) effects

> A chemical synapse has three compartments: a vesicle-filled presynaptic terminal, a narrow synaptic cleft, and a receptor-rich postsynaptic membrane.

Library: Afferent vs. efferent fibers and the motor unit
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Diagram of a sensory (afferent) nerve carrying signals from the skin to the CNS and a motor (efferent) nerve carrying commands from the CNS to muscle](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-afferent-efferent-diagram.jpg)

## Key features

| Direction | Carries impulses | Function |
|---|---|---|
| Afferent | From the periphery toward the CNS | Sensory |
| Efferent | Away from the CNS to peripheral target cells | Motor, e.g., the neuromuscular junction |

- Afferent neurons send signals from the senses, skin, muscles, and internal organs to the CNS
- Efferent neurons send commands from the CNS to muscles, glands, and organs

![Neuromuscular junction diagram labeling the motor neuron axon, axon terminal, synaptic vesicles containing acetylcholine, motor end plate, sarcolemma, T tubule, and terminal cisternae](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-nmj-diagram-acetylcholine.jpg)

## Motor end plate and motor unit

- The motor end plate is a specialized, flattened region of the muscle fiber membrane at the neuromuscular junction
- It's packed with receptors for neurotransmitters such as acetylcholine, which the motor neuron releases to make the muscle fiber contract
- The motor end plate is a special synapse between an axon and muscle cells
- Before ending in a skeletal muscle, each motor axon in the nerve forms many branches, and each branch synapses with one muscle fiber
- One motor neuron may supply a few to more than a thousand muscle fibers, depending on how precise the muscle's movement is
- A motor neuron plus all the muscle fibers it supplies is a motor unit

> A motor unit is one motor neuron plus all the muscle fibers it innervates, from a few fibers to more than a thousand.

Library: Motor end plates in stained muscle
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Light micrograph of striated skeletal muscle (AuCl2 stain, 400x) labeling a bundle of axons, an axon, synaptic knobs (boutons), motor end plates, skeletal muscle striations, and a muscle nucleus](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-motor-end-plate-lm.jpg)

![Three stained muscle panels: (b) branches of one axon with multiple motor end plates, (c) one motor end plate, (d) specific locations of multiple motor end plates](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-motor-end-plate-silver-stain.jpg)

## Identify it

- A dark-stained nerve bundle (bundle of axons) crosses the striated muscle fibers
- The bundle splits into thin terminal axonal twigs, and each twig heads to one muscle fiber
- Each twig ends in a dark cluster of synaptic knobs (boutons) sitting on the fiber; that cluster is the motor end plate
- The cross-striations of the skeletal muscle fibers show in the background
- One axon can be followed to several end plates on different fibers (panel b)

## Key features

- Silver staining shows the nerve bundle, the terminal axonal twigs, and the motor end plates on striated muscle fibers
- The first micrograph is labeled "motor nerve endings with end plates, snake, AuCl2 stain" at 400x
- Each terminal swelling in the cluster has the same basic structure as a synapse

> On silver- or AuCl2-stained muscle, a motor axon branches into twigs that each end in a dark cluster of boutons (the motor end plate) on one striated fiber.

Library: Neuromuscular junction ultrastructure
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Schematic of a neuromuscular junction labeling the motor nerve axon, cytoskeleton, myelin sheath, external lamina, Schwann cell nucleus and cytoplasm, synaptic vesicles, primary and secondary synaptic clefts, mitochondrion, muscle cell nucleus, and myofibrils](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-nmj-schematic-secondary-clefts.jpg)

![Electron micrograph of a neuromuscular junction labeling the axon terminus, synaptic vesicle, basal lamina, acetylcholine receptors, voltage-gated Na+ channels, muscle cell, and sarcomere](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-nmj-em-labeled.jpg)

![Electron micrograph of a neuromuscular junction showing a vesicle-filled nerve terminal over the folded muscle membrane, with an asterisk in the synaptic cleft and S on Schwann cell cytoplasm](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-nmj-em-active-zones.jpg)

Key for the last EM: the asterisk marks one of two active zones in the synaptic cleft; vesicles are the 50 nm circles with gray centers; S marks fingers of Schwann cell cytoplasm between the axon and the muscle fiber.

## Identify it

- The axon terminal is crowded with round synaptic vesicles
- The muscle membrane under the terminal is deeply folded into secondary synaptic clefts that run perpendicular to the primary cleft
- Basal lamina lines the clefts
- Schwann cell cytoplasm caps the terminal
- Sarcomeres of the muscle fiber sit nearby

## Key features

- The end plate sits in a recess of the muscle surface (the sole plate) and is covered by the last Schwann cell around the axon
- The Schwann cell's external lamina merges with the muscle fiber's, and the endoneurium becomes continuous with the endomysium
- The terminal has many synaptic vesicles near its irregular presynaptic membrane, plus many mitochondria and a compartment for recycling vesicles
- The sole plate has concentrated mitochondria and aggregated muscle nuclei
- Acetylcholine is the transmitter. Its receptors are concentrated at the margins of the secondary synaptic clefts.
- Acetylcholinesterase sits deeper in the clefts with the external lamina and deactivates the transmitter between impulses

> At the NMJ, acetylcholine receptors sit at the margins of the secondary synaptic clefts, and acetylcholinesterase sits deeper in the clefts with the external lamina.

Library: Satellite cells in PNS ganglia
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Diagram of a neuron cell body wrapped by a layer of satellite cells, beside an H&E micrograph of a ganglion neuron (N) with satellite cells (S) at its edge](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-satellite-cells.jpg)

Key: S, satellite cells; N, neuron.

## Identify it

- Look for a large, round neuron cell body (N) with a nucleus and nucleolus
- Small round nuclei (S) hug the edge of the cell body, forming a ring around it
- They're seen in sensory and autonomic ganglia

## Key features

- Satellite cells and Schwann cells are the only two glial cell types in the PNS
- Satellite cells surround neuronal cell bodies in sensory and autonomic ganglia
- They give the neurons structural and metabolic support
- In sensory ganglia they form a complete layer; in autonomic ganglia the layer is less distinct or complete

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Satellite cells | Schwann cells |
|---|---|---|
| Wrap around | Neuronal cell bodies | Axons |
| Found in | Sensory and autonomic ganglia | Peripheral nerve fibers |
| Nuclei | Small and rounded, ringing the soma | Stretched, lying along the fibers |
| Job | Structural and metabolic support | Myelin; insulation and faster conduction |

> Satellite cells are the small round nuclei ringing each neuron cell body in PNS ganglia.

Library: Schwann cells in peripheral nerve
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Diagram of a neuron whose axon is covered by a chain of Schwann cells, labeling the myelin sheath, nodes of Ranvier, internode, layers of myelin, axon, Schwann cell, and Schwann cell nucleus; beside an H&E longitudinal section of nerve with S on Schwann cell nuclei and F on fibroblasts](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-schwann-cells.jpg)

Key: S, Schwann cells; F, fibroblasts.

## Identify it

- In a longitudinal H&E section, nerve fibers run in wavy, parallel bundles
- Schwann cell nuclei (S) are elongated and lie along the fibers
- Fibroblasts (F) are labeled in the same field, so not every flat nucleus in a nerve is a Schwann cell
- Most nuclei in a peripheral nerve belong to Schwann cells, then fibroblasts and endothelial cells

## Key features

- Schwann cells wrap their cell membrane around one axon many times to form the myelin sheath
- Myelin protects and insulates the axon and speeds transmission along the fiber
- Schwann cells are tubular with stretched nuclei
- A single Schwann cell myelinates only one axon, and one axon has many Schwann cells along it, one per internode
- Small gaps between myelin segments are the nodes of Ranvier
- Larger-diameter fibers have thicker myelin, and small-diameter fibers stay unmyelinated
- Schwann cells can regenerate after damage and remyelinate axons

> Each Schwann cell myelinates one internode of one axon, so an axon needs many Schwann cells along its length.

Library: Myelin sheath formation by Schwann cells
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Four-step diagram of myelination: an axon sinks into a Schwann cell, a mesaxon forms, the mesaxon wraps around the axon in layers, and the compacted layers become the myelin sheath; labels axon, Schwann cell cytoplasm, mesaxon, Schwann cell nucleus, and myelin sheath](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-myelin-formation-mesaxon.jpg)

## Key features

1. A single axon invaginates into a Schwann cell
2. A mesaxon (a double layer of Schwann cell surface membrane) forms
3. The mesaxon rotates around the axon, wrapping it in concentric layers of Schwann cell cytoplasm and membrane
4. The cytoplasm is squeezed out, the inner leaflets of the membrane fuse, and the compacted layers form the myelin sheath

## Structure

- Myelin is a multilayer stack of membrane with alternating electron-dense and electron-light layers
- The major dense line is the cytoplasmic leaflets pressed together, and the intraperiod line is the outer leaflets pressed together
- Peripheral myelin has a 12 nm period by EM (18.5 nm by X-ray diffraction and freeze fracture)
- Myelin is about 70% lipid and 30% protein, so it doesn't stain with H&E
- Segments are 150 to 1000 µm long; uncompacted inner and outer mesaxons remain in each internode
- In the PNS, axons thicker than 1 µm are myelinated
- One axon has many Schwann cells, one per internode

## Function

- Myelin insulates the axon, preventing short-circuiting between axons
- It speeds conduction through saltatory conduction

> Myelin is about 70% lipid and doesn't stain with H&E, so it shows up as white space around the axon.

Library: Myelinated nerve fibers: H&E cross section and EM
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![H&E cross section of a myelinated nerve fascicle labeling an axon (center) and its myelin sheath (white space)](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-myelinated-nerve-he-cross-section.jpg)

## Identify it on H&E

- An H&E cross section of a fascicle is packed with small round profiles
- Each axon is a small pink dot in the center of a ring of white space
- The white ring is the myelin sheath, which didn't stain because myelin is mostly lipid
- Small dark nuclei scattered among the fibers are mostly Schwann cell nuclei
- Unmyelinated fibers don't have these rings

![Myelin-stained cross section in which myelinated axons appear as dark rings around pale centers (20 µm scale bar)](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-myelinated-axons-myelin-stain.jpg)

- With a stain that keeps the myelin, the same fibers show up as dark rings around pale axons

![Electron micrographs of a myelinated nerve fiber: (b) an axon (A) inside a thick dark myelin sheath (My) next to the Schwann cell nucleus (N), 1.0 µm bar; (c) high-power view of the layered myelin with Schwann cell cytoplasm outside (C) and next to the axon (Ci), 0.5 µm bar](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-myelinated-fiber-em.jpg)

Key: A, axon; My, myelin sheath; N, nucleus of Schwann cell; C, cytoplasm of Schwann cell; Ci, cytoplasm near the axon.

## Identify it on EM

- The axon (A) is a pale round profile
- The myelin sheath (My) is a thick, dark, uniform ring around the axon
- The Schwann cell nucleus (N) is a large nucleus right beside the myelinated axon, in the same Schwann cell
- At high power, myelin is a stack of many thin, evenly spaced dark lines
- Schwann cell cytoplasm sits outside the stack (C) and as a thin rim next to the axon (Ci)

## Key features

- The alternating dark and light lines are the major dense lines (cytoplasmic leaflets together) and intraperiod lines (outer leaflets together)
- Peripheral myelin repeats every 12 nm by EM
- Each Schwann cell makes one segment of myelin around one axon
- Larger-diameter fibers have thicker myelin
- The slide states that most myelinated axons are found in the PNS

> On H&E a myelinated fiber is a central axon dot inside a clear ring of unstained myelin; under EM the same myelin is a thick dark ring of stacked membrane layers.

Library: Nodes of Ranvier and saltatory conduction
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Diagram of a myelinated fiber with an enlarged internode (axon with mitochondria, myelin sheath, Schmidt-Lanterman clefts) and an enlarged node of Ranvier (Schwann cells labeled SC, basal lamina, axon), where the axon isn't covered by myelin](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-node-of-ranvier-diagram.jpg)

![Two light micrographs of nerve in longitudinal section: (a) R marks a node of Ranvier between two myelin segments; (b) R marks a node and N marks a Schwann cell nucleus](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-nodes-of-ranvier-lm.jpg)

Key: R, node of Ranvier; N, Schwann cell nuclei.

## Identify it

- In a longitudinal section, a node is the interval between two adjacent myelin sheaths (R), where the fiber narrows
- Schwann cell nuclei (N) are elongated nuclei lying along the fibers
- The diagram also labels Schmidt-Lanterman clefts within the myelin and a basal lamina over the Schwann cells

## Key features

- Nodes are short intervals where the axon isn't covered by myelin
- They're the only places where the axon is uncovered and ions can be exchanged with the extracellular fluid
- Nodes have a high density of voltage-dependent ion channels
- Depolarization happens only at the nodes, so the impulse jumps from node to node (saltatory conduction)
- The myelin between two nodes is an internode, made by one Schwann cell
- Unmyelinated fibers have no nodes

> Nodes of Ranvier are the gaps between adjacent myelin sheaths, packed with voltage-dependent ion channels that allow saltatory conduction.

Library: Unmyelinated axons in peripheral nerve
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Diagram of one Schwann cell holding many unmyelinated axons, each in its own channel connected to the surface by a mesaxon; labels mesaxon, Schwann cell cytoplasm, Schwann cell nucleus, and axons](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-unmyelinated-axons-diagram.jpg)

![H&E cross section of a non-myelinated nerve fascicle with red outlines drawn along its connective tissue covering and around small groups of fibers](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-unmyelinated-nerve-fascicle-he.jpg)

## Identify it

- In the diagram, several axons each sink into their own channel in one Schwann cell
- In the H&E fascicle, small dark nuclei are spread through pale, finely textured tissue, with no clear myelin rings around the axons
- The slide asks you to note the connective tissue coverings around the fascicle

## Key features

- One or more axons invaginate lengthwise into a Schwann cell, each in a channel, and more than one axon may share a channel
- The Schwann cell membrane seals each axon in an extracellular compartment; the mesaxon is where the Schwann cell membrane comes together
- Each Schwann cell extends only a short distance along the nerve
- There's no myelin wrapping and no nodes of Ranvier, so conduction is continuous and slower
- There are no bare axons in the PNS; every axon has a Schwann cell sheath
- Unmyelinated fibers are mostly in the autonomic nervous system, small pain fibers, and the CNS

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Myelinated | Unmyelinated |
|---|---|---|
| Schwann cell to axon | One Schwann cell per internode of one axon | One Schwann cell holds one or more axons |
| Wrapping | Many compacted myelin layers | Schwann cell membrane only |
| Nodes of Ranvier | Present | Absent |
| Conduction | Saltatory, faster | Continuous, slower |
| H&E cross section | Axon dot in a clear ring | No clear rings |

> Unmyelinated PNS axons still sit inside Schwann cells, just without myelin wraps or nodes, so conduction is continuous and slower.

Library: Peripheral nerve connective tissue layers
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![3D diagram of a peripheral nerve labeling the axon, myelin sheath, endoneurium, perineurium, fascicles, blood vessels, and epineurium](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-peripheral-nerve-layers-diagram.jpg)

![3D diagram of a peripheral nerve labeling the epineurium, perineurium, endoneurium, fascicle, adipose tissue, vasculature (artery and vein), loose connective tissue, node of Ranvier, Schwann cell, and axon](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-peripheral-nerve-3d-diagram.jpg)

## Key features

| Layer | Surrounds | Tissue |
|---|---|---|
| Epineurium | The whole nerve, grouping its fascicles | Dense irregular CT with thick collagen and elastic fibers |
| Perineurium | Each fascicle | Slightly thinner dense CT of epithelial-like cells |
| Endoneurium | Single myelinated axons with their Schwann cells, or groups of unmyelinated axons | Thin, loose, vascular (reticular) CT |

- Perineurial cells are joined by tight junctions and have a basement membrane. They isolate the endoneurial fluid and form the blood-nerve barrier.
- Endoneurial capillaries are the continuous type with tight junctions
- Epineurium holds a variable amount of fat between fascicles (more in larger nerves) and merges with the dura mater at the nerve's proximal end
- Fascicles repeatedly unite and divide along a nerve, so axons pass from one fascicle to another
- Fascicles range from about 0.05 to 3.5 mm across. The largest nerves are 1 to 10 mm (up to 20 mm for the sciatic) with about 10 fascicles on average (range 1 to 100).
- Each fascicle usually carries motor, sensory, and sympathetic fibers
- The neuron cell bodies for a nerve's axons are in sensory ganglia, autonomic ganglia, or the spinal cord
- The layering is similar to the connective tissue coverings of skeletal muscle

> Epineurium wraps the whole nerve, perineurium wraps each fascicle (and forms the blood-nerve barrier), and endoneurium wraps individual fibers.

Library: Epineurium, perineurium, and endoneurium on slides
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Light micrograph of a peripheral nerve labeling epineurium (E), nerve fibers (N), perineurium (P), blood vessels (V and A), and a fascicle (red bracket)](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-peripheral-nerve-lm-fascicles.jpg)

Key: E, epineurium; N, nerve fibers; P, perineurium; V and A, blood vessels.

![Low-power cross section of a peripheral nerve with several fascicles; a marks the epineurium surrounding the nerve and b marks the perineurium surrounding each fascicle](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-peripheral-nerve-epineurium-perineurium.jpg)

![Schematic of a nerve with three fascicles labeling epineurium, dissolved myelin sheath, axon, Schwann cell nucleus, endoneurium, and perineurium, beside an H&E fascicle labeling epineurium, perineurium, axon, myelin, and endoneurium](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-nerve-fascicle-schematic-and-he.jpg)

![H&E peripheral nerve coverings: low-power cross section with an enlarged inset labeling epineurium, perineurium, and endoneurium](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-peripheral-nerve-coverings-he.jpg)

## Identify it

- Epineurium is the outer dense connective tissue around the whole nerve that also fills the space between fascicles, holding blood vessels and fat
- Perineurium is the thin, dense rim hugging each fascicle (P, b)
- Endoneurium is the delicate tissue inside a fascicle between individual nerve fibers
- A fascicle is a round bundle of nerve fibers (N) inside its perineurium
- On H&E the myelin is dissolved, so each myelinated fiber shows an axon in a clear space

## Look-alikes

| Layer | Where on the slide | Look |
|---|---|---|
| Epineurium | Outside and between fascicles | Thick dense CT with vessels and fat |
| Perineurium | Rim of each fascicle | Thin dense layer tight around the fascicle |
| Endoneurium | Inside the fascicle | Delicate loose CT around single fibers |

> If it wraps one fascicle it's perineurium; if it wraps the whole nerve and fills the space between fascicles it's epineurium.

Library: Peripheral nerve degeneration and regeneration
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

Peripheral nerves can repair themselves, and the repair depends on Schwann cells working with macrophages. Axons can regenerate as long as the neuronal cell body isn't damaged.

![Timeline diagram of an injured motor nerve: normal neuron and muscle with the injury site dividing proximal and distal ends; at 2 weeks chromatolysis with a peripheral nucleus, macrophages, and Wallerian degeneration of the distal fiber and myelin; at 3 weeks proliferating Schwann cells and an axonal sprout entering bands of Büngner; at 3 months successful regeneration; at several months disorganized sprouts and atrophied muscle](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-nerve-regeneration-timeline.jpg)

## Wallerian degeneration (distal segment)

- Begins within 24 to 48 hours after injury
- The axon and myelin sheath break down
- Schwann cells and macrophages clear the debris
- Schwann cells proliferate and form bands of Büngner that guide regenerating axons

## Regrowth

- Axonal sprouts grow from the proximal stump, and growth cones extend toward the distal stump guided by chemical cues
- Schwann cells dedifferentiate into a repair phenotype, secrete neurotrophic factors (NGF, BDNF, GDNF), and express adhesion molecules that promote axon elongation
- The Schwann cell basement membrane compartment is critical to PNS regeneration
- Macrophages clear debris to create a permissive environment and release cytokines and growth factors that stimulate Schwann cells

## Factors affecting regeneration

- Extent and type of injury
- Distance to the target organ (longer distances reduce success)
- Age and health of the patient
- Time delay before repair
- Accuracy of fascicular alignment during surgical repair

## Identify it with toluidine blue

Degeneration and regeneration of a peripheral nerve fiber can be identified with toluidine blue staining of myelin.

![Toluidine blue cross sections of peripheral nerve: (A) undamaged nerve with mostly myelinated fibers (red arrows); (B) damaged nerve with abnormal myelin distribution (red arrows)](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-toluidine-blue-normal-vs-damaged.jpg)

- Myelin stains dark blue, so each myelinated fiber is a dark ring around a pale axon
- In the undamaged nerve (A), fibers are mostly myelinated with regular, intact rings
- In the damaged nerve (B), the myelin distribution is abnormal, with misshapen and clumped profiles in place of regular rings

## The g-ratio

![Toluidine blue nerve at 100x with an enlarged inset showing one fiber measured across the axon (B) and across the whole fiber (A), with the g-ratio formula](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-toluidine-blue-g-ratio.jpg)

- B is the axon within the myelin covering
- A spans the myelin around the axon, measured as the whole fiber diameter
- The g-ratio is the axon diameter (B) divided by the fiber diameter (A)

> After injury, the distal axon undergoes Wallerian degeneration, and proliferating Schwann cells form bands of Büngner that guide sprouts from the proximal stump.

Library: Peripheral neuropathy: symptoms and causes
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

Peripheral neuropathy is a condition that affects and can damage the nerves outside the brain and spinal cord, leading to a range of symptoms.

## Clinical symptoms

- Numbness and tingling
- Weakness and muscle atrophy
- Pain
- Burning sensations
- Difficulty with balance and coordination

## Causes

1. Autoimmunity (inflammatory demyelinative polyradiculoneuropathies)
2. Vasculitis (connective tissue diseases)
3. Systemic illness (diabetes, uremia, sarcoidosis, myxedema, acromegaly)
4. Cancer (paraneoplastic neuropathy) and chemotherapy
5. Infections (diphtheria, leprosy, Lyme disease, AIDS, herpes zoster, COVID-19)
6. Dysproteinemia (myeloma, cryoglobulinemia)
7. Nutritional deficiencies and alcoholism
8. Compression and trauma
9. Toxic industrial agents and drugs such as chemotherapy
10. Inherited neuropathies

## Chemotherapy-induced peripheral neuropathy

Chemotherapy can directly damage peripheral nerves in the hands, feet, and other parts of the body, causing tingling, numbness, and pain. Taxanes, platinum-based drugs, and vinca alkaloids are the drugs most likely to do this, and a higher dose and longer duration raise both the risk and the severity.

> Taxanes, platinum-based drugs, and vinca alkaloids are the chemotherapy drugs most likely to cause peripheral neuropathy, with risk rising with dose and duration.

Library: Axonal vs. demyelinating neuropathy
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

Peripheral neuropathy follows two main patterns of nerve damage, and both cause weakness and sensory symptoms.

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Axonal neuropathy | Demyelinating neuropathy |
|---|---|---|
| Main damage | The axon degenerates | Schwann cells and myelin |
| Myelin | Occasional secondary demyelination | Lost over a few segments (segmental demyelination) |
| Axon and cell body | Axon lost | Axon intact, cell body unchanged |
| Symptoms | Sensory loss and weakness | Weakness, pain, fatigue, numbness |
| Causes named | Trauma, nerve infarction, tumor infiltration, drugs, industrial poisons and solvents | Inflammation of nerves and nerve roots |

## Key features

- In demyelinating neuropathy, loss of saltatory conduction slows conduction velocity and can cause conduction block
- Demyelinating deficits develop rapidly but are reversible because Schwann cells make new myelin
- In many cases, though, demyelination leads to loss of axons and permanent deficits
- Most peripheral neuropathies have no specific pathologic changes
- Any active neuropathy shows macrophages removing myelin and axon debris
- Advanced axonal neuropathy may show loss of myelinated axons and increased endoneurial collagen
- A sural nerve biopsy can confirm neuropathy and separate axonal from demyelinating and acute from chronic disease, but in most cases it can't identify the cause

> Demyelinating neuropathy strips myelin over a few segments but leaves the axon intact (slow conduction, often reversible); axonal neuropathy destroys the axon itself.

Library: Wallerian degeneration histology
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

Wallerian degeneration is the fragmentation and loss of distal myelin and axons after axons are cut. The lecture uses it as the picture of axonal neuropathy.

![Two micrographs of Wallerian degeneration: a blue-stained cross section of a nerve fascicle with disrupted fibers, and a longitudinal section with a red oval around a line of elongated Schwann cell nuclei](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-wallerian-degeneration.jpg)

## Identify it

1. Axons look fragmented and irregular instead of continuous and linear, sometimes as small remnants
2. Myelin breaks down into myelin ovoids, seen as clear vacuolated spaces and pale ovoid structures
3. Numerous elongated nuclei of proliferating Schwann cells line up along the degenerated fibers, forming bands of Büngner (red oval)
4. Many dark, irregular nuclei belong to infiltrating macrophages
5. The orderly pattern of axons and myelin is lost, and the endoneurium looks looser and more vacuolated

## Key features

- Myelin ovoids are a hallmark of demyelination after axonal injury
- Bands of Büngner guide axonal regeneration
- Macrophages phagocytose myelin and axon debris, and their presence means the active cleanup phase is under way

> Wallerian degeneration shows myelin ovoids (clear vacuoles), lines of elongated Schwann cell nuclei (bands of Büngner), and dark macrophage nuclei.

Library: Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

## Key features

- Also called hereditary motor and sensory neuropathy (HMSN)
- A group of inherited peripheral neuropathies that mainly affect the myelin sheath or the axon of peripheral nerves
- Causes defective myelin formation or axonal degeneration, which impairs nerve conduction and leads to progressive weakness and sensory loss
- Most often autosomal dominant, but autosomal recessive and X-linked forms also occur
- One of the most common inherited neurological disorders, about 1 in 2,500 people
- Onset is usually in childhood or adolescence, and severity and progression vary widely

## Clinical features

- Progressive distal muscle weakness and atrophy, especially in the lower limbs ("stork leg" or "inverted champagne bottle" appearance)
- Foot drop from weak dorsiflexors
- Pes cavus (high-arched foot) and hammer toes
- Later involvement of the hand intrinsic muscles, with trouble doing fine motor tasks
- Decreased vibration, touch, and proprioception in a stocking-glove distribution, and possible sensory ataxia
- Absent or diminished deep tendon reflexes, especially at the ankles
- Scoliosis, tremor (CMT1A), or mild hearing loss in some subtypes

> Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease is an inherited (usually autosomal dominant) neuropathy with distal "inverted champagne bottle" legs, foot drop, pes cavus, and absent ankle reflexes.

Library: Guillain-Barré syndrome
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS) is a non-hereditary peripheral neuropathy.

## Key features

- Affects males and females at any age, at about 1 in 100,000
- The cause is unknown, but most cases follow a microbial infection (viral or bacterial), flu, or food poisoning
- An autoimmune attack on peripheral nerve myelin causes rapid segmental demyelination
- This leads to ascending muscle weakness and paralysis, and respiratory failure is possible
- About 70% of patients recover fully after 6 months to 2 years, while 30% report residual weakness after three years
- Relapses are rare
- The lecture's infographic calls it the most common cause of acute flaccid paralysis in the US
- Symptoms include limb weakness, difficulty swallowing, and shortness of breath
- Diagnosis is by clinical assessment, blood tests, and electromyography
- Treatment includes immunotherapy and plasmapheresis

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Charcot-Marie-Tooth | Guillain-Barré |
|---|---|---|
| Cause | Inherited | Autoimmune, usually after an infection |
| Target | Myelin or axon | Peripheral nerve myelin |
| Course | Progressive, onset in childhood or adolescence | Rapid and ascending; most recover |
| Frequency | About 1 in 2,500 | About 1 in 100,000 |

> GBS is an autoimmune attack on peripheral myelin, usually after an infection, causing rapid ascending weakness and paralysis; most patients recover.

Library: Spinal cord histology: gray and white matter
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

The spinal cord's structure is similar along its length. In cross section, a central mass of gray matter shaped like a butterfly sits inside the white matter.

![Blue-stained spinal cord cross section labeling the central mass of grey matter, posterior horn (cell bodies of sensory neurons for pain and temperature), anterior horn (cell bodies of motor neurons), lateral horns (thoracic and upper lumbar segments, cell bodies of sympathetic neurons), and the deep ventral fissure](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-spinal-cord-horns-labeled.jpg)

![H&E spinal cord cross section labeling the posterior horn (gray matter), anterior horn (gray matter), the circled central canal lined by ependymal cells, and the dura mater, with an inset of large anterior horn neurons](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-spinal-cord-he-central-canal.jpg)

![H&E spinal cord cross section labeling posterior horns, anterior horns, grey matter, white matter, central canal, pia mater, arachnoid, dura mater, and blood vessels](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-spinal-cord-meninges-labeled.jpg)

## Identify it

- Gray matter is the central butterfly; on the H&E sections it's the darker, more cellular region
- On the blue-stained section, white matter stains dark and the gray matter stays pale
- Anterior horns are the prominent horns on the same side as the deep ventral fissure
- Posterior horns reach toward the back of the cord
- Lateral horns are small side projections, present in thoracic and upper lumbar segments
- The central canal is the small opening in the middle of the gray matter
- Pia mater, arachnoid, and dura mater surround the cord

## Key features

- Anterior horns hold the cell bodies of the large alpha lower motor neurons
- Posterior horns hold small second-order sensory neurons that relay pain and temperature upward and take part in spinal reflexes
- Lateral horns hold the cell bodies of sympathetic efferent neurons
- The central canal lies in the central commissure of gray matter, is lined by ependymal cells, and contains CSF
- White matter consists of ascending sensory tracts and descending motor tracts

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Where it's greatest |
|---|---|
| Gray matter volume and cord size | Cervical and lumbar regions (limb innervation) |
| Lateral horns | Thoracic and upper lumbar segments only |
| White matter volume | Increases steadily from sacral to cervical |

> Anterior horn is motor, posterior horn is pain and temperature sensory, and the lateral horn (thoracic and upper lumbar) is sympathetic.

Library: Spinal cord histology at high power: tracts and motor neurons
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![H&E spinal cord field with large anterior horn neurons and a boxed area of white matter enlarged to show round, empty myelin sheaths around axons](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-spinal-cord-white-matter-myelin.jpg)

![H&E high-power view (panel d) of spinal cord white matter near the white commissure, with one band of fibers outlined](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-spinal-cord-white-commissure.jpg)

![H&E anterior horn gray matter (panel c) with white arrows on large multipolar motor neurons and red arrows on glial cells](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-anterior-horn-motor-neurons.jpg)

## Identify it

- White matter cut in cross section looks like a field of round, empty myelin sheaths, each around a small axon
- The white commissure lies ventral to the central canal. There, tracts running lengthwise along the cord are cut in cross section, and other tracts run from one side of the cord to the other.
- Anterior horn motor neurons (white arrows) are large multipolar cells with large nuclei, prominent nucleoli, and cytoplasm rich in Nissl bodies
- Glial cells (red arrows) are the small nuclei scattered between the neurons
- The fibrous pink background between cell bodies is neuropil

## Look-alikes

| Feature | White matter | Gray matter |
|---|---|---|
| Contents | Ascending sensory and descending motor tracts | Neuronal cell bodies, glia, and neuropil |
| H&E look | Empty myelin rings around axons | Large Nissl-rich neurons among small glial nuclei |

## Key features

- Gray matter holds large neuronal cell bodies in the anterior horns
- A collection of neuronal cell bodies in the CNS is typically called a nucleus
- Myelin doesn't stain with H&E, which is why cut tracts look empty

> Anterior horn motor neurons are large multipolar cells with big nuclei, prominent nucleoli, and Nissl-rich cytoplasm, while white matter tracts look like fields of empty myelin rings.

Library: Glial cell functions in the spinal cord and PNS
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Diagram of CNS glial cells around neurons labeling a microglial cell, an astrocyte, ependymal cells lining a surface, and oligodendrocytes whose processes myelinate several axons](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-cns-glial-cells-diagram.jpg)

The learning objectives ask for the functions of the glial cells in the spinal cord (CNS) and in the PNS. The CNS has four types, and the PNS has only two.

## Key features

| Glial cell | Where | Description | Function |
|---|---|---|---|
| Oligodendrocytes | CNS | Each one myelinates multiple axons | Form myelin in the CNS |
| Astrocytes | CNS | Most abundant cells in the brain | Important part of the blood-brain barrier |
| Microglia | CNS | Relatively small, sparse cells | Resident macrophages that phagocytose foreign or damaged material, cells, and organisms |
| Ependymal cells | CNS | Simple cuboidal or columnar epithelium lining the ventricles and spinal canal | Help regulate CSF contents |
| Schwann cells | PNS | Tubular cells with stretched nuclei along axons | Myelinate one axon each; sheath unmyelinated axons |
| Satellite cells | PNS | Small rounded nuclei ringing neuron cell bodies in ganglia | Structural and metabolic support of neurons |

## Identify it

- In the H&E cord sections, ependymal cells line the central canal
- Glial cells in cord gray matter are the small nuclei scattered between the large motor neurons
- In the diagram, astrocytes send many processes to neurons, microglia are small and bushy, and oligodendrocyte processes wrap segments of several axons

## Look-alikes

| Feature | CNS | PNS |
|---|---|---|
| Myelinating cell | Oligodendrocyte, multiple axons | Schwann cell, one axon |
| Other glia | Astrocytes, microglia, ependymal cells | Satellite cells |

> In the CNS one oligodendrocyte myelinates multiple axons, while in the PNS each Schwann cell myelinates a single axon.

Library: PNS ganglia: definition and types
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![H&E section of a spinal ganglion: large round neuron cell bodies in groups, a fascicle of nerve fibers (Fa) running through the ganglion, and a connective tissue capsule with surrounding fat](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-spinal-ganglion-fascicle.jpg)

## Identify it

- A ganglion is a cluster of large, round neuron cell bodies (ganglion cells) outside the CNS, enclosed in a connective tissue capsule
- Fascicles of nerve fibers (Fa) pass through the ganglion between groups of cell bodies
- The spinal ganglion shown sits on the posterior nerve roots of the spinal cord and holds the cell bodies of primary sensory neurons

## Key features

- Definition: aggregations of nerve cell bodies (ganglion cells) with glial cells outside the CNS, in the PNS
- Ganglia are encapsulated collections of neuronal cell bodies
- Function: relay stations that transmit impulses
- Cells inside: neurons, satellite cells, and Schwann cells
- A ganglion is the PNS counterpart of a CNS nucleus

## Types

- Sensory ganglia, which hold sensory neurons: spinal (dorsal root) ganglia
- Autonomic ganglia: sympathetic ganglia and parasympathetic ganglia, including myenteric ganglia in the gut wall

> A ganglion is an encapsulated collection of neuron cell bodies outside the CNS; sensory ganglia are the spinal (dorsal root) ganglia, and autonomic ganglia are sympathetic or parasympathetic.

Library: Dorsal root ganglion histology
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![Low-power H&E of a dorsal root (spinal) ganglion labeling the ganglion, the cell bodies of sensory neurons, and the axon bundles (nerve) beside it, with a boxed area of cell bodies](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-dorsal-root-ganglion-low-power.jpg)

![High-power H&E of a dorsal root ganglion: large round neurons, each surrounded by a ring of small satellite cell nuclei; arrows on the slide aren't defined](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-dorsal-root-ganglion-high-power.jpg)

![H&E of a dorsal root ganglion labeling a ganglion cell, its nucleus and nucleolus, satellite cells, and a capillary](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-dorsal-root-ganglion-labeled.jpg)

## Identify it

- At low power, an oval mass of neuron cell bodies sits beside axon bundles on their way to join the dorsal root
- The pseudo-unipolar neurons are large, round, and tightly packed
- Each ganglion cell has a round nucleus with a visible nucleolus
- A complete layer of satellite cells (small dark nuclei) rings each cell body
- Capillaries run between the neurons
- The associated nerve fibers are both myelinated and unmyelinated, with myelinated axons predominating

## Key features

- The dorsal root ganglion holds the cell bodies of sensory neurons, which are pseudo-unipolar
- Satellite (capsule) cells separate each cell body from the connective tissue framework
- Because the sensory neuron is unipolar, there are no synapses in a sensory ganglion
- Cell bodies are pushed to the periphery of the ganglion
- The ganglion sits on the posterior nerve roots of the spinal cord

> Dorsal root ganglion: large, tightly packed, round pseudo-unipolar neurons, each ringed by a complete layer of satellite cells, with no synapses.

Library: Lipofuscin and satellite cells in sensory ganglia
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![H&E sensory ganglion (panel b) with S on satellite cells around a neuron and L on brown lipofuscin granules in a neuron's cytoplasm, with nerve fibers in the lower half](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-sensory-ganglion-satellite-lipofuscin.jpg)

Key: S, satellite cells; L, lipofuscin granules.

## Identify it

- Satellite cells (S) have small rounded nuclei and form thin, sheet-like cytoplasmic extensions around the neurons
- Lipofuscin (L) is a brownish granular pigment in the neuron cytoplasm
- The adjoining peripheral nerve fibers (axons, dendrites, and Schwann cell nuclei) run alongside the ganglion as the wavy tissue with elongated nuclei

## Key features

- Lipofuscin builds up with age; it's cellular debris sequestered in residual bodies
- It's a fluorescent pigment formed by lipid peroxidation that accumulates in the lysosomal compartment of postmitotic cells, including neurons and heart cells
- Lipofuscin is inversely correlated with lifespan
- Large accumulations show up in degenerative diseases caused by defects in lysosomal function, such as ceroid lipofuscinosis
- Lipofuscin also appears in the lecture's autonomic and sympathetic ganglion cells

> Lipofuscin is the brown, age-related pigment (debris in lysosomal residual bodies) in ganglion cell cytoplasm.

Library: Autonomic and sympathetic ganglion histology
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![H&E autonomic ganglion labeling ganglion cells, satellite cells, and lipofuscin granules](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-autonomic-ganglion.jpg)

## Identify it

- Ganglion cells are large multipolar neurons that aren't tightly packed
- Satellite cells form one flattened layer around each neuron, less distinct or complete than in sensory ganglia
- Lipofuscin granules show as brown pigment in some ganglion cells

## Key features

- Autonomic ganglia can be sympathetic or parasympathetic
- The autonomic system (smooth muscle, cardiac muscle, and glands) uses two motor neurons
- The preganglionic neuron's cell body is in the brain or the lateral horn of spinal cord gray matter, and its lightly myelinated axon synapses on a multipolar postganglionic neuron inside the ganglion
- The postganglionic axon is unmyelinated and runs to the target cell
- Parasympathetic ganglia belong to the craniosacral division and sympathetic ganglia to the thoracolumbar division

![H&E sympathetic ganglion labeling ganglion cells (multipolar, widely spaced, eccentrically located nuclei), lipofuscin, and satellite cells (smaller in number and irregularly placed)](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-sympathetic-ganglion.jpg)

## Identify a sympathetic ganglion

- Ganglion cells are multipolar and widely spaced, with eccentrically located nuclei
- Lipofuscin shows as brown pigment in the ganglion cell cytoplasm
- Satellite cells are smaller in number and irregularly placed around the ganglion cells
- Sympathetic ganglia lie adjacent to the vertebral column

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Sensory (dorsal root) ganglion | Autonomic ganglion |
|---|---|---|
| Neurons | Pseudo-unipolar | Multipolar |
| Packing | Tightly packed | Not tightly packed, widely spaced |
| Satellite cells | Complete layer | One flattened layer, less distinct; fewer and irregular in sympathetic ganglia |
| Synapses | None | Present |
| Fibers | Mostly myelinated | Preganglionic lightly myelinated, postganglionic unmyelinated |

> For histology, autonomic ganglia have multipolar neurons and synapses, while sensory ganglia have pseudo-unipolar neurons and no synapses.

Library: Myenteric plexus ganglion histology
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System and Spinal Cord Histology

![H&E section of the gut wall with small ganglia marked PG, labeling ganglion cells (large nuclei, dispersed chromatin, prominent nucleoli) and satellite cells](library/images/msk-week-13/histo-myenteric-plexus-ganglion.jpg)

## Identify it

- Small clusters of ganglion cells sit scattered in the supporting tissue of the GI tract wall, usually between different muscle layers
- Ganglion cells have large nuclei, dispersed chromatin, and prominent nucleoli
- Satellite cells are the small nuclei next to the ganglion cells
- The slide marks the ganglia with PG

## Key features

- The myenteric (Auerbach's) plexus ganglia are parasympathetic ganglia
- The learning objectives also group them with intramural ganglia
- Like other autonomic ganglia, they contain multipolar neurons and synapses

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Myenteric (parasympathetic) | Sympathetic | Dorsal root (sensory) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Location | GI wall, between muscle layers | Next to the vertebral column | Posterior nerve roots |
| Ganglion cells | Large nuclei, dispersed chromatin, prominent nucleoli | Multipolar, widely spaced, eccentric nuclei | Pseudo-unipolar, tightly packed |
| Satellite cells | Present | Fewer, irregular | Complete layer |
| Synapses | Present | Present | None |

> Myenteric (Auerbach's) plexus ganglia are small parasympathetic ganglia scattered in the GI wall between muscle layers.

Library: Somatic vs. autonomic efferent pathways
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Anterior view diagram of the spinal cord (CNS) and peripheral nerves (PNS): a pseudounipolar sensory neuron enters through the posterior root and spinal (posterior root) ganglion from sensory receptors in the hand; a single somatic multipolar motor neuron (red) leaves through the anterior root to skeletal (striated) muscle; autonomic multipolar motor neurons (blue) leave through the anterior root as a presynaptic neuron, synapse within an autonomic ganglion, and continue as a postsynaptic neuron to glands, modified cardiac muscle, and smooth (involuntary) muscle](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-somatic-vs-autonomic-efferent-pathways.jpg)

## Where the ANS fits

The nervous system has a central part (brain and spinal cord) and a peripheral part (cranial nerves, spinal nerves, and ganglia). The PNS splits into a sensory (afferent) division and a motor (efferent) division, and each of those has somatic and visceral parts. The visceral motor part is the autonomic nervous system (ANS), which has three divisions: the enteric nervous system, the sympathetic division, and the parasympathetic division.

- Afferent = toward the CNS; efferent = away from the CNS
- Somatic = body wall and limbs; visceral = organs/viscera

## Identify it

- Somatic motor pathway: one red neuron runs from the cord all the way to skeletal muscle with no stop in between
- Autonomic motor pathway: a dashed blue presynaptic (pre-ganglionic) neuron stops in a ganglion, and a solid blue postsynaptic (post-ganglionic) neuron carries on to glands, cardiac muscle, and smooth muscle
- Both motor pathways leave through the anterior roots of the spinal nerve, while the sensory neuron enters through the posterior root

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Somatic efferent | Visceral efferent (ANS) |
|---|---|---|
| Targets | Skeletal muscle only | Smooth muscle, cardiac muscle, glands |
| Motor neurons | 1 | 2 (pre-ganglionic and post-ganglionic) |
| Where they arise | Every spinal cord level | Brainstem and specific spinal cord levels |

> Somatic efferents use one motor neuron to reach skeletal muscle; autonomic efferents use two (pre-ganglionic and post-ganglionic) to reach smooth muscle, cardiac muscle, and glands.

Library: Autonomic ganglia: paravertebral, prevertebral, intrinsic
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Three panels of autonomic ganglia: paravertebral ganglia as the beaded yellow sympathetic chain along both sides of the vertebral column from skull base to coccyx; prevertebral ganglia (arrows) on the anterolateral surface of the abdominal aorta; intrinsic ganglia as nerve networks within the wall of the intestine](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-autonomic-ganglia-paravertebral-prevertebral-intrinsic.jpg)

## Identify it

- Paravertebral: a beaded chain running down each side of the vertebral column, from the base of the skull to the coccyx
- Prevertebral: pale ganglia sitting on the front and sides of the abdominal aorta where its branches leave (arrows in the middle panel)
- Intrinsic: nerve networks inside the wall of the target organ itself (gut wall in the right panel)

## Key features

| Type | Other names | Location |
|---|---|---|
| Paravertebral | Sympathetic chain or trunk | Along either side of the vertebral column, skull base to coccyx |
| Prevertebral | Preaortic or collateral | Anterolateral surface of the abdominal aorta |
| Intrinsic | Terminal, intramural, enteric | Within the wall of, or adjacent to, the target organ |

## Which division uses them

- Sympathetic post-ganglionic neurons sit in paravertebral ganglia (the sympathetic trunk) and prevertebral ganglia
- Parasympathetic post-ganglionic neurons sit in the ganglia of the head and neck (ciliary, otic, pterygopalatine, submandibular) and in intrinsic ganglia, which the lecture's divisions figure labels "parasympathetic ganglia in or near end organs"

> Paravertebral ganglia form the sympathetic trunk beside the vertebral column, prevertebral ganglia sit on the abdominal aorta, and intrinsic ganglia sit in or next to the target organ.

Library: Sympathetic vs. parasympathetic effects by organ
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Divisions of the autonomic nervous system: sympathetic outflow on the left from cord segments T1 to L3 to the sympathetic trunk (paravertebral ganglia, with superior cervical and inferior cervical (stellate) ganglia labeled) and to celiac, superior mesenteric, and inferior mesenteric ganglia (prevertebral ganglia); parasympathetic outflow on the right from the oculomotor (CN III), facial (CN VII), glossopharyngeal (CN IX), and vagus (CN X) nerves and from S2 to S4 to ganglia in or near end organs; each organ is labeled with its sympathetic and parasympathetic effect; key shows blue = acetylcholine and red = norepinephrine](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-ans-divisions-sympathetic-parasympathetic-targets.jpg)

## Identify it

- Sympathetic side (left): the shaded cord segments run from T1 to L3, with fibers to the sympathetic trunk and to the celiac, superior mesenteric, and inferior mesenteric ganglia
- Parasympathetic side (right): outflow comes from the brainstem in CN III, VII, IX, and X and from sacral segments S2 to S4
- Key: blue = acetylcholine, red = norepinephrine

## Effects on organs with both divisions

| Target | Sympathetic | Parasympathetic |
|---|---|---|
| Pupil | Dilates | Constricts |
| Salivation and lacrimation | Inhibits | Stimulates |
| Airways | Dilates | Constricts |
| Heartbeat | Accelerates | Slows |
| Digestion (stomach) | Inhibits | Stimulates |
| Blood vessels in intestines | Constricts | Dilates |
| Urinary bladder | Relaxes | Stimulates contraction |
| Genitalia | Stimulates ejaculation | Stimulates penile erection |

The parasympathetic side also stimulates the gall bladder to release bile.

## Sympathetic-only targets in the figure

- Sweat glands: stimulates secretion
- Hair follicle: piloerection
- Liver: stimulates glucose production and release
- Systemic blood vessels: constricts
- Adrenal: stimulates secretion of adrenaline and noradrenaline

## Neurotransmitter colors in the figure

- Pre-ganglionic fibers of both divisions are drawn blue (acetylcholine)
- Parasympathetic post-ganglionic fibers are blue
- Sympathetic post-ganglionic fibers are red (norepinephrine), except the fiber to the sweat glands, which is blue
- The adrenal gets a blue fiber straight from the cord with no ganglion drawn on the way

> The sympathetic division dilates the pupil and airways and speeds the heart, while the parasympathetic division constricts the pupil and airways, slows the heart, and stimulates digestion.

Library: Sympathetic division: thoracolumbar outflow
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

## Key features

- Thoracolumbar division
- Pre-ganglionic sympathetic neurons: intermediolateral cell column (IMLCC), T1 to L2/3
- Post-ganglionic sympathetic neurons: paravertebral ganglia (the sympathetic trunk) and prevertebral ganglia
- Function: the "fight or flight" response

## Identify it: pre-ganglionic cell bodies

![Pre-ganglionic sympathetic neurons: a stained spinal cord cross section and a 3D cord drawing with arrows on the lateral horn; below, a diagram of the intermediolateral cell columns (IMLs) running through the lateral horns between the posterior and anterior horns, spanning cord levels T1 to L2(3), with the column divided into upper limb T1-6, body wall T7-11, and lower limb T11-L2(3)](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-imlcc-lateral-horn-t1-l2.jpg)

- In the stained section and the 3D cord, the arrows point to the small lateral horn of gray matter, sitting between the posterior and anterior horns
- The diagram shows the intermediolateral cell columns as two rods running through the lateral horns
- The columns span T1 to L2(3), and the level column at right groups them into upper limb (T1-6), body wall (T7-11), and lower limb (T11-L2(3))

## Identify it: post-ganglionic cell bodies

![Post-ganglionic sympathetic neurons: cadaver dissection of the posterior thoracic wall with purple arrows on the sympathetic trunk running vertically beside the vertebral column; anterior view drawing of the sympathetic trunks along both sides of the whole vertebral column, with an oval around the nerve plexus and ganglia on the abdominal aorta](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-sympathetic-trunk-cadaver-and-prevertebral-plexus.jpg)

- In the dissection, the arrows follow the sympathetic trunk as a thin, beaded cord running vertically beside the thoracic vertebral column
- In the anterior view drawing, the yellow trunks run down both sides of the vertebral column (paravertebral ganglia), and the oval surrounds the network on the abdominal aorta (prevertebral ganglia)

> Sympathetic pre-ganglionic neurons sit in the intermediolateral cell column (lateral horn) from T1 to L2/3, which is why the sympathetic division is called thoracolumbar.

Library: Somatotopic organization of the IMLCC
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Somatotopic map of the intermediolateral cell column from T1 to L2(3): a brain and cord drawing with the T1-L2(3) segment boxed, a cord slice with the intermediolateral columns as gray rods, and a level column listing parietal structures (upper limb T1-6, body wall T7-11, lower limb T11-L2(3)) and visceral structures (eye T1-2; head, salivary glands T1-3; heart, lungs, esophagus T4-6; stomach, liver, gallbladder, pancreas T7-9; small intestine T8-10; appendix, colon T10-11; kidney T11-L1; suprarenal gland T10-L1; rectum, bladder, uterus T12-L2(3))](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-imlcc-somatotopic-map.jpg)

The pre-ganglionic sympathetic neurons in the IMLCC are arranged by level, so each target gets its sympathetic supply from a predictable band of cord segments. The map lists parietal structures on the left of the level column and visceral structures on the right.

## Parietal structures

| Region | IMLCC levels |
|---|---|
| Upper limb | T1-6 |
| Body wall | T7-11 |
| Lower limb | T11-L2(3) |

## Visceral structures

| Structure | IMLCC levels |
|---|---|
| Eye | T1-2 |
| Head, salivary glands | T1-3 |
| Heart, lungs, esophagus | T4-6 |
| Stomach, liver, gallbladder, pancreas | T7-9 |
| Small intestine | T8-10 |
| Appendix, colon | T10-11 |
| Suprarenal gland | T10-L1 |
| Kidney | T11-L1 |
| Rectum, bladder, uterus | T12-L2(3) |

## The three big bands

- T1-6: head, upper limb, thoracic viscera (labeled foregut on the map)
- T7-11: body wall, abdominal viscera (labeled midgut)
- T12-L2(3): lower limb, pelvic viscera (labeled hindgut)

> Upper limb is T1-6, body wall T7-11, and lower limb T11-L2(3); heart, lungs, and esophagus get sympathetic supply from T4-6.

Library: White vs. gray rami communicantes
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Spinal cord with dorsal rootlets, ventral rootlets, spinal nerve, dorsal ramus, and ventral ramus joined to a sympathetic trunk ganglion; the enlarged inset labels the gray ramus communicans (upper) and the white ramus communicans (lower) running between the ventral ramus and the ganglion](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-white-and-gray-rami-communicantes.jpg)

## Identify it

- Rami communicantes are the short branches running between the ventral ramus and a ganglion of the sympathetic trunk (boxed and enlarged in the inset)
- Two are labeled in the inset: the gray ramus communicans and the white ramus communicans
- Other labels on the figure: dorsal rootlets, ventral rootlets, spinal nerve, dorsal ramus, ventral ramus

![Cadaver dissection of the posterior thoracic wall with a black box around a segment where rami communicantes join the sympathetic trunk](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-rami-communicantes-cadaver.jpg)

- In the dissection, the box marks a spot where short rami communicantes join the sympathetic trunk

## Key features

Rami communicantes are "communicating branches" that connect ventral rami to the paravertebral ganglia (sympathetic trunk).

| Feature | White ramus communicans | Gray ramus communicans |
|---|---|---|
| Carries | Pre-ganglionic sympathetic axons | Post-ganglionic sympathetic axons |
| Nickname | "On-ramp" | "Off-ramp" |
| Levels | T1-L2/3 only | All levels |

White rami exist only where pre-ganglionic neurons live (the IMLCC, T1-L2/3). Gray rami return post-ganglionic axons to spinal nerves at every level, so every spinal nerve can carry sympathetic fibers to its territory.

> White rami carry pre-ganglionic axons into the trunk (on-ramp) only at T1-L2/3; gray rami carry post-ganglionic axons out of the trunk (off-ramp) at all levels.

Library: Sympathetic pre-ganglionic route to the trunk
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Spinal cord and spinal nerve with a sympathetic trunk ganglion: a green pre-ganglionic sympathetic neuron starts in the lateral horn, leaves through the ventral rootlets, runs in the spinal nerve and ventral ramus, and enters the ganglion through the white ramus to synapse on a purple post-ganglionic sympathetic neuron, which returns to the ventral ramus through the gray ramus; labels for dorsal rootlets, ventral rootlets, spinal nerve, dorsal ramus, ventral ramus](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-rami-preganglionic-postganglionic-route.jpg)

## Key features

All pre-ganglionic sympathetic axons follow the same course until they reach the sympathetic trunk:

1. Cell body in the IMLCC, T1-L2/3
2. Ventral rootlets/root
3. Spinal nerve
4. Ventral ramus
5. White ramus communicans

## Identify it

- Green (pre-ganglionic) starts in the lateral horn and turns off the ventral ramus into the ganglion through the white ramus
- Purple (post-ganglionic) starts in the trunk ganglion and rejoins the ventral ramus through the gray ramus

![Sympathetic pathway diagram with a key of sensory (black) and motor (red): motor fibers leave the lateral horn and run into the sympathetic trunk, where they ascend, descend, or pass through toward a ganglion near the gut; black sensory fibers run from the gut wall back through the ganglia to the dorsal root ganglion and dorsal horn](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-sympathetic-pathway-trunk-and-gut.jpg)

- In this figure red is motor and black is sensory; the red fibers show the different directions pre-ganglionic axons can take once inside the trunk

## Four possible courses in the sympathetic trunk

1. Ascend and then synapse
2. Synapse at level of exit
3. Descend and then synapse
4. Pass through the sympathetic trunk without synapsing

> Every sympathetic pre-ganglionic axon reaches the trunk the same way (IMLCC, ventral root, spinal nerve, ventral ramus, white ramus communicans), and only then picks one of four courses.

Library: Sympathetic routes to targets: courses 1 to 3
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

In courses 1 to 3 the pre-ganglionic fiber synapses in the sympathetic trunk, and the post-ganglionic fibers exit at the level of synapse. The lecture's course figure (anterolateral view) draws presynaptic fibers as dashed lines in a different color for each course and postsynaptic fibers as solid blue lines, and each slide highlights one course.

## Course 1: ascend, then synapse

![Course 1 on the sympathetic course figure: a green dashed pre-ganglionic fiber leaves the upper thoracic cord, enters the trunk, and ascends above T1 to synapse in the superior cervical ganglion; solid blue post-ganglionic fibers reach the head (e.g., dilator muscle of iris) via a cephalic arterial branch and peri-arterial plexus; other labels show body wall, thoracic viscera via cardiopulmonary splanchnic nerves, lower limb, and abdominopelvic viscera](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-sympathetic-course-1-ascend.jpg)

- Pre-ganglionic fibers ascend to a level above T1
- Head: synapse in the superior cervical ganglion, and post-ganglionic fibers follow the blood supply to the target organ
- Cervical cardiopulmonary splanchnic nerves
- Spinal nerves to the neck, upper trunk, and upper limb: exit via gray rami communicantes, then enter spinal nerves and their ventral and dorsal rami

## Course 2: synapse at the level of exit

![Course 2 on the sympathetic course figure: a pink dashed pre-ganglionic fiber enters the trunk and synapses at the same level; solid blue post-ganglionic fibers go to the body wall via branches of spinal nerves (vasomotion, sudomotion, and pilomotion) and to the heart via cardiopulmonary splanchnic nerves](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-sympathetic-course-2-level-of-exit.jpg)

- Thoracic cardiopulmonary splanchnic nerves (figure: viscera of the thoracic cavity, such as the heart)
- Spinal nerves to the middle trunk: exit via gray rami, then enter spinal nerves and ventral and dorsal rami (figure: body wall, for vasomotion, sudomotion, and pilomotion)

## Course 3: descend, then synapse

![Course 3 on the sympathetic course figure: a light blue dashed pre-ganglionic fiber enters the trunk and descends to a low ganglion to synapse; solid blue post-ganglionic fibers go to the lower limb via branches of spinal nerves (vasomotion, sudomotion, and pilomotion)](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-sympathetic-course-3-descend.jpg)

- Pre-ganglionic fibers descend to a level below L2
- Spinal nerves to the lower trunk and lower limb: exit via gray rami, then enter spinal nerves and ventral and dorsal rami

> Courses 1 to 3 all synapse in the sympathetic trunk: ascend for the head, neck, and upper limb, synapse at the level of exit for the middle trunk and thoracic viscera, or descend for the lower trunk and lower limb.

Library: Abdominopelvic splanchnic nerves and prevertebral ganglia
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

## Course 4: pass through without synapsing

![Course 4 on the sympathetic course figure: a dark red dashed pre-ganglionic fiber leaves the lower thoracic cord, passes through the sympathetic trunk without synapsing, and synapses in a ganglion in front of the vertebral column; solid blue post-ganglionic fibers reach the viscera of the abdominopelvic cavity (e.g., stomach and intestines) via abdominopelvic splanchnic nerves](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-sympathetic-course-4-splanchnic.jpg)

- Pre-ganglionic fibers pass through the sympathetic trunk without synapsing to enter an abdominopelvic splanchnic nerve
- Pre-ganglionic fibers synapse in prevertebral ganglia
- Post-ganglionic fibers follow the arterial supply of the viscera
- Target: abdominopelvic viscera (figure: stomach and intestines)

## Identify it: thoracic splanchnic nerves

![Cadaver dissection of the posterior thoracic wall with two leader lines labeling the thoracic splanchnic nerves in the lower thoracic region beside the vertebral column](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-thoracic-splanchnic-nerves-cadaver.jpg)

- This is the same dissection used to show the sympathetic trunk; the labels point to the thoracic splanchnic nerves in its lower thoracic part, beside the vertebral bodies

## Identify it: prevertebral ganglia

![Anterior view of the abdominal aorta and its branches with arrows to pale prevertebral ganglia on its surface, listed as celiac ganglia, superior mesenteric ganglion, aorticorenal ganglia, and inferior mesenteric ganglion](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-prevertebral-ganglia-aorta.jpg)

- Pale ganglia sit on the front of the abdominal aorta where its branches arise, tied together by a nerve plexus

## Named prevertebral ganglia

- Celiac ganglia
- Superior mesenteric ganglion
- Aorticorenal ganglia
- Inferior mesenteric ganglion

> In course 4, pre-ganglionic fibers pass through the trunk without synapsing, travel in abdominopelvic splanchnic nerves, synapse in prevertebral ganglia, and the post-ganglionic fibers follow arteries to the abdominopelvic viscera.

Library: Parasympathetic division: craniosacral outflow
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Brainstem and spinal cord showing parasympathetic outflow in two boxes: cranial outflow in the oculomotor (CN III), facial (CN VII), glossopharyngeal (CN IX), and vagus (X) nerves, and sacral outflow from S2, S3, and S4; each pathway ends in parasympathetic ganglia in or near end organs, with acetylcholine labeled at both the pre-ganglionic and post-ganglionic endings; cervical, thoracic, and lumbar levels have no outflow](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-parasympathetic-craniosacral-outflow.jpg)

## Key features

- Craniosacral division
- Pre-ganglionic neurons: brainstem cranial nerve nuclei (CN III, VII, IX, and X) and sacral autonomic nuclei in the lateral horn of S2-S4
- Post-ganglionic neurons: parasympathetic ganglia of the head and neck (ciliary, otic, pterygopalatine, submandibular, remembered as "COPS") and intrinsic ganglia
- Function: the "rest and digest" response

## Identify it

- Outflow comes only from the brainstem box and the sacral box, with nothing from cervical, thoracic, or lumbar levels
- Ganglia sit at the far end of each pathway, in or near the end organs
- Acetylcholine is labeled at both synapses

![Sacral spinal cord cross section with an arrow on the lateral gray matter, the site of the sacral autonomic nuclei](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-sacral-cord-lateral-horn-section.jpg)

- On the sacral cord section, the arrow marks the lateral gray matter where the S2-S4 pre-ganglionic neurons sit

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Sympathetic | Parasympathetic |
|---|---|---|
| Nickname | Thoracolumbar | Craniosacral |
| Pre-ganglionic cell bodies | IMLCC, T1-L2/3 | CN III, VII, IX, X nuclei; lateral horn S2-S4 |
| Post-ganglionic cell bodies | Paravertebral and prevertebral ganglia | COPS ganglia and intrinsic ganglia |
| Function | Fight or flight | Rest and digest |

> Parasympathetic pre-ganglionic neurons are in the nuclei of CN III, VII, IX, and X and in the lateral horn of S2-S4, which is why the division is called craniosacral.

Library: Cranial and sacral parasympathetic pathways
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Parasympathetic pathways: cranial outflow (blue bracket) in the oculomotor n. (CN III), facial n. (CN VII), glossopharyngeal n. (CN IX), and vagus n. (CN X), and sacral outflow (red bracket) from S2-S4 in the pelvic splanchnic nn.; solid lines are preganglionic fibers and dashed lines postganglionic fibers; targets include lacrimal gland, eye, mucous glands of nose, paranasal sinuses, and palate, parotid, sublingual, and submandibular glands, larynx, trachea, bronchi, lungs, heart, stomach, liver, gallbladder, bile ducts, pancreas, suprarenal gland, kidney, intestines, descending colon, sigmoid colon, rectum, urinary bladder, prostate, and external genitalia](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-parasympathetic-pathways-cranial-sacral.jpg)

## Cranial parasympathetic outflow

- CN III, VII, and IX synapse in the parasympathetic ganglia of the head ("COPS")
- CN X synapses in cardiac, thoracic, and enteric ganglia

## Sacral parasympathetic outflow

- Starts in the lateral horn of S2-S4
- Travels in the pelvic splanchnic nerves
- Reaches the pelvic plexuses

## Identify it

- The blue bracket marks the cranial outflow and the red bracket marks S2-S4
- Solid lines are preganglionic fibers and dashed lines are postganglionic fibers, so the short dashed segments show where each ganglion sits
