- CN III, VII, and IX run to ganglia beside the lacrimal gland, eye, mucous glands of the nose, paranasal sinuses, and palate, and the parotid, sublingual, and submandibular glands
- The vagus runs a long course to the larynx, trachea, bronchi, lungs, heart, stomach, liver, gallbladder, bile ducts, pancreas, suprarenal gland, kidney, and intestines
- The pelvic splanchnic nerves run to the descending colon, sigmoid colon, rectum, urinary bladder, prostate, and external genitalia

> Cranial outflow uses CN III, VII, and IX (to the COPS ganglia) and CN X (to cardiac, thoracic, and enteric ganglia); sacral outflow from S2-S4 travels in the pelvic splanchnic nerves.

Library: Enteric nervous system: myenteric and submucosal plexuses
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Cutaway of the intestinal wall: the muscularis externa with its outer longitudinal layer and inner circular layer, the myenteric (Auerbach's) plexus as a yellow nerve network within the muscle layers, the submucosal (Meissner's) plexus as a finer network deeper in the submucosa, and the mucosa lining the lumen](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-enteric-myenteric-submucosal-plexuses.jpg)

## Key features

- Called the "gut brain": local regulation of digestive functions occurs autonomously, modulated by sympathetic and parasympathetic neurons
- Largest and most complex part of the PNS and ANS, with about 200 million neurons
- Organized into two major plexuses, the myenteric (Auerbach's) plexus and the submucosal (Meissner's) plexus

## Identify it

- Myenteric plexus: the yellow network inside the muscularis externa, in the wall musculature (the figure shows the outer longitudinal and inner circular layers around it)
- Submucosal plexus: the finer network deeper in, within the submucosa, just outside the mucosa

## Look-alikes

| Plexus | Location | Regulates | Extent |
|---|---|---|---|
| Myenteric (Auerbach's) | Wall musculature of the GI tract | Intestinal motor function (peristalsis) | Esophagus to internal anal sphincter |
| Submucosal (Meissner's) | Submucosa | Glandular secretion, electrolyte and water transport, local blood flow | Small and large intestines |

> The myenteric (Auerbach's) plexus sits in the muscle layers and controls peristalsis; the submucosal (Meissner's) plexus sits in the submucosa and regulates secretion, electrolyte and water transport, and local blood flow.

Library: Visceral afferent fibers
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Spinal cord, spinal nerve, and sympathetic trunk with a key of sensory (black) and motor (red): a somatic afferent fiber runs from the skin through the spinal nerve to the dorsal root ganglion; a visceral afferent fiber runs from the heart back through the sympathetic trunk and rami communicantes to the same spinal nerve and dorsal root ganglion, then into the dorsal horn](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-visceral-vs-somatic-afferent-fibers.jpg)

Visceral afferents belong to the sensory (afferent) division, which has somatic and visceral parts, just as the motor division does.

## Key features

- Convey sensory impulses (nociceptive or reflexive) from viscera, glands, and blood vessels to the CNS
- Visceral sensation that reaches a conscious level is generally perceived as poorly localized, dull pain
- Visceral pain follows autonomic pathways back to the CNS

## Identify it

- Somatic afferent fiber: the black fiber coming from the skin and running straight back in the spinal nerve
- Visceral afferent fiber: the black fiber coming from the heart, running back alongside the red autonomic motor fibers through the sympathetic trunk and rami before joining the spinal nerve
- Both fibers reach the dorsal root ganglion (two cell bodies drawn there) and enter the dorsal horn

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Somatic afferent | Visceral afferent |
|---|---|---|
| Source in the figure | Skin | Heart (viscera, glands, blood vessels) |
| Route to the cord | Spinal nerve | Autonomic pathways, then spinal nerve |

> Visceral afferent fibers return to the CNS along autonomic pathways, and conscious visceral pain is poorly localized and dull.

Library: Pelvic pain line and visceral pain pathways
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Diagram of visceral pain pathways by region: a parasympathetic band at the top (glossopharyngeal nerve, vagus, superior laryngeal nerve, upper thoracic vagal rami) above the thoracic pain line; a sympathetic band in a blue box between the thoracic and pelvic pain lines with liver, gallbladder, stomach, small intestine, colon, kidney, ureter, lungs, diaphragm, and uterine fundus labeled with their nerves and levels; and a parasympathetic band in a pink box below the dashed pelvic pain line with trigone, prostate, urethra, testicle, cervix and upper vagina, and parasympathetic rami (S2-4)](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-visceral-pain-lines-pathways.jpg)

## Key features

- The pelvic pain line corresponds to the inferior limit of the parietal peritoneum
- Thorax and abdomen (above the pelvic pain line): visceral pain parallels sympathetic fibers back to dorsal root ganglia and spinal cord segments T1-L2
- Pelvic cavity (below the pelvic pain line): visceral pain parallels parasympathetic fibers back to dorsal root ganglia and spinal cord segments S2-S4

## Identify it

- The blue box is the sympathetic zone and the pink box below the dashed line is the parasympathetic zone
- The figure also marks a thoracic pain line at the top of the sympathetic zone; above it the side bar reads parasympathetic, with the glossopharyngeal nerve, vagus, superior laryngeal nerve, and upper thoracic vagal rami labeled

## Labels in the sympathetic zone

| Structure | Label on figure |
|---|---|
| Stomach | Splanchnic nerves (T7-9) |
| Liver and gallbladder | R splanchnic nerves (T7-9) |
| Duodenum and jejunum | Splanchnic nerves |
| Small intestine | Splanchnic nerves (T9-11) |
| Colon | T11-L1 |
| Kidney | Lower splanchnic nerves (T10-L1) |
| Ureter | T11-L1 |
| Uterine fundus | T11-L1 |
| Peripheral diaphragm | Intercostal nerves |
| Central diaphragm | Phrenic nerve |

## Labels below the pelvic pain line

- Urethra: pelvic nerves, S2-4
- Cervix and upper vagina: S2-4
- Lower bowel: parasympathetic rami (S2-4)
- Testicle: sacral nerves (S2-4), genitofemoral nerves (L1-2), spermatic plexus (T10)
- Trigone and prostate are also labeled in this zone

> Visceral pain above the pelvic pain line (inferior limit of the parietal peritoneum) follows sympathetic fibers to T1-L2; pain below it follows parasympathetic fibers to S2-S4.

Library: Referred pain
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

## Key features

- Pain perceived at a location other than the site of origin of the painful stimulus
- Incoming somatic and visceral afferent fibers converge
- Higher brain centers cannot correctly identify the source

## Identify it: referred pain map

![Anterior view of a female figure with colored zones of referred pain labeled heart (left neck and jaw, and left chest down the inner left arm), lungs and diaphragm, liver (right shoulder and right upper abdomen), gallbladder (right shoulder and right upper abdomen), appendix, stomach, pancreas, small intestine, ovaries, colon, kidneys, urinary bladder, and ureters](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-referred-pain-map.jpg)

- Heart: two zones, the left side of the neck and jaw, and the left chest running down the inner left arm
- Lungs and diaphragm: a band across the base of the neck
- Liver: the right shoulder and the right upper abdomen
- Gallbladder: the right shoulder tip and a spot in the right upper abdomen
- Appendix: the right lower abdomen
- Stomach and pancreas: small zones in the upper abdomen
- Small intestine: around the umbilicus, with ovaries and colon just below
- Kidneys: a broad zone over the lower abdomen and upper thighs
- Urinary bladder and ureters: above the pubis and in the groin

## Compare with the dermatomes

![Dermatome map in anterior and posterior views, labeled C2 through C8, T1 through T12, L1 through L5, S1 through S5, and Co](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-referred-pain-dermatome-map.jpg)

The slide pairs the referred pain map with a dermatome map, so a referred zone can be read as a set of dermatomes. The supplemental angina slide uses the same idea: name the dermatomes of the painful area, then ask which organ gets sympathetic innervation from those cord levels.

> Referred pain happens because somatic and visceral afferent fibers converge, so higher brain centers can't correctly identify the source of the pain.

Library: Angina pectoris referred pain
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Peripheral Nervous System: Somatic and Autonomic

![Three photos (A, B, C) with red shading showing angina pain over the left chest and inner left arm (A), the upper back, shoulders, back of the neck, and arm (B), and the whole chest, left neck and jaw, and left arm (C); beside them, anterior and posterior dermatome maps labeled C2 through S2](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-angina-pain-distribution-dermatomes.jpg)

This is a supplemental slide that applies referred pain to the heart.

## Key features

- Angina pectoris is cardiac pain that is referred to the chest, left upper limb, back, neck, and/or jaw
- Photos A to C show the patterns, and the dermatome maps beside them let you name the segments involved

## Questions posed on the slide

1. What dermatomes is the pain referring to?
2. What organ receives sympathetic innervation from those spinal cord levels?

The slide doesn't print the answers. Work them out from the lecture's own figures: match the red areas to the dermatome maps, then compare those levels with the cardiac innervation diagram below and the IMLCC map (heart, lungs, esophagus T4-6; upper limb T1-6). Visceral pain from the thorax follows sympathetic fibers back to the cord, so it lands in the dermatomes of the same segments.

![Autonomic innervation of the heart: dorsal motor nucleus of vagus nerve and cardioinhibitory center (parasympathetic) in the medulla sending the vagus nerve (parasympathetic, purple) to the SA and AV nodes; cardioacceleratory center (sympathetic) sending a green pathway down the cord to levels T1 to T5, then through the sympathetic chain as the sympathetic cardiac nerve to the SA node, AV node, and heart muscle](library/images/msk-week-13/pns-cardiac-autonomic-innervation-t1-t5.jpg)

## Identify it

- Sympathetic (green): pre-ganglionic fibers leave the upper thoracic cord (T1 to T5 labeled), synapse in the sympathetic chain, and reach the heart as the sympathetic cardiac nerve
- Parasympathetic (purple): the vagus nerve from the dorsal motor nucleus in the medulla, ending near the SA and AV nodes

> Angina is cardiac pain referred to the chest, left upper limb, back, neck, and/or jaw, and the heart's sympathetic supply comes from upper thoracic levels (T1 to T5 on the slide's diagram).

Library: Vertebral column regions and functions
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Vertebral column in anterior, right lateral, and posterior views plus a medial view of the bisected column, with color bands and labels for 7 cervical, 12 thoracic, 5 lumbar, sacrum (5 segments), and coccyx (4 segments), and vertebrae numbered T1 to T12, L1 to L5, S1 to S5](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-vertebral-column-regions.jpg)

## Key features

The vertebral column extends from the skull to the tip of the coccyx. It's built from five regions, counted from top to bottom.

| Region | Number of vertebrae | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Cervical | 7 | Neck |
| Thoracic | 12 | Articulate with ribs |
| Lumbar | 5 | Lower back |
| Sacral | 5 | Fused to form the sacrum |
| Coccygeal | 3 to 5 | Fused to form the coccyx |

The handout counts 33 vertebrae in total, using 4 coccygeal vertebrae.

## Function

- Maintains posture
- Absorbs shock and supports body weight
- Aids in movement
- Protects the spinal cord and spinal nerves

> The vertebral column has 7 cervical, 12 thoracic, 5 lumbar, 5 fused sacral, and 3 to 5 fused coccygeal vertebrae.

Library: Normal vertebral curvatures
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Medial view of the vertebral column with skull, ribs, and pelvis, color banded by region, with dashed lines marking the cervical, thoracic, lumbar, and sacral curvatures](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-normal-curvatures.jpg)

## Identify it

- In a lateral view the column forms four curves, alternating in direction
- The cervical and lumbar curves are hollow toward the back
- The thoracic and sacral curves are hollow toward the front

## Key features

| Curvature | Concave toward | Type named in lecture |
|---|---|---|
| Cervical | Posteriorly | Secondary |
| Thoracic | Anteriorly | Not stated |
| Lumbar | Posteriorly | Secondary |
| Sacral | Anteriorly | Not stated |

The thoracic and sacral curvatures are concave anteriorly, and the cervical and lumbar curvatures are concave posteriorly. When these normal curves become exaggerated or a sideways curve appears, the result is one of the abnormal curvatures: kyphosis (thoracic), lordosis (lumbar), or scoliosis (lateral).

> Cervical and lumbar curvatures are concave posteriorly and are the secondary curvatures; thoracic and sacral curvatures are concave anteriorly.

Library: Abnormal vertebral curvatures: kyphosis, lordosis, and scoliosis
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Side-by-side illustration of a neutral spine and a kyphotic spine with regions labeled cervical, thoracic spine, lumbar, sacrum, and tailbone, above a lateral photo of a patient's back showing a pronounced thoracic hump](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-kyphosis.jpg)

![Illustration of two standing women in lateral view comparing normal posture with lumbar lordosis (swayback), labeled lumbar spine and curved spine in the lower back](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-lordosis.jpg)

![Posterior view diagrams comparing normal spines with scoliotic spines, and a photo of a patient's back showing a lateral curve of the spine with an uneven trunk](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-scoliosis.jpg)

## Identify it

- Kyphosis: hunchback or humpback, a rounded upper back in lateral view
- Lordosis: hollow back or sway back, a deeply hollowed lower back in lateral view
- Scoliosis: curved back seen from behind, with the spine bending to the side and the trunk looking uneven

## Key features

| Condition | Region | Defining change | Causes in lecture |
|---|---|---|---|
| Kyphosis | Thoracic | Excessive thoracic curvature | Degenerative diseases; erosion or fracture of the anterior part of one or more vertebrae; postural component |
| Lordosis | Lumbar | Excessive lumbar curvature | Weak trunk muscles; obesity; pregnancy |
| Scoliosis | Lateral | Increased lateral curvature with vertebral rotation | Idiopathic; congenital defect in vertebral development |

The handout adds detail to each one. Kyphosis typically results from erosion or fracture of the anterior part of the vertebrae, often due to osteoporosis or other aging factors. Lordosis is characterized by anterior rotation of the pelvis and is often caused by weakened trunk musculature and seen during pregnancy. Scoliosis may be due to weakness of back muscles, poor posture, congenital defects in vertebral development, and inequality in the length of the lower limbs.

Kyphosis and lordosis exaggerate curves that are already present (thoracic concave anteriorly, lumbar concave posteriorly). Scoliosis adds a sideways curve that isn't part of the normal column, and the rotation of the vertebrae is part of its definition.

> Kyphosis exaggerates the thoracic curve, lordosis exaggerates the lumbar curve, and scoliosis is a lateral curve with vertebral rotation.

Library: Parts of a typical vertebra
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Typical vertebra in superior view labeled spinous process, vertebral foramen, lamina, transverse process, pedicle, and vertebral body, and in lateral view labeled transverse process, pedicle, spinous process, and vertebral body](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-typical-vertebra-parts.jpg)

## Identify it

- Vertebral body: large anterior block
- Vertebral arch: posterior ring made of pedicles and laminae
- Pedicles: short stout processes joining the arch to the body
- Laminae: two broad flat plates closing the arch posteriorly
- Vertebral foramen: the opening bounded by the body and the arch
- Transverse processes (2): project posterolaterally from the pedicle and lamina junction
- Spinous process (1): projects posteriorly where the two laminae meet

## Key features

- The vertebral body supports body weight, and adjacent bodies are separated by intervertebral (IV) discs
- Vertebral bodies get larger inferiorly to support increasing body weight
- Spinous and transverse processes are attachment sites for muscles and ligaments

Osteoporosis commonly affects the vertebral bodies, which become weak and brittle and can fracture. It's most common in thoracic vertebrae and especially common in postmenopausal females. A laminectomy removes one or more spinous processes and their laminae to open the vertebral canal and relieve pressure on the cord or nerve roots. In spina bifida, the vertebral arch formed from the laminae (usually lumbar) fails to develop and fuse.

> The vertebral arch is made of pedicles and laminae; the body sits anterior and the spinous process projects posteriorly from where the laminae meet.

Library: Articular processes and pars interarticularis
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Lumbar vertebra in superior view and lateral view plus two articulated vertebrae, labeled superior articular process and facet, inferior articular process and facet, and pars interarticularis](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-articular-processes-pars.jpg)

## Identify it

- Superior articular process with its facet points upward
- Inferior articular process with its facet points downward
- Pars interarticularis is the bony segment between the superior and inferior articular processes
- In the articulated view, the inferior process of the upper vertebra meets the superior process of the vertebra below

## Key features

- Superior articular facet articulates with the vertebra above
- Inferior articular facet articulates with the vertebra below
- There are four articular processes per vertebra (2 superior, 2 inferior), located at the junction of the pedicles and laminae
- Paired articular processes of adjacent vertebrae form the zygapophysial (facet) joints
- A fracture of the pars interarticularis is called spondylolysis

> A fracture through the pars interarticularis, the region between the superior and inferior articular processes, is spondylolysis.

Library: Vertebral notches and the intervertebral foramen
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Lateral view of a lumbar vertebra labeled superior vertebral notch and inferior vertebral notch, next to two articulated vertebrae with the intervertebral foramen outlined in yellow between them](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-vertebral-notches-ivf.jpg)

## Identify it

- Superior vertebral notch: shallow curve on the upper edge of the pedicle
- Inferior vertebral notch: deeper curve on the lower edge of the pedicle
- When two vertebrae stack, the inferior notch of the upper vertebra and the superior notch of the lower vertebra outline an opening, the intervertebral foramen

## Function

- Each pedicle has a superior and an inferior vertebral notch
- The notches of adjacent vertebrae create the intervertebral foramen
- The intervertebral foramen allows passage of the spinal nerves

Narrowing of the intervertebral foramina is one form of spinal stenosis, which can compress the spinal nerve roots passing through.

> Superior and inferior vertebral notches of adjacent vertebrae form the intervertebral foramen, where spinal nerves pass.

Library: Vertebral canal
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Lateral view of the lumbar and sacral column with vertebral bodies sectioned, showing the spinal cord, cauda equina nerve roots, and meninges running in the vertebral canal and spinal nerves exiting between vertebrae](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-vertebral-canal.jpg)

## Identify it

- Continuous channel behind the stacked vertebral bodies
- Holds the spinal cord, its nerve roots, and the meninges
- Spinal nerves leave the canal laterally between adjacent vertebrae

## Key features

- The vertebral canal is formed when vertebrae are stacked in succession
- It's made by the adjacent vertebral foramina lined up one above the other
- It contains the spinal cord and meninges
- The posterior longitudinal ligament runs inside the canal along the back of the vertebral bodies
- The internal vertebral venous plexus lies within the canal
- The sacral hiatus is an inverted U-shaped opening leading into the sacral canal, the inferior end of the vertebral canal

Spinal stenosis is a narrowing of the vertebral canal (or of the intervertebral foramina) that compresses the spinal cord or nerve roots.

> The vertebral canal is the stack of vertebral foramina and contains the spinal cord and meninges.

Library: Cervical vertebrae (C3 to C7) and the vertebra prominens
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Superior view of a cervical vertebra labeled spinous process (bifid), vertebral body, and transverse foramen](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-cervical-vertebra.jpg)

![Superior views of vertebrae C1 to C7 stacked vertically, a lateral view of articulated C4 and C5 with a dashed line along the facet plane, and a lateral cervical radiograph with C2 to C7 labeled](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-cervical-facets-radiograph.jpg)

![Photo of the back of a person's neck and upper back, neck and back flexed with scapulae protracted, with a leader line to the C7 spinous process bump at the base of the neck](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-vertebra-prominens.jpg)

## Identify it

- Small vertebral body
- Short, bifid spinous process (split tip)
- A hole in each transverse process, the transverse foramen
- Large triangular vertebral foramen
- On a lateral radiograph, the cervical bodies stack in a gentle curve numbered C2 to C7 from the top
- C7 (vertebra prominens) makes the prominent bump at the base of the neck, easiest to see with the neck flexed

## Key features

| Part | Cervical feature |
|---|---|
| Vertebral body | Small |
| Vertebral foramen | Large and triangular, due to the cervical enlargement of the spinal cord |
| Transverse processes | Short, with transverse foramina in all cervical vertebrae |
| Spinous process | Short and bifid, except C7, which is long |
| Articular facets | Superior face superoposteriorly, inferior face anteroinferiorly; horizontal orientation |
| Motions | Flexion, extension, lateral flexion |

- The vertebral artery ascends through the transverse foramina of C6 to C1, and the vertebral vein passes through the C1 to C7 transverse foramina
- C7 has a long spinous process and serves as a surface anatomy landmark
- C1 (atlas) and C2 (axis) are atypical cervical vertebrae

> A transverse foramen identifies a cervical vertebra; C7 (vertebra prominens) has a long spinous process used as a surface landmark.

Library: Vertebral artery and the transverse foramina
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Lateral view of the cervical spine with the vertebral artery in red entering the transverse foramen of C6, ascending through the transverse foramina, and curving over the atlas](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-vertebral-artery.jpg)

## Identify it

- Red vessel climbing the side of the cervical spine inside the transverse processes
- It enters low in the neck and bends medially over the top of the atlas

## Key features

- Courses through the transverse foramina of the cervical vertebrae
- Typically enters at C6 and ascends through C1
- On the atlas it runs in the groove for the vertebral artery on the superior surface
- Passes through the foramen magnum to enter the cranial cavity
- Contributes to the posterior circulation of blood to the brain (covered further in the CPH module)

> The vertebral artery typically enters the transverse foramen at C6 and ascends through C1 to the foramen magnum.

Library: Atlas (C1)
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Superior view of the atlas labeled anterior arch, posterior arch, lateral mass, superior articular facet, and groove for the vertebral artery, with an inferior view of the skull labeling the occipital condyles](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-atlas-c1.jpg)

## Identify it

- Ring shape with no body and no spinous process
- Two lateral masses take the place of the body
- Anterior arch and posterior arch join the lateral masses
- Large kidney-shaped superior articular facets on the lateral masses
- Groove for the vertebral artery on the superior surface

## Key features

- The superior articular facets articulate with the occipital condyles of the occipital bone (atlanto-occipital joint)
- The atlanto-occipital joint allows nodding, some side bending, and limited rotation
- The inferior facets articulate with C2 (atlanto-axial joint)
- The posterior surface of the anterior arch has a facet for the dens of the axis

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Atlas (C1) | Axis (C2) |
|---|---|---|
| Body | None (lateral masses) | Present, with dens projecting up |
| Key landmark | Groove for vertebral artery | Dens (odontoid process) |

> The atlas has no body or spinous process; its lateral masses articulate with the occipital condyles.

Library: Axis (C2) and the dens
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Axis in posterosuperior and superior views labeled dens, and an anterior view of articulated C1 and C2 labeled dens and anterior arch of the atlas](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-axis-c2.jpg)

## Identify it

- Tooth-like dens (odontoid process) projecting upward from the body
- In the articulated anterior view, the dens sits just behind the anterior arch of the atlas

## Key features

- The dens projects superiorly from the body of C2
- It serves as the pivot point for rotation of the head
- It articulates with the facet for the dens on the posterior surface of the anterior arch of the atlas
- This dens joint is a pivot joint, part of the atlanto-axial joint, which lets C1 pivot around the dens of C2

> The dens of C2 articulates with the back of the atlas's anterior arch and is the pivot for head rotation.

Library: Thoracic vertebrae: costal facets and facet orientation
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Thoracic vertebra in superior view labeled transverse costal facet, and two thoracic vertebrae in lateral view labeled transverse costal facet, superior costal facet, and inferior costal facet](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-thoracic-costal-facets.jpg)

![Lateral view of two articulated thoracic vertebrae with a red dashed line along the nearly vertical facet joint plane](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-thoracic-facet-orientation.jpg)

## Identify it

- Heart-shaped vertebral body
- Costal facets on the sides of the body (superior and inferior)
- Costal facet on the tip of each transverse process
- Long spinous process sloping inferiorly to overlap the vertebra below
- Small round vertebral foramen
- In lateral view, the facet joint plane runs nearly vertically

## Key features

| Facet | Location | Articulates with |
|---|---|---|
| Superior and inferior costal facets | Vertebral body | Head of the rib (costovertebral joint) |
| Transverse costal facet | Transverse process (T1 to T10) | Tubercle of the rib (costotransverse joint) |

- Superior articular facet faces posteriorly and slightly laterally
- Inferior articular facet faces anteriorly and slightly medially
- The facets are vertically oriented, which limits excessive flexion and extension
- Motions: rotation and some lateral flexion

## Look-alikes

| Region | Facet orientation | Main motions |
|---|---|---|
| Cervical | Horizontal | Flexion, extension, lateral flexion |
| Thoracic | Vertical | Rotation, some lateral flexion |
| Lumbar | Sagittal plane | Flexion and extension, lateral flexion, limited rotation |

> Costal facets on the body take the head of the rib and the transverse costal facet takes the tubercle; vertical thoracic facets limit flexion and extension.

Library: Lumbar vertebrae
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Lumbar vertebra in superior view labeled spinous process, mammillary process, pedicle, and vertebral body, and in lateral view labeled vertebral body and spinous process](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-lumbar-vertebra.jpg)

![Lateral view of two articulated lumbar vertebrae with a red dashed line along the facet joint plane](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-lumbar-facet-orientation.jpg)

## Identify it

- Large, strong vertebral body
- Short, sturdy pedicles
- Short and stout spinous process
- Mammillary process on the superior articular process
- Triangular vertebral foramen, larger than in thoracic vertebrae
- Long, slender transverse processes with no costal facets

## Key features

| Part | Lumbar feature |
|---|---|
| Vertebral body | Larger, strong, with short heavy pedicles |
| Vertebral foramen | Triangular, larger than in thoracic vertebrae |
| Transverse processes | Long and slender, no costal facets |
| Spinous process | Short and stout |

- Articular facets are oriented in the sagittal plane
- Motions: flexion and extension, lateral flexion, and limited rotation
- Lumbar spinal stenosis and lumbar disc herniations (L4/L5, L5/S1) are common clinical problems here

> Lumbar vertebrae have large bodies, short stout spinous processes, mammillary processes, and no costal facets.

Library: Comparing cervical, thoracic, and lumbar vertebrae
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

## Key features

Each region has a few features that identify it on its own. Use this table to sort an unknown vertebra.

| Part | Cervical | Thoracic | Lumbar |
|---|---|---|---|
| Body | Small | Heart-shaped, with costal facets | Large and strong |
| Vertebral foramen | Large, triangular | Small, round | Triangular |
| Transverse process | Transverse foramen | Costal facet (T1 to T10) | Long, slender, no costal facet |
| Spinous process | Short, bifid (C7 long) | Long, slopes inferiorly | Short and stout |
| Main motions | Flexion, extension, lateral flexion | Rotation, some lateral flexion | Flexion, extension, lateral flexion, limited rotation |

## Identify it

- Hole in the transverse process: cervical
- Facets for ribs on the body or transverse process: thoracic
- Big body, no transverse foramen, no costal facets: lumbar
- No body and no spinous process: atlas (C1)
- Dens: axis (C2)
- Long spinous process at the base of the neck: C7

> Transverse foramina mean cervical, costal facets mean thoracic, and a large body with neither means lumbar.

Library: Sacrum: promontory, foramina, and hiatus
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Sacrum in anterior view labeled sacral promontory and anterior sacral foramina, and in posterior view labeled posterior sacral foramina and sacral hiatus](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-sacrum-anterior-posterior.jpg)

## Identify it

- Triangular bone made of 5 fused vertebrae
- Anterior (pelvic) surface is smooth and concave
- Posterior surface is rough and convex
- Sacral promontory: anterior projecting edge of the S1 body
- Paired foramina on both the anterior and posterior surfaces
- Sacral hiatus: inverted U-shaped opening at the lower end of the posterior surface

## Key features

- Provides strength and stability to the pelvis and transmits upper body weight to the ilium through the sacroiliac joints
- The sacral promontory is an important obstetrical landmark
- Anterior (ventral) sacral foramina transmit the anterior rami of spinal nerves
- Posterior (dorsal) sacral foramina transmit the posterior rami of spinal nerves
- The sacral hiatus leads into the sacral canal and is one site for caudal epidural anesthesia

> Anterior sacral foramina carry anterior rami and posterior sacral foramina carry posterior rami; the sacral hiatus is a caudal epidural site.

Library: Sacral joints and the coccyx
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Posterior view of the sacrum labeled superior articular facet and auricular surface](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-sacrum-articular-surfaces.jpg)

![Anterior view of the sacrum and coccyx with a bracket labeling the coccyx below the sacrum](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-coccyx.jpg)

## Identify it

- Superior articular facets: paired facets at the top of the sacrum's posterior surface
- Auricular surface: ear-shaped surface on each lateral side of the sacrum
- Coccyx: small triangular bone below the sacrum, the "tail bone"

## Key features

| Sacral surface | Articulates with | Joint |
|---|---|---|
| Superior articular facet | Inferior articular facet of L5 | Lumbosacral joint |
| Auricular surface | Ilium of the hip bone | Sacroiliac joint |

- The coccyx is a fusion of 3 to 5 rudimentary coccygeal vertebrae (the handout says 4)

> The auricular surface of the sacrum forms the sacroiliac joint with the ilium; the superior articular facet forms the lumbosacral joint with L5.

Library: Anterior and posterior longitudinal ligaments and ligamentum flavum
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Lateral view of the lumbar column with upper vertebrae sectioned, labeled anterior longitudinal ligament on the front of the bodies, posterior longitudinal ligament inside the canal on the back of the bodies, and ligamentum flavum between laminae](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-longitudinal-ligaments-flavum.jpg)

## Identify it

- Anterior longitudinal ligament: broad band down the front of the vertebral bodies and discs
- Posterior longitudinal ligament: narrow band on the back of the vertebral bodies, inside the vertebral canal
- Ligamentum flavum: pale yellow tissue between adjacent laminae

Vertebral ligaments are strong fibrous bands that hold vertebrae together, stabilize the spine, and protect the IV discs.

## Key features

| Ligament | Location | Function |
|---|---|---|
| Anterior longitudinal | Anterior to vertebral bodies and IV discs, occipital bone to sacrum | Prevents hyperextension |
| Posterior longitudinal | Posterior to vertebral bodies, within the vertebral canal | Weakly resists hyperflexion; resists posterior displacement of IV discs |
| Ligamentum flavum | Between laminae | Joins adjacent laminae |

## Whiplash

![Illustration of a rear-end car collision with an arrow to a sagittal section of the cervical spine in hyperextension, showing a torn anterior longitudinal ligament at the front of the vertebral bodies](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-whiplash.jpg)

Whiplash is due to a sudden back and forth motion of the neck, and a common cause is a rear-ended motor vehicle collision. It can result in severe hyperextension of the neck, so the anterior longitudinal ligament (the ligament that prevents hyperextension) is severely stretched and may be torn.

> The anterior longitudinal ligament prevents hyperextension, so it's the ligament stretched or torn in whiplash.

Library: Supraspinous, interspinous, and nuchal ligaments
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Lateral view of the lumbar column labeled interspinous ligaments between adjacent spinous processes and supraspinous ligament along the tips of the spinous processes](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-supraspinous-interspinous.jpg)

![Lateral view of the cervical spine and base of the skull with the fan-shaped nuchal ligament labeled, running from the occipital bone to the C7 spinous process](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-nuchal-ligament.jpg)

## Identify it

- Supraspinous ligament: continuous cord along the tips of the spinous processes
- Interspinous ligaments: thin sheets filling the gaps between adjoining spinous processes
- Nuchal ligament: thin, fan-shaped midline sheet behind the cervical spine, from the back of the skull to C7

## Key features

| Ligament | Connects |
|---|---|
| Supraspinous | Tips of the spinous processes, C7 to the sacrum |
| Interspinous | Adjacent spinous processes |
| Intertransverse | Adjacent transverse processes |
| Nuchal | External occipital protuberance to the C7 spinous process |

- Above C7, the supraspinous fibers merge with the nuchal ligament
- The nuchal ligament limits forward flexion of the head and cervical spine
- It serves as an attachment site for muscles, including the trapezius, serratus posterior superior, and splenius
- The handout calls the nuchal ligament an extremely strong band reaching the spinous processes of the cervical vertebrae
- A back sprain is an injury involving only ligamentous tissue or the ligament-bone attachment

> The nuchal ligament runs from the external occipital protuberance to C7, limits forward flexion of the head, and merges with the supraspinous ligament.

Library: Intervertebral disc structure
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Anterosuperior view of a vertebral column sectioned through an IV disc, labeled nucleus pulposus in the center and annulus fibrosus in concentric rings around it](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-iv-disc-nucleus-anulus.jpg)

![Three panels: anterosuperior view of the column sectioned through an IV disc, lateral view of a disc when recumbent labeled vertebral body and disc, and lateral view of a disc when erect and weight bearing under a 5 kg load](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-iv-disc-views.jpg)

## Identify it

- Nucleus pulposus: soft central core
- Anulus fibrosus: concentric rings surrounding the nucleus

## Key features

- IV discs join two vertebral bodies, forming symphyses (secondary cartilaginous joints)
- They're flexible shock absorbers and permit movement between vertebrae
- Thickness varies along the column, thicker in the cervical and lumbar regions
- IV discs make up 20 to 25% of the column's height
- Only the outer third of the anulus fibrosus is vascular and innervated in a non-pathologic state

| Part | Composition |
|---|---|
| Nucleus pulposus | Gelatinous material with high water content (about 90% in young people) |
| Anulus fibrosus | Concentric layers of fibrocartilage |

> An IV disc is a symphysis with a gelatinous nucleus pulposus inside a fibrocartilage anulus fibrosus.

Library: Age-related intervertebral disc changes
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

## Key features

With advancing age, the nucleus pulposus dehydrates and loses elastin and proteoglycans while gaining collagen. The discs lose their turgor (fullness), so they become stiffer and more resistant to deformation. Eventually the nucleus becomes dry and granular, and the anulus fibrosus takes on a greater share of the vertical load and often develops fissures and cavities.

## Degenerative disc disease

- Pain caused by an IV disc that has degenerated or lost its integrity
- Often results from natural stresses and minor injuries that make discs gradually lose water as the anulus fibrosus weakens
- The discs collapse, putting pressure on spinal nerves and causing pain and weakness
- Symptoms are usually in the low back or neck, depending on which discs are affected
- Treatment may include exercise, physical therapy, medications (NSAIDs, pain relievers), surgery, and spinal mobilization

Disc degeneration also matters elsewhere in this lecture. It's a common cause of spondylolisthesis, and bulging IV discs are a common cause of spinal stenosis.

> With age the nucleus pulposus dehydrates and gains collagen, so the disc stiffens and the anulus fibrosus bears more load.

Library: Herniated nucleus pulposus (L4/L5 example)
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Herniated nucleus pulposus shown in median section with a median MRI labeled L5 and S1, in transverse section labeled cauda equina with an axial MRI labeled IV disc and herniated disc, and a posterior view of the lumbosacral canal circling an L4/L5 herniation labeled L4 spinal nerve and L5 spinal nerve](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-disc-herniation-l4-l5.jpg)

## Identify it

- Sagittal MRI or section: disc material bulging backward into the vertebral canal
- Axial MRI or section: disc material pushing posterolaterally toward the nerve roots
- Posterior view: herniated L4/L5 disc pressing on the L5 nerve root

## Key features

- Herniation is protrusion of the nucleus pulposus into or through the anulus fibrosus
- It can compress nerve roots or the spinal cord, causing low back and lower limb pain or neurologic deficits
- Herniations usually extend posterolaterally, where the anulus is thin and unsupported by the longitudinal ligaments
- The posterior longitudinal ligament helps resist posterior displacement of the IV discs
- About 95% of lumbar protrusions occur at L4/L5 or L5/S1

| Disc | Nerve root affected |
|---|---|
| L4/L5 | L5 |
| C5/C6 | C6 |
| C6/C7 | C7 |

> An L4/L5 disc herniation compresses the L5 nerve root.

Library: Facet (zygapophysial) joints
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Lateral view of the lumbar column labeled inferior articular process, joint capsule, and superior articular process at a facet joint](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-facet-joint.jpg)

## Identify it

- Small paired joints on the posterior side of the column, behind the vertebral bodies
- Inferior articular process of the upper vertebra meeting the superior articular process of the vertebra below
- Wrapped in a thin joint capsule

## Key features

- Also called the zygapophysial joint
- Plane synovial joints
- Formed between the inferior articular process of one vertebra and the superior articular process of the vertebra below
- Facet orientation differs by region, which sets the motion each region allows (horizontal in cervical, vertical in thoracic, sagittal in lumbar)
- Innervated by branches of adjacent posterior rami, and sensitive to pain

> Facet (zygapophysial) joints are plane synovial joints between the inferior articular process above and the superior articular process below.

Library: Craniovertebral joints
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Posterior view of the skull base with C1 and C2 with a red dashed circle at the atlanto-occipital joint and a blue dashed circle at a lateral atlanto-axial joint, and an anterior view of C1 and C2 with a blue circle at the lateral atlanto-axial joint and a purple circle at the joint between the dens and the anterior arch](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-craniovertebral-joints.jpg)

## Identify it

- Atlanto-occipital joint: between the occipital condyles and the top of C1 (red)
- Lateral atlanto-axial joints: between the lateral masses of C1 and C2 (blue)
- Median atlanto-axial joint: between the dens and the anterior arch of C1 (purple)

## Key features

| Joint | Bones | Type and motion |
|---|---|---|
| Atlanto-occipital (paired) | Occipital condyle and C1 superior facet | Synovial; nodding, some side bending, limited rotation |
| Atlanto-axial, lateral (paired) | Lateral masses of C1 and C2 | Synovial |
| Atlanto-axial, dens | Dens and C1 anterior arch | Synovial pivot |

- The atlanto-axial joint is made of 3 synovial joints in total
- It lets C1 pivot around the dens of C2, which rotates the head

> The atlanto-axial joint is 3 synovial joints (two lateral, one at the dens) and allows the atlas to pivot around the dens.

Library: Spondylolysis and the Scotty dog sign
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Left posterolateral oblique radiograph of L3 to L5 with outlined Scotty dog shapes and a broken neck at L5, a transverse CT with arrows at bilateral pars defects, and a labeled Scotty dog drawing: superior articular process (ear), pedicle (eye), transverse process (nose), pars interarticularis (neck), inferior articular process (front leg), lamina (body), spinous process (tail), contralateral inferior articular process (hind leg)](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-spondylolysis-scotty-dog.jpg)

## Identify it

- Oblique lumbar radiograph shows a "Scotty dog" outline on each vertebra
- A lucent line across the dog's neck looks like a collar
- On transverse CT, the defect shows as a break in the bony ring at the pars

## Key features

- Bony defect or fracture in the pars interarticularis
- Common in the lower lumbar vertebrae (L5, L4)
- Affects about 6 to 8% of the general population
- A stress fracture common in young athletes with repetitive hyperextension movements

| Scotty dog part | Structure |
|---|---|
| Nose | Transverse process |
| Eye | Pedicle |
| Ear | Superior articular process |
| Neck | Pars interarticularis |
| Front leg | Inferior articular process |

> A fracture through the pars interarticularis looks like a collar on the Scotty dog's neck.

Library: Spondylolisthesis
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Lateral illustration of the lumbosacral junction with L5 slipping anteriorly over S1, next to a median MRI labeled S1 and L5 showing spondylolisthesis secondary to spondylolysis of L5](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-spondylolisthesis-mri.jpg)

## Identify it

- On sagittal imaging, one vertebral body is shifted forward relative to the one below
- Classic site: L5 sitting anterior to S1

## Key features

- Displacement of one vertebra compared to another
- Common at the L5-S1 junction, with L5 slipping anterior over S1
- Common causes are disc degeneration and sports injury
- The MRI in the lecture shows spondylolisthesis secondary to spondylolysis of L5

## Look-alikes

| Term | What it is |
|---|---|
| Spondylolysis | Defect or fracture of the pars interarticularis |
| Spondylolisthesis | Slippage of one vertebra relative to another |
| Spinal stenosis | Narrowing of the vertebral canal or intervertebral foramina |

> Spondylolisthesis is slippage of one vertebra on another, most commonly L5 sliding anteriorly over S1.

Library: Spinal stenosis of the vertebral canal
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Superior views of a normal vertebral foramen and a stenotic vertebral foramen, and a sagittal MRI viewed from the left labeled tip of conus medullaris, nerve roots of cauda equina, region of lumbar spinal stenosis (arrow), and sacrum](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-spinal-stenosis.jpg)

## Identify it

- Vertebral foramen looks smaller and more crowded than normal
- On the sagittal MRI, the arrow marks the region of lumbar spinal stenosis along the nerve roots of the cauda equina

## Key features

- Narrowing of the vertebral canal or intervertebral foramina
- Compresses the spinal cord or spinal nerve roots
- Common in the lumbar and cervical regions
- Common causes: bulging IV discs and bone spurs

## Lumbar spinal stenosis

The handout describes lumbar spinal stenosis as causing pain, numbness, or weakness in the legs. It can affect younger patients from developmental causes, but it's more often degenerative and usually affects people age 60 and older. It develops slowly and is often related to osteoarthritis, which causes bulging hardened IV discs, bone spurs, and thickened ligaments that narrow the canal. Symptoms may come from inflammation, nerve compression, or both.

> Spinal stenosis is narrowing of the vertebral canal or intervertebral foramina, commonly from bulging discs and bone spurs.

Library: Arterial supply of the vertebral column
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Superior view of a thoracic vertebra with the aorta giving off a posterior intercostal artery, which sends a spinal branch into the vertebral canal and a posterior branch to the posterior elements](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-arterial-supply.jpg)

## Identify it

- Aorta in front of the vertebral body
- Posterior intercostal (segmental) artery wrapping around the side of the body
- Spinal branch entering the vertebral canal
- Posterior branch running to the spinous and transverse processes

## Key features

- Multiple cervical and segmental arteries have branches that supply the vertebrae and surrounding ligaments
- The handout describes these as spinal branches of the major cervical and segmental arteries
- In the thoracic region the segmental vessel shown is the posterior intercostal artery from the aorta
- In the neck, the vertebral artery runs through the transverse foramina

Venous drainage parallels this arterial supply through the internal and external vertebral venous plexuses.

> Spinal branches of cervical and segmental arteries (such as posterior intercostal arteries) supply the vertebrae and ligaments.

Library: Internal and external vertebral venous plexuses
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Superior view of a lumbar vertebra with veins in blue labeled internal vertebral venous plexus inside the canal, intervertebral vein, and external vertebral venous plexus around the body](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-venous-plexus-superior.jpg)

![Sagittal view of the vertebral column with veins in blue labeled external vertebral venous plexus on the outer surface and internal vertebral venous plexus within the vertebral canal](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-venous-plexus-sagittal.jpg)

## Identify it

- Internal plexus: dense network of veins inside the vertebral canal
- External plexus: veins on the outside of the vertebrae
- Intervertebral veins link the two through the intervertebral foramina

## Key features

- Venous drainage of the vertebral column parallels the arterial supply
- Internal vertebral venous plexuses lie within the vertebral canal
- External vertebral venous plexuses lie outside the vertebral canal
- The plexuses communicate with each other
- They drain into the vertebral veins of the neck and the segmental veins of the trunk
- The plexuses lack valves, which facilitates spread of malignant cells and bacteria in the blood

> Vertebral venous plexuses are valveless and communicate, which lets malignant cells and bacteria spread.

Library: Meningeal branches and vertebral column innervation
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Transverse section through the back at a thoracic level, superior view, labeled spinal nerve and the meningeal branch returning into the vertebral canal, with the deep back muscles posteriorly](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-meningeal-branch.jpg)

## Identify it

- Spinal nerve leaving the intervertebral foramen
- Small meningeal branch turning back (recurrent) into the vertebral canal

## Key features

The (recurrent) meningeal branches of spinal nerves supply:

- Bone periosteum
- IV discs (anulus fibrosus)
- Vertebral ligaments
- Spinal cord meninges

The zygapophysial joints are innervated by branches of adjacent posterior rami. Together these two groups of nerves convey all localized pain from the vertebral column, and these structures are sensitive to pain.

## Back pain

Back pain affects about 80% of the population at some point. Acute back pain comes on suddenly and lasts days to weeks; it's chronic if it lasts more than three months. Pain sources include periosteum, ligaments, IV discs, meninges, zygapophysial joints, intrinsic back muscles, and spinal nerves or roots.

> Meningeal branches supply periosteum, discs, ligaments, and meninges; posterior rami supply the zygapophysial joints.

Library: Back muscle layers and their innervation
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Posterior view of the back with trapezius (red), latissimus dorsi (yellow), levator scapulae (green), and rhomboid minor and rhomboid major (blue) labeled](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-superficial-back-muscles.jpg)

## Key features

Back muscles are grouped into three layers. Think about them by action (learned through bony attachments) and by innervation, since patients present with abnormal movements caused by muscle injury or nerve lesions.

| Layer | Muscles | Associated with | Motor innervation |
|---|---|---|---|
| Superficial (extrinsic) | Trapezius, latissimus dorsi, levator scapulae, rhomboid major, rhomboid minor | Shoulder movements | CN XI, thoracodorsal n., dorsal scapular n. |
| Intermediate (extrinsic) | Serratus posterior superior and inferior | Thoracic cage | Intercostal nerves (anterior rami) |
| Deep (intrinsic) | Splenius, semispinalis, erector spinae | Vertebral column movement and posture | Posterior rami of spinal nerves |

- Muscles are innervated by the peripheral nervous system through cranial nerves and spinal nerves
- Motor innervation through spinal nerves runs in either anterior rami or posterior rami
- Posterior rami ultimately become cutaneous nerves that give sensation to the skin of the back and neck

> Deep (intrinsic) back muscles are innervated by posterior rami of spinal nerves.

Library: Trapezius muscle
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Posterior view of the back and upper limb with the trapezius highlighted in teal, spanning from the occiput and midline spinous processes to the shoulder](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-trapezius.jpg)

![Posterior view of the scapula, clavicle, and humerus with muscle attachment sites, leader lines labeling the trapezius insertion on the spine of the scapula, acromion, and clavicle](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-trapezius-insertion.jpg)

## Identify it

- Large triangular, superficial muscle covering the upper back and back of the neck

## Key features

| Feature | Trapezius |
|---|---|
| Proximal attachment | Occipital bone, nuchal ligament, spinous processes C7 to T12 |
| Distal attachment | Lateral 1/3 of clavicle, acromion, spine of scapula |
| Action | Elevates, retracts (middle part), and depresses the scapula |
| Motor nerve | Spinal accessory nerve (CN XI) |
| Pain and proprioception | Anterior rami of C3 and C4 |
| Blood supply | Transverse cervical artery |

## Clinical testing

Test the spinal accessory nerve by having the patient shrug the shoulders against resistance.

> The trapezius gets motor innervation from CN XI, tested by shrugging the shoulders against resistance.

Library: Latissimus dorsi muscle
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Posterior view of the back and upper limb with the latissimus dorsi highlighted in green, spreading from the lower back toward the arm](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-latissimus-dorsi.jpg)

![Anterior view of the humerus and scapula with muscle attachment sites, labeled latissimus dorsi attachment in the intertubercular groove of the humerus](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-latissimus-dorsi-humerus.jpg)

## Identify it

- Large, fan-shaped muscle of the lower back, sweeping up to the arm
- Known as the "climber's or rower's muscle"

## Key features

| Feature | Latissimus dorsi |
|---|---|
| Proximal attachment | Lower thoracic spinous processes, thoracolumbar fascia, iliac crest |
| Distal attachment | Floor of the intertubercular groove of the humerus |
| Action | Adduction, extension, and medial rotation of the arm |
| Innervation | Thoracodorsal nerve |
| Blood supply | Thoracodorsal artery |

The slides give the spinous processes as T6 to T12. The handout gives T7 to T12 and adds the inferior 3 to 4 ribs.

> The latissimus dorsi adducts, extends, and medially rotates the arm and is innervated by the thoracodorsal nerve.

Library: Rhomboid major and minor
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Two posterior views of the back and upper limb side by side, the left panel highlighting rhomboid major in pink and the right panel highlighting rhomboid minor in purple just above it, both running from the spinous processes to the medial border of the scapula](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-rhomboid-major-minor.jpg)

## Identify it

- Both lie deep to the trapezius, running obliquely from the midline to the scapula
- Rhomboid minor: narrow, thicker, and superior
- Rhomboid major: wider, flat, and more inferior
- The separation between the two isn't always distinct

## Key features

| Feature | Rhomboid minor | Rhomboid major |
|---|---|---|
| Proximal attachment | Spinous processes C7 to T1 | Spinous processes T2 to T5 |
| Distal attachment | Spine of the scapula (medial edge) | Medial border of the scapula inferior to the spine |
| Action | Retraction of the scapula | Retraction of the scapula |
| Innervation | Dorsal scapular nerve | Dorsal scapular nerve |
| Blood supply | Dorsal scapular artery | Dorsal scapular artery |

The handout adds downward rotation of the scapula and fixing the scapula to the thoracic wall as actions of both.

> Both rhomboids retract the scapula and are supplied by the dorsal scapular nerve and artery.

Library: Levator scapulae muscle
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Posterior view of the back and upper limb with the levator scapulae highlighted in blue, running from the side of the neck to the superior angle of the scapula](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-levator-scapulae.jpg)

## Identify it

- Relatively thin, strap-like muscle on the side and back of the neck
- Lies deep to the trapezius
- Runs from the upper cervical transverse processes down to the top of the scapula

## Key features

| Feature | Levator scapulae |
|---|---|
| Proximal attachment | Transverse processes of C1 to C4 |
| Distal attachment | Medial border and superior angle of the scapula |
| Action | Elevates the scapula and rotates it, directing the glenoid cavity inferiorly |
| Innervation | Dorsal scapular nerve (handout adds anterior rami of C3 and C4) |
| Blood supply | Dorsal scapular artery |

The levator scapulae shares its nerve and artery (dorsal scapular) with the rhomboids. It shares scapular elevation with the trapezius, which is innervated by CN XI instead.

> The levator scapulae elevates the scapula and turns the glenoid cavity inferiorly; it's innervated by the dorsal scapular nerve.

Library: Serratus posterior superior and inferior
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Two posterior views of the trunk with ribs and pelvis side by side, the left highlighting serratus posterior superior in pink on the upper ribs and the right highlighting serratus posterior inferior in teal on the lower ribs](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-serratus-posterior.jpg)

## Identify it

- Thin rectangular muscles of the intermediate layer
- Superior one lies deep to the rhomboids, angling down to the upper ribs
- Inferior one lies deep to the latissimus dorsi, angling up to the lower ribs

## Key features

| Feature | Serratus posterior superior | Serratus posterior inferior |
|---|---|---|
| Proximal attachment | Nuchal ligament, spinous processes C7 to T3 | Spinous processes T11 to L2 |
| Distal attachment | Superior borders of ribs 2 to 4 | Inferior borders of ribs 8 to 12 |
| Action | Proprioception; elevates ribs | Proprioception; depresses ribs |
| Innervation | 2nd to 5th intercostal nerves | 9th to 12th intercostal nerves |

The intercostal nerves here are anterior rami (T2 to T5 and T9 to T12). These muscles are associated with movements of the thoracic cage, and the handout ties the rib movement to respiration.

> Serratus posterior superior elevates ribs 2 to 4; serratus posterior inferior depresses ribs 8 to 12; both are innervated by intercostal nerves.

Library: Splenius muscle
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Posterior view of the neck and upper thorax labeled splenius capitis running up to the skull and splenius cervicis running up to the cervical vertebrae](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-splenius.jpg)

## Identify it

- Thick, flat muscle on the posterolateral side of the neck, deep to the trapezius
- Fibers run upward and laterally from the midline like a bandage ("splenius" is Latin for bandage)

## Key features

| Feature | Splenius |
|---|---|
| Inferior (origin) | Nuchal ligament and spinous processes C7 to T6 |
| Superior (insertion) | Occipital bone and superior nuchal line (capitis); transverse processes C1 to C3 or C4 (cervicis) |
| Unilateral action | Lateral flexion of the neck and rotation of the head to the same side |
| Bilateral action | Extension of the head and neck |
| Innervation | Posterior rami of spinal nerves |

The two parts are named by where they attach: capitis to the head, cervicis to the neck. The handout notes you don't need to distinguish them.

> Splenius acting alone rotates the head to the same side; both sides together extend the head and neck.

Library: Semispinalis muscle
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Posterior view of the vertebral column, ribs, and pelvis with the semispinalis muscle labeled as a long vertical muscle beside the spinous processes reaching the skull](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-semispinalis.jpg)

## Identify it

- Vertically oriented muscle close to the spinous processes
- Lies deep to the splenius
- Each bundle spans 4 to 6 vertebral levels

## Key features

| Feature | Semispinalis |
|---|---|
| Superior attachment | Occipital bone between the superior and inferior nuchal lines; spinous processes 4 to 6 segments above |
| Inferior attachment | Transverse processes from C4 down to the thoracic region |
| Action | Extends and contralaterally rotates the head and vertebral column |
| Innervation | Posterior rami of spinal nerves |

- Three parts named by superior attachment: semispinalis thoracis, cervicis, and capitis (you don't need to distinguish them)
- The slides give the inferior attachment as C4 to T12, and the handout as C4 to T10

## Look-alikes

| Muscle | Rotation of the head |
|---|---|
| Splenius | Same side |
| Semispinalis | Opposite side (contralateral) |

> Semispinalis extends and contralaterally rotates the head and vertebral column.

Library: Erector spinae muscles
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

![Posterior view of the deep back muscles labeled spinalis muscle (medial), longissimus muscle (intermediate), and iliocostalis muscle (lateral)](library/images/msk-week-13/vert-erector-spinae.jpg)

## Identify it

- Vertical column of muscles next to the spinal column, between the spinous processes and the angles of the ribs
- Three columns from medial to lateral: spinalis, longissimus, iliocostalis
- Longissimus is usually the widest

## Key features

| Feature | Erector spinae |
|---|---|
| Origin | Broad tendon from the posterior iliac crest, posterior sacrum, sacroiliac ligaments, and sacral and inferior lumbar spinous processes |
| Iliocostalis insertion | Angles of ribs and cervical transverse processes |
| Longissimus insertion | Ribs between tubercle and angle, thoracic and cervical transverse processes, mastoid process |
| Spinalis insertion | Upper thoracic spinous processes and skull |
| Action | Unilateral: lateral flexion of the column; bilateral: extension of the column and head |
| Innervation | Posterior rami of spinal nerves |

Deep back muscles help maintain posture and control movements of the vertebral column.

> Erector spinae columns from medial to lateral are spinalis, longissimus, and iliocostalis, innervated by posterior rami.

Library: Back sprain, strain, and spasm
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Vertebral Column and Back

## Key features

The handout separates three common back injuries by which tissue is involved.

| Injury | Tissue involved | Cause or trigger |
|---|---|---|
| Back sprain | Only ligamentous tissue, or the ligament-bone attachment | Excessively strong contractions with movements such as excessive extension or rotation |
| Back strain | Muscle fibers stretched or microscopically torn, especially the erector spinae | Weight not properly balanced on the vertebral column |
| Back spasm | One or more muscle groups contract suddenly and involuntarily | Protective response after injury or to inflammation, for example of ligaments |

- Back strain is the most common cause of low back pain
- Spasms cause cramps, pain, involuntary movement, and a distortion of the vertebral column

These link back to the structures in this lecture. Sprains involve the vertebral ligaments, strains most often involve the erector spinae, and the pain is carried by the meningeal branches and posterior rami that supply the back.

> A sprain involves ligaments; a strain involves muscle fibers (especially erector spinae) and is the most common cause of low back pain.

Library: Connective tissue definition and members
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Two H&E panels: loose connective tissue with scattered cells in a mesh of thin fibers (letters F and E mark structures the slide does not define), and epithelial tissue made of closely packed cells lining a surface](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-loose-ct-vs-epithelium.jpg)

## Identify it

- Connective tissue: cells are few and far between, and the extracellular matrix (ECM) is usually predominant
- In the lecture's comparison, epithelial tissue is a sheet of closely packed cells, while loose connective tissue is mostly fibers with scattered cells

## Key features

- Broadly, connective tissue is cells plus ECM, and the presence of ECM is what classifies a tissue as connective tissue
- The body has four tissue types: epithelial, muscle, nervous, and connective
- Bone, cartilage, adipose tissue, tendons, and blood are all connective tissue
- Vascularity varies: the dermis of the skin is highly vascularized, while cartilage, ligaments, and tendon are relatively avascular (which matters for wound healing)

![Diagram of an arm showing the four tissue types, with connective tissue examples labeled: loose connective tissue (protein fibers, soft extracellular matrix, cells), bone, cartilage, and blood](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-tissue-types-diagram.jpg)

| Connective tissue | Matrix (from the lecture figure) |
|---|---|
| Loose connective tissue | Soft matrix with protein fibers; padding under skin and elsewhere |
| Bone and cartilage | Hard or stiff matrix |
| Blood | Liquid matrix |

> Connective tissue is cells plus extracellular matrix, and the ECM (not the cells) is what makes bone, cartilage, adipose tissue, and blood count as connective tissue.

Library: Mesenchymal cells and connective tissue cell origins
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![H&E of fetal kidney mesenchyme, labeled mesenchymal cells, collagen fibres, and cell processes](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-fetal-kidney-mesenchymal-cells.jpg)

## Identify it

- Undifferentiated cells with slender cell processes
- Scattered among fine collagen fibers in a loose, pale matrix

## Key features

- Connective tissue cells come from two sources: the mesenchyme and hematopoietic stem cells
- Mesenchymal cells are undifferentiated cells derived from the mesoderm (multipotent stem cells)
- Mesenchyme is abundant in the embryo; in adults, mesenchymal stem cells are found in bone marrow but are less abundant
- Hematopoietic stem cells (also in bone marrow) give rise to macrophages, mast cells, and the cells seen in blood

![Mesengenic process diagram: a mesenchymal stem cell (MSC) proliferates and commits to osteogenesis, chondrogenesis, myogenesis, marrow stroma, tendogenesis or ligamentogenesis, and adipogenesis, maturing into osteocytes (bone), hypertrophic chondrocytes (cartilage), myotubes (muscle), stromal cells (marrow), tendon and ligament fibroblasts, adipocytes, and dermal and other cells (connective tissue)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-mesengenic-process.jpg)

## Lineages

| Source | Cells it gives rise to |
|---|---|
| Mesenchymal stem cell | Osteocytes, chondrocytes, myotubes, marrow stromal cells, tendon and ligament fibroblasts, adipocytes |
| Hematopoietic stem cell | Macrophages, mast cells, blood cells |

> Connective tissue cells come either from multipotent mesenchymal cells (fibroblasts, adipocytes, bone and cartilage cells) or from hematopoietic stem cells (macrophages, mast cells, blood cells).

Library: Fibroblasts: active and inactive forms
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Drawings of a large branched fibroblast and a thin spindle-shaped fibroblast, above two H&E sections of fibroblasts in connective tissue](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-fibroblast-active-inactive.jpg)

## Identify it

- Inactive fibroblast: smaller, spindle-shaped cell with a dark-staining nucleus
- Active fibroblast: cell has grown in size, with lighter-staining cytoplasm and a denser arrangement of endoplasmic reticulum (ER)

![Electron micrograph of a fibroblast in active state, labeled RER and GA, surrounded by collagen fibers in cross-section and collagen fibers in longitudinal section (scale bar 1.0 µm)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-fibroblast-em-active-state.jpg)

## Key features

- One of the most abundant cell types in connective tissue and one of the main cells that secretes ECM (the "work horse" of connective tissue)
- In the activated state, fibroblasts are highly secretory and produce the abundant matrix fibers of the ECM
- The fibroblast is the main producer of collagen; because collagen is secreted, an active fibroblast has abundant ER and a very active secretory pathway
- Other connective tissue cells can make collagen too, but the fibroblast does most of the work
- Fibroblasts have a very important role in wound healing and fibrosis
- A reticular cell is a specialized fibroblast that secretes reticular fibers (type III collagen)

> An active fibroblast is enlarged with pale cytoplasm and abundant ER for collagen secretion; an inactive fibroblast is small and spindle-shaped with a dark nucleus.

Library: White adipose tissue
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![H&E of white adipose tissue at low power and at higher power, labeled nucleus, lipid droplet, and blood vessel](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-white-adipose-tissue.jpg)

## Identify it

- Cells look "empty": the large lipid droplet is washed out during histological processing
- The lipid droplet takes up most of the cytoplasm
- Nucleus is pushed to the periphery of the cell
- Small blood vessels run between the adipocytes

## Key features

- Adipocytes are specialized for energy storage, with fat stored as triglycerides
- Found in fat pads, around organs, and subcutaneously (deeper layers of the skin), where they function like bubble wrap
- The type of fat most often seen in adults is "white fat"
- They're not just for energy storage: adipocytes secrete signaling molecules called adipokines
- Adipokines help regulate eating habits, and some play important roles in glucose metabolism

> White adipocytes look empty because the triglyceride in their single large lipid droplet washes out during processing, leaving the nucleus pushed to the cell periphery.

Library: Brown adipose tissue
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![H&E of brown adipose tissue beside an electron micrograph of brown fat labeled Ld (lipid droplet) and M (mitochondria)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-brown-adipose-he-em.jpg)

## Identify it

- Contains lipid droplets, but the droplets are much smaller than in white fat
- Mitochondria are much more abundant
- Nuclei are not pushed to the periphery of the cell

## Function

- Brown adipose tissue is found in infants (and many animals)
- Brown fat cells have a modified electron transport system: instead of producing ATP, it produces heat
- Its main function is thermal regulation
- Beige fat cells, found in adults, have properties in between brown and white fat cells, and their presence generally correlates with better health indices
- Converting white fat into brown or beige fat is an active area of research

## Look-alikes

| Feature | White fat | Brown fat |
|---|---|---|
| Lipid droplet | One large droplet filling most of the cell | Smaller droplets |
| Mitochondria | Fewer | Much more abundant |
| Nucleus | Pushed to the periphery | Not pushed to the periphery |
| Main job | Energy storage, adipokines | Heat production |
| Where | Most fat in adults | Infants |

> Brown fat has small lipid droplets, central nuclei, and abundant mitochondria whose modified electron transport system makes heat instead of ATP.

Library: Mast cells and basophils
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Electron micrograph of a mast cell packed with numerous dark, electron-dense granules, with cytoplasmic projections at its surface](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-mast-cell-em.jpg)

## Identify it

- Under the electron microscope, numerous electron-dense granules fill the cytoplasm
- Mast cells often have more numerous cytoplasmic projections than basophils
- Found in tissue (solid connective tissue)

## Key features

- Derived from hematopoietic stem cells in the bone marrow
- Part of the innate immune system
- Granules contain histamine, heparin (a glycosaminoglycan, or GAG), proteases, cytokines, and mediators of inflammation
- Mast cells are covered with IgE receptors
- They act as beacons, detecting infection or foreign substances and releasing molecules that recruit and stimulate other immune cells

![Electron micrograph of a basophil with numerous granules, labeled lobes of nucleus](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-basophil-em.jpg)

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Mast cell | Basophil |
|---|---|---|
| Location | Tissue | Blood |
| Granules | Numerous, with histamine | Numerous, with histamine |
| Origin | Hematopoietic stem cells | Hematopoietic stem cells |
| Cytoplasmic projections | More numerous | Fewer |

> Mast cells and basophils both carry numerous histamine granules, but mast cells live in connective tissue and basophils circulate in blood.

Library: Mast cell degranulation and allergy
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Schematic of a mast cell with IgE antibodies on its surface binding an allergen, histamine-containing granules inside, and degranulation releasing inflammatory mediators that cause symptoms (rashes, wheezing, vomiting)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-mast-cell-degranulation.jpg)

## Mechanism

1. An allergen binds the IgE receptors on the surface of a mast cell
2. IgE receptor activation starts downstream signaling inside the cell
3. The cell degranulates, releasing histamine and other cytokines by regulated exocytosis
4. Histamine binds histamine receptors on blood vessels and increases their permeability
5. Additional immune cells (such as neutrophils) and other mediators of inflammation are recruited

These released molecules are responsible for the symptoms of allergy.

## Clinical links

- Allergic rhinitis: runny nose, sneezing, and itchy, red eyes; the common allergen is pollen
- Anaphylaxis: a very severe, life-threatening allergic reaction (bee sting, peanuts) caused by massive mast cell degranulation and the inflammation that follows
- Antihistamines such as diphenhydramine (Benadryl) and loratadine (Claritin) bind the histamine receptor and block histamine from binding

![Diagram of a mast cell releasing histamine toward histamine receptors on blood vessels and a nerve, with anti-histamine occupying the histamine receptor](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-antihistamine-mechanism.jpg)

> Allergen binding to IgE on mast cells triggers degranulation (regulated exocytosis) of histamine, which increases blood vessel permeability; antihistamines block the histamine receptor.

Library: Macrophages: origin and tissue-specific names
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Scanning electron micrograph of a macrophage with rod-shaped bacteria on and around it](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-macrophage-sem.jpg)

## Key features

- "Macrophage" literally translates to "big eater"
- Derived from hematopoietic stem cells in the bone marrow
- Part of the innate immune system, and a professional phagocytic cell
- Phagocytoses bacteria and other pathogens, dead cells, and cellular debris (the clean-up crew)
- Needs abundant lysosomes, which are acidic, degradative organelles full of acid hydrolases
- Also an antigen presenting cell: it shows degraded peptides from what it has eaten to T cells, giving it a role in the adaptive immune response

![Diagram of the macrophage lineage: HSC, GM-CFU, M-CFU, monoblast, and pro-monocyte in bone marrow; inflammatory and resident monocytes in peripheral blood; and tissue macrophages named osteoclast (bone), microglial cell (CNS), alveolar macrophage (lung), Kupffer cell (liver), histiocyte (connective tissue), and white-pulp, red-pulp, marginal-zone, and metallophilic macrophages (spleen)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-macrophage-lineage.jpg)

## Tissue names

Once macrophages settle in a tissue they take on slightly different roles and get different names, but they share one lineage and are all phagocytic.

| Tissue | Macrophage name |
|---|---|
| Bone | Osteoclast |
| Liver | Kupffer cell |
| Central nervous system | Microglial cell |
| Lung | Alveolar macrophage |
| Connective tissue | Histiocyte |

When identifying these cells in histology or pathology, use the tissue name: "osteoclast" may be the correct answer where "macrophage" isn't enough.

> Macrophages come from hematopoietic stem cells and take tissue-specific names: osteoclast in bone, Kupffer cell in liver, microglial cell in the CNS.

Library: Macrophages in tuberculosis and leishmaniasis
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of granuloma formation in tuberculosis: Mycobacterium taken up by alveolar macrophages, infected macrophages and mononuclear cells gathering, foamy macrophages and lymphocytes forming a granuloma surrounded by a fibrous cuff with caseum in the center, and a necrotic, caseating granuloma center releasing free mycobacteria into the airway](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-tuberculosis-granuloma.jpg)

Some pathogens have evolved ways to survive inside the macrophage, the cell that's supposed to kill them.

## Mycobacterium tuberculosis

- Causative agent of tuberculosis
- Phagocytosed by alveolar macrophages
- Once inside, the bacterium prevents fusion of the phagosome with the lysosome, so it avoids being killed and lives inside the macrophage
- The immune response tries to seal off the infected cells by forming a granuloma, which is usually pretty effective
- Symptoms appear and the patient becomes contagious only when some unknown event triggers the granuloma to burst

## Leishmania

- A protozoan parasite transmitted by the sandfly
- Present in the promastigote form within the sandfly
- After infecting the macrophage, it transforms into the amastigote form, which is very resistant to killing
- Unlike M. tuberculosis, it does get targeted to the lysosome, but the amastigote survives and thrives inside the lysosome

## Comparison

| Pathogen | How it survives the macrophage |
|---|---|
| Mycobacterium tuberculosis | Blocks phagosome-lysosome fusion |
| Leishmania (amastigote) | Tolerates the harsh lysosome environment |

> M. tuberculosis survives in macrophages by blocking phagosome-lysosome fusion, while Leishmania amastigotes survive inside the lysosome itself.

Library: Macrophage foam cells in atherosclerosis
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of an atherosclerotic plaque in the intima: monocytes entering past endothelial cells (persistent influx), foam cells loaded with cholesterol releasing inflammatory cytokines, defective egress, a necrotic core from secondary necrosis due to defective efferocytosis, and a thinning fibrous cap, above the smooth muscle cells of the media](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-atherosclerosis-foam-cells.jpg)

## Key features

Atherosclerosis is essentially a non-resolving inflammatory disease, and macrophages play a prominent role in it.

1. Build-up of plaque recruits macrophages
2. Macrophages try to ingest and metabolize the lipid
3. The very high lipid content at the plaque transforms them into "foam cells"
