4. Foam cells are sick and don't function properly: they become less motile and secrete high levels of inflammatory mediators
5. The high lipid content is associated with endoplasmic reticulum (ER) stress
6. Sustained ER stress can kill these cells, forming a necrotic core
7. The plaque becomes destabilized and can form a blood clot, putting the patient at risk of myocardial infarction or stroke

## What to remember

The lecture calls this a highly simplified view of an active research area. The two things to know are that there's a connection between macrophages and atherosclerosis, and the general mechanism (lipid-loaded foam cells, ER stress, necrotic core, unstable plaque).

> In atherosclerosis, lipid-loaded macrophages become foam cells; sustained ER stress kills them and forms a necrotic core that destabilizes the plaque.

Library: Extracellular matrix: fibers and ground substance
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of the extracellular matrix above a cell, labeled proteoglycan molecule, proteoglycan complex, polysaccharide molecule, collagen fiber, integrin, plasma membrane, and microfilaments of cytoskeleton](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-ecm-diagram.jpg)

## Key features

- The extracellular matrix (ECM) is the combination of fibers and ground substance
- The two types of fibers are collagen and elastic (reticular fibers are a specialized fiber made of type III collagen)
- People often think of the fibers first because they're easy to see under the microscope
- Ground substance is hard to visualize with histological techniques, but it's present in the ECM and enables the tissue to withstand compressive forces
- The three main kinds of ground substance are glycosaminoglycans (GAGs), proteoglycans, and multi-adhesive glycoproteins
- Integrins in the plasma membrane link the ECM outside the cell to the cytoskeleton inside the cell

## ECM components

| Component | Examples | Main job |
|---|---|---|
| Fibers | Collagen, elastic | Tensile strength (collagen) and elasticity (elastic) |
| Ground substance | GAGs, proteoglycans, multi-adhesive glycoproteins | Resist compressive forces, adhesion |

> The ECM is fibers (collagen and elastic) plus ground substance (GAGs, proteoglycans, and multi-adhesive glycoproteins), and the ground substance lets tissue withstand compression.

Library: Collagen alpha chains and collagen types
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Molecular model of the collagen triple helix, with red, green, and blue representing different alpha chains](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-collagen-triple-helix.jpg)

## Key features

- Proteins called "collagen" are the most abundant proteins in the human body, but collagen isn't a single protein
- There are 25 collagen genes, and each is known as an alpha chain
- Three alpha chains come together into a triple helix, the basic unit of collagen
- Inside the cell this triple helix is called procollagen; outside the cell, after its terminal non-helical segments are cleaved off, it's called tropocollagen
- Mixing and matching alpha chains (like letters of an alphabet) gives 28 collagen types, named with Roman numerals
- Each type has a unique composition of alpha chains suited to particular functions
- Every third amino acid is glycine, because the helix is wound so tightly that only glycine's side chain is small enough to fit in the groove
- Proline and a modified proline, hydroxyproline, are also common
- The fibroblast is the main cell that produces collagen

## Collagen classes in this lecture

| Class | Types | Key point |
|---|---|---|
| Fibril forming | I, II, III | Most common types |
| Fibril associated (FACIT) | IX, XII | Don't form fibrils; organize other fibrils |
| Network forming | IV, VII | Form a mesh in the basal lamina |

> Collagen is a triple helix of three alpha chains (25 genes, 28 types), and glycine sits at every third position because only it fits inside the tightly wound helix.

Library: Collagen synthesis: procollagen to type I fiber
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of type I collagen assembly: (a) pro-alpha peptide chains, two alpha1(I) and one alpha2(I), self-assemble into (b) procollagen; procollagen peptidase removes the loose termini to give (c) tropocollagen, which self-assembles into (d, e) a collagen fibril and then (f) a type I collagen fiber](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-type-i-collagen-assembly.jpg)

Type I collagen is a fibril-forming collagen. It's the most abundant collagen type and is found almost everywhere.

## The problem

Collagen peptides and procollagen molecules tend to self-associate into fibrils, fibers, and bundles. If fibrils formed inside the cell, they would interfere with normal cellular processes and be toxic (the slide puts it as "the cell is dead"). Fibril formation has to happen in the extracellular space.

## The solution

1. Three alpha chains form a triple helix inside the cell, called procollagen
2. Procollagen has terminal ends that don't form a helix, and these ends prevent fibril formation
3. Procollagen is secreted from the cell
4. A protease that's only active in the extracellular space cleaves off the terminal ends, giving tropocollagen
5. Tropocollagen molecules pack together into a fibril, stabilized by lateral interactions and hydrogen bonds between hydroxylated proline residues
6. Numerous fibrils pack together into a fiber, and fibers associate into a bundle

Like the cable of a suspension bridge, made of many smaller cables, this layered structure is ideally suited for high tensile strength.

> Procollagen's non-helical terminal ends block fibril formation inside the cell; an extracellular protease removes them, so tropocollagen assembles into fibrils only outside the cell.

Library: Dense irregular versus dense regular connective tissue
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![H&E of skin labeled stratum corneum, epidermis, papillary dermis, reticular dermis, and hypodermis](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-dermis-layers-he.jpg)

## Identify it

- Dense irregular (deeper layers of the skin): collagen bundles run in many different directions
- Dense regular (tendon): densely packed collagen fibers all aligned in a single plane

![H&E of tendon showing densely packed collagen fibers running in parallel](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-tendon-dense-regular-he.jpg)

## Key features

- Dermis and bone both contain mostly type I collagen, yet the collagen is organized differently
- Mechanical forces on skin act in many different directions, so its collagen runs in many directions
- In tendons, ligaments, and bone, collagen fibers are organized to provide strength in one particular direction
- Fibroblasts and other cells in the ECM can shape how collagen is organized
- Fibril associated (FACIT) collagens also help organize collagen into higher order structures

![Electron micrograph of bone collagen arranged in stacked layers of densely packed fibrils (scale bar 5 µm)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-bone-collagen-em.jpg)

## Look-alikes

| Type | Collagen arrangement | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Dense irregular | Many directions | Deeper layers of the skin |
| Dense regular | Aligned in a single plane | Tendons, ligaments |

> The same type I collagen is organized differently by tissue: aligned in one plane in tendon (dense regular) and running in many directions in the deeper skin (dense irregular).

Library: Tendon fiber bundle hierarchy
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of tendon structure labeled collagen fibril, collagen fiber, primary fiber bundle (subfascicle), secondary fiber bundle (fascicle), tertiary fiber bundle, endotenon, epitenon, and paratenon, beside a drawing of the Achilles tendon at the heel](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-tendon-structure-diagram.jpg)

## Key features

Tendons and ligaments are dense regular connective tissue. They contain few cells, are relatively avascular, and are built from large bundles of collagen oriented in a single plane, which gives strength in one direction.

Like a type I collagen fiber, a tendon is assembled in steps, from small units into larger and larger bundles:

1. Collagen fibril
2. Collagen fiber
3. Primary fiber bundle (subfascicle)
4. Secondary fiber bundle (fascicle)
5. Tertiary fiber bundle

The lecture figure also labels the connective tissue layers around these bundles (endotenon, epitenon, and paratenon) without describing them further.

## Why it matters

The same features that make a tendon strong (few cells, poor blood supply, tightly aligned collagen) make tendon and ligament injuries much harder to repair than a skin wound.

> A tendon is dense regular connective tissue built as a hierarchy: collagen fibrils form fibers, which form primary, secondary, and tertiary fiber bundles.

Library: FACIT fibril associated collagens
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of type IX collagen molecules bound along fibrils of type II collagen](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-facit-type-ix-on-type-ii.jpg)

## Key features

- Fibril associated collagens don't form fibrils; they're also called FACIT collagens
- Classic examples are type IX and type XII
- Their triple helix is interrupted by non-helical segments, which makes them more flexible
- They retain their propeptides (terminal ends), and this inhibits fibril formation
- They bind laterally along fibrils formed by other collagen types and also bind other ECM molecules
- They're thought to help organize fibrillar collagens into fibers and fiber bundles

![Diagram of a procollagen molecule labeled N-terminal propeptide, telopeptides, a 300 nm triple-helix domain, and C-terminal propeptide, with the collagen portion marked within the full procollagen](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-procollagen-domains.jpg)

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Fibril forming collagens | FACIT collagens |
|---|---|---|
| Examples | I, II, III | IX, XII |
| Propeptides | Cleaved after secretion | Retained |
| Form fibrils | Yes | No |
| Role | Build fibrils and fibers | Organize other collagen fibrils |

> FACIT collagens (types IX and XII) keep their propeptides, so they can't form fibrils; they bind along other collagen fibrils and help organize them.

Library: Type II and type III collagen
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

## Type II collagen

- A fibril forming collagen and the predominant collagen type in cartilage
- Mutations in genes for type II collagen, and for type IX collagen, are associated with Stickler syndrome
- Because type IX is a FACIT collagen that organizes collagen fibers in many tissues, these mutations cause a wide range of symptoms

## Stickler syndrome findings

- Myopia and increased risk of retinal detachment
- Hearing loss
- Cleft palate
- Flattened face and small nose
- A variety of joint defects

## Type III collagen

- Technically a fibril forming collagen, but it forms specialized fibers called reticular fibers, which are thin and branching
- Reticular fibers are made by reticular cells, a specialized type of fibroblast
- Don't confuse reticular cells with reticulocytes (immature red blood cells)
- Reticular fibers form a support structure for organs such as the liver, spleen, adipose tissue, and lymph nodes

## Summary

| Type | Fiber | Where |
|---|---|---|
| II | Fibril forming | Cartilage |
| III | Reticular fibers (thin, branching) | Supporting liver, spleen, adipose, lymph nodes |

> Stickler syndrome comes from mutations in type II or type IX collagen genes and causes myopia, retinal detachment risk, hearing loss, cleft palate, and joint defects.

Library: Network forming collagens and the basal lamina
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of type IV collagen assembly: single alpha-chain monomers with a 7S end, a triple-helical domain, and an NC1 domain form a protomer (a trimer of alpha chains); NC1 hexamers join protomers into dimers; 7S domains join them into a tetramer; and these build a meshwork suprastructure](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-type-iv-collagen-network.jpg)

## Network forming collagens

- Types IV and VII
- They don't form fibrils; they form an intricate network, or "mesh"
- Each triple helix forms a dimer, and dimers associate to form a tetramer
- The end result is a meshwork of collagen that acts as a scaffold for cells
- They're found mainly in the basement membrane (basal lamina)

## Basal lamina

- Found between an epithelial layer and the underlying connective tissue, and also surrounds muscles and blood vessels
- Made of a meshwork of ECM proteins: type IV collagen, glycoproteins such as laminin and fibronectin, and GAGs
- Serves as a scaffold and separates the cellular layer from the underlying connective tissue
- Forms a physical barrier between cells and the underlying tissue (think cancer metastasis)
- In the kidney, it's specialized as a highly selective filtration device

![Diagram of the basal lamina on a plasma membrane, labeled type IV collagen, laminin, nidogen, perlecan, and integrin, with an inset showing how these components bind one another](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-basal-lamina-components.jpg)

Laminin (a basal lamina glycoprotein) isn't the same as lamin, which is an intermediate filament of the nuclear envelope.

> Network forming collagens (types IV and VII) build a mesh instead of fibrils, and type IV collagen is the main collagen of the basal lamina.

Library: Hemidesmosomes and integrins
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of a hemidesmosome: (A) keratin filaments in an epithelial cell anchored by hemidesmosomes to the basal lamina; (B) enlarged view labeled keratin, dystonin, plectin, integrin (alpha6 beta4), collagen XVII, laminin, and collagen](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-hemidesmosome.jpg)

## Hemidesmosomes

- Hemidesmosomes connect the epithelial layer to the basal lamina
- Integrins on epithelial cells interact with collagens and glycoproteins in the basal lamina
- Integrins in hemidesmosomes connect to intermediate filaments (keratin in the figure)

## Integrins

- Cell-surface receptors in the plasma membrane that link the cellular layer to the underlying connective tissue
- Each integrin is a complex of an alpha chain and a beta chain
- There are 24 genes for alpha chains and 9 genes for beta chains, so there are many integrin types
- Many integrins bind glycoproteins such as fibronectin and laminin outside the cell
- Inside the cell, most integrins bind the actin cytoskeleton, connecting the ECM to actin

![Diagram of active integrins, each with an alpha subunit and a beta subunit, binding an extracellular matrix protein outside the cell and linking through talin and vinculin to actin filaments inside the cell](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-integrin-actin-link.jpg)

## Look-alikes

| Integrin setting | Binds outside | Links inside to |
|---|---|---|
| Most integrins | Fibronectin, laminin | Actin cytoskeleton |
| Hemidesmosome integrin | Basal lamina collagens and glycoproteins | Intermediate filaments |

> Most integrins link the ECM to the actin cytoskeleton, but integrins in hemidesmosomes link the basal lamina to intermediate filaments.

Library: Dystrophin-glycoprotein complex
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of the dystrophin-glycoprotein complex in skeletal muscle: laminin 2 in the extracellular matrix binds alpha- and beta-dystroglycan in the sarcolemma, dystrophin links dystroglycan to actin filaments in the cytoplasm, and the sarcoglycan complex, sarcospan, syntrophins, and alpha-dystrobrevin associate with it](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-dystrophin-glycoprotein-complex.jpg)

## Key features

In addition to integrins, skeletal muscle cells have an additional link between the cell and the ECM, the dystrophin-glycoprotein complex.

1. Laminin in the ECM binds dystroglycan
2. Dystroglycan is a glycoprotein found in the ECM and on the plasma membrane
3. On the cellular side, dystroglycan binds dystrophin
4. Dystrophin binds the actin cytoskeleton inside the cell

The sarcoglycan complex associates with these proteins and helps stabilize the complex.

## Disease links

| Mutated protein | Disease |
|---|---|
| Dystrophin | Duchenne muscular dystrophy |
| Sarcoglycan complex | Limb-girdle muscular dystrophy |

## Big picture

Both integrins and the dystrophin-glycoprotein complex connect the cellular layer to the underlying connective tissue. The lecture asks you to think about why skeletal muscle, in particular, needs this extra link.

> In skeletal muscle, dystrophin links dystroglycan (which binds laminin) to actin; dystrophin mutations cause Duchenne muscular dystrophy and sarcoglycan mutations cause limb-girdle muscular dystrophy.

Library: Alport syndrome
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Glomerular filtration barrier: a diagram labeled endothelial cell with holes (fenestrations), glomerular basement membrane (GBM), epithelial cell foot processes attached by a thin membrane, and proteins kept in the capillary, beside an electron micrograph labeled fenestrae, endothelium, BM, and podocyte processes (P2)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-glomerular-basement-membrane.jpg)

## Normal filtration

In the kidney, the basal lamina is specialized for filtration. Proteins and larger molecular weight components are retained in the circulation, while small molecules and metabolic wastes pass through the basement membrane and leave as urine.

## Key findings

- Caused by mutations in type IV collagen genes
- Defective glomerular basement membrane: the basal lamina is weakened and kidney filtration is compromised
- Blood in the urine and high levels of protein in the urine (proteinuria)
- The most common form is X-linked, so males are often more severely affected
- Type IV collagen is also found in the ear and in the eye (lens), so hearing loss and vision loss are often seen

> Alport syndrome is caused by type IV collagen mutations (most often X-linked) that weaken the glomerular basement membrane, giving hematuria, proteinuria, and hearing and vision loss.

Library: Elastic fibers in loose connective tissue and the aorta
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Loose connective tissue spread labeled elastic fibers, collagen fibers, fibroblast nuclei, mesothelial cell nuclei, and a mast cell](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-loose-ct-elastic-fibers.jpg)

## Identify it

- Elastic fibers are thin and stain darkly
- Collagen fibers are thicker and more lightly stained in the same section
- In the wall of the aorta, elastic fibers appear as many dark, wavy lines

![H&E of an elastic artery (aorta) with arrows pointing to elastic fibers](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-elastic-artery-aorta.jpg)

## Key features

- As the name suggests, elastic fibers give tissues elasticity
- The character of a tissue depends on its proportion of collagen fibers versus elastic fibers
- Elastic fibers are most abundant in tissues that need to expand and then regain their original shape
- This is typified by arteries, and especially the aorta

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Elastic fibers | Collagen fibers |
|---|---|---|
| Thickness | Thin | Thicker |
| Staining | Darker | Lighter |
| Property | Elasticity | Tensile strength |
| Classic site | Aorta | Tendon, dermis, bone |

> Elastic fibers are thin and stain darker than the thicker, lighter collagen fibers, and they're especially abundant in the aorta.

Library: Elastin, fibrillin, and elastic recoil
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Schematic of an elastic fiber with an elastin core surrounded by microfibrils, above scanning electron micrographs of a fiber meshwork (scale bar 100 µm) and of the aorta (scale bar 1 mm)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-elastic-fiber-schematic-sem.jpg)

## Assembly

Like collagen, elastic fibers are assembled step by step.

1. Microfibrils form first
2. Microfibrils contain the protein fibrillin
3. The microfibrils organize elastin polypeptides into a fiber
4. Elastin is the main protein component of the finished fiber

## Why elastic fibers stretch and recoil

- Elastin has many hydrophobic segments, so it naturally avoids water
- In the relaxed state, elastin takes a random coiled conformation with the hydrophobic segments tucked inside the coil
- Lysine residues in elastic fibers are cross-linked
- When force is applied, the fibers stretch, and the cross-links keep them from breaking
- When the force is removed, the hydrophobic nature of elastin makes the fiber coil back into its favored relaxed conformation

The result is a fiber with the properties of a rubber band.

![Diagram of an elastic fiber made of elastin molecules joined by cross-links, shown relaxed as random coils and stretched into extended chains](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-elastin-stretch-relax.jpg)

> Fibrillin-containing microfibrils form first and organize elastin into a fiber; cross-linked, hydrophobic elastin stretches without breaking and recoils like a rubber band.

Library: Glycosaminoglycans and hyaluronic acid
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Chemical structure of a GAG chain: a repeating disaccharide of N-acetylglucosamine and glucuronic acid carrying negatively charged carboxyl and sulfate groups](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-gag-repeating-disaccharide.jpg)

## Ground substance

Ground substance is hard to see with histological techniques, but it enables tissue to withstand compressive forces. Its three main kinds are GAGs, proteoglycans, and multi-adhesive glycoproteins.

## GAGs (glycosaminoglycans)

- Unbranched polysaccharides
- Very negatively charged
- The charge gives them a high affinity for water, so they retain water in the matrix
- This gives the matrix the consistency of a gel and lets the tissue resist compression

## Hyaluronic acid versus other GAGs

- Hyaluronic acid is the largest and most abundant GAG
- It's often found associated with proteins, but it isn't covalently attached to a protein
- Other GAGs (heparan sulfate, dermatan sulfate, chondroitin sulfate, keratan sulfate) are covalently linked to protein, forming a proteoglycan

![Size comparison of a hyaluronan molecule (MW 8 x 10^6, spanning about 300 nm) with a globular protein, glycogen, spectrin, and collagen](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-hyaluronan-size-comparison.jpg)

| GAG | Linked to protein? |
|---|---|
| Hyaluronic acid | No |
| Heparan, dermatan, chondroitin, keratan sulfate | Yes (proteoglycan) |

> GAGs are unbranched, highly negatively charged polysaccharides that hold water, giving the matrix a gel consistency that resists compression; hyaluronic acid is the largest and isn't attached to protein.

Library: Proteoglycans and aggrecan
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of a proteoglycan linkage: a GAG chain attached to a serine of the core protein through a link tetrasaccharide of xylose, galactose, galactose, and glucuronic acid](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-proteoglycan-core-protein-link.jpg)

## Key features

- A proteoglycan is a protein backbone (core protein) covalently linked to GAGs
- Proteoglycans are negatively charged because their GAGs are highly negatively charged
- Like free GAGs, they help tissue resist compressive forces
- They can also bind and sequester signaling molecules such as fibroblast growth factor and certain chemokines, so they help regulate the biology of nearby cells

## Aggrecan

- The major component of cartilage
- Has over 100 GAG chains
- It's huge, big enough to be seen with electron microscopy (most protein complexes are too small to see by EM)

## Structure (from the lecture figures)

- Each GAG chain attaches to a serine on the core protein through a link tetrasaccharide (xylose, galactose, galactose, glucuronic acid)
- In the aggrecan aggregate, many aggrecan core proteins carrying keratan sulfate and chondroitin sulfate chains attach through link proteins to one long hyaluronan molecule

![Diagram of an aggrecan aggregate (scale 1 µm): aggrecan core proteins bearing keratan sulfate and chondroitin sulfate chains, attached by link proteins to a hyaluronan molecule](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-aggrecan-aggregate.jpg)

> Aggrecan, the major proteoglycan of cartilage, is a core protein carrying over 100 GAG chains, which makes it large enough to see by electron microscopy.

Library: Multi-adhesive glycoproteins: fibronectin and laminin
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of a multi-adhesive glycoprotein dimer joined by disulfide bonds near the C termini, labeled self-association, collagen-binding, cell-binding, and heparin-binding sites, a domain, and a module](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-fibronectin-binding-sites.jpg)

## Key features

- Glycoproteins are proteins covalently linked to carbohydrate, but the protein component predominates
- The carbohydrates are usually short, branched structures
- Most have binding sites for collagen, GAGs, and integrins, hence the name multi-adhesive glycoprotein
- Fibronectin has binding sites for GAGs, collagen, and integrins (on cell surfaces)
- Laminin attaches epithelial cells to the underlying basal lamina, and in skeletal muscle it binds dystroglycan
- Laminin and lamin aren't the same thing: lamin is an intermediate filament of the nuclear envelope

![Diagram of a cross-shaped glycoprotein built from alpha, beta, and gamma chains, labeled globular domains, an alpha-helical region, globular regions, and collagen-binding, cellular-binding, entactin-binding, integrin-binding, and dystroglycan-binding regions](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-laminin-binding-regions.jpg)

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Proteoglycan | Multi-adhesive glycoprotein |
|---|---|---|
| Carbohydrate | GAG chains | Short, branched sugars |
| Main role | Resist compression, sequester signals | Bind collagen, GAGs, and integrins |
| Example | Aggrecan | Fibronectin, laminin |

> Multi-adhesive glycoproteins such as fibronectin and laminin are mostly protein with short branched sugars, and they bind collagen, GAGs, and integrins.

Library: Skin wound healing: injury and inflammation
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Timeline of normal cutaneous wound healing from trauma to 14 days: haemostasis, inflammation, and cell migration; then cell proliferation, ECM synthesis, granulation tissue, angiogenesis, and re-epithelialisation; then remodelling, wound closure, and contraction, with the cell types active at each stage](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-wound-healing-stages-timeline.jpg)

## Sequence

1. Tissue injury exposes platelets to the ECM, which starts clotting and helps seal the wound
2. Chemokines are released at the wound and attract neutrophils and mast cells
3. Mast cell degranulation (histamine release) starts the inflammation phase, with swelling and redness
4. Neutrophils phagocytose bacteria and other pathogens, then die
5. Dead neutrophils and other cellular debris make up pus
6. About 2 days after injury, macrophages are recruited and activated
7. Macrophages phagocytose dead neutrophils, remaining bacteria, and tissue debris

## Details from the lecture figures

- At the time of injury, platelets adhere to the exposed collagen and to each other
- Platelets release clotting factors, PDGF, and TGF-beta to start the repair process
- By the first day, neutrophils attach to endothelial cells in the vessel walls around the wound (margination)
- They then change shape to move through cell junctions (diapedesis) and migrate to the wound (chemotaxis), which marks the beginning of the inflammatory phase

![Drawing of a fresh skin wound labeled epidermis, dermis, red blood cell, fibroblast, fixed tissue monocyte, platelet, and blood vessel, with a caption describing platelets adhering to exposed collagen and releasing clotting factors, PDGF, and TGF-beta](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-wound-injury-platelets.jpg)

![Drawing of the early inflammatory phase labeled neutrophil, fibroblast, margination, diapedesis, and fixed tissue monocyte, with a caption describing neutrophils leaving vessels and migrating to the wound by the first day](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-wound-inflammation-neutrophils.jpg)

> Mast cell histamine release starts the inflammation phase; neutrophils die after eating bacteria (forming pus), and macrophages arrive about 2 days later to clear the debris.

Library: Skin wound healing: proliferation and remodeling
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Drawing of the late inflammatory phase labeled scab, fibroblast, wound macrophage, and fixed tissue monocyte, with a caption describing wound macrophages releasing PDGF and TGF-beta to recruit fibroblasts and begin the proliferative phase](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-wound-proliferative-phase.jpg)

## Proliferative phase

- Fibroblasts are recruited to the wound, become activated, and start synthesizing collagen
- The main collagen made in the wound is type I
- The collagen fibers form a more permanent seal over the wound area

## Re-epithelialization and remodeling

- Epithelial cells eventually migrate to cover the surface of the wound (re-epithelialization)
- The underlying connective tissue is remodeled, and collagen fibers are cross-linked and organized
- Remodeling involves continued synthesis and degradation of ECM components as the tissue tries to reach a new equilibrium

![Drawing of the remodeling phase under restored epidermis, labeled fibroblast, fixed tissue monocyte, blood vessel, and macrophage](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-wound-remodeling-phase.jpg)

## Outcome

Collagen organization after healing is never as good as the original. ECM integrity appears to return to only about 80% of what it was before the wound, so repeated injury to the same tissue has consequences. Even so, this type of skin wound healing is relatively easy for the body to manage compared with tendon injury.

> In the proliferative phase, activated fibroblasts lay down mainly type I collagen, and remodeled skin regains only about 80% of its original ECM integrity.

Library: Defective skin wound healing: ulcers and keloids
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

Wound healing can go wrong at two different stages, either stalling in inflammation or overshooting in remodeling.

## Stuck in inflammation: chronic non-healing dermal ulcer

- Common name: bedsore
- Often seen in patients who are immobile, such as elderly immobile patients and people with spinal cord injury
- The pathology involves abnormal inflammation, and healing doesn't progress beyond the inflammation stage

## Stuck in contraction and remodeling: keloid scar

- Caused by excessive activation of fibroblasts
- Activated fibroblasts make copious amounts of collagen
- Some people are more susceptible than others to keloid formation during wound healing

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Chronic dermal ulcer | Keloid scar |
|---|---|---|
| Stalled phase | Inflammation | Contraction and remodeling |
| Problem | Abnormal inflammation | Excess fibroblast activation |
| Result | Wound that won't heal | Copious collagen |
| Who | Immobile patients | Susceptible individuals |

> A bedsore is a wound stuck in the inflammation phase, while a keloid is a wound stuck in remodeling with overactive fibroblasts making excess collagen.

Library: Tendinopathy histology and repair
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Diagram of a tendon running from muscle (myotendinous junction) to bone (enthesis), comparing healthy tendon (type I collagen fibrils, tenocytes, interfascicular matrix, immune cell, nerve fibre, blood vessel) with tendinopathy (type III collagen fibrils, degenerative type I collagen fibrils, neovessels)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-healthy-tendon-vs-tendinopathy.jpg)

## Background

- Tendons and ligaments are dense regular connective tissue, with thick collagen bundles arranged along a single plane
- A normal healthy tendon has few cells and is relatively avascular
- Excessive strain on a tendon (such as repetitive activities) can cause tendinopathy, often near the site of insertion into bone

![Schematic of healthy tendon (flat, elongated, evenly distributed cells; densely packed, aligned type 1 collagen) versus tendinopathy (rounded and uneven cells with tendon damage; disorganized type 1 and 3 collagen fibers; capillary ingrowth)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-tendinopathy-schematic.jpg)

## Identify it

- More cellular, and the cells are more rounded
- Collagen bundles are disorganized and not correctly aligned
- With chronic injury, new blood vessels and nerves grow into the tissue, which is associated with pain

![H&E of tendon labeled disorganized collagen (upper area) and normal collagen (lower area)](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-tendinopathy-he.jpg)

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Healthy tendon | Tendinopathy |
|---|---|---|
| Cells | Few, flat, elongated, evenly spaced | More numerous, rounded, uneven |
| Collagen | Mostly type I, densely packed and aligned | Types I and III, disorganized |
| Vessels and nerves | Relatively avascular | Ingrowth of new vessels and nerves |

Because of their biology, tendons and ligaments are hard to repair, and even a successful repair never restores 100% of the tissue's integrity and strength.

> Tendinopathy turns aligned, sparsely cellular type I collagen into a disorganized, more cellular tissue containing type III collagen, with ingrowth of vessels and nerves.

Library: Fibrosis after organ and surgical injury
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Connective Tissue Histology

![Cross section of a heart after myocardial infarction, with black arrows pointing to pale collagen scar deposits](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-myocardial-infarction-collagen-scar.jpg)

## Key features

- Wound healing isn't just a surface event: internal organs can also be "wounded" by physical trauma or by lack of blood flow, and they need repair
- Essentially the same repair process takes place, and the result is fibrosis
- Fibroblasts are the main cell responsible for producing the collagen
- After a myocardial infarction, a collagen scar forms in the heart

## Kidney fibrosis

1. Acute kidney injury can result in fibrosis
2. Kidney epithelial cells are replaced with ECM (mostly collagen)
3. This compromises kidney function
4. Compromised function can lead to even more fibrosis
5. This vicious cycle can eventually result in kidney failure

## Surgical fibrosis

Fibrosis can also develop after a surgical intervention.

![Photo of an eye after surgery, with an arrow pointing to an area of fibrosis](library/images/msk-week-13/ct-surgical-fibrosis-eye.jpg)

> Fibrosis is a fibroblast-made collagen scar that follows organ injury, as after myocardial infarction or acute kidney injury, where it can drive a vicious cycle toward kidney failure.

Library: Acne vulgaris: presentation and diagnosis
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of a forehead covered with many small skin-colored comedones and a few red papules, acne vulgaris](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-acne-vulgaris-forehead-comedones-papules.jpg)

## Identify it

- Comedones must be present to make the diagnosis
- Papules and pustules are often present too
- Face, chest, and back
- Common during the teenage years, 20s, and 30s

## Key features

- Very common: adolescents more than adults, with an incidence of up to 85%
- Significant psychological impact, including depression and poor self-image
- Long-term sequelae are postinflammatory hyperpigmentation and permanent scarring
- Effective treatments are available, and the choice depends on the severity and the category of lesions

## Severity categories

The lecture sorts acne into four categories, from mildest to most severe. Each one has its own page with its treatment.

| Category | Lesions |
|---|---|
| Comedonal | Comedones; the mildest form |
| Mild inflammatory | Papules and pustules with comedones, none to few deep nodules |
| Moderate inflammatory | Tender nodules plus comedones, papules, and pustules |
| Nodulocystic | Many deep-seated nodules and cysts plus inflammatory papules and pustules |

> Acne vulgaris is diagnosed by the presence of comedones, often with papules and pustules.

Library: Acne pathogenesis: comedo to nodule
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Diagram of the pathogenesis of acne in four stages (early comedo, later comedo shown closed and open, inflammatory papule/pustule, nodule/cyst), labeled epidermis, sebaceous lobule, and follicular epithelium, with a key for corneocytes, sebum, and P. acnes](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-acne-pathogenesis-comedo-to-nodule.jpg)

## Early comedo

- Hyperkeratosis and increased corneocyte cohesiveness in the upper sebaceous follicle lead to microcomedo formation
- Androgens stimulate sebum production

## Later comedo

- Shed keratin and sebum accumulate
- Whorled lamellar concretions form
- The comedo may be closed or open

| Comedo | Description |
|---|---|
| Closed | No obvious follicular opening |
| Open | Dilated follicular opening; the keratin plug darkens from oxidized lipids and melanin |

## Inflammatory papule or pustule

- *Propionibacterium acnes* proliferates and upregulates innate immune responses (for example, through TLRs)
- Mild inflammation, mainly neutrophils, increases when the comedo wall ruptures
- The sebaceous lobule regresses

## Nodule or cyst

- Marked inflammation, mainly T cells
- May lead to scarring

> Inflammation in acne papules and pustules is mainly neutrophils, while nodules and cysts show marked inflammation that's mainly T cells.

Library: Comedonal acne
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of a forehead with many closed and open comedones and a few small papules, comedonal acne](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-comedonal-acne-forehead.jpg)

## Identify it

- Comedones on the face
- The mildest form of acne

## Treatment

First-line treatment is a **topical retinoid**, a vitamin A analog.

| Drug | Mechanism of action |
|---|---|
| Tretinoin | Normalizes follicular keratinization |
| Tazarotene | Normalizes follicular keratinization |
| Adapalene | Normalizes follicular keratinization |

- Normalized keratinization means less follicular plugging and less comedone formation
- Retinoids take at least 6 weeks to be effective
- This fits the first step of acne pathogenesis, where hyperkeratosis and sticky corneocytes in the upper follicle form the microcomedo

> First-line treatment for comedonal acne is a topical retinoid (tretinoin, tazarotene, adapalene), which normalizes follicular keratinization and takes at least 6 weeks to work.

Library: Mild inflammatory acne
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of a cheek and chin below the lower lip with red papules, pustules, and comedones, mild inflammatory acne](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-mild-inflammatory-acne-cheek.jpg)

## Identify it

- Papules and pustules with comedones
- None to few deep nodules

## Treatment

| Drug | Type | Mechanism of action |
|---|---|---|
| Tretinoin, adapalene | Topical retinoid | Normalizes follicular keratinization |
| Clindamycin, erythromycin | Topical antibiotic | Kills acne-causing bacteria (*Propionibacterium acnes*) |
| Benzoyl peroxide face wash (4-6%) | Topical wash | Kills acne-causing bacteria (*Propionibacterium acnes*) |

The step up from comedonal acne is adding something that kills *P. acnes* (a topical antibiotic or benzoyl peroxide) to the retinoid. In the pathogenesis diagram, *P. acnes* proliferation is what drives the inflammatory papule and pustule stage.

> Mild inflammatory acne adds a topical antibiotic (clindamycin, erythromycin) or benzoyl peroxide, which kill *Propionibacterium acnes*, to a topical retinoid.

Library: Moderate inflammatory acne and hormonal treatment
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of the side of a face with scattered red papules and tender nodules on the cheek and jaw, moderate inflammatory acne](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-moderate-inflammatory-acne-cheek.jpg)

## Identify it

- Tender nodules along with comedones, papules, and pustules
- In females, flares may precede the menstrual cycle and favor the chin and jawline

![Clinical photo of the cheek and chin beside the lips with inflamed papules and small nodules, hormonal pattern of moderate acne in a female](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-hormonal-acne-chin-jawline.jpg)

## Treatment

- Tretinoin
- Benzoyl peroxide face wash
- Oral antibiotics: doxycycline 100 mg BID

## Added options in females

- Oral contraceptive pills
- Spironolactone, a K+ sparing diuretic and antihypertensive
- Spironolactone is also an anti-androgen that alters sebum production in response to hormones
- Spironolactone has similar efficacy to oral antibiotics

Moderate acne is where oral antibiotics enter the plan. For a female whose acne tracks with her cycle along the chin and jawline, oral contraceptive pills and spironolactone are extra choices, and spironolactone works about as well as oral antibiotics.

> In females with moderate inflammatory acne, spironolactone (an anti-androgen that alters sebum production) has similar efficacy to oral antibiotics.

Library: Nodulocystic acne
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Two clinical photos of cheeks with many deep nodules, cysts, and inflamed papules and pustules, nodulocystic acne](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-nodulocystic-acne-cheek-two-patients.jpg)

## Identify it

- Many deep-seated nodules and cysts
- Inflammatory papules and pustules alongside them
- Leads to scarring if untreated

## Treatment

Antibiotics with topical treatments can be considered, but many patients require **isotretinoin**.

| Isotretinoin feature | Detail |
|---|---|
| Drug class | Oral retinoid |
| Major risk | Severe teratogen |
| Prescribing | Monthly prescription registered with the iPledge monitoring program |
| Mechanism | Induces sebocyte apoptosis |
| Result | Normalized keratinization and decreased *P. acnes* |

Because nodulocystic acne scars, treatment shouldn't be delayed. The lecture stresses that scarring is difficult to treat and easier to prevent with early isotretinoin.

> Many patients with nodulocystic acne need isotretinoin, an oral retinoid and severe teratogen that requires monthly iPledge registration.

Library: Isotretinoin for acne
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Two profile photos of a patient with the eyes blocked out, shown on the isotretinoin slide: on the left many inflamed papules, nodules, and dark spots on the cheek and jaw, and on the right much clearer skin](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-isotretinoin-before-after.jpg)

## Mechanism

- Oral retinoid
- Induces sebocyte apoptosis, which leads to normalized keratinization and decreased *P. acnes*

## Course and monitoring

- 6 to 8 month treatment course
- Weight-based dosing to reach a goal dose
- Monthly prescription through the iPledge monitoring program
- Labs: LFTs, lipids, and a monthly pregnancy test

## Side effects

- Dryness of skin and mucous membranes
- Increased sun sensitivity
- Inflammatory bowel disease
- Hair loss
- Joint pain
- Depression
- LFT and triglyceride elevations
- **Severe birth defects**

## Role

Isotretinoin is highly effective. Many nodulocystic acne patients need it, and starting it early is the easier way to deal with scarring, since established scars are difficult to treat. When used properly, it's a safe and highly effective medication.

> Isotretinoin causes severe birth defects, so it requires iPledge registration and a monthly pregnancy test, plus LFT and lipid monitoring.

Library: Acne treatment by severity
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

## Treatment ladder

Acne treatment depends on the severity and the category of lesions. Therapy is added as lesions get deeper and more inflamed.

| Category | Lesions | Treatment |
|---|---|---|
| Comedonal | Comedones (mildest) | Topical retinoid: tretinoin, tazarotene, adapalene |
| Mild inflammatory | Papules/pustules with comedones, few or no nodules | Tretinoin or adapalene; topical clindamycin or erythromycin; benzoyl peroxide wash |
| Moderate inflammatory | Tender nodules, comedones, papules, pustules | Tretinoin; benzoyl peroxide wash; oral doxycycline 100 mg BID |
| Moderate, female | Flares before menses on chin and jawline | Add oral contraceptive pills or spironolactone |
| Nodulocystic | Many deep nodules and cysts | Antibiotics with topicals; many need isotretinoin |

## Mechanisms of action

| Drug | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| Topical retinoids | Normalize follicular keratinization, less plugging and comedone formation |
| Topical antibiotics, benzoyl peroxide | Kill *Propionibacterium acnes* |
| Spironolactone | Anti-androgen; alters sebum production in response to hormones |
| Isotretinoin | Induces sebocyte apoptosis; normalizes keratinization and decreases *P. acnes* |

## Timing

- Topical retinoids take at least 6 weeks to work
- Isotretinoin needs a 6 to 8 month course

> Acne treatment is chosen by severity: topical retinoid for comedonal, add antibacterials for inflammatory, oral antibiotics for moderate, and isotretinoin for many nodulocystic patients.

Library: Acne sequelae: scarring and hyperpigmentation
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of the lower face with many small depressed acne scars on both cheeks](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-acne-scarring-cheeks.jpg)

## Scarring

- Permanent
- Difficult to treat
- Easier to prevent with early initiation of isotretinoin
- When used properly, isotretinoin is safe and highly effective

![Clinical photo of a cheek in darker skin with many dark brown to purple spots where acne lesions healed, postinflammatory hyperpigmentation](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-acne-postinflammatory-hyperpigmentation.jpg)

## Postinflammatory hyperpigmentation

- Common with all acne types
- Worse in darker skin types
- Worsened by picking and popping lesions

## Treating the hyperpigmentation

- Tincture of time, since it can take months to fade
- Prevention of new acne lesions
- Sunscreen

## Look-alikes

| Sequela | Permanent? | Approach |
|---|---|---|
| Scarring | Yes | Hard to treat; prevent with early isotretinoin |
| Postinflammatory hyperpigmentation | No, fades over months | Time, preventing new lesions, sunscreen |

> Acne scarring is permanent and best prevented with early isotretinoin, while postinflammatory hyperpigmentation fades with time and is worse in darker skin.

Library: Rosacea pathophysiology and subtypes
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of the central face with redness of the nose and cheeks and scattered red papules, rosacea](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-rosacea-central-face.jpg)

## Pathophysiology

Rosacea is an inflammatory disorder due to immune and neurovascular dysregulation, with a possible role for an exaggerated immune reaction to demodex. A genetic predisposition plus triggers (alcohol, sun, spicy foods) lead to two problems.

| Dysregulation | Result |
|---|---|
| Neurovascular dysregulation | Flushing, sensitivity |
| Abnormal innate immune response | Inflammatory papules and pustules, no comedones |

## Four subtypes

| Subtype | Key findings |
|---|---|
| Erythematotelangiectatic | Facial redness, telangiectasias, burning, flushing |
| Papulopustular | Erythematous papules and pustules on the central face over background erythema |
| Phymatous | Papulopustular plus sebaceous gland hypertrophy and fibrosis; men, nose |
| Ocular | Blepharitis with eyelid swelling, conjunctival injection, dry eyes |

## Triggers to avoid

- Alcohol
- Sun
- Spicy foods

> Rosacea has inflammatory papules and pustules but no comedones, which separates it from acne vulgaris.

Library: Erythematotelangiectatic rosacea
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of the nose, cheeks, and mouth with diffuse facial redness and fine visible vessels, erythematotelangiectatic rosacea](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-erythematotelangiectatic-rosacea.jpg)

## Identify it

- Facial redness
- Telangiectasias
- Skin sensitivity with burning and flushing

## Pathophysiology

This subtype reflects the neurovascular side of rosacea, where neurovascular dysregulation causes flushing and sensitivity.

## Treatment

| Treatment | Purpose or mechanism |
|---|---|
| Avoid triggers | Alcohol, sun, spicy foods |
| Sunscreen | Sun is a trigger |
| Gentle skin care | Skin is sensitive (burning, flushing) |
| Pulse dye laser | Procedural option |
| Beta blockers | For flushing |
| Topical alpha2 agonist (brimonidine) | Blood vessel constriction |

> Brimonidine, a topical alpha2 agonist, treats erythematotelangiectatic rosacea by constricting blood vessels, and beta blockers help flushing.

Library: Papulopustular rosacea
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of the central face with many red papules and pustules on a background of redness over the nose, cheeks, and chin, papulopustular rosacea](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-papulopustular-rosacea.jpg)

## Identify it

- Erythematous papules and pustules
- Typically on the central face
- Background of erythema
- No comedones

## Treatment

| Treatment | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| Sunscreen, gentle skin care | Supportive |
| Avoid triggers | Alcohol, sun, spicy foods |
| Topical metronidazole | Anti-inflammatory rather than antimicrobial |
| Topical ivermectin | Kills demodex mites on the skin |
| Low-dose doxycycline | Anti-inflammatory dose |

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Acne vulgaris | Papulopustular rosacea |
|---|---|---|
| Comedones | Present, needed for diagnosis | Absent |
| Distribution | Face, chest, back | Central face |
| Background | No background redness described | Background of erythema |
| Doxycycline | Oral antibiotic, 100 mg BID | Low dose, anti-inflammatory |
| Topical agents | Retinoids, antibiotics, benzoyl peroxide | Metronidazole, ivermectin |

> Topical metronidazole works in papulopustular rosacea as an anti-inflammatory rather than an antimicrobial, and topical ivermectin kills demodex mites.

Library: Phymatous rosacea
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of a man's nose and cheek with a markedly enlarged, thickened, bumpy nose and red papules on the cheek, phymatous rosacea](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-phymatous-rosacea-nose.jpg)

## Identify it

- Papulopustular rosacea plus sebaceous gland hypertrophy and fibrosis
- Most common in men, on the nose
- Can result in disfigurement

## Pathophysiology

Sebaceous glands undergo hypertrophy and fibrosis in response to chronic, longstanding inflammation. That's why it builds on papulopustular rosacea instead of appearing on its own.

## Treatment

| Treatment | Type |
|---|---|
| Surgical excision | Procedural |
| Ablative laser treatments | Procedural |

> Phymatous rosacea is sebaceous gland hypertrophy and fibrosis from chronic inflammation, most commonly on the nose in men, and it's treated with surgical excision or ablative laser.

Library: Ocular rosacea
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Three photos of ocular rosacea: (A) an eye with a red, swollen lid margin and red conjunctiva, (B) a nearly closed eye with eyelid changes, and (C) both eyes with red, inflamed eyelid margins and conjunctival injection](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-ocular-rosacea-eyelids.jpg)

## Identify it

- Blepharitis with eyelid swelling
- Conjunctival injection
- Dry eyes and frequent blinking
- Often seen together with other rosacea subtypes

## Treatment

| Treatment | Notes |
|---|---|
| Artificial tears | For dry eyes |
| Low-dose doxycycline | Same anti-inflammatory dosing used in papulopustular rosacea |

> Ocular rosacea is blepharitis with eyelid swelling and conjunctival injection, treated with artificial tears and low-dose doxycycline.

Library: Hidradenitis suppurativa
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of an axilla with linear, scarred sinus tracts and a few inflamed nodules, hidradenitis suppurativa](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-hidradenitis-axilla-sinus-tracts-scars.jpg)

## Identify it

- Also known as acne inversa
- Inflamed nodules and sterile abscesses, then draining sinus tracts and scarring
- Intertriginous sites with apocrine glands: axilla, inframammary region, groin, buttocks

![Clinical photo of the buttocks and upper thigh with inflamed nodules, draining lesions, and scarring, hidradenitis suppurativa](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-hidradenitis-buttocks.jpg)

## Pathophysiology

1. Unknown trigger
2. Occlusion of pilosebaceous units in skin with apocrine glands
3. Inflammation of apocrine glands
4. Inflamed nodules and sterile abscesses
5. Draining sinus tracts and scarring

## Who gets it

- Starts at or soon after puberty
- Females more than males, African Americans more than Caucasians
- Obesity and tobacco use are risk factors

## Course

- Chronic and progressive, and difficult to treat
- Physical and emotional sequelae: painful, disfiguring, malodorous
- Early treatment minimizes complications
- Divided into Hurley stages by severity, and treatment for all stages includes weight loss and smoking cessation

> Hidradenitis suppurativa starts with occlusion of pilosebaceous units in apocrine gland skin (axilla, inframammary, groin, buttocks) and progresses to sterile abscesses, sinus tracts, and scarring.

Library: Hurley staging and treatment of hidradenitis suppurativa
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Hurley stage I hidradenitis suppurativa: axilla with a few red inflammatory nodules and no sinus tracts or scars](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-hidradenitis-hurley-stage-1-axilla.jpg)

![Hurley stage II hidradenitis suppurativa: two photos of axillae with inflamed nodules, abscesses, and a few sinus tracts and scars](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-hidradenitis-hurley-stage-2.jpg)

![Hurley stage III hidradenitis suppurativa: an axilla with extensive interconnected sinus tracts, crusting, and scarring, and a groin and buttock region with widespread nodules and scarring](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-hidradenitis-hurley-stage-3.jpg)

## Hurley stages

| Stage | Lesions | Sinus tracts and scars |
|---|---|---|
| I | Inflammatory nodules, sterile abscesses | None |
| II | Inflammatory nodules, sterile abscesses | Few |
| III | Nodules and abscesses, often covering the entire region | Multiple, interconnected |

## Treatment by stage

| Stage | Treatment |
|---|---|
| All stages | Weight loss, smoking cessation |
| I | Topical antibiotics, antiseptic washes, intralesional steroids |
| I | Oral antibiotics (rifampin + clindamycin, doxycycline) for anti-inflammatory effects |
| II | Oral antibiotics |
| II | Spironolactone or finasteride (anti-androgen effects) |
| II | Adalimumab (TNF-alpha inhibitor); surgical excision |
| III | Biologics: adalimumab, infliximab, secukinumab |
| III | Surgical excision |

The stage III biologics are anti-inflammatory monoclonal antibodies against TNF-alpha or IL-17. Surgical excision is an option from stage II on, and biologics become the main medical therapy in stage III, when sinus tracts are multiple and interconnected.

> Hurley stage depends on sinus tracts and scars: none in stage I, few in stage II, and multiple interconnected tracts in stage III.

Library: Hyperhidrosis: primary vs. secondary
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of the sole and toes of a foot with shiny, moist skin from excessive sweating (panel A), hyperhidrosis](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-hyperhidrosis-sole-of-foot.jpg)

## Identify it

- Excessive sweating
- Primary disease favors the palms, soles, and axillae

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Primary | Secondary |
|---|---|---|
| Cause | No underlying disease | Underlying disease, medication, etc. |
| Family history | 80% have one | Not a feature listed |
| Sites | Palms, soles, axillae | Not specified |

## Secondary causes

- Febrile illness
- Hyperthyroidism
- Lymphoma or other cancer
- Medications: amphetamines, insulin, sulfonylureas, SSRIs, opioids

The first question with a sweaty patient is whether there's something driving it. If there's no apparent secondary cause and the sweating is focal, the next step is checking the diagnostic criteria for primary hyperhidrosis.

> Primary hyperhidrosis has no underlying disease, a family history in 80%, and affects the palms, soles, and axillae; secondary hyperhidrosis comes from illness (fever, hyperthyroidism, lymphoma) or drugs.

Library: Primary hyperhidrosis diagnostic criteria
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Table titled Criteria for the Diagnosis of Primary Hyperhidrosis listing focal visible excess sweating, present at least 6 months, no apparent secondary causes, and at least two supporting features](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-primary-hyperhidrosis-criteria-table.jpg)

![Clinical photo of both palms glistening with sweat, primary hyperhidrosis](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-primary-hyperhidrosis-palms.jpg)

## Criteria

1. Focal, visible excess sweating
2. Present for at least 6 months
3. No apparent secondary causes
4. At least two of the supporting features below

## Supporting features

- Bilateral and symmetric
- Impairs activities of daily life
- At least one episode per week
- Age of onset under 25 years
- Positive family history
- Stops during sleep

## Identify it

- Palms, soles, and axillae
- Visible sweat, as on the glistening palms in the photo

> Primary hyperhidrosis needs focal, visible sweating for at least 6 months with no secondary cause, plus at least two features such as bilateral symmetry, onset before 25, family history, or stopping during sleep.

Library: Primary hyperhidrosis treatment
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

## Treatment

| Treatment | Mechanism of action | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Topical aluminum chloride 12-20% (DrySol) | Physically occludes eccrine sweat glands | Topical |
| Glycopyrrolate, oxybutynin | Block muscarinic receptors on eccrine sweat glands | Topical or oral anticholinergics |
| Botulinum toxin injections | Blocks acetylcholine release | Repeat every 6-9 months |

## How they fit together

- Aluminum chloride works by plugging the eccrine glands physically
- Anticholinergics and botulinum toxin both act on cholinergic signaling to eccrine sweat glands, one by blocking the muscarinic receptor and the other by blocking acetylcholine release
- Botulinum toxin isn't permanent, so it requires repeated treatments every 6 to 9 months

> Primary hyperhidrosis is treated with topical aluminum chloride (occludes eccrine glands), anticholinergics like glycopyrrolate and oxybutynin (block muscarinic receptors), or botulinum toxin (blocks acetylcholine release, repeated every 6 to 9 months).

Library: Vitiligo
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photos of vitiligo: sharply bordered white depigmented patches on both knees, and white patches on the backs of two hands](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-vitiligo-knees-hands.jpg)

## Identify it

- Depigmentation of the skin (white patches)
- Commonly on the hands and feet
- Commonly on perioral and periocular skin

## Pathophysiology

Autoimmunity targets melanocytes, so the skin loses its pigment.

## Associations

Vitiligo is associated with other autoimmune diseases:

- Alopecia areata
- Autoimmune hypothyroidism
- Graves disease

## Treatment

- Topical steroids
- Narrowband UVB
- Ruxolitinib

> Vitiligo is depigmentation from autoimmunity targeting melanocytes, and it's associated with alopecia areata, autoimmune hypothyroidism, and Graves disease.

Library: Pityriasis alba
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of a child's lower face with pale, hypopigmented patches on the cheeks around the mouth, pityriasis alba](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-pityriasis-alba-face.jpg)

## Identify it

- Hypopigmented, round patches with slight scale
- On the face of young children
- May worsen in the summer

## Key features

- Typically in children with atopic dermatitis, and it may represent a mild variant of it
- The lecture lists it among hypopigmented rashes as atopic dermatitis (pityriasis alba)
- Benign, and it resolves with age

## Treatment

| Treatment | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Moisturizers | Help the dry appearance |
| 1% hydrocortisone | Helps the dry appearance |

Because it's benign and resolves with age, treatment is aimed at the dry look of the patches. For how it compares with the other hypopigmented rashes, see the look-alikes table on the tinea versicolor page.

> Pityriasis alba is hypopigmented, slightly scaly round patches on the face of young children with atopic dermatitis; it's benign and resolves with age.

Library: Tinea versicolor
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of the upper back with many pale, hypopigmented patches that merge across the skin, tinea versicolor](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-tinea-versicolor-back.jpg)

![Close clinical photo of a tanned shoulder with many small round hypopigmented patches, some merging, tinea versicolor](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-tinea-versicolor-shoulder.jpg)

## Identify it

- Hypopigmented patches, most commonly on the chest and back
- Often in teenagers and young adults
- Appearance worsens in the summer

## Pathophysiology

A common cutaneous reaction caused by overgrowth of *Malassezia* yeast. The yeast decreases melanin synthesis and tanning of the underlying skin, so the patches stand out more in summer.

## Treatment

- Topical azoles
- Fluconazole
- Over-the-counter dandruff shampoo

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Pityriasis alba | Tinea versicolor | Vitiligo |
|---|---|---|---|
| Color | Hypopigmented, slight scale | Hypopigmented | Depigmented |
| Sites | Face | Chest and back | Hands, feet, perioral, periocular |
| Patients | Young children | Teens, young adults | Not specified |
| Cause | May be a mild variant of atopic dermatitis | *Malassezia* yeast overgrowth | Autoimmunity against melanocytes |
| Treatment | Moisturizers, 1% hydrocortisone | Azoles, dandruff shampoo | Topical steroids, NB-UVB, ruxolitinib |

> Tinea versicolor is *Malassezia* yeast overgrowth that decreases melanin synthesis, giving hypopigmented patches on the chest and back that worsen in summer.

Library: Hair loss disorders compared
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

## Disorders of hair

The lecture's list of hair disorders:

- Androgenic alopecia
- Tinea capitis
- Telogen effluvium
- Drug-induced hair loss
- Deficiency-induced hair loss

Alopecia areata is covered in detail as well. Hair loss can have a profound emotional and psychological impact on patients.

## Look-alikes

| Disorder | Pattern | Cause | Course or treatment |
|---|---|---|---|
| Alopecia areata | Circular, smooth patches; no erythema or scale | T cell autoimmune destruction of follicles | Often self-resolves in 6 months; steroids |
| Tinea capitis | Mimics alopecia areata, with scaling and itching | Dermatophyte infection of hair follicles | Oral antifungals for 4-8 weeks |
| Telogen effluvium | Diffuse, sudden loss | Faster hair cycling after illness, pregnancy, surgery, stress | Self-resolves in 6-12 months |
| Androgenic alopecia | Men: frontal and vertex; women: wider part | Androgens miniaturize follicles | Minoxidil, anti-androgens |

## Sorting clues

- Patchy loss with smooth skin and no symptoms points to alopecia areata
- Patchy loss with scale and itch points to tinea capitis
- Diffuse sudden shedding after a stressful event points to telogen effluvium
- A progressive pattern (frontal and vertex in men, widening part in women) points to androgenic alopecia

> Alopecia areata and tinea capitis both cause patchy hair loss, but tinea capitis has scaling and itching while alopecia areata is smooth and asymptomatic.

Library: Alopecia areata
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Two clinical photos of alopecia areata: a round, smooth bald patch on the back of the scalp, and a round patch of hair loss being held open in dark curly hair](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-alopecia-areata-scalp-patches.jpg)

## Identify it

- Circular areas of hair loss
- Smooth skin, with no erythema or scaling
- Asymptomatic
- Common, non-scarring alopecia

## Pathophysiology

T cell mediated autoimmune destruction of hair follicles.

## Who gets it

- Occurs in all ages and races
- Often a positive family history
- Often affects children and young adults

## Variants

![Clinical photo of a scalp with complete hair loss (panel C), from the alopecia areata variants slide](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-alopecia-totalis-scalp.jpg)

| Variant | Hair lost |
|---|---|
| Alopecia totalis | All scalp hair |
| Alopecia universalis | All body hair |

![Three clinical photos from the alopecia areata variants slide: patchy hair loss in a beard, loss of eyebrow hair, and extensive scalp hair loss with a few remaining tufts](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-alopecia-areata-beard-eyebrow-scalp.jpg)

## Associations

- Atopy (allergies, asthma, atopic dermatitis)
- Hashimoto's thyroiditis
- Vitiligo

> Alopecia areata is a non-scarring, T cell mediated autoimmune destruction of hair follicles that causes smooth, circular, asymptomatic patches of hair loss.

Library: Alopecia areata treatment and prognosis
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

## Treatment by extent

| Extent | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Localized | Topical corticosteroids |
| Localized | Intralesional corticosteroids |
| Localized | Topical calcineurin inhibitors |
| Widespread or rapidly progressive | Oral steroids |
| Widespread or rapidly progressive | Tofacitinib (JAK/STAT inhibitor) |

The systemic options are immunosuppressants that decrease T cell function. That matches the disease mechanism, since alopecia areata is T cell mediated autoimmune destruction of hair follicles.

## Prognosis

- Usually self-resolves within 6 months
- Localized disease often responds well to treatment
- May periodically recur
- More extensive hair loss is treatment resistant

## Putting it together

Localized disease is treated locally and often responds well. Widespread or rapidly progressive loss gets systemic immunosuppression, and more extensive hair loss is the form most likely to resist treatment.

> Localized alopecia areata is treated with topical or intralesional corticosteroids or topical calcineurin inhibitors, while widespread disease gets oral steroids or tofacitinib (a JAK/STAT inhibitor).

Library: Tinea capitis
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Clinical photo of the scalp with an area of thinned hair and white scale, tinea capitis](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-tinea-capitis-scalp-scale.jpg)

## Identify it

- Dermatophyte fungal infection of hair follicles
- Can mimic alopecia areata
- Differences from alopecia areata include scaling and itching

## Diagnosis

- Scraping and microscopic examination of scale
- Fungal culture

![Light micrograph of a positive scale scraping after applying potassium hydroxide (KOH), showing long, branching fungal hyphae](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-tinea-capitis-koh-hyphae.jpg)

## Treatment

| Drug | Duration |
|---|---|
| Terbinafine | 4-8 weeks |
| Griseofulvin | 4-8 weeks |
| Fluconazole | 4-8 weeks |

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Tinea capitis | Alopecia areata |
|---|---|---|
| Cause | Dermatophyte infection | T cell autoimmune |
| Scale | Present | None |
| Itching | Present | Asymptomatic |
| Treatment | Oral antifungals | Corticosteroids, calcineurin inhibitors |

> Tinea capitis mimics alopecia areata but has scaling and itching; confirm it with a KOH scraping or fungal culture and treat with oral antifungals for 4 to 8 weeks.

Library: Telogen effluvium
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

## Identify it

- Diffuse, sudden hair loss
- Follows illness, hospitalization, pregnancy, surgery, or other stressful events

## Pathophysiology

Telogen effluvium is caused by more rapid cycling of hair and increased shedding. In normal conditions, about 100 hairs a day are lost.

| Hair cycle phase | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Anagen | Growth |
| Catagen | Regression |
| Telogen | Rest |

## Course

- Self-resolves in 6 to 12 months
- No specific treatment is given in the lecture

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Telogen effluvium | Alopecia areata |
|---|---|---|
| Pattern | Diffuse | Circular patches |
| Course | Resolves in 6-12 months | Often resolves within 6 months, may recur |

> Telogen effluvium is diffuse, sudden shedding after illness, pregnancy, surgery, or stress from faster hair cycling, and it self-resolves in 6 to 12 months.

Library: Androgenic alopecia
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Spectrum of Acneiform Disorders, Pigmentation, and Hair

![Drawing of male pattern baldness progressing from a full hairline to frontal recession and then loss at the vertex and crown](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-androgenic-alopecia-male-pattern.jpg)

![Four photos of the top of the scalp viewed from above showing progressive widening of the central part line, the pattern seen in women with androgenic alopecia](library/images/msk-week-13/derm-androgenic-alopecia-part-widening.jpg)

## Identify it

- Male and female pattern baldness
- Often familial
- Men present with loss of the frontal scalp and vertex
- Women present with widening of the part line

## Pathophysiology

Circulating androgen hormones (testosterone) lead to progressive miniaturization of hair follicles.

## Treatment

| Treatment | Mechanism of action |
|---|---|
| Topical minoxidil | Increases blood circulation to hair follicles to prevent miniaturization |
| Finasteride | Anti-androgen; decreases androgen stimulation of follicles |
| Spironolactone | Anti-androgen; decreases androgen stimulation of follicles |
| Dutasteride | Anti-androgen; decreases androgen stimulation of follicles |

Spironolactone and finasteride show up elsewhere in this lecture for the same anti-androgen reason: spironolactone for hormonal acne in females, and both for Hurley stage II hidradenitis suppurativa.

> Androgenic alopecia comes from androgens causing progressive miniaturization of hair follicles; men lose the frontal scalp and vertex, and women get a widening part line.

Library: Channelopathies: ion channel diseases
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Diagram titled Ion Channel Diseases "Channelopathies" showing a cell ringed by ion channels and labeled disorders, with cystic fibrosis, hypertension, and epilepsy boxed; other labels include long QT, myotonia, migraine, deafness, diabetes, multiple sclerosis, and dementia](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-channelopathies-disease-ring.jpg)

## Key features

- Channelopathies are human disorders caused by disturbing the function of ion channels
- Genetic disorders are known to be caused by defective ion channels, and genome wide association studies (GWAS) are expected to reveal more
- Ion channels are a site for therapeutic interventions
- Toxins and venoms act by targeting ion channels
- The lecture's disease ring highlights cystic fibrosis, hypertension, and epilepsy, and also lists conditions such as long QT, myotonia, migraine, deafness, kidney stones, diabetes, asthma, multiple sclerosis, and dementia
- The slide also pairs hydrocephalus with AQP4

## Timing of channelopathies in brain development

![Timeline of ion channel disease and neurodevelopmental processes: developmental channelopathy (embryo, fetus) causing brain malformations and postnatal channelopathy (neonate to adult) causing epilepsy and encephalopathy, with voltage gated and ligand gated channel genes listed for each](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-channelopathy-timeline-genes.jpg)

Developmental channelopathies act in the embryo and fetus and cause brain malformations. Postnatal channelopathies act from the neonate through adulthood and cause epilepsy and encephalopathy.

| Channel | Brain malformations | Epilepsy and encephalopathy |
|---|---|---|
| Na+ (voltage gated) | SCN: 3A | SCN: 1A, 2A, 3A, 8A, 1B |
| K+ (voltage gated) | None listed | KCN: A2, B1, H5, Q2, Q3, T1 |
| Ca2+ (voltage gated) | None listed | CACNA: A1A, A2D2, 1E |
| Glutamate (ligand gated) | GRIN: 1A, 2B | GRIN: 1A, 2A, 2B, 2D |
| GABA (ligand gated) | None listed | GABAR: A1, B1, B2, B3, G2 |

> Channelopathies are disorders caused by disturbed ion channel function, and ion channels are also targets for drugs, toxins, and venoms.

Library: Autoimmune ion channel disorders
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

## Key findings

Autoimmune neurological disorders can target ion channels and their associated proteins. The lecture's table lists the channel or protein targeted, the disorder, and its clinical features.

| Target | Disorder | Clinical features |
|---|---|---|
| Alpha1 AChR | Myasthenia gravis | Muscle weakness, fatigue |
| Alpha3 AChR | Autoimmune autonomic neuropathy | Orthostatic hypotension, urinary retention |
| CASPR2 and LGI1 (with Kv1, VGKC) | Morvan's syndrome | Encephalopathy, peripheral nerve hyperexcitability, dysautonomia |
| Contactin-2 (with Kv1, VGKC) | Limbic encephalitis | Memory loss, seizures, personality changes, psychiatric features |
| Unknown protein with Kv1 (VGKC) | Acquired neuromyotonia | Muscle fasciculations, cramps, pseudomyotonia |
| Alpha1A (VGCC) | Lambert Eaton myasthenic syndrome | Muscle weakness, fatigue |
| VGCC | Cerebellar ataxia 2 | Ataxia, small-cell lung cancer |
| GluN1a (NMDA receptor) | NMDAR encephalitis | Epilepsy, abnormal movement, decreased consciousness |
| GluR2/GluR3 (AMPA receptor) | Limbic encephalitis | Often with cancer, isolated psychiatric symptoms |
| Gly alpha1 (glycine receptor) | PERM | Stiffness, rigidity, cognitive involvement, brainstem dysfunction |
| Kir4.1 (inward rectifying K+) | Multiple sclerosis | Weakness, ataxia, fatigue, chronic pain, visual problems |
| AQP4 (aquaporin) | Neuromyelitis optica | Muscle weakness, reduced sensation including blindness |
| mGluR1 | Cerebellar ataxia | Prominent seizures, Ophelia syndrome often with cancer |
| mGluR5 | Limbic encephalitis | Prominent seizures, Ophelia syndrome often with cancer |

- VGKC means voltage-gated K+ channel, and VGCC means voltage-gated Ca2+ channel
- PERM is progressive encephalomyelitis with rigidity and myoclonus
- Detection methods in the table include radioimmunoassay, cell-based assay, indirect immunohistochemistry, ELISA, and Western blot

> Autoimmunity against the alpha1 AChR causes myasthenia gravis, and autoimmunity against voltage-gated Ca2+ channels (alpha1A) causes Lambert Eaton myasthenic syndrome; both present with muscle weakness and fatigue.

Library: Transmembrane potential and its measurement
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Diagram of a neuron with a boxed segment of axon enlarged, showing a voltmeter connected to a microelectrode inside the cell and a microelectrode outside the cell across the plasma membrane, with negative charges inside and positive charges outside](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-voltmeter-microelectrode-membrane-potential.jpg)

## Electrical potential difference

The lecture starts with a battery. When it's connected, electrons flow from the anode through the wire into the cathode and generate electricity. Over time the electron concentration at the anode falls, and once it equals the concentration at the cathode the current stops and the battery is dead.

## Key features

- Most cells in the body are electrically charged
- A voltmeter connected to an electrode inside a cell and a reference electrode outside detects an instantaneous negative voltage
- In living cells this voltage ranges from -10 mV to -100 mV
- The electrical potential difference between the inside and outside of a cell is called the transmembrane potential (Em), resting potential, or membrane potential
- Only some cells can generate rapid, transient changes in membrane potential, called electrical impulses, which are mediated by the flow of charged ions

## Function

Electrical signals mediate many functions:

- Transmission of information among neurons
- Muscular contraction
- Hormone secretion
- Vascular tone

> The membrane potential is the voltage difference between the inside and outside of a cell, measured with an electrode inside against a reference electrode outside, and it's negative at rest.

Library: Ion concentration gradients across the membrane
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Diagram of the cell membrane with extracellular fluid on the left (many Na+ and Cl-, net charge positive) and cytoplasm on the right (many K+, net charge negative), with K+ leaving through a channel](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-ion-distribution-ecf-cytoplasm.jpg)

## Key features

The transmembrane potential is generated by diffusion of ions down electrochemical gradients. This happens because charged ions are heterogeneously distributed across the surface membrane.

| Ion | Inside the cell | Extracellular fluid |
|---|---|---|
| K+ | Relatively high | Very little |
| Anionic proteins (Pr-) | Relatively high | Virtually none |
| Na+ | Relatively low | High |
| Cl- | Relatively low | High |

- The membrane is slightly permeable to K+, Na+, and Cl-
- The membrane is impermeable to Pr-
- In the figure, K+ moves out through a channel, leaving the cytoplasm with a net negative charge and the extracellular fluid with a net positive charge

> Cells keep K+ and anionic proteins high inside and Na+ and Cl- high outside, and the membrane is impermeable to the anionic proteins.

Library: Potassium equilibrium potential and opposing forces
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Model cell permeable only to K+, with K+ leaving through a channel and negative charges left behind; text notes outside K+ 5 mM and inside K+ 150 mM, force 1 concentration gradient and force 2 electrical gradient, and graphs show outside K+ rising and Vm falling until both plateau at about -90.8 mV](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-potassium-equilibrium-opposing-forces.jpg)

## How K+ reaches equilibrium

The lecture's model cell has [K+]o = 5 mM and [K+]i = 150 mM and is permeable only to K+.

1. K+ diffuses out of the cell down its concentration gradient (force 1)
2. Each K+ that leaves leaves a negative charge behind, which attracts K+ back in (force 2, the electrical gradient)
3. When the two opposing forces equal each other, net movement of K+ is zero
