4. Vm plateaus at about -90.8 mV, the equilibrium potential for K+

## Nernst potential

The transmembrane potential at which the electrical force across the membrane exactly opposes the concentration force for a particular ion is the Nernst potential, or equilibrium potential. At the equilibrium potential:

- Ions are in equilibrium
- There is no net flux of ions across the membrane
- Each ion has its own specific Nernst potential

Whenever possible, ions tend to be in equilibrium, meaning they'll try to set the transmembrane potential equal to their own equilibrium potential.

## Predicting the response to added Na+

![Membrane potential trace starting at -91 mV; after Na+ is injected into the cell Vm rises to about -45 mV, then returns to -91 mV as K+ ions move out and bring Vm back to the K+ equilibrium](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-sodium-injection-vm-trace.jpg)

Adding Na+ inside a cell that's still permeable only to K+ moves Vm to about -45 mV. K+ then moves out, because there's less negative charge keeping it in, and Vm returns to the K+ equilibrium potential.

> At an ion's equilibrium potential the concentration force and the electrical force are equal and opposite, so there's no net flux of that ion.

Library: Nernst equation and equilibrium potentials
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

## Key features

- The equilibrium potential of an ion is calculated with the Nernst equation
- It's mostly determined by the ratio of that ion's concentrations outside and inside the membrane
- Other factors are constants: the gas constant, temperature, and the valence of the ion
- The larger the concentration ratio, the larger the value of the equilibrium potential

## The equation as the lecture gives it

- E_ion = RT/zF * ln([K+]o/[K+]i)
- E_ion = 58.2 * log10([K+]o/[K+]i)
- At body temperature (37°C): E_ion = 61.5 * log10([K+]o/[K+]i)

| Symbol | Meaning |
|---|---|
| E_ion | Equilibrium potential for the permeant ion |
| R | Universal gas constant (8.31 joules/mole/Kelvin) |
| T | Temperature in Kelvin (°C + 273) |
| F | Faraday constant (96,500 coulombs/mole) |
| z | Valence (electrical charge) of the ion |
| ln | Natural log |
| [ion]o and [ion]i | Outside and inside concentrations of the ion |

## Equilibrium potentials of key ions in mammalian cells

| Ion | Cytosolic | Extracellular | E at 37°C |
|---|---|---|---|
| K+ | High | Low | Negative |
| Na+ | Low | High | Positive |
| Ca2+ | 100 nM | 1.2 mM | +126 mV |
| Cl- | 9 mM | 150 mM | -70 mV |

- Because Cl- is negatively charged, the order of the concentration terms is reversed compared with Na+ and K+
- The lecture's worked K+ example (5 mM outside, 150 mM inside) gives an equilibrium potential of about -90.8 mV

> At 37°C the lecture's simplified Nernst equation is E_ion = 61.5 * log10([ion]o/[ion]i), so the equilibrium potential depends only on the concentration ratio across the membrane.

Library: Resting membrane potential by cell type
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Table titled Most Cells Have Membrane Potentials listing membrane potential in mV for neuron, skeletal muscle, cardiac muscle (bracketed together), adipose cell, thyroid cell, fibroblast, yeast, Neurospora crassa, E. coli, and mitochondria, beside a diagram of a potentiometer with a salt-filled microelectrode in the cytosol and a reference electrode in the bathing medium](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-resting-potential-cell-types-table.jpg)

## Key features

- Different cell types have different transmembrane potentials
- This is due to different ionic concentration profiles across the membrane
- It's also due to differences in the relative membrane permeability to those ions
- The potential is measured with a potentiometer between a microelectrode filled with conducting salt solution, placed in the cytosol, and a reference electrode in contact with the bathing medium

## Values from the lecture's table

| Cell type | Membrane potential |
|---|---|
| Skeletal muscle | -85 mV |
| Cardiac muscle | -90 mV |
| Adipose cell | -40 mV |
| Thyroid cell | -40 mV |
| Fibroblast | -10 mV |
| Yeast | -120 mV |
| Neurospora crassa | -200 mV |
| E. coli | -140 mV |
| Mitochondria | -140 mV |

The table brackets neuron, skeletal muscle, and cardiac muscle together as the excitable cells.

> Membrane potential differs by cell type because of differences in ionic concentration profiles and in relative membrane permeability to those ions.

Library: Relative permeability and resting membrane potential
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Diagram of a membrane with a labeled K+ channel and Na+ channel, with the permeability ratio PK+:PNa+:PCl- approximately 50:1:10 boxed below](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-relative-permeability-k-na-cl.jpg)

## Key features

- The ability of a particular ion to influence Vrest is largely determined by the relative permeability of the membrane to that ion
- The more permeable the membrane is to an ion, the stronger the influence of that ion's equilibrium potential on Vrest
- When permeability to an ion increases, that ion moves across the membrane down its electrochemical gradient, displacing Vrest toward its equilibrium potential
- Under resting conditions, membrane permeability is much higher for K+ than for Na+ and Cl-
- At rest, PK+:PNa+:PCl- is about 50:1:10

## How Vrest is set

The resting membrane potential of a cell is determined by the combined action of the membrane's relative permeability to each ion and that ion's equilibrium potential. The Goldman-Hodgkin-Katz (GHK) equation describes this quantitatively. The lecture's figure of the resting membrane shows K+ channels, a Na+ channel, and the Na+-K+ pump side by side.

> Vrest moves toward the equilibrium potential of the ion the membrane is most permeable to, and at rest that's K+ (PK+:PNa+:PCl- about 50:1:10).

Library: Ion channel properties and gating
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Diagram of an ion channel in the membrane changing from closed to open (gating) with a change in Vm, with a large arrow showing ion flow through the open pore from extracellular to intracellular side](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-channel-gating-closed-open.jpg)

## Key features

- Biological membranes are largely impermeable to electrically charged ions
- Ions cross the membrane through highly specialized membrane proteins, the ion channels

Ion channel properties:

- High rate of ion exchange
- Ion specificity
- Susceptible to regulation

## Gating

Ion channels can be in a closed or open configuration. Opening or closing can be triggered by different factors:

| Trigger | Channel type |
|---|---|
| Change in membrane potential | Voltage-gated |
| Binding of a neurotransmitter | Ligand-gated |
| Stretching of the membrane | Stretch-activated |

Other triggers exist as well. In the open configuration, ions flow through the membrane and generate a current and a change in membrane potential.

> Ion channels open or close (gate) in response to voltage, ligand binding, or membrane stretch, and ion flow through an open channel creates a current that changes the membrane potential.

Library: Leak, voltage-gated, ligand-gated, and signal-gated channels
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Diagram of four channel types in the membrane between exterior and cytosol: (a) resting K+ channel, boxed as leak K+ channels, always open and setting the resting membrane potential; (b) voltage-gated Na+ channel opening with a change in membrane potential; (c) ligand-gated channel opening when an extracellular neurotransmitter binds; (d) signal-gated channel opening when an intracellular molecule binds](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-ion-channel-types-leak-voltage-ligand-signal.jpg)

## Key features

| Channel | What opens it |
|---|---|
| Resting (leak) K+ channel | Always open |
| Voltage-gated channel | Opens transiently in response to a change in membrane potential |
| Ligand-gated channel | Opens (or closes) in response to a specific extracellular neurotransmitter |
| Signal-gated channel | Opens (or closes) in response to a specific intracellular molecule |

- The leak K+ channels are the ones the lecture highlights for the resting membrane potential
- In the figure, the resting K+ channel lets K+ move out of the cell, with positive charge outside and negative charge inside at rest
- The voltage-gated, ligand-gated, and signal-gated channels in the figure each let Na+ move into the cell once open
- The figure also shows the opposite gradients of the two ions, with Na+ higher outside and K+ higher inside

> Leak K+ channels are always open and underlie the resting membrane potential, while gated channels open only in response to voltage, a ligand, or an intracellular signal.

Library: Voltage-gated Na+ and K+ channel states
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Diagram of a voltage-gated channel closed at -70 mV and open at -50 mV, with an ion moving through the open channel down its concentration gradient, between extracellular fluid and cytosol](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-voltage-gated-channel-closed-open.jpg)

![Diagram of voltage-gated Na+ and K+ channels: both closed in a polarized membrane (normal state, positive outside and negative inside), and both open in a depolarized membrane (negative outside and positive inside) with Na+ moving in and K+ moving out](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-voltage-gated-na-k-polarized-depolarized.jpg)

## Identify it

- Two side-by-side states of the same channel, closed and open
- The label that changes between panels is the membrane voltage or the charge across the membrane, not a bound ligand
- Na+ and K+ channels drawn together, closed in the polarized membrane and open in the depolarized one

## Key features

- In a polarized (normal state) membrane, the outside is positive and the inside is negative, and voltage-gated Na+ and K+ channels are closed
- Depolarization of the membrane opens the channels
- Through the open Na+ channel, Na+ moves into the cell
- Through the open K+ channel, K+ moves out of the cell
- In the first figure, the open channel lets an ion move down its concentration gradient

> Depolarization opens voltage-gated channels, letting Na+ flow into the cell and K+ flow out.

Library: Ligand-gated, mechanically gated, and leak channels
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

## Ligand-gated channel

![Diagram of a ligand-gated channel closed until the neurotransmitter ACh binds its receptor site, then open with ions moving in response to their gradients; arrows show Na+ and Ca2+ concentration gradients pointing into the cell and the K+ concentration gradient pointing out](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-ligand-gated-channel-ach.jpg)

- A neurotransmitter, the ligand, is required to open the channel (ACh in the figure)
- The neurotransmitter attaches to the receptor site, and this chemical stimulus opens the channel
- Ions move in response to their gradients: Na+ and Ca2+ gradients point into the cell and the K+ gradient points out

## Mechanically gated channel

![Diagram of a mechanically gated channel closed by a lid-like cap, then open with Na+ and Ca2+ ions passing through the pore between extracellular fluid and cytosol](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-mechanically-gated-channel.jpg)

- The channel opens with a mechanical stimulus such as stretching of the membrane (stretch-activated channels)
- In the figure, Na+ and Ca2+ pass through the open channel

## Leak channel

![Diagram of a leakage channel closed, then randomly opening so that ions move through it down their concentration gradient from cytosol to extracellular fluid](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-leak-channel-random-opening.jpg)

- A leakage channel randomly opens
- Ions then move through it down their concentration gradient

## Comparing the three

| Channel | Opens with |
|---|---|
| Ligand-gated | Neurotransmitter binding to its receptor site |
| Mechanically gated | Mechanical force, such as membrane stretch |
| Leak | Random opening |

Once any of these channels is open, ions flow through the membrane down their gradients, generating a current and a change in membrane potential.

> Ligand-gated channels open when a neurotransmitter binds, mechanically gated channels open with membrane stretch, and leak channels open randomly.

Library: Nerve resting potential: K+ leak, Na+ leak, and the pump
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Diagram of the nerve resting membrane potential: Na+ higher outside and K+ higher inside; K+ loss through abundant leakage channels establishes a negative membrane potential; Na+ entry through leakage channels reduces the negative potential slightly; Na+-K+ ATPases (pumps) maintain the concentration gradients, resulting in the resting membrane potential](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-nerve-resting-potential-leak-and-pump.jpg)

## Building the resting potential step by step

1. If the nerve membrane were permeable only to K+, Vrest would equal EK+
2. Adding some permeability to Na+ lets ENa+ contribute to Vrest, shifting the membrane toward ENa+
3. Because PK+ is much higher than PNa+, EK+ still dominates, and ENa+ only depolarizes the membrane by a few mV
4. If the membrane were mostly permeable to Na+, Vrest would equal ENa+
5. The Na+-K+ pump also contributes to the resting membrane potential

## Key features

- K+ loss through abundant leakage channels establishes a negative membrane potential
- Na+ entry through leakage channels reduces the negative membrane potential slightly
- Na+-K+ ATPases (pumps) maintain the concentration gradients that the resting potential depends on
- The figure shows Na+ concentration higher outside the cell and K+ concentration higher inside

> K+ leak sets a negative resting potential near EK+, a small Na+ leak makes it slightly less negative, and the Na+-K+ pump maintains the gradients.

Library: Na+-K+ pump and the resting potential
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Six-step diagram of the Na+-K+ pump: (a) Na+ binds from the intracellular fluid, (b) with three Na+ bound, ATP is split to ADP and the pump is phosphorylated, (c) three Na+ are released to the extracellular fluid, (d) K+ binds from outside and the phosphate is released, (e) two K+ are bound, (f) K+ is released into the cell](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-na-k-pump-cycle.jpg)

## Key features

- The Na+-K+ pump is an active ion transport mechanism that consumes ATP when it's active
- It moves 3 Na+ ions out while it moves 2 K+ ions in
- This imbalanced flow of charge leaves an excess of negative charge inside the membrane, so the pump is electrogenic
- It contributes about -4 mV to Vrest
- It's critical for maintaining ion gradients across the membrane, leading to accumulation of K+ inside the cell

## Reading the pump cycle

1. Na+ binds the pump from the intracellular side
2. ATP is split to ADP and the pump is phosphorylated
3. Three Na+ are released to the extracellular fluid
4. K+ binds from outside and the phosphate is released
5. Two K+ are carried across
6. K+ is released into the cell

> The Na+-K+ pump moves 3 Na+ out and 2 K+ in using ATP, which makes it electrogenic (about -4 mV of Vrest) and maintains the ion gradients.

Library: Goldman-Hodgkin-Katz equation
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

## The equation as the lecture gives it

Vm = (RT/F) ln[ (pK[K+]o + pNa[Na+]o + pCl[Cl-]i) / (pK[K+]i + pNa[Na+]i + pCl[Cl-]o) ]

- p is the membrane permeability to each ion
- o and i are the outside and inside concentrations
- The Cl- terms are flipped compared with K+ and Na+ (inside concentration on top, outside on the bottom), because Cl- is negatively charged

## Key features

- The GHK equation combines each ion's relative permeability with its concentration gradient to give the resting membrane potential
- At rest, PK+:PNa+:PCl- is about 50:1:10
- So the Nernst potential for K+ is about 50 times more influential than the Nernst potential for Na+ in determining Vrest of the spinal motor neuron
- The Vrest of a spinal motor neuron is about -75 mV, a value much closer to EK than to ENa

## Why it matters

The Nernst equation gives the equilibrium potential for one ion. The GHK equation combines all the permeant ions, each weighted by its permeability, which is why Vrest lands closest to EK when K+ permeability dominates at rest.

> The GHK equation weights each ion by its permeability, and because PK+:PNa+:PCl- is about 50:1:10 at rest, Vrest sits much closer to EK than to ENa.

Library: Causes of hypokalemia and hyperkalemia
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

## Hypokalemia (low K+ in the fluid)

- Inadequate intake of K+ is very unlikely, even with the worst diet, but it's seen with anorexia and bulimia (for example, irregular heartbeats)
- Use of diuretics, which get rid of excess fluid and salts; most (not all) increase K+ excretion
- Diuretics are abused by athletes in sports where weight matters and are given to people with a buildup of fluid (edema), such as people with high blood pressure
- Hyperaldosteronism: high aldosterone increases Na+ reabsorption and K+ secretion by the kidney, causing water retention along with sodium, and hypokalemia
- Excessive vomiting and diarrhea, especially in infants; replacement should provide fluids and, most importantly, electrolytes (the slide's example is Gatorade versus Pedialyte)

## Hyperkalemia (high K+ in the fluid)

- Kidney failure: excess K+ is normally removed by the kidneys, so kidney failure causes K+ imbalances
- Excessive administration of K+, as could happen in a hospital setting, such as giving KCl instead of NaCl saline for dehydration; this can cause fibrillation and death
- Addison's disease: decreased aldosterone secretion decreases kidney excretion of K+ and causes hyperkalemia

## Treatment of hyperkalemia

- It's difficult to get rid of excess potassium
- If the heart isn't in fibrillation (not a critical stage), give diuretics, which lower blood K+ but take a while
- Ion exchange resins pull K+ out into the feces
- Hemodialysis is used in kidney failure

| Aldosterone | Effect on K+ |
|---|---|
| High (hyperaldosteronism) | More K+ secretion, hypokalemia |
| Low (Addison's disease) | Less K+ excretion, hyperkalemia |

> Hyperaldosteronism causes hypokalemia, and decreased aldosterone (Addison's disease) causes hyperkalemia.

Library: Hypokalemia and hyperkalemia: effects on membrane potential
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Graph of membrane potential against extracellular K+ with K+:Na+ permeability 50:1: outside K+ of 2 mM gives -100 mV (hyperpolarized), 5 mM gives -70 mV (normal), and 10 mM gives -40 mV (depolarized)](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-extracellular-k-membrane-potential-graph.jpg)

## Why K+ matters most

Many diseases share a common mechanism, altered electrolyte balance. Excitable cells (nerves, muscle cells, cardiac myocytes) depend on the resting membrane potential, so changes in ion concentrations, especially K+, profoundly affect their function.

| Condition | Membrane change | Effect on activity |
|---|---|---|
| Hypokalemia | More K+ diffuses out, Vm more negative (hyperpolarized) | Less excitable, activity slows |
| Hyperkalemia | More K+ in the ECF, Vm less negative (depolarized) | Activity speeds up |

## Predicting the change

- In the lecture's graph (K+:Na+ permeability 50:1), raising [K+]out from 5 mM to 10 mM moves Vm from -70 mV to -40 mV, and lowering it to 2 mM moves Vm to -100 mV
- Hypokalemia slows things down: the brain slows, GI motility drops, heart rate weakens, and weakness develops
- A hyperpolarized cell is less sensitive to excitation, and severe hypokalemia can lead to a hyperpolarization block
- Hyperkalemia speeds things up: increased brain electrical activity with seizures, fibrillations, and twitching muscles
- A depolarized cell can't repolarize and becomes refractory, and severe hyperkalemia can cause a depolarization block

## Clinical symptoms

Severe muscle weakness, paresthesias, muscle cramps and spasms, ECG changes, cardiac arrhythmia, dysrhythmias, and seizures in CNS neurons.

> Hypokalemia hyperpolarizes cells and makes them less excitable, while hyperkalemia depolarizes them, and severe hyperkalemia can cause a depolarization block.

Library: Depolarization, hyperpolarization, overshoot, and repolarization
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Labeled action potential trace from RMP: a local graded potential reaches the threshold level, depolarization (opening of voltage-gated Na+ channels), the peak above 0 mV, repolarization (closure of Na+ and opening of voltage-gated K+ channels), and hyperpolarization (voltage-gated K+ channels remain open after the potential reaches resting level)](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-action-potential-labeled-phases.jpg)

## What changes the membrane potential

In excitable cells, electrical signals are mediated by changes in the resting membrane potential, which result from the flow of charged ions across the membrane. In most cases this comes from a change in membrane permeability, meaning the opening or closing of ion channels. Ion flow is determined by two factors, permeability and driving force (DF = Vrest - E_ion).

## Definitions

- **Depolarization**: membrane potential becomes more positive (increases)
- **Hyperpolarization**: membrane potential becomes more negative (decreases)
- **Overshoot**: membrane potential is from 0 mV to positive mV
- **Repolarization**: membrane potential moves back toward the resting membrane potential

## Reading the trace

- A local potential change (graded potential) brings the membrane from rest to the threshold level
- Depolarization comes from opening of voltage-gated Na+ channels
- Repolarization comes from closure of Na+ channels and opening of voltage-gated K+ channels
- The after-hyperpolarization happens because voltage-gated K+ channels remain open after the potential reaches resting level

There are two major classes of change in membrane potential, graded potentials and action potentials.

> Ion flow depends on permeability and driving force (Vrest - E_ion); Na+ channel opening depolarizes the membrane, and K+ channel opening repolarizes and then hyperpolarizes it.

Library: Membrane potential terminology
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

## Terms describing the membrane potential

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| Potential (potential difference) | Voltage difference between two points due to separated charges of opposite sign |
| Membrane potential | Voltage difference between the inside and outside of a cell |
| Equilibrium potential | Voltage across a membrane producing a flux of an ion equal but opposite to the flux from its concentration gradient |
| Resting membrane potential | Steady potential of an unstimulated cell |
| Graded potential | Change of variable amplitude and duration, conducted decrementally; no threshold or refractory period |
| Action potential | Brief all-or-none depolarization that reverses polarity in neurons; has threshold and refractory period; conducted without decrement |
| Synaptic potential | Graded change in the postsynaptic neuron after neurotransmitter release; EPSP (depolarizing) or IPSP (hyperpolarizing) |
| Receptor potential | Graded potential at the peripheral endings of afferent neurons (or in receptor cells) in response to a stimulus |
| Pacemaker potential | Spontaneous graded potential change in certain specialized cells |
| Threshold potential | Membrane potential at which an action potential is initiated |

## Key distinctions

- Graded potentials have no threshold and no refractory period and fade with distance
- Action potentials have a threshold and a refractory period and travel without decrement
- Synaptic potentials can go either way: an EPSP depolarizes and an IPSP hyperpolarizes

> A graded potential varies in size and decays with distance with no threshold, while an action potential is all-or-none, has a threshold and refractory period, and is conducted without decrement.

Library: Graded potentials and their properties
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Four sets of graded potential traces from -70 mV: (a) a depolarization and a hyperpolarization after stimuli, (b) a small response to a weak stimulus and a larger one to a strong stimulus, (c) a response measured at the stimulus site and a smaller one measured 1 mm away, (d) temporal summation of repeated X stimuli and spatial summation of X plus Y](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-graded-potential-properties-traces.jpg)

## Key features

- Graded potentials are brief, relatively small changes in membrane potential
- They're called graded because their amplitude varies with the magnitude of the stimulus that evokes them
- They result from changes in membrane permeability due to opening or closing of ion channels

## Types

- **Receptor potential**: a change in membrane potential arising from a sensory transduction process; typically depolarizing
- **Synaptic potential**: a synaptically released neurotransmitter binds postsynaptic receptors, causing a depolarizing or hyperpolarizing change
- **Pacemaker potential**: a gradual increase in voltage across the membrane of pacemaker cells between action potentials

## General properties

1. Depolarizing (excitatory) or hyperpolarizing (inhibitory)
2. Amplitude is proportional to the intensity of the stimulus
3. Amplitude decays over time and with distance from where the signal was generated
4. They show temporal and spatial summation

Summation of graded potentials is a common mechanism by which action potentials are evoked.

> Graded potentials can be excitatory or inhibitory, scale with stimulus strength, decay with time and distance, and summate.

Library: Temporal and spatial summation of graded potentials
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Membrane potential trace with a dashed threshold potential line above the resting potential: single Ex1 inputs stay below threshold; temporal summation of two quick Ex1 inputs reaches threshold and fires an action potential; spatial summation of Ex1 plus Ex2 fires an action potential; Ex1 plus In1 cancel (EPSP-IPSP cancellation); small neuron diagrams show one excitatory input, two excitatory inputs, and one excitatory plus one inhibitory input](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-summation-ex-in-inputs-trace.jpg)

## Key features

- In most cases, action potentials in neurons are evoked by summation of underlying graded potentials
- EPSPs (excitatory postsynaptic potentials) and IPSPs (inhibitory postsynaptic potentials) sum at the axon hillock, and an action potential fires when the sum reaches the threshold of activation

## Types of summation

| Type | What happens |
|---|---|
| Temporal summation | The same presynaptic terminal is activated repeatedly within a brief time, and the graded potentials add into a single larger potential |
| Spatial summation | Separate presynaptic terminals release neurotransmitter at the same time (or within a very narrow time frame) onto separate membrane regions, and the potentials add |
| EPSP-IPSP cancellation | Excitatory and inhibitory graded potentials cancel each other out |

## Reading the trace

- One excitatory input alone (Ex1) gives a small depolarization that stays below threshold
- Many excitatory potentials from one neuron arriving quickly can reach threshold (temporal)
- Excitatory potentials from many neurons arriving together can reach threshold (spatial)
- An excitatory and an inhibitory input together (Ex1 + In1) cancel and don't reach threshold

> Temporal summation is repeated input from one presynaptic terminal, spatial summation is simultaneous input from separate terminals, and either can bring the axon hillock to threshold.

Library: Graded potentials vs. action potentials
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

## Look-alikes

| Feature | Graded (electrotonic) potential | Action potential |
|---|---|---|
| Initiation | Subthreshold depolarization or hyperpolarization | Depolarization to or above threshold |
| Process | Passive, dependent on resistive and capacitive membrane properties | Active, dependent on gating of voltage-dependent ion channels |
| Response | Graded | All-or-none |
| With distance | Amplitude decreases along the membrane | Amplitude stays constant along the membrane |
| Threshold and refractory period | Neither | Both |

## Key features

- Graded potentials can be depolarizing or hyperpolarizing, and their size follows stimulus strength
- Action potentials are brief all-or-none depolarizations that reverse polarity in neurons
- Graded potentials are conducted decrementally, while action potentials are conducted without decrement
- Graded potentials that summate to threshold are what usually trigger an action potential

> Graded potentials are passive, graded, and decremental, while action potentials are active (voltage-gated channels), all-or-none, and keep their amplitude with distance.

Library: Action potential waveforms by cell type
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Action potential traces compared: brief spikes in a neuron and in skeletal muscle, a ventricular cardiac myocyte action potential with a long plateau, a cardiac pacemaker cell action potential with a slow rising ramp, and types of smooth muscle action potentials (spike, plateau, and spikes riding on slow waves)](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-action-potential-waveforms-cell-types.jpg)

## Key features

| Cell | Waveform |
|---|---|
| Neuron | Brief spike |
| Skeletal muscle | Brief spike |
| Ventricular cardiac myocyte | Fast upstroke followed by a long plateau |
| Cardiac pacemaker cell | Slow depolarizing ramp leading into each action potential |
| Smooth muscle | Spike, plateau, or spikes on top of slow waves |

## Unitary smooth muscle

- Typical action potential (spike potential) elicited by an external stimulus
- Repetitive spike potentials elicited by slow waves that occur spontaneously
- Action potential with a plateau, which produces a prolonged contraction
- Voltage-gated Ca2+ channels are important for action potential generation in unitary smooth muscle, not Na+ channels

## Identify it

- A long flat plateau lasting hundreds of milliseconds points to a ventricular cardiac myocyte or a plateau-type smooth muscle action potential
- A slow ramp with no flat resting baseline points to a pacemaker cell
- Bursts of spikes on top of a slow oscillation point to smooth muscle slow waves

> Neuron and skeletal muscle action potentials are brief spikes, cardiac ventricular action potentials have a plateau, and unitary smooth muscle action potentials depend on voltage-gated Ca2+ channels rather than Na+ channels.

Library: Action potential phases and Na+ and K+ permeability
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Two graphs: (a) three phases of an action potential, with phase 1 rising from threshold, phase 2 falling, and phase 3 dipping below the resting potential; (b) permeability changes during the action potential, with PNa rising sharply and briefly as Na+ channels open, and PK rising later and staying high longer after K+ channels open](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-action-potential-phases-pna-pk.jpg)

## Key features

The action potential is a plot of membrane potential over time. It runs from the resting membrane potential, through the action potential, and back to the resting state. Membrane depolarization increases the permeability to both Na+ and K+, but the two changes don't happen at the same time. K+ permeability rises more slowly than Na+ permeability and stays increased for a relatively longer time.

| Phase | Permeability | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Depolarizing (rising) | Only PNa+ increased | Membrane depolarizes and overshoots |
| Repolarizing (falling) | PNa+ decays while PK+ increases | Membrane repolarizes |
| After-hyperpolarization | PK+ still high | Extra hyperpolarization beyond the resting potential |

## Sequence on the trace

1. Sodium channels open at threshold, and depolarization begins
2. Potassium channels open, more slowly
3. Sodium channels close at the peak
4. Repolarization follows as K+ leaves
5. Potassium channels start to close during the after-hyperpolarization, and the resting state returns

> During the action potential PNa+ rises first and briefly, while PK+ rises later and stays high longer, which causes repolarization and then after-hyperpolarization.

Library: Myelination and action potential propagation
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Diagram of a neuron with axon hillock and myelinated axon, enlarged to show myelin sheath segments separated by nodes of Ranvier; local current flows inside the axon from an active node to the next node, and the direction of action potential propagation jumps from node to node](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-myelinated-axon-saltatory-propagation.jpg)

## Key features

- The ability of action potentials to propagate down a nerve is strongly modulated by myelination
- A myelinated fiber is an axon wrapped many times by the membrane of a specialized glial cell, the Schwann cell
- This membrane, the myelin sheath, contains the lipid sphingomyelin, which electrically insulates the axon and dramatically reduces the ability of ions to flow across it
- Nodes of Ranvier are very brief (2 to 3 micrometer) uninsulated spaces between two adjacent Schwann cells
- In a myelinated fiber, action potentials occur only at the nodes of Ranvier, which gives saltatory conduction

## Non-myelinated vs. myelinated

![Comparison of non-myelinated propagation, with action potentials regenerated continuously along the whole axon, and myelinated propagation, with action potentials only at the gaps between myelin segments, labeled saltatory conduction](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-nonmyelinated-vs-myelinated-propagation.jpg)

| Fiber | Where action potentials occur |
|---|---|
| Non-myelinated | Continuously along the entire axon |
| Myelinated | Only at the nodes of Ranvier |

> In a myelinated fiber, the insulating myelin sheath confines action potentials to the nodes of Ranvier, producing saltatory conduction.

Library: Demyelinating disorders of the CNS
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

## Key findings

Myelin insulates the axon and lets action potentials jump between nodes of Ranvier, so disorders that strip myelin disrupt action potential propagation. The lecture groups CNS demyelinating disorders into five categories.

| Category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Inflammatory and immune | MS, optic neuritis, ADEM, paraneoplastic encephalomyelitis, rheumatoid arthritis, systemic lupus erythematosus, Behcet's disease, Sjogren disease |
| Infectious | HIV, PML, Lyme disease, neurosyphilis, HTLV-1 |
| Granulomatous | Sarcoidosis, Wegener granulomatosis, lymphoid granulomatosis |
| Myelin disorders | Metachromatic leukodystrophy, adrenoleukodystrophy/adrenomyeloneuropathy, globoid cell (Krabbe's) leukodystrophy, Alexander disease, Canavan disease |
| Toxic and metabolic | B12 deficiency, central pontine myelinolysis, carbon monoxide, radiation, PRES |

- Multiple sclerosis is the inflammatory and immune example listed first
- The leukodystrophies sit in the myelin disorders group
- B12 deficiency and central pontine myelinolysis are the toxic and metabolic causes

> CNS demyelinating disorders fall into inflammatory/immune, infectious, granulomatous, myelin (leukodystrophy), and toxic/metabolic groups.

Library: Channel states in the nerve and skeletal muscle action potential
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Cycle diagram around an action potential trace showing voltage-gated Na+ and K+ channel states at five numbered stages: 1 resting state, 2 depolarization to threshold, 3 rising phase, 4 falling phase, 5 undershoot; the Na+ channel's inactivation loop is labeled](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-nerve-action-potential-channel-states.jpg)

## Channel states at each stage

1. **Resting state**: the gated Na+ and K+ channels are closed, and ungated channels maintain the resting potential
2. **Depolarization**: a stimulus opens some Na+ channels, and Na+ inflow depolarizes the membrane; if depolarization reaches threshold, it triggers an action potential
3. **Rising phase**: depolarization opens most Na+ channels while K+ channels remain closed, and Na+ influx makes the inside positive relative to the outside
4. **Falling phase**: most Na+ channels become inactivated, blocking Na+ inflow, and most K+ channels open, so K+ outflow makes the inside negative again
5. **Undershoot**: Na+ channels close but some K+ channels are still open; as these K+ channels close and Na+ channels become unblocked, the membrane returns to its resting state

## Key features

- The Na+ channel has an inactivation loop that blocks Na+ inflow during the falling phase
- Stimuli that don't reach threshold produce failed initiations, with no action potential
- K+ channels that stay open cause the hyperpolarization after the spike
- The Na+-K+ pump maintains the resting potential

> During the falling phase Na+ channels are inactivated and K+ channels are open, and K+ channels still open afterward cause the undershoot.

Library: Absolute and relative refractory periods
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

![Nerve action potential with the absolute refractory period shaded red over the upstroke and peak (about the first millisecond) and the relative refractory period shaded yellow over repolarization and after-hyperpolarization](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-absolute-relative-refractory-period.jpg)

## Absolute refractory period

- Another stimulus, no matter how strong, won't lead to a second action potential
- It lasts from the opening of the Na+ channels until the channels reset just after the peak
- Opening of Na+ channels spontaneously leads to their inactivation, and inactivated channels can't be reopened

## Relative refractory period

- A strong enough stimulus (greater than originally needed) may start another action potential
- Na+ channels begin to recover from inactivation

## Function

- Ensures that each action potential is an all-or-none event
- Enforces one-way transmission of nerve impulses

## Cardiac comparison

![Ventricular cardiac action potential: voltage-gated channels open and Na+ influx cause rapid depolarization, Na+ channels close at the peak, slow Ca2+ channels open to form the plateau, slow Ca2+ channels close and repolarization follows, and K+ channels close at the end; a long absolute refractory period spans most of the action potential, followed by a short relative refractory period](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-cardiac-action-potential-refractory.jpg)

In the ventricular cardiac action potential, slow Ca2+ channels hold a plateau, and the absolute refractory period covers most of the action potential before a short relative refractory period.

> During the absolute refractory period inactivated Na+ channels can't reopen, so no stimulus can fire a second action potential, and during the relative refractory period a stronger than normal stimulus can.

Library: Natural toxins that target ion channels
Folder: MSK / Week 13 / Membrane Properties of Excitable Cells: Ion Channels and Action Potentials

## Key findings

Toxins and venoms act by targeting ion channels. The lecture gives these examples.

| Toxin | Source | Action |
|---|---|---|
| Maculotoxin | Blue-ringed octopus | Blocks sodium channels |
| Batrachotoxin | Poison arrow frog | Prevents sodium channels from closing |
| Apamin | Honey bee | Blocks Ca2+-activated potassium channels |
| Capsaicin | Cayenne pepper | Excites peripheral nerve endings |

## Blue-ringed octopus venom

The venom can cause nausea, respiratory arrest, heart failure, severe and sometimes total paralysis, and blindness, and it can kill within minutes if not treated. Death is usually from suffocation due to paralysis of the diaphragm.

## Fugu (Japanese pufferfish)

- Fugu can be lethally poisonous and must be carefully prepared by specially licensed chefs in Japan
- The lecture also shows a headline suggesting the Japanese pufferfish could be the ultimate painkiller

## Apamin and the action potential

The lecture pairs apamin with the action potential trace and boxes the late repolarization and after-hyperpolarization, the part of the trace where potassium channels start to close.

> Maculotoxin blocks Na+ channels, batrachotoxin keeps Na+ channels from closing, and apamin blocks Ca2+-activated K+ channels.

![Diagram of thermoTRP channels placed along a temperature scale from 0 to 60, each drawn as six membrane-spanning segments: TRPA1 (horseradish, cinnamon, garlic), TRPM8 (mint), TRPV4, TRPV3 (camphor), TRPV1 (chili), and TRPV2, with ankyrin binding repeats, a TRP box, and a PDZ binding site marked](library/images/msk-week-13/memb-thermotrp-channels-activators.jpg)

## PIEZO channels and the baroreceptor reflex

The lecture shows research on how arterial baroreceptors sense blood pressure. Stretch-sensitive baroreceptor neurons give acute control of heart rate and blood pressure.

- The mechanically activated ion channels PIEZO1 and PIEZO2 are together required for baroreception
- Removing both Piezo1 and Piezo2 in the nodose and petrosal sensory ganglia of mice abolished the drug-induced baroreflex and aortic depressor nerve (AND) activity
- Mice lacking Piezos had labile hypertension and increased blood pressure variability
- Activating Piezo2-positive sensory afferents was enough to start the baroreflex
- A second study shown reports that arterial baroreceptors sense blood pressure through decorated aortic claws, formed by PIEZO2 fibers of the aortic depressor nerve

## ThermoTRP channels

ThermoTRPs are TRP channels that function across a range of temperatures, and the figure links each one to natural compounds.

| Channel | Compounds shown |
|---|---|
| TRPA1 | Horseradish, cinnamon, garlic |
| TRPM8 | Mint |
| TRPV3 | Camphor |
| TRPV1 | Chili |
| TRPV4, TRPV2 | None shown |

> PIEZO1 and PIEZO2 are mechanically activated channels required for baroreceptor sensing of blood pressure, and thermoTRP channels respond to temperature and to compounds such as mint (TRPM8) and chili (TRPV1).
